# BIB215 — (untitled)

## Course objectives

- 1. Discuss key events in the life and ministry of the apostle Paul, including the background issues that prompted his letter to the Roman church.
- 2. Defend Paul’s right to be called an apostle, missionary, theologian, and servant of Jesus Christ.
- 3. Describe how Paul develops in his letter to the Romans the gospel message involving people’s need of a Savior, God’s provision of a Savior, God’s shaping process, and the Holy Spirit’s empowering of the believer.
- 4. Evaluate Paul’s discourse on Israel’s election, unbelief, and restoration and relate this to his primary ministry to Gentiles.
- 5. Apply in your cultural context and in your own life Paul’s practical teaching with respect to living the Christian life and relating to all people, including non-Christians, authorities, and other believers.
- 6. Preach or teach the gospel message as expounded by the apostle Paul in his letter to the Roman church.

## 1 - Introduction to Romans

### The Apostle Paul

#### 1.1.1 — Identify characteristics that describe the apostle Paul prior to and following his conversion.

In order to get a better grasp of Paul’s letter to the Roman church, we will explore his life—who he was, where he came from, and what he did. First, we will review how he portrays himself. Then, we will consider his activities before he met Christ. Finally, we will survey his work as an apostle of Jesus Christ.

A Self-Portrait

In his letters to the churches, Paul paints a portrait of himself, but it is in pieces. We need to find these pieces scattered throughout his writings and put them together. His self-disclosure reveals the kind of person he was and what influenced his ministry. You will benefit by reading each Scripture passage cited in his self-portrait.

I am a Jew (Acts 21:39)

“I am a Jew.” This is how Paul describes himself to the Roman commander and his troops that had just rescued him from an angry mob trying to kill him. And no, Paul was not that Egyptian that the commander asked about who led a band of terrorists. Paul simply says, “I am a Jew from Tarsus in Cilicia” (Acts 21:39).

Apart from his conversion, perhaps the most important key to understanding Paul is his Jewish heritage. Although he was an apostle to the Gentiles, he constantly identified himself as a Jew, even when talking to Gentiles.

He explained to the Philippians that he was “a Hebrew [born] of Hebrews” (3:5). He was a true Hebrew of unmixed ancestry. Both of his parents were Jews. In his day, Jews were categorized as either Hebrew or Hellenist. Hebraic Jews held firmly to Jewish customs and culture and spoke Hebrew or Aramaic. (Of course, many also spoke Greek.) Hellenists, on the other hand, were generally born outside of Palestine*, spoke Greek and embraced Greek views and practices. Their synagogue worship was in Greek rather than in the Hebrew language.

We could classify Paul as a Hellenist since he was born outside of Palestine, spoke Greek fluently, and knew that culture. However, he insisted on identifying himself as a Hebrew.

Paul grew up in Tarsus, an important commercial center. And as a center of Greek and Roman culture, Tarsus established a university (considered the third best at that time after Athens and Alexandria). It is unlikely that Paul studied there or that his strict Jewish parents permitted him to be involved with Gentiles. Nevertheless, this environment undoubtedly influenced his outlook on life and later proved beneficial in his ministry to Gentiles.

An Apostle of the Jews

Paul began his education at an early age, and through diligent study he became a doctor of Jewish law and qualified to teach. Eventually, he became an influential religious leader. Whether or not he was a member of the Sanhedrin is uncertain.

Gamaliel, the leading Pharisee of his day, taught Paul. When he says, “Under Gamaliel I was thoroughly trained in the law of our fathers” (Acts 22:3), he means that he received the best Jewish education possible. Not only was Gamaliel the most famous Jewish teacher of the first century, but he was also highly influential and “honored by all the people” (Acts 5:34–40).

Paul’s statement that he was “brought up in this city” (Acts 22:3) suggests that he went to Jerusalem at an early age—perhaps as a teenager. His parents probably wanted him to receive an orthodox education as well as to avoid corruption from Gentile contact.

The Pharisees erroneously taught that the oral law (or tradition) was equal to the written Word. Jesus confronted the Pharisees and teachers of the Law on this point. “Why,” he asked, “do you break the command of God for the sake of your tradition? . . . You nullify the word of God for the sake of your tradition” (Matthew 15:3, 6).

As a doctor of the Law and a strict Pharisee, Paul defended the Jewish faith before becoming a defender of the Christian faith. For instance, he demonstrated great zeal in debating the Sadducees—the liberals of his day who did not believe in spirits, angels, or the resurrection.

Why does Paul refer to his persecution of the church to illustrate his zeal (Philippians 3:6)? Understand that before his conversion he was convinced that Jesus’ followers were heretics. In Paul’s mind, he was defending the truth. Do you recall what Jesus said earlier to His disciples? “They will put you out of the synagogue, in fact, a time is coming when anyone who kills you will think he is offering a service to God” (John 16:2). That is what Paul thought.

Paul’s Missionary Journeys

FIRST JOURNEY

circa AD 45–49

Acts 13:4–14:28

The Syrian Antioch church commissioned and sent out Paul and Barnabas, accompanied by John Mark.

They began their ministry on Cyprus, then traveled to the southern region of the province of Galatia, where they focused their efforts. Great numbers of people responded to the gospel. In spite of opposition by the Jews, the apostles planted churches in Pisidian Antioch, Iconium, Lystra, and Derbe. After revisiting these churches, they returned to Antioch.

At this time—and probably before the Jerusalem Council—Paul wrote Galatians.

SECOND JOURNEY

circa AD 50–53

Acts 15:39–18:22

Paul and Silas strengthened the churches in Syria, Cilicia, and Galatia and delivered the decisions made by the Jerusalem Council. Timothy joined the team at Lystra.

The Holy Spirit would not allow them to preach in the provinces of Asia and Bithynia. Then, after a vision at Troas that included the call, “Come over to Macedonia and help us” (Acts 16:9), they (now joined by Luke) crossed the Aegean Sea into Europe. They planted churches in the Macedonian cities of Philippi, Thessalonica, and Berea.

Next, they traveled south to the province of Achaia, where they established churches in Athens and Corinth. Paul remained in Corinth about a year and a half.

Priscilla and Aquila then traveled with Paul from Corinth to Ephesus, where the apostle reasoned with a few Jews in the synagogue. At their invitation to stay, he replied, “I will come back if it is God’s will” (Acts 18:21). Then he returned to Jerusalem and Antioch.

THIRD JOURNEY

circa AD 54–58

Acts 18:23–21:17

Paul apparently began his third missionary journey alone. As on his second journey, Paul visited the churches in the regions of Galatia and Phrygia and encouraged them to grow in the grace of God.

Paul then traveled to Ephesus, where he ministered for almost three years. God did extraordinary miracles through Paul, and a strong church was established. The gospel impacted the entire region, “so that all the Jews and Greeks who lived in the province of Asia heard the word of the Lord” (Acts 19:10).

As a result of this powerful response, the silversmiths who made shrines to the Greek goddess Artemis began losing clients. Since their business profits were threatened, they stirred up the citizens against Paul. Soon the whole city was in an uproar, and Paul was forced to leave.

From Ephesus he traveled through Macedonia, then to Corinth. During his stay in Corinth, Paul wrote his Epistle to the Romans. Finally, on his return to Jerusalem, he was arrested and imprisoned for two years in Caesarea.

An Apostle of Jesus Christ

(As we trace major events in Paul’s ministry, refer to the table, Pauline Chronology.)

Paul’s encounter with the risen Christ (Acts 9:1–10) radically changed his outlook on himself and his world. He says it like this: “I consider everything a loss compared to the surpassing greatness of knowing Christ Jesus my Lord” (Philippians 3:8).

Like other Jews of his day, Paul—before his conversion— tried to merit God’s approval and establish his righteousness by strictly observing the Law. From this external legalistic standard Paul said, “As for legalistic righteousness, [I am] faultless” (Philippians 3:6). After his conversion, however, he looked to Jesus Christ as the source of his righteousness (Romans 8:10; 1 Corinthians 1:30; Galatians 2:20–21).

Following his conversion Paul went into Arabia and later returned to Damascus (Galatians 1:17–18). In Arabia he had opportunity to pray, reflect on his encounter with Christ, and review Scripture. Luke reports that Paul preached in the Damascus synagogues, saying “Jesus is the Son of God,” and baffled the Jews “by proving that Jesus is the Christ” (Acts 9:20–22).

After a brief visit to Jerusalem where he met Peter and James, Paul went to Syria and Cilicia, where he ministered for about ten years (Acts 9:30; Galatians 1:21–23). Then Barnabas brought him to Antioch, where they worked together to teach great numbers of people (Acts 11:25–26). Syrian Antioch, the third largest city in the Roman Empire (after Rome and Alexandria), was destined to become the hub of world missions outreach. Its cosmopolitan nature permitted greater tolerance for racial and religious differences than existed in Jerusalem. The Antiochian church commissioned and sent out Barnabas and Paul as its first missionaries.

Several principles were at work in Antioch that we can apply today to develop a strong missionary church. We see in Acts 13:1–3 that missionary ministry did not come out of planning sessions but out of prayer sessions. The prophets and teachers focused on God—fasting, praying, and worshipping the Lord. They were sensitive to the voice of the Spirit and responded to His direction. This is in keeping with Jesus’ directions: “Ask the Lord of the harvest . . . to send out workers into his harvest field” (Matthew 9:38).

Paul’s Journey to Rome

circa AD 59–60 Acts 27:1–28:16

In graphic detail, Luke describes the dramatic events that unfolded on Paul’s journey to Rome. In the uncertainty of the violent storm that eventually destroyed Paul’s ship, an angel reassured Paul that he must stand before Caesar and that all on board the ship would survive.

Paul lived in Rome under guard for two years. The Romans permitted him to live in his own rented house, have visitors, and share the gospel. On one occasion, many Jews came to Paul’s house, and from morning to evening, he explained and declared to them the kingdom of God. Some were convinced and believed.

During this time, Paul wrote his Epistle to the Philippians, reassuring these friends that his chains had not hindered the gospel but rather moved it forward; it had “really served to advance the gospel” (Philippians 1:12).

The whole palace guard—possibly nine thousand men—and many others knew Paul was in chains for Christ. His chains encouraged the believers in Rome to share their faith fearlessly. From the time of his arrest, many opportunities arose for him to witness: to the Jerusalem crowd, the Sanhedrin, Felix, Festus, and Agrippa. Add to this the 274 mariners and soldiers on board the ship with him and Luke en route to Rome. Following the shipwreck, Paul prayed for many sick people on the island of Malta, and they were healed by the power of Christ. Yes, Paul’s chains served to advance the gospel.

**Q:** List as many words as you can that describe Saul of Tarsus BEFORE his conversion.

**A:** Answer should include the words like these: educated, Jew, bold, dedicated, religious, Pharisee, thorough, sincere, zealous for the Law, intelligent, energetic, violent, strict, determined, influential, in an organization, under authority

**Q:** Would you have chosen Saul of Tarsus to preach the gospel and plant churches? Briefly state how God prepared him to be an apostle of Jesus Christ.

**A:** Your answer. His life-changing encounter with the living Christ was vital. God had to subdue his violent disposition, but He used Paul’s readiness to defend the truth of the gospel at any cost.

**Q:** Read Acts 6–8:1 before answering interactive questions 3–5.

**Q:** What impact do you think Stephen’s sermon had on Paul and his future ministry?

**A:** Stephen spoke with such wisdom and power that his testimony and martyrdom must have had a powerful impact on Paul and been confirmed in his heart following his own conversion.

**Q:** We know the Jews were furious (Acts 7:54). Why do you suppose this was so?

**A:** Probably they knew in their hearts that Stephen’s message was true.

**Q:** What impressions do you get about Stephen based on his prayer for his persecutors?

**A:** Stephen was moved by love, compassion, and forgiveness when he prayed.

**Q:** Read Acts 6:8–11; 9:19–25 and compare the Jewish response to Stephen’s ministry with their response to Paul’s ministry in Damascus. What conclusion do you reach?

**A:** The Jews were so fearful of the gospel message that, as they did with Stephen, they conspired to kill Paul.

An Apostle of the Jews

Paul began his education at an early age, and through diligent study he became a doctor of Jewish law and qualified to teach. Eventually, he became an influential religious leader. Whether or not he was a member of the Sanhedrin is uncertain.

Gamaliel, the leading Pharisee of his day, taught Paul. When he says, “Under Gamaliel I was thoroughly trained in the law of our fathers” (Acts 22:3), he means that he received the best Jewish education possible. Not only was Gamaliel the most famous Jewish teacher of the first century, but he was also highly influential and “honored by all the people” (Acts 5:34–40).

Paul’s statement that he was “brought up in this city” (Acts 22:3) suggests that he went to Jerusalem at an early age—perhaps as a teenager. His parents probably wanted him to receive an orthodox education as well as to avoid corruption from Gentile contact.

The Pharisees erroneously taught that the oral law (or tradition) was equal to the written Word. Jesus confronted the Pharisees and teachers of the Law on this point. “Why,” he asked, “do you break the command of God for the sake of your tradition? . . . You nullify the word of God for the sake of your tradition” (Matthew 15:3, 6).

As a doctor of the Law and a strict Pharisee, Paul defended the Jewish faith before becoming a defender of the Christian faith. For instance, he demonstrated great zeal in debating the Sadducees—the liberals of his day who did not believe in spirits, angels, or the resurrection.

Why does Paul refer to his persecution of the church to illustrate his zeal (Philippians 3:6)? Understand that before his conversion he was convinced that Jesus’ followers were heretics. In Paul’s mind, he was defending the truth. Do you recall what Jesus said earlier to His disciples? “They will put you out of the synagogue, in fact, a time is coming when anyone who kills you will think he is offering a service to God” (John 16:2). That is what Paul thought.

Paul’s Missionary Journeys

FIRST JOURNEY

circa AD 45–49

Acts 13:4–14:28

The Syrian Antioch church commissioned and sent out Paul and Barnabas, accompanied by John Mark.

They began their ministry on Cyprus, then traveled to the southern region of the province of Galatia, where they focused their efforts. Great numbers of people responded to the gospel. In spite of opposition by the Jews, the apostles planted churches in Pisidian Antioch, Iconium, Lystra, and Derbe. After revisiting these churches, they returned to Antioch.

At this time—and probably before the Jerusalem Council—Paul wrote Galatians.

SECOND JOURNEY

circa AD 50–53

Acts 15:39–18:22

Paul and Silas strengthened the churches in Syria, Cilicia, and Galatia and delivered the decisions made by the Jerusalem Council. Timothy joined the team at Lystra.

The Holy Spirit would not allow them to preach in the provinces of Asia and Bithynia. Then, after a vision at Troas that included the call, “Come over to Macedonia and help us” (Acts 16:9), they (now joined by Luke) crossed the Aegean Sea into Europe. They planted churches in the Macedonian cities of Philippi, Thessalonica, and Berea.

Next, they traveled south to the province of Achaia, where they established churches in Athens and Corinth. Paul remained in Corinth about a year and a half.

Priscilla and Aquila then traveled with Paul from Corinth to Ephesus, where the apostle reasoned with a few Jews in the synagogue. At their invitation to stay, he replied, “I will come back if it is God’s will” (Acts 18:21). Then he returned to Jerusalem and Antioch.

THIRD JOURNEY

circa AD 54–58

Acts 18:23–21:17

Paul apparently began his third missionary journey alone. As on his second journey, Paul visited the churches in the regions of Galatia and Phrygia and encouraged them to grow in the grace of God.

Paul then traveled to Ephesus, where he ministered for almost three years. God did extraordinary miracles through Paul, and a strong church was established. The gospel impacted the entire region, “so that all the Jews and Greeks who lived in the province of Asia heard the word of the Lord” (Acts 19:10).

As a result of this powerful response, the silversmiths who made shrines to the Greek goddess Artemis began losing clients. Since their business profits were threatened, they stirred up the citizens against Paul. Soon the whole city was in an uproar, and Paul was forced to leave.

From Ephesus he traveled through Macedonia, then to Corinth. During his stay in Corinth, Paul wrote his Epistle to the Romans. Finally, on his return to Jerusalem, he was arrested and imprisoned for two years in Caesarea.

An Apostle of Jesus Christ

(As we trace major events in Paul’s ministry, refer to the table, Pauline Chronology.)

Paul’s encounter with the risen Christ (Acts 9:1–10) radically changed his outlook on himself and his world. He says it like this: “I consider everything a loss compared to the surpassing greatness of knowing Christ Jesus my Lord” (Philippians 3:8).

Like other Jews of his day, Paul—before his conversion— tried to merit God’s approval and establish his righteousness by strictly observing the Law. From this external legalistic standard Paul said, “As for legalistic righteousness, [I am] faultless” (Philippians 3:6). After his conversion, however, he looked to Jesus Christ as the source of his righteousness (Romans 8:10; 1 Corinthians 1:30; Galatians 2:20–21).

Following his conversion Paul went into Arabia and later returned to Damascus (Galatians 1:17–18). In Arabia he had opportunity to pray, reflect on his encounter with Christ, and review Scripture. Luke reports that Paul preached in the Damascus synagogues, saying “Jesus is the Son of God,” and baffled the Jews “by proving that Jesus is the Christ” (Acts 9:20–22).

After a brief visit to Jerusalem where he met Peter and James, Paul went to Syria and Cilicia, where he ministered for about ten years (Acts 9:30; Galatians 1:21–23). Then Barnabas brought him to Antioch, where they worked together to teach great numbers of people (Acts 11:25–26). Syrian Antioch, the third largest city in the Roman Empire (after Rome and Alexandria), was destined to become the hub of world missions outreach. Its cosmopolitan nature permitted greater tolerance for racial and religious differences than existed in Jerusalem. The Antiochian church commissioned and sent out Barnabas and Paul as its first missionaries.

Several principles were at work in Antioch that we can apply today to develop a strong missionary church. We see in Acts 13:1–3 that missionary ministry did not come out of planning sessions but out of prayer sessions. The prophets and teachers focused on God—fasting, praying, and worshipping the Lord. They were sensitive to the voice of the Spirit and responded to His direction. This is in keeping with Jesus’ directions: “Ask the Lord of the harvest . . . to send out workers into his harvest field” (Matthew 9:38).

Paul’s Journey to Rome

circa AD 59–60 Acts 27:1–28:16

In graphic detail, Luke describes the dramatic events that unfolded on Paul’s journey to Rome. In the uncertainty of the violent storm that eventually destroyed Paul’s ship, an angel reassured Paul that he must stand before Caesar and that all on board the ship would survive.

Paul lived in Rome under guard for two years. The Romans permitted him to live in his own rented house, have visitors, and share the gospel. On one occasion, many Jews came to Paul’s house, and from morning to evening, he explained and declared to them the kingdom of God. Some were convinced and believed.

During this time, Paul wrote his Epistle to the Philippians, reassuring these friends that his chains had not hindered the gospel but rather moved it forward; it had “really served to advance the gospel” (Philippians 1:12).

The whole palace guard—possibly nine thousand men—and many others knew Paul was in chains for Christ. His chains encouraged the believers in Rome to share their faith fearlessly. From the time of his arrest, many opportunities arose for him to witness: to the Jerusalem crowd, the Sanhedrin, Felix, Festus, and Agrippa. Add to this the 274 mariners and soldiers on board the ship with him and Luke en route to Rome. Following the shipwreck, Paul prayed for many sick people on the island of Malta, and they were healed by the power of Christ. Yes, Paul’s chains served to advance the gospel.

**Q:** List as many words as you can that describe Saul of Tarsus BEFORE his conversion.

**A:** Answer should include the words like these: educated, Jew, bold, dedicated, religious, Pharisee, thorough, sincere, zealous for the Law, intelligent, energetic, violent, strict, determined, influential, in an organization, under authority

**Q:** Would you have chosen Saul of Tarsus to preach the gospel and plant churches? Briefly state how God prepared him to be an apostle of Jesus Christ.

**A:** Your answer. His life-changing encounter with the living Christ was vital. God had to subdue his violent disposition, but He used Paul’s readiness to defend the truth of the gospel at any cost.

**Q:** Read Acts 6–8:1 before answering interactive questions 3–5.

**Q:** What impact do you think Stephen’s sermon had on Paul and his future ministry?

**A:** Stephen spoke with such wisdom and power that his testimony and martyrdom must have had a powerful impact on Paul and been confirmed in his heart following his own conversion.

**Q:** We know the Jews were furious (Acts 7:54). Why do you suppose this was so?

**A:** Probably they knew in their hearts that Stephen’s message was true.

**Q:** What impressions do you get about Stephen based on his prayer for his persecutors?

**A:** Stephen was moved by love, compassion, and forgiveness when he prayed.

**Q:** Read Acts 6:8–11; 9:19–25 and compare the Jewish response to Stephen’s ministry with their response to Paul’s ministry in Damascus. What conclusion do you reach?

**A:** The Jews were so fearful of the gospel message that, as they did with Stephen, they conspired to kill Paul.

### Paul’s Letter to the Roman Church

#### 1.2.1 — Describe the historical setting, purpose, and key concepts of the book of Romans.

Paul’s letter to the church in Rome addresses some of the same themes found in his other epistles. However, Romans treats many of these themes with greater detail. In this lesson, we will consider the background issues, key concepts, and highlights of the letter. We will also look at various views of the origin of the Roman church.

Finally, we will explore Paul’s introductory remarks. His greetings reveal how he perceives himself in relation to Christ, and his discussion of the proposed visit provides some insight into the Roman church. Then his theme introduces us to key words we will encounter in further study of this letter.

Occasion and Circumstances of Romans

The Author

Paul wrote his letter to the Romans at Corinth near the end of his third missionary journey, probably AD 57. Gaius, a friend and convert, opened his home to Paul during his three-month visit (Acts 20:2–3).

Paul had just completed his most successful ministry. Multitudes were turning from idolatry to Christ in Ephesus where he had been ministering for three years (Acts 20:31). “All the Jews and Greeks who lived in the province of Asia heard the word of the Lord” (Acts 19:10). Luke explains that Paul held daily discussions in the lecture hall of a man named Tyrannus “from about ten o’clock till three. This continued for two years” (Acts 19:9–10, AMPC). It is important to understand that Paul’s ministry involved more than academic instruction. God worked through Paul unusual miracles, healing diseases and delivering those possessed of evil spirits (Acts 19:11–12). In this way, the good news of Christ “spread widely and grew in power” (Acts 19:20).

For about ten years, from AD 47 to 57, Paul and his companions had ministered in the regions bordering the Aegean Sea that separates Europe from Asia. On the European side, in Greece, they had preached the gospel and planted churches in the principal cities of Macedonia and Achaia. On the Asian side (now Turkey), they carried out a similar work in the provinces of Galatia and Asia.

Paul and his colleagues had established churches and appointed leaders in such places as Antioch, Iconium, Philippi, Thessalonica, Corinth, and Ephesus. Thus Paul writes, “I have now finished my work in these regions” (Romans 15:23, NLT). Consequently, he set his sights on Spain, the oldest colony in the West and an important center of Roman civilization. A pioneer by choice, Paul’s ambition was to preach to people who had never heard the gospel (15:18–24).

Before going to Spain, however, Paul felt compelled to return to Jerusalem with a gift. The churches in Macedonia and Achaia had taken up an offering for believers there who were going through hard times. With the completion of that task, Paul planned to visit Rome on his way to Spain. These are the plans he shared with the Romans.

The Roman Church

At the time Paul wrote his letter, he had never visited Rome. Nevertheless, he was well informed about the situation there. Paul met Priscilla and Aquila (16:3–4), for example, in Corinth. They had recently come from Italy because Claudius Caesar had expelled all Jews from Rome (Acts 18:1–4). They worked with Paul in Corinth for a year and a half and then traveled with him to Ephesus (Acts 18:9–10, 18–19). During this time, Paul had ample opportunity to learn about the Roman church.

In his introductory remarks to the Romans, Paul says, “your faith is being reported all over the world” (1:8). And he commends them for being “full of goodness, complete in knowledge and competent to instruct one another” (15:14). In addition, he demonstrates an understanding of the friction between Jewish and Gentile believers in discussing the “weak” and the “strong” in Romans 14–15. Furthermore, Paul mentions at least three different house churches in chapter 16.

The first believers in Rome were most likely Jews and converts to Judaism. By the time Paul wrote Romans, however, Gentiles had become the majority. Members included the first convert in the province of Asia (16:5), fellow-countrymen who were converted before him and imprisoned with him (16:7). Almost a third of those Paul lists in Romans 16 were women engaged in ministry, including Phoebe, whom Paul esteemed highly. She is credited with carrying Paul’s letter to Rome.

Jews established a community in Rome as early as the second century BC In Paul’s day, an estimated 50,000 Jews were living in Rome. On at least two occasions, they were ordered to leave Rome: 1) under Emperor Tiberias in AD 19; 2) and then again under Emperor Claudius, probably in AD 49. In each instance they were allowed to return. The Edict of Claudius had a profound effect on the Roman church, since Jews had been an essential part of it from the beginning.

Suetonius, a Roman historian says, “Since the Jews constantly made disturbances at the instigation of Chrestus, [Claudius] expelled them from Rome.” Suetonius evidently confused “Crestus” for “Christus,” the Latin name for “Christ” (Edwards 1992, 9). These Jewish disturbances were probably due to the preaching of the gospel. Throughout Acts Luke records a persistent pattern of disturbances created by the Jews to the preaching of the gospel. (See Acts 13:50; 14:2; 19; 17:5, 13; 18:6, 12–13; 21:27–31.)

The edict most likely lapsed when Claudius died in AD 54. Conceivably, the Jews then began returning to Rome—a couple of years before Paul wrote Romans.

Purpose of Romans

Several things prompted Paul to write his letter to the Romans. One reason was to inform them of his intended visit. The Romans, for the most part, knew him only by name, since he had never visited their city. So he presents to them his apostolic credentials, mission, and message. Friends and former colleagues now living in Rome would be delighted to know his plans.

The faith of the Romans was becoming well known, and Paul desired to see them grow even stronger. Therefore, he wrote this Spirit-inspired exposition of the gospel. This written form would further strengthen the believer’s faith and at the same time make it more difficult for false teachers to distort the message of Christ.

For some time Paul had longed to visit these Roman believers that he had prayed for continuously (1:9–10). He thanks God for them and desires to impart “some spiritual gift” (1:11). But to avoid an air of superiority he quickly adds, “that is, that you and I may be mutually encouraged by each other’s faith” (1:12).

Since Paul faced tremendous challenges in the coming days, he appealed to the Romans for prayer support. He felt “compelled by the Spirit” to go to Jerusalem, even though the Spirit warned him of prison and hardships (Acts 20:22–24). He urged the Roman believers to join him in his struggle by praying for him. For instance, “Pray that I will be rescued from those in Judea who refuse to obey God. Pray also that Christians there will be willing to accept the donation I am taking to Jerusalem” (Romans 15:31, NLT).

We now know that the most important reason for Paul’s letter to the Romans is that the Holy Spirit inspired him to write. It was not simply a human impulse. Rather, he was divinely motivated.

Romans is the last letter Paul wrote before his imprisonment. Now, at almost sixty years of age, he sends his presentation of the gospel of Jesus Christ to Rome.

KEY CONCEPTS OF ROMANS

Theme

A righteousness from God —a broad term that includes concepts such as justification and sanctification . The gospel reveals how we receive this righteousness.

Key words

righteousness, faith, law, all, sin

Key verses

Romans 1:16–17; 3:21–25

Key chapters

Romans 6–8 give the basis of Christian living and explain: our relationship to law and grace; how we are delivered from sin; how to live in victory through the empowerment of the Holy Spirit.

Highlights of Romans

1. Introduction (Romans 1:1–17). Paul introduces himself from the standpoint of his relationship to Christ, explains his purpose for writing, and summarizes his gospel message.

2. Humanity’s problem: sin (Romans 1:18–3:20). Jews and Gentiles alike are unrighteous: “There is no one righteous, not even one” (3:10). Consequently, all people are guilty before God, under condemnation, and in need of a Savior.

3. God’s provision: a Savior (Romans 3:21–5:21). Although all of us are guilty of sin, God nevertheless demonstrates His love to us by sending a Savior: “While we were still sinners, Christ died for us” (5:8). God imputes (credits) us with His righteousness when we put our faith in Jesus Christ.

4. God’s process: shaping believers into the image of His Son (Romans 6–8). We are saved—justified and born again—the moment we receive Christ as our Savior. At the same time, God begins to remold us into the likeness of His Son. Theologians call this shaping process sanctification.

5. God’s plan: restore Israel (Romans 9–11). Paul deals with Israel’s election and unbelief. He argues that God has not rejected His people because a remnant remains. Eventually, when the full number of Gentiles has come to salvation, all Israel will be saved.

6. God’s purpose: apply Christ’s righteousness to the believer’s daily living (Romans 12:1–15:13). Theology must be translated into living. Paul considers practical aspects of the Christian life: the relationship of the believer to other believers, to unbelievers, to governing authorities, to those who are weak in the faith, and to those who are strong.

7. Conclusion (Romans 15:14–16:27). In his closing comments, Paul provides insight into his Gentile ministry and his planned visit. He also sends greetings to friends and former colleagues now living in Rome.

**Q:** List three values of the book of Romans (see chapter introduction).

**A:** Theological value: Paul develops major doctrines pertaining to redemption, including sin, salvation, justification, and sanctification; God’s mercy and grace; and the basis for spiritual growth and victory. Practical value: Paul applies the theological doctrines to Christlike conduct. Spiritual value: These doctrines have influenced believers down through the ages who demonstrate in their own lives the power of the gospel message.

**Q:** Describe the historical setting of Paul’s letter to the Romans.

**A:** Paul wrote to the Roman church from Corinth or its neighboring port city. Tertius is important to the writing of Romans because he wrote what Paul dictated to him. Paul planned to visit Spain after going to Rome.

**Q:** Identify the Roman provinces where Paul evangelized extensively between AD 47 and 57.

**A:** Macedonia, Galatia, Asia, Achaia

**Q:** Summarize four important reasons for Paul’s letter to the Roman church.

**A:** The Holy Spirit inspired Paul to write; therefore, this letter is of divine origin. Paul wanted to inform the Romans of his visit. Paul wrote to strengthen the people’s faith and protect them from false teachers. Paul wrote to ask for their prayer support of his ministry.

**Q:** Read Romans 1:16–17; 3:21–25, and explain the significance of the following five key words as expressed in these key verses: righteousness, faith, law, all, sin.

**A:** Your answer may be similar to this: The gospel reveals a righteousness from God that is by faith. This righteousness comes through faith in Jesus Christ. The righteousness from God is apart from law. All people have sinned, but all who have faith in Jesus are justified freely by His grace. Because of sin, all people fall short of the glory of God (are separated from God).

### Paul’s Introduction to Romans (Romans 1:1–17)

#### 1.3.1 — Assess three views of the origin of the church in Rome, and identify descriptions of the church based on Romans 16.

The Roman Church

The origin of the Roman church is uncertain. Tradition says Peter founded it. But there is no evidence to support this view. Scholars believe Peter probably first visited Rome in AD 60—after Paul’s arrival. The faith of the Romans was “being reported all over the world” (1:8) long before Peter and Paul made their visits.

A second view reasons that Christians, like other citizens, visited Rome. Coming from such places as Jerusalem, Antioch, Thessalonica, Philippi, Corinth, and Ephesus they shared their faith with Roman acquaintances. Supporters of this view point to almost thirty friends, associates, and converts of Paul from across the empire who were living in Rome at the time he wrote (Romans 16). Undoubtedly these believers contributed to the growth of the church. However, we have no evidence to uphold this view.

The most likely view of the origin of the early church goes back to those present in Jerusalem for the Feast of Pentecost. Among those gathered on the Day of Pentecost were “visitors from Rome (both Jews and converts to Judaism)” (Acts 2:10–11). According to this view, the first believers were converted through Peter’s preaching, filled with the Spirit, and then took the good news back to Rome. However, Luke does not specifically identify the converts by city or nation. For this reason, we say that Romans were most likely among the new converts.

Paul’s closing remarks provide insight into the Roman church. Members met in homes. They were cosmopolitan in nature and had suffered persecution. While the first believers were most likely Jews, the church had become multinational in its makeup. Members included the first convert in the province of Asia (Romans 16:5) and Paul’s relatives (this probably means “fellow-countrymen”; 16:7) who were converted before him and imprisoned with him. Almost a third of those on Paul’s list were women engaged in ministry, including Phoebe, whom Paul esteemed highly. Some commentators suggest that she was a businesswoman or possibly a legal representative. It is possible that she carried Paul’s letter to Rome. Now, in light of these observations, read Romans 16 again and look for the things we have mentioned here.

Jews established a community in Rome as early as the second century BC. On at least two occasions, the government expelled them but later permitted their return. Aquila and Priscilla, who worked with Paul in Corinth and Ephesus, were expelled in AD 49 when Claudius Caesar ordered Jews deported (Acts 18:2). They probably became believers before meeting Paul and lived in Rome at the time he wrote this letter to the Roman church (Romans 16:3–5).

Gentile Christians apparently outnumbered Jewish believers in AD 57, when Paul wrote this letter. Could this be a reason why, as the apostle to the Gentiles, he felt constrained to send them this exposition of the gospel?

Paul’s Greetings (1:1–7)

Paul opens his letter according to the custom of his day: identifying himself as the author, naming the Roman believers as the recipients, and giving them greetings. Since he had never visited Rome, he begins by presenting himself and his credentials. Then he expands his opening remarks to lay a foundation for his theological arguments.

“Paul, a servant of Christ Jesus, called to be an apostle and set apart for the gospel of God” (Romans 1:1). Paul portrays himself as “servant,” “apostle,” and “set apart.” Each term expresses an aspect of his relationship to Jesus Christ. Servant suggests submission to His Lordship; apostle speaks of Paul’s authority as Christ’s messenger; and set apart affirms that he is the Lord’s chosen instrument.

The word servant (Greek, doulos ) means “slave” or “bond servant.” Paul was not a hired servant with a temporary contract. Rather, he was like a bond servant who chose to commit his life freely and fully to his Master. In this way, he served fervently with his “whole heart” (1:9).

In calling himself a servant, Paul may have had in mind men like Moses, Joshua, David, and the prophets who were called “servants of the Lord.” The teaching and example of Jesus undoubtedly burned in his heart as well. Christ “did not come to be served, but to serve, and to give his life as a ransom for many” (Mark 10:45). Jesus taught His disciples, “Whoever wants to become great among you must be your servant, and whoever wants to be first must be slave of all” (10:43–44).

A sense of God’s call and a commitment to Jesus Christ and the gospel message are vital for ministers of the gospel. Can you testify to a call and sense of mission similar to Paul’s? Such a commitment is of prime importance for those called to serve Him. Perhaps you would like to think and pray about this now, and commit yourself wholly to the Lord Jesus Christ, to the gospel, and to the mission He has for you.

After describing himself as an apostle “set apart for the gospel of God,” Paul gives a brief explanation of the origin of the gospel and the nature of the Savior (Romans 1:2–6). Let us consider this brief review of the gospel Paul preached: “the gospel he promised beforehand through his prophets in the Holy Scriptures” (1:2). Here Paul tells us that the gospel did not originate at Christ’s coming; His coming and the redemption He brought were announced and recorded by the prophets long before. In fact, the entire Old Testament foretells it. As the apostle Peter explains, God established His redemptive plan before the world began (1 Peter 1:20). Do you remember what Jesus said to His disciples on the road to Emmaus? “‘How foolish you are, and how slow of heart to believe all that the prophets have spoken!’ And beginning with Moses and all the Prophets, he explained to them what was said in all the Scriptures concerning himself” (Luke 24: 25, 27; see also v. 44).

In his opening remarks, Paul first introduced himself and his message; then he shows how the gospel is rooted in Scripture. Next he focuses on Jesus Christ, who is the heart of the gospel: “regarding his Son, who as to his human nature was a descendant of David, and who through the Spirit of holiness was declared with power to be the Son of God by his resurrection from the dead” (Romans 1:3–4). In truth, without Jesus Christ there is no gospel. He is both human and divine. As another translation says, Jesus “came as a man, born into King David’s royal family line” and He “was shown to be the Son of God when God powerfully raised him from the dead by means of the Holy Spirit” (1:3–4, NLT).

Declared to be the Son of God (Romans 1:4) does not mean that Jesus became the Son when He was raised from the dead. Neither was being the Son of God a position He attained through His miraculous resurrection. Rather, He always was and is the Son; verse 4 means that His resurrection demonstrates this fact (see Philippians 2:5–11).

What is the gospel, and how does Paul explain it? First, it is “the gospel of God” (Romans 1:1); that is, it is from God and about God. Then, it reveals “a righteousness from God” (1:17) and is “regarding his Son” (1:3). This good news is not a formula, but a person—the Lord Jesus Christ. Paul committed himself to a person, not a philosophy. God commissioned Paul to preach Christ, not a religion. Thus Paul proclaimed the good news that God, in the person of Jesus Christ, came to earth on a redemptive mission. This is the heart of the gospel. Jesus Christ is God’s good news.

Paul continues his focus on Jesus Christ, God’s eternal Son: “Through him and for his name’s sake, we received grace and apostleship to call people from among all the Gentiles to the obedience that comes from faith” (Romans 1:5). God commissioned Paul to “call people from among all the Gentiles.” At the time of Paul’s conversion, the Lord told Ananias (who laid his hands on Paul that he might receive his sight and be filled with the Spirit), “This man is my chosen instrument to carry my name before the Gentiles and their kings and before the people of Israel” (Acts 9:15). Paul explains to the Romans that the gospel which was first revealed to the Jews is to be preached to all people—Jews and Gentiles alike.

Paul desired to reproduce himself in his Gentile converts. He wanted his “spiritual children” to become like him: committed to Christ and involved in His mission in the world. Calling Gentiles to “the obedience that comes from faith” was an important aspect of his apostolic ministry. Paul could have mentioned other qualities that are desirable for disciples, such as being knowledgeable of God’s will and ways, being effective preachers of the gospel, or being filled with the Spirit and with compassion. But just as Jesus identified obedience as essential (Matthew 28:18–20), this is Paul’s focus as well when he speaks of obedience that comes from faith. For example, notice how Paul uses faith and obedience in these parallel passages (italics added):

Romans 1:8: “ Your faith is being reported all over the world.”

Romans 16:19: “Everyone has heard about your obedience .”

Hebrews 11:8 also illustrates this faith-obedience relationship: “ By faith Abraham, when called to go . . . , obeyed and went” (italics added). We cannot separate obedience from faith and still have true faith; they are inseparable.

In Ephesians 2:8–10, Paul explains how salvation by faith (and without works) actually leads to good works. Does James contradict Paul when he says, “A person is justified by what he does and not by faith alone” (James 2:24)? The answer is, “No.” James uses the term “what he does” (Greek, ergon ) in a sense different from Paul’s use of works in Ephesians 2:9. Paul emphasizes that salvation rests on God’s grace alone, not on our efforts (Romans 4:3; Galatians 3:6; Ephesians 2:8–10). James, on the other hand, distinguishes true faith with its deeds from false faith without deeds (James 2:14–26). Our actions or deeds confirm that our faith is genuine, not superficial.

Next in his opening remarks, Paul reminds the Romans, “And you also are among those who are called to belong to Jesus Christ” (Romans 1:6). Not only was Paul called (1:1) but so were the Romans. In truth, God calls everyone to belong to Jesus Christ. What a tragedy that all do not respond!

Now Paul returns to his greeting: “To all in Rome who are loved by God and called to be saints ” (Romans 1:7). The message here is that God’s love is not conditional or based on our fulfillment of certain requirements. He calls us because He loves us. When we put our trust in Christ as Savior, we become saints. Saints is not a title attained by merit. Rather, we are saints because we are “set apart” from the world and to God the moment we receive Christ as Savior.

“Grace and peace to you from God our Father and from the Lord Jesus Christ” (Romans 1:7). “Grace and peace to you.” Paul’s greeting here takes the form of a blessing or prayer. Grace describes the favor God extends to everyone through Jesus Christ. Peace portrays the well-being of those who receive His grace. These two words sum up the source and effect of our salvation.

“Lord Jesus Christ” (Romans 1:7). Each word in this title is significant. Jesus means “Savior” and speaks of His humanity. He came to earth at a specific point in history in order to save us from our sins (see 1 Timothy 2:5–6). Christ (or Messiah ) means “the anointed one” and describes His ministry (see Acts 10:38). Lord speaks of His sovereignty and deity. He is our Master, Ruler, and King. All authority in heaven and earth has been given to Him.

Paul’s Proposed Visit (1:8–15)

Now Paul resumes his greeting with the words, “First, I thank my God through Jesus Christ for all of you, because your faith is being reported all over the world” (Romans 1:8). First, I thank God for all of you. Paul links his greetings to his proposed visit by following his usual pattern (Galatians is an exception), thanking God for them and commending the believers in Rome on one of their strong points: Your faith is being reported all over the world. This moved Paul to encourage them with the assurance of “how constantly I remember you in my prayers at all times” (1:9–10). What an insight into a successful ministry! That an apostle would pray for churches to whom he had ministered is no surprise. But to pray regularly for a church he had never visited is noteworthy. Here is a prayer life we can imitate (see 1 Thessalonians 1:6–7; 2:14).

“I pray that now at last by God’s will the way may be opened for me to come to you” (Romans 1:10). Paul not only prayed for these believers, but he also sought for an opportunity to minister to them.

“I long to see you so that I may impart to you some spiritual gift to make you strong—that is, that you and I may be mutually encouraged by each other’s faith” (Romans 1:11–12). Impart can also be translated “share,” as in 1 Thessalonians 2:8. Paul is not saying that he distributes spiritual gifts; rather, he shares with others what God has given to him. “To make you strong” expresses his desire for these Romans.

The expression mutually encouraged (Romans 1:12) is not found elsewhere in the Bible. Nevertheless, it characterizes believers and describes their relationship to one another in Christ. As each part of our physical body benefits the whole, so each believer has unique qualities that can enrich the body of Christ.

Next, Paul writes, “I planned many times to come to you . . . in order that I might have a harvest among you, just as I have had among the other Gentiles” (Romans 1:13). As we saw earlier, Paul wanted the Romans to be aware that he prayed for them and that he had planned many times to visit them but had been “prevented from doing so until now” (1:13). The term harvest includes not only winning new converts but also strengthening the church (1:11), just as Paul had done among Gentiles in other places.

Paul explains why he wants to visit the church in Rome: “I am obligated both to Greeks and non-Greeks, both to the wise and the foolish. That is why I am so eager to preach the gospel also to you who are at Rome” (Romans 1:14–15). The New Living Translation clarifies what Paul says in verse 14: “I have a great sense of obligation to people in our culture and to people in other cultures, to the educated and the uneducated alike.” Paul was eager to preach the gospel to all people, whatever their language, culture, or status in life.

What practical value is in Paul’s introductory remarks (Romans 1:8–15)? Can you identify leadership traits that are applicable to you? Observe that he led not only by exhortation but also by example. What does this suggest to you concerning your own ministry?

**Q:** We have indicated which of these three views is the “most likely” one. Do you agree with this view? Give evidence to support your response.

**A:** Your answer. There seems to be strong biblical support for the third view based on Acts 2:10–11. One might argue that people from many nations saw and heard what happened on the Day of Pentecost not by coincidence, but by an act of God. We know that three thousand people in this diverse crowd accepted the gospel message Peter preached. The Romans among them likely took the message back to their own people, as did the converts from other nations.

**Q:** Read the first verse in each of these Epistles: Romans, 1 and 2 Corinthians, Galatians, Ephesians, Colossians, 1 and 2 Timothy. What title does Paul use to describe himself in Romans 1 that he does not use in these other passages?

**A:** A servant of Jesus Christ

**Q:** Read the following Scripture passage, and summarize what each teaches about the origin and names of God’s Son: (a) John 1:1, 14; (b) Matthew 1:22–23; (c) 1 Timothy 2:5.

**A:** a He was the Word; He was God; and He was with God in the beginning. He is the one and only Son who came from the Father. b He is called Immanuel, meaning, “God with us.” c He is the mediator between God and humanity.

**Q:** Did God answer Paul’s prayer about mutual encouragement (Romans 1:10–12)? For the answer, read Acts 28:14–16 and Philippians 1:12–14.

**A:** The answer is “yes.” Paul was encouraged and the brothers in the Lord were encouraged.

**Q:** Describe what is implied about Paul’s harvest in Rome based on these passages: Acts 28:17, 23–24; Philippians 1:12–14; Philippians 4:22.

**A:** Acts 28:17, 23–24 There were Jewish converts as a result of Paul’s witness. Philippians 1:12–14 Roman believers were encouraged by Paul’s witness to speak more courageously. Philippians 4:22 There were converts in Caesar’s household as a result of Paul’s witness.

#### 1.3.2 — Identify the principles for Christian life and ministry Paul exemplified and emphasized in Romans 1:1–17, and apply them to your own life and ministry.

The Roman Church

The origin of the Roman church is uncertain. Tradition says Peter founded it. But there is no evidence to support this view. Scholars believe Peter probably first visited Rome in AD 60—after Paul’s arrival. The faith of the Romans was “being reported all over the world” (1:8) long before Peter and Paul made their visits.

A second view reasons that Christians, like other citizens, visited Rome. Coming from such places as Jerusalem, Antioch, Thessalonica, Philippi, Corinth, and Ephesus they shared their faith with Roman acquaintances. Supporters of this view point to almost thirty friends, associates, and converts of Paul from across the empire who were living in Rome at the time he wrote (Romans 16). Undoubtedly these believers contributed to the growth of the church. However, we have no evidence to uphold this view.

The most likely view of the origin of the early church goes back to those present in Jerusalem for the Feast of Pentecost. Among those gathered on the Day of Pentecost were “visitors from Rome (both Jews and converts to Judaism)” (Acts 2:10–11). According to this view, the first believers were converted through Peter’s preaching, filled with the Spirit, and then took the good news back to Rome. However, Luke does not specifically identify the converts by city or nation. For this reason, we say that Romans were most likely among the new converts.

Paul’s closing remarks provide insight into the Roman church. Members met in homes. They were cosmopolitan in nature and had suffered persecution. While the first believers were most likely Jews, the church had become multinational in its makeup. Members included the first convert in the province of Asia (Romans 16:5) and Paul’s relatives (this probably means “fellow-countrymen”; 16:7) who were converted before him and imprisoned with him. Almost a third of those on Paul’s list were women engaged in ministry, including Phoebe, whom Paul esteemed highly. Some commentators suggest that she was a businesswoman or possibly a legal representative. It is possible that she carried Paul’s letter to Rome. Now, in light of these observations, read Romans 16 again and look for the things we have mentioned here.

Jews established a community in Rome as early as the second century BC. On at least two occasions, the government expelled them but later permitted their return. Aquila and Priscilla, who worked with Paul in Corinth and Ephesus, were expelled in AD 49 when Claudius Caesar ordered Jews deported (Acts 18:2). They probably became believers before meeting Paul and lived in Rome at the time he wrote this letter to the Roman church (Romans 16:3–5).

Gentile Christians apparently outnumbered Jewish believers in AD 57, when Paul wrote this letter. Could this be a reason why, as the apostle to the Gentiles, he felt constrained to send them this exposition of the gospel?

Paul’s Greetings (1:1–7)

Paul opens his letter according to the custom of his day: identifying himself as the author, naming the Roman believers as the recipients, and giving them greetings. Since he had never visited Rome, he begins by presenting himself and his credentials. Then he expands his opening remarks to lay a foundation for his theological arguments.

“Paul, a servant of Christ Jesus, called to be an apostle and set apart for the gospel of God” (Romans 1:1). Paul portrays himself as “servant,” “apostle,” and “set apart.” Each term expresses an aspect of his relationship to Jesus Christ. Servant suggests submission to His Lordship; apostle speaks of Paul’s authority as Christ’s messenger; and set apart affirms that he is the Lord’s chosen instrument.

The word servant (Greek, doulos ) means “slave” or “bond servant.” Paul was not a hired servant with a temporary contract. Rather, he was like a bond servant who chose to commit his life freely and fully to his Master. In this way, he served fervently with his “whole heart” (1:9).

In calling himself a servant, Paul may have had in mind men like Moses, Joshua, David, and the prophets who were called “servants of the Lord.” The teaching and example of Jesus undoubtedly burned in his heart as well. Christ “did not come to be served, but to serve, and to give his life as a ransom for many” (Mark 10:45). Jesus taught His disciples, “Whoever wants to become great among you must be your servant, and whoever wants to be first must be slave of all” (10:43–44).

A sense of God’s call and a commitment to Jesus Christ and the gospel message are vital for ministers of the gospel. Can you testify to a call and sense of mission similar to Paul’s? Such a commitment is of prime importance for those called to serve Him. Perhaps you would like to think and pray about this now, and commit yourself wholly to the Lord Jesus Christ, to the gospel, and to the mission He has for you.

After describing himself as an apostle “set apart for the gospel of God,” Paul gives a brief explanation of the origin of the gospel and the nature of the Savior (Romans 1:2–6). Let us consider this brief review of the gospel Paul preached: “the gospel he promised beforehand through his prophets in the Holy Scriptures” (1:2). Here Paul tells us that the gospel did not originate at Christ’s coming; His coming and the redemption He brought were announced and recorded by the prophets long before. In fact, the entire Old Testament foretells it. As the apostle Peter explains, God established His redemptive plan before the world began (1 Peter 1:20). Do you remember what Jesus said to His disciples on the road to Emmaus? “‘How foolish you are, and how slow of heart to believe all that the prophets have spoken!’ And beginning with Moses and all the Prophets, he explained to them what was said in all the Scriptures concerning himself” (Luke 24: 25, 27; see also v. 44).

In his opening remarks, Paul first introduced himself and his message; then he shows how the gospel is rooted in Scripture. Next he focuses on Jesus Christ, who is the heart of the gospel: “regarding his Son, who as to his human nature was a descendant of David, and who through the Spirit of holiness was declared with power to be the Son of God by his resurrection from the dead” (Romans 1:3–4). In truth, without Jesus Christ there is no gospel. He is both human and divine. As another translation says, Jesus “came as a man, born into King David’s royal family line” and He “was shown to be the Son of God when God powerfully raised him from the dead by means of the Holy Spirit” (1:3–4, NLT).

Declared to be the Son of God (Romans 1:4) does not mean that Jesus became the Son when He was raised from the dead. Neither was being the Son of God a position He attained through His miraculous resurrection. Rather, He always was and is the Son; verse 4 means that His resurrection demonstrates this fact (see Philippians 2:5–11).

What is the gospel, and how does Paul explain it? First, it is “the gospel of God” (Romans 1:1); that is, it is from God and about God. Then, it reveals “a righteousness from God” (1:17) and is “regarding his Son” (1:3). This good news is not a formula, but a person—the Lord Jesus Christ. Paul committed himself to a person, not a philosophy. God commissioned Paul to preach Christ, not a religion. Thus Paul proclaimed the good news that God, in the person of Jesus Christ, came to earth on a redemptive mission. This is the heart of the gospel. Jesus Christ is God’s good news.

Paul continues his focus on Jesus Christ, God’s eternal Son: “Through him and for his name’s sake, we received grace and apostleship to call people from among all the Gentiles to the obedience that comes from faith” (Romans 1:5). God commissioned Paul to “call people from among all the Gentiles.” At the time of Paul’s conversion, the Lord told Ananias (who laid his hands on Paul that he might receive his sight and be filled with the Spirit), “This man is my chosen instrument to carry my name before the Gentiles and their kings and before the people of Israel” (Acts 9:15). Paul explains to the Romans that the gospel which was first revealed to the Jews is to be preached to all people—Jews and Gentiles alike.

Paul desired to reproduce himself in his Gentile converts. He wanted his “spiritual children” to become like him: committed to Christ and involved in His mission in the world. Calling Gentiles to “the obedience that comes from faith” was an important aspect of his apostolic ministry. Paul could have mentioned other qualities that are desirable for disciples, such as being knowledgeable of God’s will and ways, being effective preachers of the gospel, or being filled with the Spirit and with compassion. But just as Jesus identified obedience as essential (Matthew 28:18–20), this is Paul’s focus as well when he speaks of obedience that comes from faith. For example, notice how Paul uses faith and obedience in these parallel passages (italics added):

Romans 1:8: “ Your faith is being reported all over the world.”

Romans 16:19: “Everyone has heard about your obedience .”

Hebrews 11:8 also illustrates this faith-obedience relationship: “ By faith Abraham, when called to go . . . , obeyed and went” (italics added). We cannot separate obedience from faith and still have true faith; they are inseparable.

In Ephesians 2:8–10, Paul explains how salvation by faith (and without works) actually leads to good works. Does James contradict Paul when he says, “A person is justified by what he does and not by faith alone” (James 2:24)? The answer is, “No.” James uses the term “what he does” (Greek, ergon ) in a sense different from Paul’s use of works in Ephesians 2:9. Paul emphasizes that salvation rests on God’s grace alone, not on our efforts (Romans 4:3; Galatians 3:6; Ephesians 2:8–10). James, on the other hand, distinguishes true faith with its deeds from false faith without deeds (James 2:14–26). Our actions or deeds confirm that our faith is genuine, not superficial.

Next in his opening remarks, Paul reminds the Romans, “And you also are among those who are called to belong to Jesus Christ” (Romans 1:6). Not only was Paul called (1:1) but so were the Romans. In truth, God calls everyone to belong to Jesus Christ. What a tragedy that all do not respond!

Now Paul returns to his greeting: “To all in Rome who are loved by God and called to be saints ” (Romans 1:7). The message here is that God’s love is not conditional or based on our fulfillment of certain requirements. He calls us because He loves us. When we put our trust in Christ as Savior, we become saints. Saints is not a title attained by merit. Rather, we are saints because we are “set apart” from the world and to God the moment we receive Christ as Savior.

“Grace and peace to you from God our Father and from the Lord Jesus Christ” (Romans 1:7). “Grace and peace to you.” Paul’s greeting here takes the form of a blessing or prayer. Grace describes the favor God extends to everyone through Jesus Christ. Peace portrays the well-being of those who receive His grace. These two words sum up the source and effect of our salvation.

“Lord Jesus Christ” (Romans 1:7). Each word in this title is significant. Jesus means “Savior” and speaks of His humanity. He came to earth at a specific point in history in order to save us from our sins (see 1 Timothy 2:5–6). Christ (or Messiah ) means “the anointed one” and describes His ministry (see Acts 10:38). Lord speaks of His sovereignty and deity. He is our Master, Ruler, and King. All authority in heaven and earth has been given to Him.

Paul’s Proposed Visit (1:8–15)

Now Paul resumes his greeting with the words, “First, I thank my God through Jesus Christ for all of you, because your faith is being reported all over the world” (Romans 1:8). First, I thank God for all of you. Paul links his greetings to his proposed visit by following his usual pattern (Galatians is an exception), thanking God for them and commending the believers in Rome on one of their strong points: Your faith is being reported all over the world. This moved Paul to encourage them with the assurance of “how constantly I remember you in my prayers at all times” (1:9–10). What an insight into a successful ministry! That an apostle would pray for churches to whom he had ministered is no surprise. But to pray regularly for a church he had never visited is noteworthy. Here is a prayer life we can imitate (see 1 Thessalonians 1:6–7; 2:14).

“I pray that now at last by God’s will the way may be opened for me to come to you” (Romans 1:10). Paul not only prayed for these believers, but he also sought for an opportunity to minister to them.

“I long to see you so that I may impart to you some spiritual gift to make you strong—that is, that you and I may be mutually encouraged by each other’s faith” (Romans 1:11–12). Impart can also be translated “share,” as in 1 Thessalonians 2:8. Paul is not saying that he distributes spiritual gifts; rather, he shares with others what God has given to him. “To make you strong” expresses his desire for these Romans.

The expression mutually encouraged (Romans 1:12) is not found elsewhere in the Bible. Nevertheless, it characterizes believers and describes their relationship to one another in Christ. As each part of our physical body benefits the whole, so each believer has unique qualities that can enrich the body of Christ.

Next, Paul writes, “I planned many times to come to you . . . in order that I might have a harvest among you, just as I have had among the other Gentiles” (Romans 1:13). As we saw earlier, Paul wanted the Romans to be aware that he prayed for them and that he had planned many times to visit them but had been “prevented from doing so until now” (1:13). The term harvest includes not only winning new converts but also strengthening the church (1:11), just as Paul had done among Gentiles in other places.

Paul explains why he wants to visit the church in Rome: “I am obligated both to Greeks and non-Greeks, both to the wise and the foolish. That is why I am so eager to preach the gospel also to you who are at Rome” (Romans 1:14–15). The New Living Translation clarifies what Paul says in verse 14: “I have a great sense of obligation to people in our culture and to people in other cultures, to the educated and the uneducated alike.” Paul was eager to preach the gospel to all people, whatever their language, culture, or status in life.

What practical value is in Paul’s introductory remarks (Romans 1:8–15)? Can you identify leadership traits that are applicable to you? Observe that he led not only by exhortation but also by example. What does this suggest to you concerning your own ministry?

**Q:** We have indicated which of these three views is the “most likely” one. Do you agree with this view? Give evidence to support your response.

**A:** Your answer. There seems to be strong biblical support for the third view based on Acts 2:10–11. One might argue that people from many nations saw and heard what happened on the Day of Pentecost not by coincidence, but by an act of God. We know that three thousand people in this diverse crowd accepted the gospel message Peter preached. The Romans among them likely took the message back to their own people, as did the converts from other nations.

**Q:** Read the first verse in each of these Epistles: Romans, 1 and 2 Corinthians, Galatians, Ephesians, Colossians, 1 and 2 Timothy. What title does Paul use to describe himself in Romans 1 that he does not use in these other passages?

**A:** A servant of Jesus Christ

**Q:** Read the following Scripture passage, and summarize what each teaches about the origin and names of God’s Son: (a) John 1:1, 14; (b) Matthew 1:22–23; (c) 1 Timothy 2:5.

**A:** a He was the Word; He was God; and He was with God in the beginning. He is the one and only Son who came from the Father. b He is called Immanuel, meaning, “God with us.” c He is the mediator between God and humanity.

**Q:** Did God answer Paul’s prayer about mutual encouragement (Romans 1:10–12)? For the answer, read Acts 28:14–16 and Philippians 1:12–14.

**A:** The answer is “yes.” Paul was encouraged and the brothers in the Lord were encouraged.

**Q:** Describe what is implied about Paul’s harvest in Rome based on these passages: Acts 28:17, 23–24; Philippians 1:12–14; Philippians 4:22.

**A:** Acts 28:17, 23–24 There were Jewish converts as a result of Paul’s witness. Philippians 1:12–14 Roman believers were encouraged by Paul’s witness to speak more courageously. Philippians 4:22 There were converts in Caesar’s household as a result of Paul’s witness.

### Paul’s Theme (1:16–17)

#### 1.4.1 — Given the key words from Romans 1:16–17, identify the essence of Paul’s theme that is developed in the remainder of his letter to the Romans. The Apostle Paul

Romans 1:16–17 summarizes the theme of the book—God’s righteousness. Paul develops this theme by showing how God’s righteousness is revealed (chapters 1–8), vindicated (chapters 9–11), and applied (chapters 12–16). To conclude this lesson, let us consider in verses 16 and 17 the key concepts—gospel of Jesus Christ, salvation, everyone who believes, righteousness, and faith—that introduce us to Paul’s development of his theme.

I am not ashamed of the gospel, because it is the power of God for the salvation of everyone who believes: first for the Jew, then for the Gentile. For in the gospel a righteousness from God is revealed, a righteousness that is by faith from first to last, just as it is written: “The righteous will live by faith.”

— Romans 1:16–17

The Gospel of Jesus Christ

“I am not ashamed of the gospel” (Romans 1:16). Roman citizens were accustomed to watching world leaders—senators, scholars, soldiers, Caesars—move about their city in pomp and ceremony. In the coming days, a man of small stature would walk down these same streets, chained to a Roman soldier. “Probably a criminal on his way to judgment,” the few who noticed would imagine. Nothing about his appearance would suggest power or influence. Neither would those who heard him say, “I am a servant of Jesus Christ” suspect that he represented the Creator of the universe. Nevertheless, this unimposing man had a message for the world’s ills. Firm determination marked his face, and he would not be intimidated. He simply declared, “I am not ashamed of the gospel.”

We must not lose the significance of this word gospel . It is good news! It has power to impart life to the desperate and dying, deliverance to the condemned, and hope and healing to the hurting! Thus, Paul was “eager to preach the gospel” (Romans 1:15) in Rome.

Not everyone saw the benefits of Paul’s message. The powerful ridiculed it, and the Jews stumbled over it. How could a crucified Christ bring deliverance? Jews demanded miraculous signs and Greeks looked for wisdom (1 Corinthians 1:22–23). Romans might have asked, “How can this carpenter condemned as a criminal save anyone? If He could not save himself, how can He save us?” Unbelievers scoffed at the gospel. Nevertheless, Paul says, “it is the power of God for the salvation of everyone who believes” (Romans 1:16).

The Romans understood power. Their armies conquered the Mediterranean nations and controlled most of the Western world. Their political leaders determined the fate of multitudes—declaring what was right or wrong, deciding who lived or died. But Paul describes a different kind of power—a power that sets people free from the bondage of sin and Satan.

Without question, God’s power is limitless. Yet He chooses to limit himself, at times, to our understanding and faith. Many Jews missed their Messiah because they misunderstood Scripture. A crucified Christ bewildered them, became an obstacle to their faith, and they stumbled. God often uses what the world considers weakness to display His power (see 2 Corinthians 12:9–10).

Another example of misunderstanding God’s ways is given in 2 Kings 5. Naaman, the Aramean military commander, had preconceived notions of God’s ways. In fact, he nearly missed the healing of his leprosy because he thought God should do things differently. These examples should cause us to ask, Do we sometimes limit God by our own preconceived ideas?

The gospel of Jesus Christ is powerful; it changes lives. It transformed Paul from persecutor to apostle. The pages of church history are filled with similar examples. In fact, every true believer today—including you and me—is a testimony of the power of the gospel to change lives.

Salvation

“The gospel is the power of God for the salvation of everyone who believes” (Romans 1:16). A deep-sea diver wears a pressurized suit to descend into the sea. Assistants on the surface pump air through a hose so that he or she can breathe. This hose is called a lifeline because the diver will die without it. Likewise, God is our life source. Sin broke the lifeline between God and humankind, and humanity died spiritually. However, salvation through Jesus Christ restores life.

Salvation is a general term that includes justification, sanctification, and glorification . It is the opposite of “perishing” or “being lost” (see Luke 19:10; John 3:16). The word is used in both a positive and negative sense. Negatively, it means to save from God’s wrath (Romans 5:9). Positively, it means that we are raised up with Christ and God has “seated us with him in the heavenly realms in Christ Jesus, in order that in the coming ages he might show the incomparable riches of his grace” (Ephesians 2:6–7). The full revelation of what it means to become sons of God will not occur until the end (Romans 8:18–19, 30). “What we will be has not yet been made known. But we know that when he appears, we shall be like him, for we shall see him as he is” (1 John 3:2–3). See the following chart.

Salvation

(Adapted from Hendriksen, 1981, 100)

In Its Negative Sense

In Its Positive Sense

To save is to rescue people from sin’s

Guilt (Eph. 1:7; Col. 1:14)

Pollution (Rom. 6:6, 17; 7:21–25)

Slavery (Rom. 7:24–25; Gal. 5:1)

Punishment:

1. Alienation from God (Eph. 2:12)

2. The wrath of God (Eph. 2:3)

3. Everlasting death (Eph. 2:1–3)

To save is to bring people to a state of

Righteousness (Rom. 3:21–26; 5:1)

Holiness (Rom. 6:1–4; 12:1–2)

Freedom (Gal. 5:1; 2 Cor. 3:17)

Blessedness:

1. Fellowship with God (Eph. 2:13)

2. God’s love poured out in the heart (Rom. 5:5)

3. Everlasting life (Eph. 2:1, 4–5; Col. 3:1–4)

Everyone Who Believes

Salvation is available to all—Jews and Gentiles alike—who exercise faith in Christ. “First for the Jew, then for the Gentile” is the historical order (Romans 1:16). Jews were given the first opportunity to accept the gospel (see Acts 3:25–26). Scripture reveals this pattern. God chose Abraham and blessed him so that through him all nations might be blessed (Genesis 12:1–3). When Jesus first sent out His disciples, He instructed them to go only to “the lost sheep of Israel” (Matthew 10:6). Later, after His death and resurrection, He commissioned them to take the gospel to all nations (Matthew 28:18–20). Paul followed this same pattern by preaching first to the Jews, then to the Gentiles (Acts 13:5, 14, 46–47; 14:1; 17:1–2, 10, 17; 18:4, 19; 19:8). God intended for the Jews to be a light to the Gentiles and take salvation to the ends of the earth (Acts 13:47; Isaiah 49:6; Genesis 12:1–3; Exodus 19:5–6). Yet with privilege comes responsibility—we observe this same priority in judgment: “There will be trouble and distress for every human being who does evil: first for the Jew, then for the Gentile ” (Romans 2:9, italics added).

Righteousness

In Romans 1:17, Paul declares, “For in the gospel a righteousness from God is revealed.” Righteousness (Greek, dikaiosyne ) can refer either to “God’s righteousness” or “the righteousness God gives to those who believe” (Comfort 1994, 22). Usually, we speak of God’s righteousness in relation to His character or activity. But here it refers to a righteousness God imparts to those who put their trust in Christ as Savior. That is, we are credited as righteous, or declared to be “in the right” in God’s sight.

The word revealed in Romans 1:17 pictures the uncovering of something that was hidden. Unless God had chosen to reveal himself and His ways to us, we would be lost. This is what the gospel does. It makes known to us “a righteousness from God.” It is the story of God’s effort to reveal himself through the life, death, and resurrection of His Son.

Faith

Paul speaks in Romans 1:17 of “a righteousness that is by faith from first to last.” He tells the Roman believers, and us, that we receive this righteousness from God by faith. As Paul develops his teaching, he explains more fully the concept of God’s crediting us with righteousness as we put our trust in Christ’s sacrificial death on our behalf. When he says, “by faith from first to last,” he uses the word faith in two ways: (1) as the crisis act of faith that brings us into a right relationship with God—right standing—and (2) as the continuing active faith that reveals God’s righteousness in a person’s daily life—right living.

Then Paul supports his teaching by saying, “As it is written: ‘The righteous will live by faith’” (Romans 1:17). Paul includes the phrase “As it is written” because he wants the Romans to understand that he is not introducing a new concept. Rather, his teaching is founded on the unchangeable Word of God. Thus he quotes the prophet Habakkuk (2:4) to show that faith was clearly taught in the Hebrew Scriptures. (See also Hebrews 10:38.)

Bible scholars view Habakkuk’s statement in two ways:

1. Some insist that it is better translated: “But the righteous by faith will live.” They attach the words by faith to the words the righteous instead of will live to underscore how believers are justified. Because we are justified by faith, we will live; that is, God gives us eternal life because we trust Him.

2. Others follow the NIV: “The righteous will live by faith.” Here the emphasis is on how the righteous live—by faith. Those who favor this view argue that the context of Galatians 3:11 (where Paul quotes this same passage) clearly favors “will live by faith.” The Galatians passage contrasts observing the law with living by faith.

Rather than considering these as rival views, it seems wiser to see them as two aspects of our salvation. First, we enter into a right relationship with God by faith; then, we continue to walk with Him by faith.

We conclude this introductory lesson to Romans with an illustration from the life of Augustine of Hippo (AD 354–430), just one of many great historical examples of how the power of the gospel changes lives.

The Power of the Gospel

Augustine, a professor of oratory in Milan, struggled for years with carnal desire and lust. Then, one day he picked up a copy of Paul’s letter to the Romans and read:

Don’t participate in wild parties and getting drunk, or in adultery and immoral living, or in fighting and jealousy. But let the Lord Jesus Christ take control of you, and don’t think of ways to indulge your evil desires.

— Romans 13:13–14, NLT

“In an instant,” he later wrote, “the light of confidence flooded into my heart and all the darkness of doubt was dispelled” (Edwards 1992, 1). As a result, this fourth-century North African (from present-day Algeria) committed his life to Christ and became a prominent church leader. His writings still influence theological thought today.

**Q:** Review the Salvation chart and Romans 1:16–17, and describe one important lesson that you can apply to the following: (a) Your daily life; (b) Your ministry to unbelievers.
