**A:** Your answers. I trust that this lesson has given you a new determination to preach the gospel of Jesus Christ with great zeal and to pattern your life after the examples of Jesus Christ and His servant Paul. 2 CHAPTER

## 2 - People Need a Savior: Romans 1:18–3:20

### The Gentiles Are Guilty (Romans 1:18–32)

#### 2.1.1 — Identify those to whom Paul speaks in Romans 1:18–32 as well as the source and content of his message in this passage.

In Chapter 1, we identified unique features of Paul’s letter to the Romans. We also examined background issues of Romans, such as the origin and characteristics of the church and Paul’s purpose in writing the letter. Then we looked at Paul’s introductory remarks.

Bible teachers sometimes liken this introductory section to a courtroom scene. Charges are presented, the evidence is weighed, and the verdict is given. Paul begins his case with the evidence against the Gentiles. The verdict: Gentiles are guilty and “without excuse” (Romans 1:18–32).

The Jews listen carefully as Paul presents his case. Then, as the verdict is rendered, they applaud. “Yes,” they say without hesitation, “the Gentiles are guilty!” To this Paul responds, “You are also guilty and without excuse.” Why? The answer is given in Romans 2:1: “At whatever point you judge the other, you are condemning yourself, because you who pass judgment do the same things.”

Finally, Paul sums up the verdict of the righteous Judge: “There is no one righteous, not even one” (Romans 3:10). To preface this, he says, “Jews and Gentiles alike are all under sin” (3:9). Paul confronts all people with the painful truth of their guilt for one reason. He wants them to appreciate and respond to God’s offer of a Savior. Sinful, guilty people need a Savior. That is the heart of the gospel message.

In Romans 1:18–32, Paul explains the guilt of the Gentiles in the light of God’s wrath. It is important to observe that he presents this explanation as divine revelation. In this section, we will consider the revelation, nature, and consequences of God’s wrath.

The Revelation of God’s Wrath

Some scholars reject the idea that Paul describes Gentiles in Romans 1:18–32. For example, they say, “He does not use the word Gentile anywhere in this passage.” Furthermore, they argue, “Jews are guilty of the same sins.” The scholars conclude that Paul describes sinners in general, not just Gentiles. Admittedly, they make a valid point since sin crosses all ethnic boundaries.

Nevertheless, there are three reasons why we should identify this section with Gentiles. First, Paul maintains that these people are guilty and without excuse based on their knowledge of God from creation (Romans 1:18–32). The fact that the Law is not mentioned suggests they are Gentiles and not Jews. Second, the shift to another group of people in 2:1—later described as Jews (2:17)—implies that this first group consisted of Gentiles. Third, Paul’s conclusion in 3:9 shows that he was describing two groups of people: Gentiles in 1:18–32 and Jews beginning with chapter 2. In 3:9, Paul says, “We have already made the charge that Jews and Gentiles alike are all under sin.”

With Paul’s statement, “The wrath of God is being revealed from heaven” (Romans 1:18), he introduces us to a second revelation. We briefly looked at the first revelation in his opening remarks: “In the gospel a righteousness from God is revealed” (1:17). Now he presents a second one: “The wrath of God is being revealed from heaven.” Is Paul being inconsistent? Do these concepts contradict each other? The answer is, “No.” In a sense, they are opposite sides of the same coin. The wrath of God gives meaning both to the gospel and to God’s righteousness. By bringing these revelations together, Paul contrasts God’s righteousness with humankind’s unrighteousness. As he explains later, “Our unrighteousness brings out God’s righteousness more clearly” (3:5).

Paul is not sharing insights and conclusions reached through study or meditation. Instead, he presents divine revelation—revealed from heaven. God himself is actively involved in writing Scripture. It is “God-breathed” (2 Timothy 3:16–17; 2 Peter 1:20–21) and therefore authoritative. (See Revelation chart.)

Revelation

(Adapted from Hayford 1995, 746–747)

God reveals himself, His will, and His ways to humankind.

Without this revelation, individuals are unable to know God or His ways.

God Has Revealed Himself to Humanity in Four Ways:

1

Through human conscience in an innate sense of difference between right and wrong (Romans 2:14–15)

2

Through the natural creation by which He has displayed His glory to all humankind (Psalm 19:1–6; Romans 1:20)

3

Through His Word (Micah 6:8; John 20:31; 2 Timothy 3:15)

4

By His Son (John 14:9). Jesus Christ is His fullest and final revelation.

In the past God spoke to our forefathers through the prophets

at many times and in various ways,

but in these last days he has spoken to us by his Son,

whom he appointed heir of all things,

and through whom he made the universe.

—Hebrews 1:1–2

Christ “ made Him [God] known ” (John 1:18), gave us the words the Father gave Him (John 17:8), and revealed God’s self-giving love at the cross.

God reveals himself, His will, and His ways to us. Without this revelation, we are unable to know God or His ways. That is what Paul emphasized about the gospel in his letter to the Galatians: “I received it by revelation from Jesus Christ” (Galatians 1:12). He was not teaching a message he had fabricated but what he had received directly from Jesus Christ.

There are two kinds of revelation: general and special (see chart following). God makes himself known to all people everywhere through conscience and nature. This is called general revelation. For instance, the wonders of God’s creation in the natural world reveal God. Creation gives evidence that a Higher Power made us and the world we live in. Therefore, we should seek to find Him, learn what He requires, and obey Him to the best of our ability.

God also reveals himself through His Word. In the past He spoke through the prophets, but in these last days He has spoken to us by His Son (Hebrews 1:1–2). This is called special revelation.

Two Kinds of Revelation

GENERAL

SPECIAL

Creator and Judge Revealed in

Nature

History

Providence

Conscience

God and His Truth Revealed in

Bible

Prophecy

Miracles

Experience

Jesus Christ

The Nature of God’s Wrath

What is the nature of God’s wrath, and how is His wrath expressed? Consider how Paul describes it. He begins with this statement, “The wrath of God is being revealed from heaven against all the godlessness and wickedness of men who suppress the truth” (Romans 1:18). “The wrath of God” is God’s response to unrighteousness. Some people may protest, “For God to express anger is contrary to His character. After all, God is love.” Objections like this arise from human reasoning and ignore biblical evidence. “Revealed from heaven” stresses divine origin, not human logic. For example, look in your concordance at the number of references to God’s wrath and anger.

Wrath (Greek, orge ) is not a sudden emotional outburst of anger against something that has momentarily offended God. Rather, Paul explains, it is a gradual storing up that eventually results in eternal condemnation (Boice 1991). Paul writes, “Because of your stubbornness and your unrepentant heart, you are storing up wrath against yourself for the day of God’s wrath” (Romans 2:5).

God demonstrates extraordinary restraint toward those who are unrighteous because He desires that none perish (Ephesians 2:3–5; 2 Peter 3:9). God’s wrath is actually an expression of His love. It is not unreasonable or unjust. If He did not become angry at sin, He would not be holy or righteous. Because of His righteous nature, He resists sin and opposes evil.

Let us illustrate what we have just said. Suppose you have a young son and you see him being attacked by a wild animal. How would you react? What emotion would you display? Would you stand by smiling as the animal killed your son—or would you angrily fight to rescue him? Failure to defend him would make you a negligent parent.

Sin is more dangerous and destructive than any wild animal, since it ultimately destroys not only the body but also the soul. God in His love does not want to see His children destroyed. His very goodness makes Him react to those things that would destroy them. Therefore, God’s wrath is His response to sin. He opposes sin and wickedness because He is holy. He manifests His wrath not from sinful passion but because of His very character—His holiness.

God’s wrath is being revealed (Romans 1:18). Does this refer to future judgment? While we must not deny in any way a final end-time judgment (Romans 2:5; 1 Thessalonians 1:10; 2 Thessalonians 1:7–10; Revelation 19:15; 20:11–15), note that Paul uses the present tense here: is being revealed. God’s wrath is a continuing process in the present. Wickedness diminishes the value of life and carries a penalty in the present as well as in the future (see Romans 1:27; 6:23). Misery often comes from a rejection of God and His way.

The words godlessness and wickedness cast different shades of meaning on unrighteousness. Godlessness (Greek, asebia ) essentially points to offenses against God, while wickedness (Greek, adikia ) focuses on violations in human relationships (Edwards 1992). Wrong human relationships often reflect a wrong relationship with God.

Reasons for God’s Wrath

We please God when we respond in faith to what He says or reveals. On the other hand, we displease Him when we respond in unbelief (see Hebrews 11:6). To respond in faith to God’s revelation means to accept it. To respond in unbelief is to reject it. The Gentiles angered God because they rejected His revelation. For instance, Paul indicates that they (1) knew God but rejected Him and suppressed the truth and (2) exchanged the immortal God for images. By reading the entire passage (Romans 1:18–32), you can identify additional examples of unbelief.

The Gentiles knew God (Romans 1:21). They knew Him through creation and through their inner consciousness. God’s invisible qualities, Paul indicates, “have been clearly seen, being understood from what has been made” (1:20). In verse 19, Paul emphasizes that “what may be known about God is plain to them, because God has made it plain to them.” Here is a paradox—seeing what is invisible. The Amplified Bible translates verse 19 like this: “For that which is known about God is evident to them and made plain in their inner consciousness, because God [himself] has shown it to them.” Another version, the New Living Translation, says, “For the truth about God is known to them instinctively. God has put this knowledge in their hearts.”

God also reveals himself through creation: “The heavens declare the glory of God; the skies proclaim the work of his hands” (Psalm 19:1). God’s inner qualities are “understood from what has been made” (Romans 1:20). Furthermore, God created all people with an inner consciousness so that they can instinctively know Him. Accordingly, the Gentiles knew the following things about God: His invisible qualities—His eternal power and divine nature (1:20), His glory (1:23), His truth (1:25), and His righteous decree (1:32). Thus Paul declares that they are “without excuse” (1:20) because they “knew God” (1:21), they “exchanged the truth of God for a lie” (1:25), and they “did not think it worthwhile to retain the knowledge of God” (1:28). Even if they did not hear the gospel message, Paul affirms that they are without excuse! This suggests that if we reject the truth God gives us, He will not give us more.

The Gentiles suppressed the truth (Romans 1:18). People are without excuse when they “suppress the truth by their wickedness” (1:18). Although humans began with a knowledge of God and the truth (Genesis 2–3), they foolishly turned from God and suppressed the truth. As a result, they began to devolve (degenerate). Popular opinion today teaches just the opposite—that people evolved from inferior beasts into superior humans, that they began in ignorance and are gradually discovering the truth. Clearly this passage indicates that people willfully suppressed the truth so that they could live as they desired. They “exchanged the truth of God for a lie” (Romans 1:25). We see here that to suppress the truth is a demonstration of wickedness. To suppress truth means to “push away” or “prevent the truth from being known” by refusing to live by it. Eventually, this leads to a denial of reality (1:21–23), a lie (1:25), a depraved mind (1:28), and the approval of unrighteousness (1:32).

The Gentiles exchanged the immortal God for images (Romans 1:23). People rejected their Creator—the immortal God—and did the unthinkable: they turned around and created their own gods (images). In exchange for the all-powerful, all-wise, immortal God, they formed their own mortal gods of wood, stone, gold, and silver that could not see, hear, talk, or move and were powerless to save (see Isaiah 40:18–20). Eventually these images would degenerate and decay. Their images were “made to look like mortal man and birds and animals and reptiles” (Romans 1:23). When people reject the Creator, they end up worshiping the creature.

It was the greatest honor God did to man that he made man in the image of God; but it is the greatest dishonor man has done to God that he has made God in the image of man.

—Matthew Henry

The Consequences of God’s Wrath

Now let us turn our thoughts to the consequences of rejecting God and turning away from Him. Edwards says, “Although God made humanity free to refuse him, he did not make it free from the consequences of its refusal” (1992, 48).

The expression “God gave them over” is one that Paul uses three times to mark the downward influence of sin: “God gave them over in the sinful desires of their hearts” (Romans 1:24), “God gave them over to shameful lusts” (1:26), and “God . . . gave them over to a depraved mind” (1:28). These three passages reveal the gradually degenerating nature of sin. Sinful desires degenerate into shameful lusts and then further degenerate into a depraved mind.

Morris says that Paul “is not thinking of automatic sequence so much as the divine sentence. This is a moral universe and God has so made it that sin cannot but reap its reward” (1965, 82–183). Boice puts it this way: “When we run away from God we think our way will be uphill, because we want it to be so. But the way is actually downhill. We are pulled down by the law of moral gravity—when God lets go” (1991, 1:179). This explains how God abandons the wicked to their sins as an act of judgment. Moral evil has an inherent tendency to produce misery (Boice, 1991). God reveals His wrath against wicked people by letting sin run its course.

“God gave them over in the sinful desires of their hearts to sexual impurity” (Romans 1:24). Idolatry leads to immorality. When people reject God’s authority, they replace it with their own authority. In effect, they take God’s place and do what they want without restraint.

In what ways are idolatry and immorality linked together? Compare Paul’s comment here with the story of the Israelites and the golden calf. The Israelites “made it into an idol” and “got up to indulge in revelry” (Exodus 32:4, 6). In his letter to the Corinthian church, Paul specifically refers to this wilderness incident: “Do not be idolaters, as some of them were . . . to indulge in pagan revelry. We should not commit sexual immorality, as some of them did—and in one day twenty-three thousand of them died” (1 Corinthians 10:7–8).

Sinful desires (Greek, epithumia ) is sometimes translated “lusts” (Romans 1:24, 26) and sometimes “coveting” (7:7). The Greek Old Testament uses this same Greek word to translate the tenth commandment: “You shall not covet” (Exodus 20:17). Sinful desires may include a broad range of lusts (Comfort, 1992).

“God gave them over to shameful lusts” (Romans 1:26). Unrestrained, a person degenerates from sinful desires to shameful lusts (or “vile affections and degrading passions,” Amplified). Men and women alike who persist in removing moral restraints end up in perverted sexual practices. As a consequence, they receive “in themselves the due penalty for their perversion” (1:27).

“He gave them over to a depraved mind” (Romans 1:28). Note again the downward spiral: sinful desires to shameful lusts to a depraved mind. God removed His restraint—He “gave them over” as a form of judgment “since they did not think it worthwhile to retain the knowledge of God” (1:28). The word depraved means “debased, corrupt, deteriorated, perverted.” A depraved mind is an “evil mind” (NLT), a “useless mind” (CEV), or a “base and condemned mind” (Amplified). The price is high for rejecting God. Read Romans 1:29–32 for more examples of evil that come from rejecting God.

Jesus explained to His disciples that a person is not defiled by violating external rules but by an impure heart. He named a number of evils (Mark 7:20–22) and said, “All these evils come from inside and make a man unclean” (7:23).

Paul then declares, in Romans 1:32, “They not only continue to do these very things but also approve of those who practice them.” Here is the extreme sin—approving rather than regretting the sins of others (NIV Study Bible note, 1985).

Let us consider the concept of retribution for a moment. This principle which operates in the moral universe is that God rewards people according to their deeds. Good deeds are rewarded in a positive way, while evil deeds are punished. In other words, it is the principle of sowing and reaping: “The one who sows to please his sinful nature, from that nature will reap destruction; the one who sows to please the Spirit, from the Spirit will reap eternal life” (Galatians 6:8).

As Paul explains, individuals rejected God, then boasted of their wisdom and “claimed to be wise.” In reality, however, “they became fools” (Romans 1:22). The word fools here does not mean “intellectual deficiency”; rather, it means “moral dullness.” This led to the “degrading of their bodies” (1:24), abandoning “natural relations” (1:26–27), and receiving “in themselves the due penalty for their perversion” (1:27). An aspect of retribution is that “God gave them over” (1:24). It is a terrible thing when God abandons a wicked person to his or her own ways and lets sin run its course. This is the worst judgment God can give to sinful people—to lift His restraining hand and allow them to do as they please.

God manifests His wrath by paying sinners what they deserve. The punishment fits the offense. The just payment for wickedness and rebellion is death. Rejecting God, the source of life, leads to death. People reap what they sow. “The wages of sin is death” (6:23). Sin in any form progressively grows stronger, spreads, and becomes worse. Whoever chooses to abandon God and truth for his or her own way launches on a downward slope that leads to destruction.

This clear picture of the downward spiral of sin and its consequences should cause every believer to share Paul’s earnest and heartfelt desire to rescue those who have refused to acknowledge God and have instead chosen the path of sin.

**Q:** Considering all of the evidence in Romans 1:18–32, whom may we conclude are the recipients of God’s wrath Paul describes in this passage?

**A:** The Gentiles who are guilty

**Q:** Based on our study of Romans 1:18, describe God’s wrath in your own words.

**A:** Students answers will vary but should include something like, Wrath as used in this passage includes the idea of a gradual storing up of wrath. The wrath of God is an expression of His righteousness. Wrath is a part of God’s activity in the present. Wrath is God’s response to sin.

**Q:** Why did God hold the Gentiles responsible for their actions?

**A:** They rejected what they knew was true and exchanged worship of the Creator for what was man-made. They turned away from what was instinctively known and denied what was clear in their inner consciousness.

**Q:** In view of the progressive nature of sin, what is the most important thing a Christian can do to escape the consequences of God’s wrath (Romans 1:18–32)?

**A:** A Christian must choose to give the authority over his or her life to Jesus Christ and determine to follow Him and obey His will.

**Q:** What can you do to emphasize the importance of this truth in your ministry to others?

**A:** By word and example teach that the only way to resist the downward path of sin is to honor God and make Him Lord of one’s life in every situation.

**Q:** What is the best description of divine retribution?

**A:** Just payment

#### 2.1.2 — Select statements that describe the nature, expression, and reasons for God’s wrath.

In Chapter 1, we identified unique features of Paul’s letter to the Romans. We also examined background issues of Romans, such as the origin and characteristics of the church and Paul’s purpose in writing the letter. Then we looked at Paul’s introductory remarks.

Bible teachers sometimes liken this introductory section to a courtroom scene. Charges are presented, the evidence is weighed, and the verdict is given. Paul begins his case with the evidence against the Gentiles. The verdict: Gentiles are guilty and “without excuse” (Romans 1:18–32).

The Jews listen carefully as Paul presents his case. Then, as the verdict is rendered, they applaud. “Yes,” they say without hesitation, “the Gentiles are guilty!” To this Paul responds, “You are also guilty and without excuse.” Why? The answer is given in Romans 2:1: “At whatever point you judge the other, you are condemning yourself, because you who pass judgment do the same things.”

Finally, Paul sums up the verdict of the righteous Judge: “There is no one righteous, not even one” (Romans 3:10). To preface this, he says, “Jews and Gentiles alike are all under sin” (3:9). Paul confronts all people with the painful truth of their guilt for one reason. He wants them to appreciate and respond to God’s offer of a Savior. Sinful, guilty people need a Savior. That is the heart of the gospel message.

In Romans 1:18–32, Paul explains the guilt of the Gentiles in the light of God’s wrath. It is important to observe that he presents this explanation as divine revelation. In this section, we will consider the revelation, nature, and consequences of God’s wrath.

The Revelation of God’s Wrath

Some scholars reject the idea that Paul describes Gentiles in Romans 1:18–32. For example, they say, “He does not use the word Gentile anywhere in this passage.” Furthermore, they argue, “Jews are guilty of the same sins.” The scholars conclude that Paul describes sinners in general, not just Gentiles. Admittedly, they make a valid point since sin crosses all ethnic boundaries.

Nevertheless, there are three reasons why we should identify this section with Gentiles. First, Paul maintains that these people are guilty and without excuse based on their knowledge of God from creation (Romans 1:18–32). The fact that the Law is not mentioned suggests they are Gentiles and not Jews. Second, the shift to another group of people in 2:1—later described as Jews (2:17)—implies that this first group consisted of Gentiles. Third, Paul’s conclusion in 3:9 shows that he was describing two groups of people: Gentiles in 1:18–32 and Jews beginning with chapter 2. In 3:9, Paul says, “We have already made the charge that Jews and Gentiles alike are all under sin.”

With Paul’s statement, “The wrath of God is being revealed from heaven” (Romans 1:18), he introduces us to a second revelation. We briefly looked at the first revelation in his opening remarks: “In the gospel a righteousness from God is revealed” (1:17). Now he presents a second one: “The wrath of God is being revealed from heaven.” Is Paul being inconsistent? Do these concepts contradict each other? The answer is, “No.” In a sense, they are opposite sides of the same coin. The wrath of God gives meaning both to the gospel and to God’s righteousness. By bringing these revelations together, Paul contrasts God’s righteousness with humankind’s unrighteousness. As he explains later, “Our unrighteousness brings out God’s righteousness more clearly” (3:5).

Paul is not sharing insights and conclusions reached through study or meditation. Instead, he presents divine revelation—revealed from heaven. God himself is actively involved in writing Scripture. It is “God-breathed” (2 Timothy 3:16–17; 2 Peter 1:20–21) and therefore authoritative. (See Revelation chart.)

Revelation

(Adapted from Hayford 1995, 746–747)

God reveals himself, His will, and His ways to humankind.

Without this revelation, individuals are unable to know God or His ways.

God Has Revealed Himself to Humanity in Four Ways:

1

Through human conscience in an innate sense of difference between right and wrong (Romans 2:14–15)

2

Through the natural creation by which He has displayed His glory to all humankind (Psalm 19:1–6; Romans 1:20)

3

Through His Word (Micah 6:8; John 20:31; 2 Timothy 3:15)

4

By His Son (John 14:9). Jesus Christ is His fullest and final revelation.

In the past God spoke to our forefathers through the prophets

at many times and in various ways,

but in these last days he has spoken to us by his Son,

whom he appointed heir of all things,

and through whom he made the universe.

—Hebrews 1:1–2

Christ “ made Him [God] known ” (John 1:18), gave us the words the Father gave Him (John 17:8), and revealed God’s self-giving love at the cross.

God reveals himself, His will, and His ways to us. Without this revelation, we are unable to know God or His ways. That is what Paul emphasized about the gospel in his letter to the Galatians: “I received it by revelation from Jesus Christ” (Galatians 1:12). He was not teaching a message he had fabricated but what he had received directly from Jesus Christ.

There are two kinds of revelation: general and special (see chart following). God makes himself known to all people everywhere through conscience and nature. This is called general revelation. For instance, the wonders of God’s creation in the natural world reveal God. Creation gives evidence that a Higher Power made us and the world we live in. Therefore, we should seek to find Him, learn what He requires, and obey Him to the best of our ability.

God also reveals himself through His Word. In the past He spoke through the prophets, but in these last days He has spoken to us by His Son (Hebrews 1:1–2). This is called special revelation.

Two Kinds of Revelation

The Nature of God’s Wrath

What is the nature of God’s wrath, and how is His wrath expressed? Consider how Paul describes it. He begins with this statement, “The wrath of God is being revealed from heaven against all the godlessness and wickedness of men who suppress the truth” (Romans 1:18). “The wrath of God” is God’s response to unrighteousness. Some people may protest, “For God to express anger is contrary to His character. After all, God is love.” Objections like this arise from human reasoning and ignore biblical evidence. “Revealed from heaven” stresses divine origin, not human logic. For example, look in your concordance at the number of references to God’s wrath and anger.

Wrath (Greek, orge ) is not a sudden emotional outburst of anger against something that has momentarily offended God. Rather, Paul explains, it is a gradual storing up that eventually results in eternal condemnation (Boice 1991). Paul writes, “Because of your stubbornness and your unrepentant heart, you are storing up wrath against yourself for the day of God’s wrath” (Romans 2:5).

God demonstrates extraordinary restraint toward those who are unrighteous because He desires that none perish (Ephesians 2:3–5; 2 Peter 3:9). God’s wrath is actually an expression of His love. It is not unreasonable or unjust. If He did not become angry at sin, He would not be holy or righteous. Because of His righteous nature, He resists sin and opposes evil.

Let us illustrate what we have just said. Suppose you have a young son and you see him being attacked by a wild animal. How would you react? What emotion would you display? Would you stand by smiling as the animal killed your son—or would you angrily fight to rescue him? Failure to defend him would make you a negligent parent.

Sin is more dangerous and destructive than any wild animal, since it ultimately destroys not only the body but also the soul. God in His love does not want to see His children destroyed. His very goodness makes Him react to those things that would destroy them. Therefore, God’s wrath is His response to sin. He opposes sin and wickedness because He is holy. He manifests His wrath not from sinful passion but because of His very character—His holiness.

God’s wrath is being revealed (Romans 1:18). Does this refer to future judgment? While we must not deny in any way a final end-time judgment (Romans 2:5; 1 Thessalonians 1:10; 2 Thessalonians 1:7–10; Revelation 19:15; 20:11–15), note that Paul uses the present tense here: is being revealed. God’s wrath is a continuing process in the present. Wickedness diminishes the value of life and carries a penalty in the present as well as in the future (see Romans 1:27; 6:23). Misery often comes from a rejection of God and His way.

The words godlessness and wickedness cast different shades of meaning on unrighteousness. Godlessness (Greek, asebia ) essentially points to offenses against God, while wickedness (Greek, adikia ) focuses on violations in human relationships (Edwards 1992). Wrong human relationships often reflect a wrong relationship with God.

Reasons for God’s Wrath

We please God when we respond in faith to what He says or reveals. On the other hand, we displease Him when we respond in unbelief (see Hebrews 11:6). To respond in faith to God’s revelation means to accept it. To respond in unbelief is to reject it. The Gentiles angered God because they rejected His revelation. For instance, Paul indicates that they (1) knew God but rejected Him and suppressed the truth and (2) exchanged the immortal God for images. By reading the entire passage (Romans 1:18–32), you can identify additional examples of unbelief.

The Gentiles knew God (Romans 1:21). They knew Him through creation and through their inner consciousness. God’s invisible qualities, Paul indicates, “have been clearly seen, being understood from what has been made” (1:20). In verse 19, Paul emphasizes that “what may be known about God is plain to them, because God has made it plain to them.” Here is a paradox—seeing what is invisible. The Amplified Bible translates verse 19 like this: “For that which is known about God is evident to them and made plain in their inner consciousness, because God [himself] has shown it to them.” Another version, the New Living Translation, says, “For the truth about God is known to them instinctively. God has put this knowledge in their hearts.”

God also reveals himself through creation: “The heavens declare the glory of God; the skies proclaim the work of his hands” (Psalm 19:1). God’s inner qualities are “understood from what has been made” (Romans 1:20). Furthermore, God created all people with an inner consciousness so that they can instinctively know Him. Accordingly, the Gentiles knew the following things about God: His invisible qualities—His eternal power and divine nature (1:20), His glory (1:23), His truth (1:25), and His righteous decree (1:32). Thus Paul declares that they are “without excuse” (1:20) because they “knew God” (1:21), they “exchanged the truth of God for a lie” (1:25), and they “did not think it worthwhile to retain the knowledge of God” (1:28). Even if they did not hear the gospel message, Paul affirms that they are without excuse! This suggests that if we reject the truth God gives us, He will not give us more.

The Gentiles suppressed the truth (Romans 1:18). People are without excuse when they “suppress the truth by their wickedness” (1:18). Although humans began with a knowledge of God and the truth (Genesis 2–3), they foolishly turned from God and suppressed the truth. As a result, they began to devolve (degenerate). Popular opinion today teaches just the opposite—that people evolved from inferior beasts into superior humans, that they began in ignorance and are gradually discovering the truth. Clearly this passage indicates that people willfully suppressed the truth so that they could live as they desired. They “exchanged the truth of God for a lie” (Romans 1:25). We see here that to suppress the truth is a demonstration of wickedness. To suppress truth means to “push away” or “prevent the truth from being known” by refusing to live by it. Eventually, this leads to a denial of reality (1:21–23), a lie (1:25), a depraved mind (1:28), and the approval of unrighteousness (1:32).

The Gentiles exchanged the immortal God for images (Romans 1:23). People rejected their Creator—the immortal God—and did the unthinkable: they turned around and created their own gods (images). In exchange for the all-powerful, all-wise, immortal God, they formed their own mortal gods of wood, stone, gold, and silver that could not see, hear, talk, or move and were powerless to save (see Isaiah 40:18–20). Eventually these images would degenerate and decay. Their images were “made to look like mortal man and birds and animals and reptiles” (Romans 1:23). When people reject the Creator, they end up worshiping the creature.

It was the greatest honor God did to man that he made man in the image of God; but it is the greatest dishonor man has done to God that he has made God in the image of man.

—Matthew Henry

The Consequences of God’s Wrath

Now let us turn our thoughts to the consequences of rejecting God and turning away from Him. Edwards says, “Although God made humanity free to refuse him, he did not make it free from the consequences of its refusal” (1992, 48).

The expression “God gave them over” is one that Paul uses three times to mark the downward influence of sin: “God gave them over in the sinful desires of their hearts” (Romans 1:24), “God gave them over to shameful lusts” (1:26), and “God . . . gave them over to a depraved mind” (1:28). These three passages reveal the gradually degenerating nature of sin. Sinful desires degenerate into shameful lusts and then further degenerate into a depraved mind.

Morris says that Paul “is not thinking of automatic sequence so much as the divine sentence. This is a moral universe and God has so made it that sin cannot but reap its reward” (1965, 82–183). Boice puts it this way: “When we run away from God we think our way will be uphill, because we want it to be so. But the way is actually downhill. We are pulled down by the law of moral gravity—when God lets go” (1991, 1:179). This explains how God abandons the wicked to their sins as an act of judgment. Moral evil has an inherent tendency to produce misery (Boice, 1991). God reveals His wrath against wicked people by letting sin run its course.

“God gave them over in the sinful desires of their hearts to sexual impurity” (Romans 1:24). Idolatry leads to immorality. When people reject God’s authority, they replace it with their own authority. In effect, they take God’s place and do what they want without restraint.

In what ways are idolatry and immorality linked together? Compare Paul’s comment here with the story of the Israelites and the golden calf. The Israelites “made it into an idol” and “got up to indulge in revelry” (Exodus 32:4, 6). In his letter to the Corinthian church, Paul specifically refers to this wilderness incident: “Do not be idolaters, as some of them were . . . to indulge in pagan revelry. We should not commit sexual immorality, as some of them did—and in one day twenty-three thousand of them died” (1 Corinthians 10:7–8).

Sinful desires (Greek, epithumia ) is sometimes translated “lusts” (Romans 1:24, 26) and sometimes “coveting” (7:7). The Greek Old Testament uses this same Greek word to translate the tenth commandment: “You shall not covet” (Exodus 20:17). Sinful desires may include a broad range of lusts (Comfort, 1992).

“God gave them over to shameful lusts” (Romans 1:26). Unrestrained, a person degenerates from sinful desires to shameful lusts (or “vile affections and degrading passions,” Amplified). Men and women alike who persist in removing moral restraints end up in perverted sexual practices. As a consequence, they receive “in themselves the due penalty for their perversion” (1:27).

“He gave them over to a depraved mind” (Romans 1:28). Note again the downward spiral: sinful desires to shameful lusts to a depraved mind. God removed His restraint—He “gave them over” as a form of judgment “since they did not think it worthwhile to retain the knowledge of God” (1:28). The word depraved means “debased, corrupt, deteriorated, perverted.” A depraved mind is an “evil mind” (NLT), a “useless mind” (CEV), or a “base and condemned mind” (Amplified). The price is high for rejecting God. Read Romans 1:29–32 for more examples of evil that come from rejecting God.

Jesus explained to His disciples that a person is not defiled by violating external rules but by an impure heart. He named a number of evils (Mark 7:20–22) and said, “All these evils come from inside and make a man unclean” (7:23).

Paul then declares, in Romans 1:32, “They not only continue to do these very things but also approve of those who practice them.” Here is the extreme sin—approving rather than regretting the sins of others (NIV Study Bible note, 1985).

Let us consider the concept of retribution for a moment. This principle which operates in the moral universe is that God rewards people according to their deeds. Good deeds are rewarded in a positive way, while evil deeds are punished. In other words, it is the principle of sowing and reaping: “The one who sows to please his sinful nature, from that nature will reap destruction; the one who sows to please the Spirit, from the Spirit will reap eternal life” (Galatians 6:8).

As Paul explains, individuals rejected God, then boasted of their wisdom and “claimed to be wise.” In reality, however, “they became fools” (Romans 1:22). The word fools here does not mean “intellectual deficiency”; rather, it means “moral dullness.” This led to the “degrading of their bodies” (1:24), abandoning “natural relations” (1:26–27), and receiving “in themselves the due penalty for their perversion” (1:27). An aspect of retribution is that “God gave them over” (1:24). It is a terrible thing when God abandons a wicked person to his or her own ways and lets sin run its course. This is the worst judgment God can give to sinful people—to lift His restraining hand and allow them to do as they please.

God manifests His wrath by paying sinners what they deserve. The punishment fits the offense. The just payment for wickedness and rebellion is death. Rejecting God, the source of life, leads to death. People reap what they sow. “The wages of sin is death” (6:23). Sin in any form progressively grows stronger, spreads, and becomes worse. Whoever chooses to abandon God and truth for his or her own way launches on a downward slope that leads to destruction.

This clear picture of the downward spiral of sin and its consequences should cause every believer to share Paul’s earnest and heartfelt desire to rescue those who have refused to acknowledge God and have instead chosen the path of sin.

**Q:** Considering all of the evidence in Romans 1:18–32, whom may we conclude are the recipients of God’s wrath Paul describes in this passage?

**A:** The Gentiles who are guilty

**Q:** Based on our study of Romans 1:18, describe God’s wrath in your own words.

**A:** Students answers will vary but should include something like, Wrath as used in this passage includes the idea of a gradual storing up of wrath. The wrath of God is an expression of His righteousness. Wrath is a part of God’s activity in the present. Wrath is God’s response to sin.

**Q:** Why did God hold the Gentiles responsible for their actions?

**A:** They rejected what they knew was true and exchanged worship of the Creator for what was man-made. They turned away from what was instinctively known and denied what was clear in their inner consciousness.

**Q:** In view of the progressive nature of sin, what is the most important thing a Christian can do to escape the consequences of God’s wrath (Romans 1:18–32)?

**A:** A Christian must choose to give the authority over his or her life to Jesus Christ and determine to follow Him and obey His will.

**Q:** What can you do to emphasize the importance of this truth in your ministry to others?

**A:** By word and example teach that the only way to resist the downward path of sin is to honor God and make Him Lord of one’s life in every situation.

**Q:** What is the best description of divine retribution?

**A:** Just payment

#### 2.1.3 — Assess what Romans 1:18–32 teaches about the consequences of God’s wrath, and apply this to your life and ministry.

In Chapter 1, we identified unique features of Paul’s letter to the Romans. We also examined background issues of Romans, such as the origin and characteristics of the church and Paul’s purpose in writing the letter. Then we looked at Paul’s introductory remarks.

Bible teachers sometimes liken this introductory section to a courtroom scene. Charges are presented, the evidence is weighed, and the verdict is given. Paul begins his case with the evidence against the Gentiles. The verdict: Gentiles are guilty and “without excuse” (Romans 1:18–32).

The Jews listen carefully as Paul presents his case. Then, as the verdict is rendered, they applaud. “Yes,” they say without hesitation, “the Gentiles are guilty!” To this Paul responds, “You are also guilty and without excuse.” Why? The answer is given in Romans 2:1: “At whatever point you judge the other, you are condemning yourself, because you who pass judgment do the same things.”

Finally, Paul sums up the verdict of the righteous Judge: “There is no one righteous, not even one” (Romans 3:10). To preface this, he says, “Jews and Gentiles alike are all under sin” (3:9). Paul confronts all people with the painful truth of their guilt for one reason. He wants them to appreciate and respond to God’s offer of a Savior. Sinful, guilty people need a Savior. That is the heart of the gospel message.

In Romans 1:18–32, Paul explains the guilt of the Gentiles in the light of God’s wrath. It is important to observe that he presents this explanation as divine revelation. In this section, we will consider the revelation, nature, and consequences of God’s wrath.

The Revelation of God’s Wrath

Some scholars reject the idea that Paul describes Gentiles in Romans 1:18–32. For example, they say, “He does not use the word Gentile anywhere in this passage.” Furthermore, they argue, “Jews are guilty of the same sins.” The scholars conclude that Paul describes sinners in general, not just Gentiles. Admittedly, they make a valid point since sin crosses all ethnic boundaries.

Nevertheless, there are three reasons why we should identify this section with Gentiles. First, Paul maintains that these people are guilty and without excuse based on their knowledge of God from creation (Romans 1:18–32). The fact that the Law is not mentioned suggests they are Gentiles and not Jews. Second, the shift to another group of people in 2:1—later described as Jews (2:17)—implies that this first group consisted of Gentiles. Third, Paul’s conclusion in 3:9 shows that he was describing two groups of people: Gentiles in 1:18–32 and Jews beginning with chapter 2. In 3:9, Paul says, “We have already made the charge that Jews and Gentiles alike are all under sin.”

With Paul’s statement, “The wrath of God is being revealed from heaven” (Romans 1:18), he introduces us to a second revelation. We briefly looked at the first revelation in his opening remarks: “In the gospel a righteousness from God is revealed” (1:17). Now he presents a second one: “The wrath of God is being revealed from heaven.” Is Paul being inconsistent? Do these concepts contradict each other? The answer is, “No.” In a sense, they are opposite sides of the same coin. The wrath of God gives meaning both to the gospel and to God’s righteousness. By bringing these revelations together, Paul contrasts God’s righteousness with humankind’s unrighteousness. As he explains later, “Our unrighteousness brings out God’s righteousness more clearly” (3:5).

Paul is not sharing insights and conclusions reached through study or meditation. Instead, he presents divine revelation—revealed from heaven. God himself is actively involved in writing Scripture. It is “God-breathed” (2 Timothy 3:16–17; 2 Peter 1:20–21) and therefore authoritative. (See Revelation chart.)

Revelation

(Adapted from Hayford 1995, 746–747)

God reveals himself, His will, and His ways to humankind.

Without this revelation, individuals are unable to know God or His ways.

God Has Revealed Himself to Humanity in Four Ways:

1

Through human conscience in an innate sense of difference between right and wrong (Romans 2:14–15)

2

Through the natural creation by which He has displayed His glory to all humankind (Psalm 19:1–6; Romans 1:20)

3

Through His Word (Micah 6:8; John 20:31; 2 Timothy 3:15)

4

By His Son (John 14:9). Jesus Christ is His fullest and final revelation.

In the past God spoke to our forefathers through the prophets

at many times and in various ways,

but in these last days he has spoken to us by his Son,

whom he appointed heir of all things,

and through whom he made the universe.

—Hebrews 1:1–2

Christ “ made Him [God] known ” (John 1:18), gave us the words the Father gave Him (John 17:8), and revealed God’s self-giving love at the cross.

God reveals himself, His will, and His ways to us. Without this revelation, we are unable to know God or His ways. That is what Paul emphasized about the gospel in his letter to the Galatians: “I received it by revelation from Jesus Christ” (Galatians 1:12). He was not teaching a message he had fabricated but what he had received directly from Jesus Christ.

There are two kinds of revelation: general and special (see chart following). God makes himself known to all people everywhere through conscience and nature. This is called general revelation. For instance, the wonders of God’s creation in the natural world reveal God. Creation gives evidence that a Higher Power made us and the world we live in. Therefore, we should seek to find Him, learn what He requires, and obey Him to the best of our ability.

God also reveals himself through His Word. In the past He spoke through the prophets, but in these last days He has spoken to us by His Son (Hebrews 1:1–2). This is called special revelation.

Two Kinds of Revelation

GENERAL

SPECIAL

Creator and Judge Revealed in

Nature

History

Providence

Conscience

God and His Truth Revealed in

Bible

Prophecy

Miracles

Experience

Jesus Christ

The Nature of God’s Wrath

What is the nature of God’s wrath, and how is His wrath expressed? Consider how Paul describes it. He begins with this statement, “The wrath of God is being revealed from heaven against all the godlessness and wickedness of men who suppress the truth” (Romans 1:18). “The wrath of God” is God’s response to unrighteousness. Some people may protest, “For God to express anger is contrary to His character. After all, God is love.” Objections like this arise from human reasoning and ignore biblical evidence. “Revealed from heaven” stresses divine origin, not human logic. For example, look in your concordance at the number of references to God’s wrath and anger.

Wrath (Greek, orge ) is not a sudden emotional outburst of anger against something that has momentarily offended God. Rather, Paul explains, it is a gradual storing up that eventually results in eternal condemnation (Boice 1991). Paul writes, “Because of your stubbornness and your unrepentant heart, you are storing up wrath against yourself for the day of God’s wrath” (Romans 2:5).

God demonstrates extraordinary restraint toward those who are unrighteous because He desires that none perish (Ephesians 2:3–5; 2 Peter 3:9). God’s wrath is actually an expression of His love. It is not unreasonable or unjust. If He did not become angry at sin, He would not be holy or righteous. Because of His righteous nature, He resists sin and opposes evil.

Let us illustrate what we have just said. Suppose you have a young son and you see him being attacked by a wild animal. How would you react? What emotion would you display? Would you stand by smiling as the animal killed your son—or would you angrily fight to rescue him? Failure to defend him would make you a negligent parent.

Sin is more dangerous and destructive than any wild animal, since it ultimately destroys not only the body but also the soul. God in His love does not want to see His children destroyed. His very goodness makes Him react to those things that would destroy them. Therefore, God’s wrath is His response to sin. He opposes sin and wickedness because He is holy. He manifests His wrath not from sinful passion but because of His very character—His holiness.

God’s wrath is being revealed (Romans 1:18). Does this refer to future judgment? While we must not deny in any way a final end-time judgment (Romans 2:5; 1 Thessalonians 1:10; 2 Thessalonians 1:7–10; Revelation 19:15; 20:11–15), note that Paul uses the present tense here: is being revealed. God’s wrath is a continuing process in the present. Wickedness diminishes the value of life and carries a penalty in the present as well as in the future (see Romans 1:27; 6:23). Misery often comes from a rejection of God and His way.

The words godlessness and wickedness cast different shades of meaning on unrighteousness. Godlessness (Greek, asebia ) essentially points to offenses against God, while wickedness (Greek, adikia ) focuses on violations in human relationships (Edwards 1992). Wrong human relationships often reflect a wrong relationship with God.

Reasons for God’s Wrath

We please God when we respond in faith to what He says or reveals. On the other hand, we displease Him when we respond in unbelief (see Hebrews 11:6). To respond in faith to God’s revelation means to accept it. To respond in unbelief is to reject it. The Gentiles angered God because they rejected His revelation. For instance, Paul indicates that they (1) knew God but rejected Him and suppressed the truth and (2) exchanged the immortal God for images. By reading the entire passage (Romans 1:18–32), you can identify additional examples of unbelief.

The Gentiles knew God (Romans 1:21). They knew Him through creation and through their inner consciousness. God’s invisible qualities, Paul indicates, “have been clearly seen, being understood from what has been made” (1:20). In verse 19, Paul emphasizes that “what may be known about God is plain to them, because God has made it plain to them.” Here is a paradox—seeing what is invisible. The Amplified Bible translates verse 19 like this: “For that which is known about God is evident to them and made plain in their inner consciousness, because God [himself] has shown it to them.” Another version, the New Living Translation, says, “For the truth about God is known to them instinctively. God has put this knowledge in their hearts.”

God also reveals himself through creation: “The heavens declare the glory of God; the skies proclaim the work of his hands” (Psalm 19:1). God’s inner qualities are “understood from what has been made” (Romans 1:20). Furthermore, God created all people with an inner consciousness so that they can instinctively know Him. Accordingly, the Gentiles knew the following things about God: His invisible qualities—His eternal power and divine nature (1:20), His glory (1:23), His truth (1:25), and His righteous decree (1:32). Thus Paul declares that they are “without excuse” (1:20) because they “knew God” (1:21), they “exchanged the truth of God for a lie” (1:25), and they “did not think it worthwhile to retain the knowledge of God” (1:28). Even if they did not hear the gospel message, Paul affirms that they are without excuse! This suggests that if we reject the truth God gives us, He will not give us more.

The Gentiles suppressed the truth (Romans 1:18). People are without excuse when they “suppress the truth by their wickedness” (1:18). Although humans began with a knowledge of God and the truth (Genesis 2–3), they foolishly turned from God and suppressed the truth. As a result, they began to devolve (degenerate). Popular opinion today teaches just the opposite—that people evolved from inferior beasts into superior humans, that they began in ignorance and are gradually discovering the truth. Clearly this passage indicates that people willfully suppressed the truth so that they could live as they desired. They “exchanged the truth of God for a lie” (Romans 1:25). We see here that to suppress the truth is a demonstration of wickedness. To suppress truth means to “push away” or “prevent the truth from being known” by refusing to live by it. Eventually, this leads to a denial of reality (1:21–23), a lie (1:25), a depraved mind (1:28), and the approval of unrighteousness (1:32).

The Gentiles exchanged the immortal God for images (Romans 1:23). People rejected their Creator—the immortal God—and did the unthinkable: they turned around and created their own gods (images). In exchange for the all-powerful, all-wise, immortal God, they formed their own mortal gods of wood, stone, gold, and silver that could not see, hear, talk, or move and were powerless to save (see Isaiah 40:18–20). Eventually these images would degenerate and decay. Their images were “made to look like mortal man and birds and animals and reptiles” (Romans 1:23). When people reject the Creator, they end up worshiping the creature.

It was the greatest honor God did to man that he made man in the image of God; but it is the greatest dishonor man has done to God that he has made God in the image of man.

—Matthew Henry

The Consequences of God’s Wrath

Now let us turn our thoughts to the consequences of rejecting God and turning away from Him. Edwards says, “Although God made humanity free to refuse him, he did not make it free from the consequences of its refusal” (1992, 48).

The expression “God gave them over” is one that Paul uses three times to mark the downward influence of sin: “God gave them over in the sinful desires of their hearts” (Romans 1:24), “God gave them over to shameful lusts” (1:26), and “God . . . gave them over to a depraved mind” (1:28). These three passages reveal the gradually degenerating nature of sin. Sinful desires degenerate into shameful lusts and then further degenerate into a depraved mind.

Morris says that Paul “is not thinking of automatic sequence so much as the divine sentence. This is a moral universe and God has so made it that sin cannot but reap its reward” (1965, 82–183). Boice puts it this way: “When we run away from God we think our way will be uphill, because we want it to be so. But the way is actually downhill. We are pulled down by the law of moral gravity—when God lets go” (1991, 1:179). This explains how God abandons the wicked to their sins as an act of judgment. Moral evil has an inherent tendency to produce misery (Boice, 1991). God reveals His wrath against wicked people by letting sin run its course.

“God gave them over in the sinful desires of their hearts to sexual impurity” (Romans 1:24). Idolatry leads to immorality. When people reject God’s authority, they replace it with their own authority. In effect, they take God’s place and do what they want without restraint.

In what ways are idolatry and immorality linked together? Compare Paul’s comment here with the story of the Israelites and the golden calf. The Israelites “made it into an idol” and “got up to indulge in revelry” (Exodus 32:4, 6). In his letter to the Corinthian church, Paul specifically refers to this wilderness incident: “Do not be idolaters, as some of them were . . . to indulge in pagan revelry. We should not commit sexual immorality, as some of them did—and in one day twenty-three thousand of them died” (1 Corinthians 10:7–8).

Sinful desires (Greek, epithumia ) is sometimes translated “lusts” (Romans 1:24, 26) and sometimes “coveting” (7:7). The Greek Old Testament uses this same Greek word to translate the tenth commandment: “You shall not covet” (Exodus 20:17). Sinful desires may include a broad range of lusts (Comfort, 1992).

“God gave them over to shameful lusts” (Romans 1:26). Unrestrained, a person degenerates from sinful desires to shameful lusts (or “vile affections and degrading passions,” Amplified). Men and women alike who persist in removing moral restraints end up in perverted sexual practices. As a consequence, they receive “in themselves the due penalty for their perversion” (1:27).

“He gave them over to a depraved mind” (Romans 1:28). Note again the downward spiral: sinful desires to shameful lusts to a depraved mind. God removed His restraint—He “gave them over” as a form of judgment “since they did not think it worthwhile to retain the knowledge of God” (1:28). The word depraved means “debased, corrupt, deteriorated, perverted.” A depraved mind is an “evil mind” (NLT), a “useless mind” (CEV), or a “base and condemned mind” (Amplified). The price is high for rejecting God. Read Romans 1:29–32 for more examples of evil that come from rejecting God.

Jesus explained to His disciples that a person is not defiled by violating external rules but by an impure heart. He named a number of evils (Mark 7:20–22) and said, “All these evils come from inside and make a man unclean” (7:23).

Paul then declares, in Romans 1:32, “They not only continue to do these very things but also approve of those who practice them.” Here is the extreme sin—approving rather than regretting the sins of others (NIV Study Bible note, 1985).

Let us consider the concept of retribution for a moment. This principle which operates in the moral universe is that God rewards people according to their deeds. Good deeds are rewarded in a positive way, while evil deeds are punished. In other words, it is the principle of sowing and reaping: “The one who sows to please his sinful nature, from that nature will reap destruction; the one who sows to please the Spirit, from the Spirit will reap eternal life” (Galatians 6:8).

As Paul explains, individuals rejected God, then boasted of their wisdom and “claimed to be wise.” In reality, however, “they became fools” (Romans 1:22). The word fools here does not mean “intellectual deficiency”; rather, it means “moral dullness.” This led to the “degrading of their bodies” (1:24), abandoning “natural relations” (1:26–27), and receiving “in themselves the due penalty for their perversion” (1:27). An aspect of retribution is that “God gave them over” (1:24). It is a terrible thing when God abandons a wicked person to his or her own ways and lets sin run its course. This is the worst judgment God can give to sinful people—to lift His restraining hand and allow them to do as they please.

God manifests His wrath by paying sinners what they deserve. The punishment fits the offense. The just payment for wickedness and rebellion is death. Rejecting God, the source of life, leads to death. People reap what they sow. “The wages of sin is death” (6:23). Sin in any form progressively grows stronger, spreads, and becomes worse. Whoever chooses to abandon God and truth for his or her own way launches on a downward slope that leads to destruction.

This clear picture of the downward spiral of sin and its consequences should cause every believer to share Paul’s earnest and heartfelt desire to rescue those who have refused to acknowledge God and have instead chosen the path of sin.

**A:** The Gentiles who are guilty

**A:** Students answers will vary but should include something like, Wrath as used in this passage includes the idea of a gradual storing up of wrath. The wrath of God is an expression of His righteousness. Wrath is a part of God’s activity in the present. Wrath is God’s response to sin.

### The Jews Are Guilty (Romans 2:1–29; 3:1–8)

#### 2.2.1 — Summarize the main ideas concerning (1) those whom Paul addressed in Romans 2:1–16 and (2) the principles of God’s judgment for sin in

Identity of Person or Persons Addressed (2:1–16)

In contrast to Gentiles, Jews knew God through scriptural revelation. As the guardians of the Law, they read it, memorized it, talked about it, and made it their standard for living. They wrongly assumed, however, that because of these privileges they were righteous. In Romans 2, Paul turns his attention to reasons why the Jews are guilty of sin and deserving of God’s wrath.

While it is clear that Paul addresses the Jews beginning with verse 17, the identity of the person or persons addressed in 2:1–16 is less clear. Some theologians argue that these verses describe a good person according to human standards, perhaps a person such as Seneca, a well-known stoic moralist and a contemporary of Paul. Did Paul imagine that someone like Seneca would be listening to his condemnation of the Gentiles?

The theologian F. F. Bruce indicates that this gifted writer saw himself as a moral guide—exalting moral values, denouncing hypocrisy, championing equality for all, and rejecting idolatry. Such a person might respond to Paul by saying, as Bruce suggests, “Yes, that is perfectly true of great masses of mankind, and I concur in your judgment—but there are others, of course, like myself, who deplore these tendencies as much as you” (1985, 82). Although a convincing argument can be made here for the Gentile moralist, most Bible scholars agree that Paul is primarily directing his words to a Jewish critic.

“You, therefore, have no excuse, you who pass judgment on someone else, for at whatever point you judge the other, you are condemning yourself, because you who pass judgment do the same things” (Romans 2:1). Here Paul uses a common form of argument in his day, called a diatribe . Basically, this sort of argument states an opponent’s anticipated idea or objection and then refutes it.

Paul’s point is that while high standards of conduct are good, they are inadequate, because what counts is how one lives. This principle applies whether Paul is addressing the Jewish critic or the Gentile moralist. For instance, Seneca, the moralist, tolerated in himself vices that were not unlike those he condemned in others. The Jewish critic, on the other hand, proudly identified himself with God’s chosen people, looked down on others, condemned their conduct, but did the same things. The ability to set high moral standards and discern between good and evil can lead to pride and blind people to their practice of the very things they condemn in others. Compare what Paul says here with the teaching of Jesus in Matthew 7:1–5: “Why do you look at the speck of sawdust in your brother’s eye and pay no attention to the plank in your own eye?” (7:3).

Principles of Judgment Revealed (Romans 2:5–16)

Thus far in our study of Paul’s letter to the Romans, we have considered the revelation of God’s righteousness (1:17), the revelation of God’s wrath (1:18), and a general revelation of God through creation (1:19–20). Now, beginning with Romans 2:5, Paul reveals principles of future judgment: “the day of God’s wrath, when his righteous judgment will be revealed.”

Here Paul is speaking of a future end-time judgment. Only God knows the exact time in history; but “the day of God’s wrath” is certain, and His judgment will be righteous. Note that this differs from our earlier study where Paul describes judgment in the present: “The wrath of God is being revealed” (Romans 1:18).

Paul enumerates several principles that God will apply in the future judgment. Observe that the word judge is used in two ways: it may mean “condemn” or “evaluate or distinguish.” Paul warns the reader not to condemn others; that is, do not judge in a hypocritical or self-righteous way. Rather, one is to be discerning, distinguishing between right and wrong, good and evil (see 1 Thessalonians 5:21–22; Philippians 3:2; 1 John 4:1).

Test everything. Hold on to the good.

Avoid every kind of evil.

—1 Thessalonians 5:21–22

Seven Principles of Divine Judgment in Romans 2

Seven Principles of Divine Judgment (Romans 2)

1

God’s judgment is certain. (2:3)

2

God’s judgment is universal. (2:9–10)

3

God’s judgment is based on truth. (2:2)

4

God’s judgment is based on what a person does. (2:6)

5

God’s judgment is impartial. (2:11)

6

God’s judgment is based on light given. (2:12)

7

God’s judgment is through Jesus Christ. (2:16)

1. God’s judgment is certain. Paul writes, “Do you think you will escape God’s judgment?” (Romans 2:3). An offender of human laws may escape judgment for various reasons, but God’s judgment is certain. Even those who consider themselves morally superior to others will be judged along with the wicked. Paul connects the certainty of judgment with the need for repentance. “God’s kindness leads you toward repentance” (2:4). Beware of taking God’s mercy for granted. God shows “kindness, tolerance and patience” (2:4) to allow people an opportunity to turn from sin, turn to Him, and escape punishment. He does not want anyone to perish, but is giving everyone more time to repent (2 Peter 3:9).

2. God’s judgment is universal. All will be judged: “first for the Jew, then for the Gentile” (Romans 2:9–10). Just as salvation is for everyone who believes—“first for the Jew, then for the Gentile” (1:16)—so it is concerning judgment. These two passages remind us again that greater privilege brings greater responsibility.

3. God’s judgment is based on truth. People make mistakes in judgment, but God never does. His judgment is free of error because it is “based on truth” (Romans 2:2). God knows all the truth, and He is impartial. The self-righteous pointed an accusing finger at the sins of the Gentiles, but Paul responds, “You, therefore, have no excuse . . . because you . . . do the same things” (2:1, 3).

4. God’s judgment is based on what a person does. “God will give to each person according to what he has done” (Romans 2:6). Jesus taught this same principle when He said, “The Son of Man . . . will reward each person according to what he has done” (Matthew 16:27; see other examples in Jeremiah 17:10; Matthew 7:21; 25:31–46; John 5:28–29). Paul talks about this principle in other writings as well, such as 2 Corinthians 5:10; 11:15; Galatians 6:7–9; and 2 Timothy 4:14. John writes, “The dead were judged according to what they had done,” and he quotes Jesus’ words, “Behold, I am coming soon! My reward is with me, and I will give to everyone according to what he has done” (Revelation 20:12–13; 22:12).

Let me add a word of caution that we not misinterpret Romans 2:6–10. Paul does not contradict his emphasis in Galatians and Romans on justification by faith. He has not shifted to a message of salvation by good works. Here he is describing God’s method of judgment, not His method of justification. He rewards everyone according to his or her deeds.

“To those who by persistence in doing good seek glory, honor and immortality, he will give eternal life” (Romans 2:7). This is possible only through Christ and God’s righteousness. Our deeds are important because they outwardly express our inner faith. True faith in God—what is in our heart—will manifest itself in what we do. That is, God does not simply look at our profession of faith; rather, He looks at the life we live.

5. God’s judgment is impartial. “For God does not show favoritism” (Romans 2:11). He does not favor a person because of his or her nationality, possessions, or position in life. Moses described God as “mighty and awesome, who shows no partiality and accepts no bribes” (Deuteronomy 10:17). Peter told Cornelius, “I now realize how true it is that God does not show favoritism but accepts men from every nation who fear him and do what is right” (Acts 10:34–35). Since God shows no favoritism, James exhorts believers: “Don’t show favoritism. If you show favoritism, you sin” (James 2:1, 9). In this passage, James specifically speaks against favoring the rich over the poor.

6. God’s judgment is based on light given. “All who sin apart from the law will also perish apart from the law, and all who sin under the law will be judged by the law” (Romans 2:12). God judges people according to the light they have received. The greater the revelation of truth, the greater the obligation.

The Jews, who received the Law, had greater opportunity than the Gentiles. But even in this, we see God’s justice. Paul explains, “For it is not those who hear the law who are righteous in God’s sight, but it is those who obey the law who will be declared righteous” (Romans 2:13). Increased opportunity means increased responsibility. Knowledge of the truth must be translated into practice.

Although the Gentiles did not have the Law, Paul writes, “The requirements of the law are written on their hearts, their consciences also bearing witness, and their thoughts now accusing, now even defending them” (Romans 2:15). God revealed himself through creation and through inner consciousness (1:19–20; 2:15). Here Paul says that God judges on the basis of our response to that revelation. These “requirements of the law . . . written on their hearts” are not a formal code but part of humankind’s conscious moral being. Stott comments: “It seems that Paul is envisaging a debate in which three parties are involved: our hearts (on which the requirements of the law have been written), our consciences (prodding and reproving us), and our thoughts (usually accusing us, but sometimes even excusing us)” (1994, 87).

What does conscience mean? Hayford defines it as “a person’s inner awareness of conforming to the will of God or departing from it, resulting in either a sense of approval or condemnation” (1995, 575). It is “like a law written in the human heart” (Romans 2:15, CEV). A conscience can be “guilty” (Hebrews 10:22), “corrupt” (Titus 1:15), or “seared as with a hot iron” (1 Timothy 4:1–2)—that is, having “lost all feeling” (CEV).

The blood of Jesus Christ can cleanse a guilty conscience so that it no longer accuses and condemns (Hebrews 9:14; 10:22). Paul talks about a “good” conscience (1 Timothy 1:19), and Peter observes that a “conscience toward God” enables one to endure unjust suffering (1 Peter 2:19, KJV).

You may ask, “Can those who have never heard the gospel be saved by obeying the voice of conscience? Will they go to heaven if they obey the voice of their conscience?” How many people do you suppose obey their conscience on every occasion without fail? Have you? No one has been fully able to obey his or her conscience any more than a Jew could fully obey the Law. Paul’s purpose is to convince the moralist who condemns, as well as the Jew who boasts in the Law, that all people need the Savior.

7. God’s judgment is through Jesus Christ. Paul affirms, “This will take place on the day when God will judge men’s secrets through Jesus Christ, as my gospel declares” (Romans 2:16). This is a distinctive feature of Paul’s message and a new concept for the Jew. See John 5:22, 27; Acts 17:31. Although the Jews taught that there would be a final end-time judgment, they believed that God alone would sit in judgment on the world. Thus, when Paul says, “God will judge through Jesus Christ,” he introduces a new teaching (Fitzmyer, 1993).

Advantages and Responsibilities of a Jew (2:17–29)

Paul now talks directly to the Jews who boasted about their privileges but failed to measure up to their profession. They claimed six advantages of being a Jew (Romans 2:17–18) and made five more claims that provide insight into the way they saw themselves in relation to the Gentile world (2:19–20). The following chart summarizes these claims.

SIX ADVANTAGES CLAIMED BY THE JEWS (Romans 2:17–18)

1. They are called Jews.

2. They rely on the Law.

3. They glory in God.

4. They know God’s will.

5. They have a right standard of values.

6. They are instructed in the Law.

“You call yourself a Jew” (v. 17)

“You rely on the law” (v. 17)

“You . . . brag about your relationship to God” (v. 17)

“You know his will” (v. 18)

“You . . . approve of what is superior” (v. 18)

“You are instructed by the law” (v. 18)

FIVE CLAIMS OF JEWS IN RELATION TO THE GENTILE WORLD (Romans 2:19–20)

1. A Guide to the Blind

2. A Light for Those in the Dark

3. An Instructor of Fools

4. A Teacher of Infants

5. A Possessor of Knowledge and Truth

“You are convinced that you are a guide for the blind” (2:19)

“A light for those who are in the dark” (v. 19)

“An instructor of the foolish” (v. 20)

“A teacher of infants [the young or immature]” (v. 20)

“Because you have in the law the em-bodiment of knowledge and truth” (v. 20)

The Jews took great satisfaction in their role as guardians of the Law. Possession of God’s Word gave them a sense of superiority over others. They looked down on others as blind, ignorant, and immature and saw themselves as guides, instructors, and beacon lights to the Gentiles.

What does this suggest to us Christians about our attitude? Have we received great advantages and privileges? Jesus made this very point: “From everyone who has been given much, much will be demanded; and from the one who has been entrusted with much, much more will be asked” (Luke 12:48). To whom much is given, much is required! The greater our knowledge of God and His will, the greater our accountability will be to Him. The Jews were chosen to tell the nations about the true God—to be His witnesses—but they came short of their responsibility and failed to bring praise to God. They did not live according to their claims.

Are Paul’s comments limited to the Jews of his day, or is there a wider application? Where do we put our trust? In our years of service? In what we have been given? In the important Christian leaders we know? In our accomplishments? Or is our assurance based on our trust in Jesus Christ?

Is religion the opiate of the people, as some claim? It may act like a drug for those who trust in religious knowledge and activity, giving them a false sense of security, but this is certainly not true of those who have a vital experience with the living God.

Read Romans 2:25–29. In verse 25, Paul declares, “Circumcision has value if you observe the law, but if you break the law, you have become as though you had not been circumcised.” The Jew who failed to keep the Law was no better off than the Gentile. The essential thing is a heart that is right with God. Jeremiah 9:25–26 makes a similar point. The nations that surrounded Judah practiced circumcision. The prophet clearly identified the problem when he said, “Your bodies are circumcised, but your hearts are unchanged” (Jeremiah 9:26, CEV). As Paul explains, the Holy Spirit alone can fix the problem: “True circumcision is not a cutting of the body but a change of heart produced by God’s Spirit” (Romans 2:29, NLT).

Some theologians describe water baptism as the New Testament equivalent to circumcision. In other words, today some people trust in water baptism in the same way that Jews trust in circumcision. Yet these rites were intended to serve as outward signs of an inward commitment to God. Neither has meaning without the inward commitment it symbolizes.

Three Questions (3:1–8)

Anticipating the questions his Jewish audience will raise about his teaching, Paul acknowledges and answers them. Later, in Romans 9–11, he discusses them more fully. For now, let us consider what he asks in Romans 3:1, 3, and 5.

Question 1

“What advantage, then, is there in being a Jew, or what value is there in circumcision?” (Romans 3:1). If Jews are as guilty as Gentiles, it is logical to ask this question. Paul gives a brief answer that he develops more fully in 9:4–5. Here he simply says, “First of all, they have been entrusted with the very words of God” (3:2). What a tremendous advantage this is! Being entrusted with divine revelation is indeed a great honor.

Question 2

“What if some did not have faith? Will their lack of faith nullify God’s faithfulness?” (Romans 3:3). Does Israel’s unbelief undermine God’s character? Obviously, He punishes evil deeds (3:5). But does this mean that His promises and plans have failed? Has He canceled His covenant? Are the Jews disinherited? Paul answers, “Of course not! Though everyone else in the world is a liar, God is true” (3:4, NLT). The fact that some people break their promises does not mean God will break His. Paul wrote to Timothy on this subject, saying, “If we are faithless, he [God] will remain faithful, for he cannot disown himself” (2 Timothy 2:13).

Question 3

“But if our unrighteousness brings out God’s righteousness more clearly, what shall we say? That God is unjust in bringing his wrath on us?” (Romans 3:5). We might ask, is this argument valid? If my evil deeds display God’s goodness, is it wrong for Him to punish me? After all, I may argue, “He benefits from my blunder.” Paul’s answer to this human logic is, “Certainly not!” Otherwise, “how could God judge the world?” (3:6). God delights in exercising “kindness, justice and righteousness on earth” (Jeremiah 9:24). God, of course, will judge everyone fairly, including Paul’s critics who falsely accused him of saying, “Let us do evil that good may result” (Romans 3:8).

**Q:** Whom does Paul address in Romans 2:1–16?

**A:** Valid arguments can be made that Paul addressed either a Gentile moralist or a Jewish critic in this passage, but most scholars favor the view that Jews are essentially in focus here.

**Q:** State in one sentence the main idea of Romans 2:1–16.

**A:** Your answer should be similar to this: If you judge others for their sins but are guilty of the same sins, God, who is tolerant and patient, will reveal His righteous judgment against you in the day of His wrath.

**Q:** How would you answer a business executive in your church who says that doing business requires some casual lying and deception and that God even seems to bless this dishonest activity?

**A:** Student’s own answer but could include something like, God’s kindness leads towards repentance, and one can mistake God’s patience for His approval of one’s conduct.

**Q:** Describe the relationship between salvation and judgment as presented in Romans 1 and 2.

**A:** Salvation is through Jesus Christ, and God’s judgment will also be through Jesus Christ.

**Q:** In this section we have reviewed seven main ideas concerning God’s judgment and have seen that all people will be judged. What will be the basis of this judgment?

**A:** Judgment will be based on truth (the gospel), the light (revelation of truth) a person has received, and what a person does.

**Q:** Summarize Paul’s teaching in Romans 2:17–29.

**A:** Student’s answers will vary but should include, more is required of those who have been entrusted with great privileges; superior knowledge leads to pride; circumcision and other such rites have value only as a symbol of one’s inward commitment to God; greater privilege brings greater accountability.

**Q:** Based on Paul’s three questions and responses to the Jews, state three truths that we can derive from this passage and apply in our own Christian lives.

**A:** In any order: Christians, like Jews, have been entrusted with the Word of God. Our unbelief does not do away with God’s faithfulness. God is fair in all of His judgments.
