(Philippians 3:8–9)

Keep in mind, then, that Paul talks about two kinds of righteousness:

1. “A righteousness of my own”—that is, a legalistic righteousness based on self-effort at observing the Law in order to merit God’s approval (Philippians 3:6)

2. “The righteousness that comes from God and is by faith” (Philippians 3:9)

God’s Righteousness Revealed

GOD

MAN

His wrath is revealed against all godlessness & wickedness

(Romans 1:18)

an expression of His righteousness and love

(Romans 1:18–3:20)

BUT NOW a righteousness from God has been made

known

(Romans 3:21)

receives God’s righteousness through faith in Jesus Christ

(Romans 3:22)

his faith is credited as righteousness

(Romans 4:5; 3:22–8:39)

Romans 3:21–22 introduces the second main theological section of Romans and restates the theme. In the next chart we show a comparison of Paul’s statement in 1:16–17 and his restatement in 3:21–22. You would do well to memorize both of these passages. Note the five key elements of the theme:

The Theme of Romans (1:16–17)

The Theme Restated (3:21–22)

A righteousness from God is revealed (1:17)

For everyone who believes (1:16)

First for the Jew, then for the Gentile (1:16)

By faith from first to last (1:17) The righteous will live by faith (1:17)

A righteousness from God has been made known (3:21)

To all who believe (3:22)

There is no difference (3:22) Through faith in Jesus Christ to all who believe (3:22)

This righteousness from God comes through faith (3:22)

A righteousness from God (Romans 3:21). Righteousness describes God’s character as well as His initiative in providing a Savior. Here, it refers to God’s method of bringing people into a right relationship with himself. Righteousness simply means “rightness” (Laurin 1988, 120). A righteous person is right before God. Do you recall Paul’s opening remarks in this letter? The gospel reveals this “righteousness from God” (1:17). In a word, this righteousness originates in Jesus Christ; He is the source. When we receive Him into our lives, we receive His righteousness (1 Corinthians 1:30). That is how God makes us acceptable in His sight.

Paul clarifies his meaning about this righteousness from God by saying (1) it is “apart from law” (Romans 3:21); (2) “the Law and the Prophets testify” about it (3:21); and (3) “it comes through faith in Jesus Christ” (3:22).

Apart from law (Romans 3:21). These three words encourage Gentiles but puzzle Jews. “Apart from law? How can this be?” they reason. “After all, God gave us the Law.” Since Jewish life revolved around the Law, the Jews resisted this idea—striving instead to merit God’s approval by Law observance. Before his conversion, Paul followed this same pattern.

“Apart from law” in this passage simply means that we cannot obtain this righteousness by observing the Law. It is a gift. If we could merit it, it would cease to be a gift. Does this mean that Paul rejects the Law? Absolutely not. The law is “holy, righteous and good” (7:12), and it supports Paul’s teaching, as we will see in a moment. It reveals sin (3:20), shows us our need of a Savior, and points us to Christ (Galatians 3:24). Does this mean that it was an instrument of salvation? No; God never intended it to be. As we saw earlier, a person “is not justified by observing the law, but by faith in Jesus Christ” (Galatians 2:16).

The Law and the Prophets testify (Romans 3:21) about this righteousness from God. They record the good news that God promised long ago (1:2) and substantiate Paul’s teaching. The gospel is neither an afterthought nor a new doctrine. For example, Moses recounts how the disobedience of Adam and Eve opened the door for sin to enter the human race. This story includes God’s judgment on Satan and the first promise of a Savior: “I will put enmity between you and the woman, and between your offspring and hers; he will crush your head, and you will strike his heel” (Genesis 3:15). Moses also records God’s promised blessings to Abraham that extend to all peoples (12:1–3) and come through his offspring (seed), who is Christ (22:18; Galatians 3:8, 16). Furthermore, the prophet Habakkuk affirmed, “The righteous will live by his faith” (Habakkuk 2:4). Thus the Law and the Prophets bear witness to this righteousness we receive through Christ.

Through faith (Romans 3:22). Paul places great emphasis on faith—a faith centered on Jesus Christ, who shed His blood for our redemption. “This righteousness from God comes through faith” (3:22) — suggesting a gift, not a reward for achievement. Good works do not unite us with Christ; rather, they grow out of that union. The order is first grace, then faith in Christ, then good works (Ephesians 2:8–10). The gospel does not reject good works; it identifies them as an outgrowth and evidence of our salvation—not the means of obtaining it.

A helpful technique in Bible study is to note the repetition of key words, since they provide a clue to the writer’s purpose and emphasis in a given passage. Faith is such a word. Underline faith each time you find it in Romans 3:21–31. How often does it occur? Observe how Paul stresses faith in this passage:

Verse 22:

“This righteousness from God comes through faith in Jesus Christ to all who believe.”

Verse 25:

“Through faith in his blood”

Verse 26:

God justifies those who have faith in Jesus.

Verse 27:

Faith excludes boasting.

Verse 28:

“A man is justified by faith apart from observing the law.”

Verse 30:

“God . . . will justify the circumcised [Jew] by faith and the uncircumcised [Gentile] through that same faith.”

Verse 31:

We do not “nullify the law by this faith . . . . Rather, we uphold the law.”

How many times did you underline faith ? In the NIV, it appears eight times. What is faith? Charles Spurgeon, the great English preacher, wrote:

Faith is not a blind thing; for faith begins with knowledge. It is not a speculative thing, for faith believes facts of which it is sure. It is not an unpractical, dreamy thing; for faith trusts, and stakes its destiny upon the truth of revelation. . . . Faith . . . is the eye which looks. . . . Faith is the hand which grasps. . . . Faith is the mouth which feeds upon Christ. (Boice 1991, 1:108)

“This righteousness from God” (Romans 3:22) is within the grasp of all who reach out in faith to Jesus Christ. It is available to Jew and Gentile alike since “there is no difference.” Here is hope for the thief and the tax collector, the prostitute and the priest, the greater sinner and the lesser sinner.

“There is no difference, for all have sinned and fall short of the glory of God” (Romans 3:22–23). To sin (Greek, hamartano ) literally means, “to miss the mark.” This brief summary reminds us of our need to be made right in God’s sight. Paul is not saying that ethnic, cultural, or religious differences no longer exist; they do. Yet sin makes us alike in God’s sight and admonishes us of our need for a Savior. “The glory of God” in 3:23 refers to what God intended humans to be when He created them. Sin broke their relationship with God, and they fell short of this glory. The gospel shows how this relationship can be restored through faith in Jesus Christ.

The Greek verb tenses in verses 22 and 23 suggest an interesting parallel. A specific act in the past has a continuing result in the present. That is, “all have sinned [definite past act] and fall short [are falling short—a continuing present result] of the glory of God” (Romans 3:23). Likewise, the single act of faith (3:22) brings righteousness followed by a continuing response of belief in God (see also 1:17). Repentance and faith in Christ interrupt the continuing consequence of sin.

Parallel in Romans 3:22–23

CRISIS ACT

CONTINUING RESULT

Righteousness by faith

Believing

Sin

Falling short of the glory of God

Salvation Defined (3:24–26)

Salvation is a broad term that includes not only deliverance from sin but also abundant life in Jesus Christ. A variety of words describe the many facets of our salvation in Christ. Conversion , for example, depicts our response to God’s offer of salvation and consists of turning away from sin in repentance and turning toward Jesus Christ in faith. While conversion is our response to God, regeneration is God’s response to us. When we repent and believe, God imparts life. We are “born again”—as Jesus explained to Nicodemus (John 3:1–21). Paul depicts this as a death-to-life experience: “God, who is rich in mercy, made us alive with Christ even when we were dead in transgressions” (Ephesians 2:4–5). After we are united with Christ, God continues to work in us, molding us into Christ’s image. This process of spiritual growth is called sanctification.

In Romans 3:24–26, three words stand out: redemption, propitiation , and justification . Each one portrays a different side of salvation, helping us to grasp what God has done for us through the death of His Son on the Cross. These three words form what James Boice calls “the salvation triangle” (1991, 1:380; see chart following). Although not alike, they are related, tied together, inseparable. Together, they help us to understand how a righteous God can justify the wicked and still remain just. We will consider the significance of each of these three words now.

The Salvation Triangle

(Adapted from Boice 1991, 382)

REDEMPTION

Jesus Christ redeemed us. He ransomed [purchased] us at the price of His own blood. It is what He did for us, not what we do.

PROPITIATION

This describes what Jesus Christ did for us in relation to the Father. He turns aside God’s wrath against sin through His sacrifice of atonement (3:25). By taking our punishment on himself, He satisfies God’s justice.

JUSTIFICATION

This describes the act of a judge in a court of law who acquits an accused person. On the basis of Christ’s redemptive and propitiatory work, God justifies those who put their trust in Him. Jesus paid the price for our salvation by dying in our place. In this way, God is just in justifying the ungodly.

FAITH

“This righteousness from God comes through faith in Jesus Christ to all who believe” (3:22). “He . . . justifies those who have faith in Jesus” (3:26).

Redemption, propitiation, and justification are inextricably bound together. Any one is impossible without the others. God’s condition for receiving salvation is faith in Jesus Christ.

Redemption

In Romans 3:24, Paul writes that all “are justified freely by his grace through the redemption that came by Christ Jesus.” Redemption (Greek, apolytrosis ) is the language of the marketplace—the language of buying and selling. In Paul’s day, people bought and sold every conceivable kind of item in the open market, including slaves.

The Old Testament speaks of redeeming people, property, animals, and even a nation. Redemption can mean “to rescue, deliver, ransom, or recover what was lost” (Numbers 3:49; Psalm 111:9; 130:7). The law of Moses provided for a near relative—a kinsman-redeemer—to aid a family member in distress. For instance, if a family member was forced to sell his property under dire circumstances, the kinsman-redeemer could buy it back. Or, he could redeem a relative captured or sold into slavery. For examples, see Leviticus 25:25–28, 47–49; Ruth 4:6; Jeremiah 32:6–16. These references help us to grasp what Jesus Christ did in redeeming us. He ransomed us from the slave market of sin by paying for our freedom with His own blood; that is, He gave His life as a ransom (Matthew 20:28). By His death He delivered us from the bondage of sin. Peter says, “It was not with perishable things such as silver or gold that you were redeemed . . . but with the precious blood of Christ, a lamb without blemish or defect” (1 Peter 1:18–19).

The deliverance of the Israelites from Egyptian bondage pictures redemption and includes release from slavery and entry into a new life of freedom. The Lord “brought you out” and He “will bring you to” the Promised Land (Exodus 6:6–8). The Passover lamb (Exodus 12) is part of that picture. The Israelites applied the blood of the lamb to the door frames of their houses, as Moses directed, and escaped judgment, since the Lord “passed over” those who were “under the blood.”

Isaiah describes God’s lamb as a man: “He was led like a lamb to the slaughter, and . . . he did not open his mouth” (Isaiah 53:7, italics added). John the Baptist identified this man as Jesus: “Look, the Lamb of God, who takes away the sin of the world!” (John 1:29, 36). Paul is also precise in comparing the Passover lamb to Christ: “For Christ, our Passover lamb, has been sacrificed” (1 Corinthians 5:7). Through Christ’s sacrifice, those who put their faith in Him escape judgment and receive eternal life.

Propitiation

“God presented him as a sacrifice of atonement, through faith in his blood” (Romans 3:25). Paul borrows from the language of the Jewish sacrificial system to explain the believer’s salvation. The essential idea here is that God’s holiness requires punishment for sin. At the same time, God in love intercedes on our behalf by presenting His Son as a propitiation or “atoning sacrifice for our sins” (1 John 2:2; 4:10). His sacrifice provides the grounds for our reconciliation with God. Propitiation is related to the Greek word hileos , meaning “merciful.”

“Sacrifice of atonement” (Greek, hilasterion ) in the NIV translation of Romans 3:25 is translated as propitiation in the KJV and NASB. Christ’s sacrifice is depicted as appeasing the wrath of God. The RSV and NEB translate it expiation , meaning “to provide a covering for sin.” The NIV text note gives this alternative translation of 3:25: God presented him, that is, Jesus Christ, “ as the one who would turn aside his wrath , taking away sin” (emphasis added; see also Hebrews 2:17).

Paul explains that God reveals His wrath against sin (Romans 1:18) and, at the same time, initiates a way to avert it. “God presented him [Jesus Christ] as a sacrifice of atonement, through faith in his blood” (3:25). In other words, Jesus Christ—not people—turns aside God’s wrath through His sacrificial death. Christ took our punishment on himself to satisfy God’s justice. Here is the idea of substitution—the innocent dying in the place of the guilty. Christ, who was without sin, died for us (2 Corinthians 5:21). “Thus God himself gave himself to save us from himself” (Stott 1994, 115).

So Paul places God’s love beside His wrath. The apostle John does the same thing in one of the best-known chapters in the Bible. Read what he says about God’s wrath and His love in John 3:16 and 36.

The “sacrifice of atonement” in Romans 3:25 is a reminder of the Old Testament sacrificial system and, more specifically, the atonement cover (mercy seat) of the Ark of the Covenant. The word translated as sacrifice of atonement here ( hilasterion ) is translated as atonement cover in Hebrews 9:5.

The ministry of the high priest on the Day of Atonement is instructive (Leviticus 16). Once each year, he entered the innermost room of the tabernacle, called the Most Holy Place. It housed one piece of furniture—the Ark of the Covenant. This gold-covered, portable wooden chest held the stone tablets, that is, the Ten Commandments. The atonement cover, or mercy seat, was a slab of pure gold. Formed together with it in one piece were two angelic-like figures with outstretched wings facing each other. God said that His presence would dwell between these cherubim. On the Day of Atonement, the most important day of the Jewish calendar, the high priest went behind the curtain with sacrificial blood and sprinkled it on the atonement cover. The fundamental idea symbolized here is this: From between the cherubim, God looks down but does not see the Law the people had broken. Instead, He sees the blood of the sacrifice and is satisfied that punishment has been measured out. Thus His wrath is turned aside, and fellowship is restored.

Because these annual sacrifices could never fully deal with sin, they were constantly repeated: “an annual reminder of sins, because it is impossible for the blood of bulls and goats to take away sins” (Hebrews 10:3–4). The sacrifices foreshadowed the coming “Lamb of God, who takes away the sin of the world” (John 1:29). As our great high priest, Jesus Christ offered His own blood as a sacrifice for sinners “once for all” (Hebrews 7:27; 9:11–14, 26; 10:10–12). His blood does more than “cover over” sin; it removes sin permanently.

We therefore see that Christ’s suffering on the Cross redeems sinners and turns aside God’s wrath. It also vindicates God’s justice: “He did this to demonstrate his justice” (Romans 3:25). God publicly displayed His justice through the Cross. But what about His forbearance in leaving “sins committed beforehand unpunished” (3:25)? Does this mean that He condoned evil? Actually, it is God’s extraordinary kindness, tolerance, and patience that lead to repentance (2:4; Acts 14:16–17; 17:30; 2 Peter 3:9). The Cross concerns not only the past but also the present. “He did it to demonstrate his justice at the present time” (Romans 3:26). God dealt adequately with sin at Calvary “so as to be just and the one who justifies [those who have] faith in Jesus” (3:26).

Justification

Justification is a legal term used in a court of law. Paul uses it to explain how God makes sinful people acceptable to Him. In the original language, it is closely related to righteousness . “The Greek word for ‘justification’ ( dikaiosyne ) is built on the word for ‘right’ or ‘righteousness’ ( dikaios )” (Boice 1991, 1:381).

“Justified freely by his grace” (Romans 3:24) describes what God does for all who believe in Jesus Christ as Savior (3:22). The word justify means “to pronounce, accept, and treat as just, i.e., as on the one hand, not penally liable, and on the other, entitled to all the privileges due to those who have kept the law” (Harrison 1987, 304).

The word justify , then, has both a positive and a negative meaning. In a negative sense, God declares the believer in Christ “not guilty.” In other words, he or she is no longer liable to punishment. The person’s sins are forgiven. Paul and Barnabas preached this to the Galatians in Pisidian Antioch: “I want you to know that through Jesus the forgiveness of sins is proclaimed to you. Through him everyone who believes is justified from everything you could not be justified from by the law of Moses” (Acts 13:38–39). In a positive sense, justify means that God declares the believer righteous : “His faith is credited as righteousness” (Romans 4:5–6).

Those who believe are “justified freely by his grace” (Romans 3:24). Everyone is guilty of sin and deserves judgment. Nevertheless, God graciously extends His favor and mercy to all through His Son. Paul says that God does this in several ways. He justifies us by grace : “For it is by grace you have been saved” (Ephesians 2:8). He justifies us freely . “And this not from yourselves, it is the gift of God—not by works, so that no one can boast” (2:8–9). He justifies us through faith (Romans 3:22)—a faith centered on Jesus Christ. He justifies us “through the redemption that came by Christ Jesus” (3:24). “He has rescued us from the dominion of darkness and brought us into the kingdom of the Son he loves, in whom we have redemption, the forgiveness of sins” (Colossians 1:13–14).

God pronounces a person righteous the moment he or she believes in Christ as Savior. While some teach that individuals can never know for certain if they are in a right relationship with God until after death, Paul makes it clear that believers can have the full assurance of being right before God now, in this present life.

Reflect again on the salvation triangle. God justifies sinners based on the twofold work of Christ. First of all, Christ rescues us from sin by redeeming us. (This is represented by the bottom line of the triangle.) Secondly, He turns aside God’s wrath through His sacrificial death (represented by the left side of the triangle). Therefore, on the basis of Christ’s redemptive and propitiatory work, God is just in justifying the wicked (represented by the right side of the triangle).

Three Questions Answered (3:27–31)

Paul now concludes his presentation up to this point. His conclusion consists of asking and answering three questions that might arise relative to what he has said about justification by faith.

1. Where, then, is boasting? (Romans 3:27)

2. Is God the God of Jews only? Is he not the God of Gentiles too? (3:29)

3. Do we, then, nullify the law by this faith? (3:31)

Where, Then, Is Boasting? (Romans 3:27)

To boast is to express pride in oneself or in one’s accomplishments. While this tendency obviously exists among all people, Jews in particular were guilty of it. As God’s chosen people, they boasted about their relationship to God and their possession of the Law (see 2:17–24). Furthermore, their zeal in Law observance led to pride.

Do you recall the parable about the Pharisee and the tax collector (Luke 18:9–14)? Jesus told this story for those who considered themselves better than others and who scorned everyone else. The self-confident Pharisee boasted in his own goodness—proudly pointing out that he did not cheat or commit adultery like other sinners. He paid his tithes; in fact, he even went beyond the requirements of the Law by fasting twice a week. Was he exaggerating? Probably not. Unquestionably, he was superior to the tax collector—much to his own satisfaction. But no one gains God’s favor on the basis of superiority to others.

The tax collector in this story is helpful too. His prayer—“God, have mercy on me, a sinner” (Luke 18:13)—demonstrates how we should approach God. He admitted his sin and put his trust in God’s goodness and mercy. Because of this, he—rather than the Pharisee—went home justified before God. “For everyone who exalts himself will be humbled, and he who humbles himself will be exalted” (18:14).

Righteousness “comes through faith in Jesus Christ” (Romans 3:22). Since it is by grace through faith, boasting is “excluded” (3:27). No one can earn righteousness or take credit for it; rather, “it is the gift of God—not by works, so that no one can boast” (Ephesians 2:8–9). Law observance gives rise to boasting because it is rooted in human effort. Therefore, Paul wrote, “We maintain that a man is justified by faith apart from observing the law” (Romans 3:28). Paul does not boast in himself or in his accomplishments. Like Jeremiah, he says, “Let him who boasts boast in the Lord” (1 Corinthians 1:31; Jeremiah 9:24).

Is God the God of Jews Only? Is He Not the God of Gentiles Too? (Romans 3:29)

God’s choice of Abraham never meant the exclusion of others. From the beginning, God intended to make him an instrument of blessing to all nations (Genesis 12:1–3). Paul concludes that “there is only one God” (Romans 3:30) who justifies Jews and Gentiles in the same way—through faith in Jesus Christ.

Do We, Then, Nullify the Law by This Faith? (Romans 3:31)

This, Paul suggests, is the question asked by the Jew who listens intently to him. Paul’s response is, “Not at all! Rather, we uphold the law” (3:31). “But how is this possible?” the Jew asks. “Law and faith seem to oppose each other.” Consider this: The person who confesses, “I have sinned,” essentially acknowledges that the Law is right. Through confession, he or she upholds the Law.

Here is another sense in which faith upholds the Law. A Christian who lives “according to the Spirit” fulfills “the righteous requirements of the law” (Romans 8:4; see also 13:8). Reflect on these words of Jesus in His Sermon on the Mount: “Do not think that I have come to abolish the Law or the Prophets; I have not come to abolish them but to fulfill them” (Matthew 5:17). Jeremiah, speaking God’s words, prophesied, “I [the Lord] will put my law in their minds and write it on their hearts” (Jeremiah 31:33). In addition, Paul writes, “It is God who works in you to will and to act according to his good purpose” (Philippians 2:13). The Spirit empowers the believer to fulfill the intent of the Law. Thus, justification by faith does not nullify the Law.

**Q:** What do the words “But now” in Romans 3:21 point to?

**A:** A logical turning point in Paul’s argument and a historical crossroads on God’s timetable.

**Q:** What do Paul’s words,“a righteousness from God, apart from law,” mean?

**A:** This righteousness is not obtainable by observing the Law. With the coming of Christ, God reveals what He had determined even before Creation. Faith in Jesus Christ is the basis for receiving “a righteousness from God, apart from law.”

**Q:** By now you may have memorized Romans 1:16–17 and 3:21–22. From memory, try to list the five key elements of the theme of Romans emphasized in these two passages.

**A:** The gospel reveals a righteousness from God. It is for all who believe. It is to the Jew first and then to the Gentile. It is through faith in Jesus Christ. The righteous live by faith.

**Q:** List as many words or phrases as you can that are related in meaning to the word redemption .

**A:** Student’s answers will vary but could include recover , pay a ransom , and rescue .

**Q:** What is meant by “sacrifice of atonement”?

**A:** Satisfying God’s judgment, propitiation, turning aside wrath, and atonement cover

**Q:** When does God pronounce a person righteous?

**A:** When the person accepts Christ as Savior.

**Q:** How would you define justification by faith?

**A:** Justification is based on Christ’s redemptive work, which is Christ’s sacrificial death to turn away God’s wrath. It becomes a reality in our life when we put our trust in Christ and means that God declares the believer in Christ “not guilty” and “righteous.”

**Q:** Explain why this statement is incorrect: Those who sincerely pursue righteous activities with right motives have earned the right to boast of their accomplishments.

**A:** Pursuing righteous activities with right motives simply means that, in gratitude for the salvation God freely gives to all who come to Him through Jesus Christ, we respond in loving, sacrificial service to Him. Our purpose is to glorify Him, not ourselves.

**Q:** Consider the three passages in the preceding paragraph—Matthew 5:17, Jeremiah 31:33, and Philippians 2:13—in relation to justification by faith. Briefly explain your understanding of the relationship of Christian faith to the Old Testament law.

**A:** Your answer should include these ideas: Those who respond to the gospel in faith, by confessing their sins and turning to Christ, sustain the Law. Believers who live by the Spirit (allowing the Spirit of Christ to lead and guide them), exhibit the intent of the Law and in this way uphold it.

#### 3.1.3 — Using given Scripture passages, explain Paul’s response to three questions his readers might ask.

In Romans 1:18–3:20, which we studied in Chapter 2, Paul established people’s need of a Savior. The need is broad because everyone—from Gentile idol worshipper to enlightened Jew—is under condemnation. The need is desperate—a matter of life or death. “The soul who sins . . . will die” (Ezekiel 18:4). The need is compelling and demands our attention. Unless we recognize our guilt, we will not value the gospel or turn to the Savior.

The gospel alone satisfies our need for cleansing, deliverance from sin, and a new nature. It takes us beyond religious rules to an all-sufficient Savior.

“Do this and live,” the Law commands

But gives me neither feet nor hands.

A better word the gospel brings.

It bids me fly and gives me wings.

—(Wuest 1955, 113)

Paul gives us the basic principles of Christ’s redemptive work in Romans 3:21–31, compressing the very heart of his teaching into this short passage. In essence, his message is that God makes us acceptable in His sight through our faith in the Savior.

Then, to illustrate his teaching, Paul moves from principles to persons —inviting Abraham and David to step out of the Law and the Prophets to confirm his message (Romans 4:1–25). Next, Paul recites the benefits of justification (5:1–11) and contrasts Adam and Christ (5:12–21), who sum up his message to this point. Adam represents the condemnation of all people (1:18–3:20), while Christ represents the believer’s justification (3:21–5:11).

Righteousness Revealed

But now a righteousness from God, apart from law, has been made known, to which the Law and the Prophets testify. This righteousness from God comes through faith in Jesus Christ to all who believe. There is no difference, for all have sinned and fall short of the glory of God, and are justified freely by his grace through the redemption that came by Christ Jesus. God presented him as a sacrifice of atonement, through faith in his blood. He did this to demonstrate his justice, . . . so as to be just and the one who justifies the man who has faith in Jesus. (Romans 3:21–26)

But now (Romans 3:21) introduces a major turning point and a restatement of Paul’s main theme in Romans. Here Paul turns our attention from people’s sinful state (1:18–3:20) to God’s provision of a Savior. He does this by shifting from the subject of wrath (1:18) to the subject of righteousness (3:21)—that is, from judgment to justification . Let us now consider Paul’s development of this theme of righteousness.

Before , Paul says, we were under “the wrath of God” (Romans 1:18). But now we have access to “a righteousness from God” (3:21). Before , we were under condemnation. But now there is “no condemnation for those who are in Christ Jesus” (8:1). Before , sin enslaved us. But now “you have been set free from sin” (6:22). Before , Law bound us. But now , “by dying to what once bound us, we have been released from the law so that we serve in the new way of the Spirit” (7:6). Before , Gentiles were separate from Christ, without hope and without God. But now “in Christ Jesus you who once were far away have been brought near through the blood of Christ” (Ephesians 2:13; see also vv. 11–12).

The repeated expression “but now” signals not only a logical transition in Paul’s argument but also a historical moment on God’s redemptive timetable: “when the time had fully come, God sent his Son” (Galatians 4:4).

The gospel Paul preached was new , in a historical sense; that is, it began with the coming of Christ. In another sense, it was old , inasmuch as it was part of God’s plan from the beginning. “This grace was given us in Christ Jesus before the beginning of time , but it has now been revealed through the appearing of our Savior” (2 Timothy 1:9–10, italics added). Peter says essentially the same thing: Christ “was chosen before the creation of the world , but was revealed in these last times for your sake” (1 Peter 1:20, italics added).

Paul’s encounter with the resurrected Christ near Damascus illustrates this turning point (Acts 9:1–19). Before this, he was blind to God’s provision, groping about, striving to establish his own righteousness. Then Christ opened his eyes. Like the blind man that Jesus healed, Paul could testify, “I was blind but now I see!” (John 9:25, italics added). This encounter totally changed his life and his outlook. He wrote these words to the Philippian church:

I consider everything a loss compared to the surpassing greatness of knowing Christ Jesus my Lord. . . . I consider them rubbish, that I may gain Christ and be found in him, not having a righteousness of my own that comes from the law, but that which is through faith in Christ—the righteousness that comes from God and is by faith. (Philippians 3:8–9)

Keep in mind, then, that Paul talks about two kinds of righteousness:

1. “A righteousness of my own”—that is, a legalistic righteousness based on self-effort at observing the Law in order to merit God’s approval (Philippians 3:6)

2. “The righteousness that comes from God and is by faith” (Philippians 3:9)

God’s Righteousness Revealed

GOD

MAN

His wrath is revealed against all godlessness & wickedness

(Romans 1:18)

an expression of His righteousness and love

(Romans 1:18–3:20)

BUT NOW a righteousness from God has been made

known

(Romans 3:21)

receives God’s righteousness through faith in Jesus Christ

(Romans 3:22)

his faith is credited as righteousness

(Romans 4:5; 3:22–8:39)

Romans 3:21–22 introduces the second main theological section of Romans and restates the theme. In the next chart we show a comparison of Paul’s statement in 1:16–17 and his restatement in 3:21–22. You would do well to memorize both of these passages. Note the five key elements of the theme:

The Theme of Romans (1:16–17)

The Theme Restated (3:21–22)

A righteousness from God is revealed (1:17)

For everyone who believes (1:16)

First for the Jew, then for the Gentile (1:16)

By faith from first to last (1:17) The righteous will live by faith (1:17)

A righteousness from God has been made known (3:21)

To all who believe (3:22)

There is no difference (3:22) Through faith in Jesus Christ to all who believe (3:22)

This righteousness from God comes through faith (3:22)

A righteousness from God (Romans 3:21). Righteousness describes God’s character as well as His initiative in providing a Savior. Here, it refers to God’s method of bringing people into a right relationship with himself. Righteousness simply means “rightness” (Laurin 1988, 120). A righteous person is right before God. Do you recall Paul’s opening remarks in this letter? The gospel reveals this “righteousness from God” (1:17). In a word, this righteousness originates in Jesus Christ; He is the source. When we receive Him into our lives, we receive His righteousness (1 Corinthians 1:30). That is how God makes us acceptable in His sight.

Paul clarifies his meaning about this righteousness from God by saying (1) it is “apart from law” (Romans 3:21); (2) “the Law and the Prophets testify” about it (3:21); and (3) “it comes through faith in Jesus Christ” (3:22).

Apart from law (Romans 3:21). These three words encourage Gentiles but puzzle Jews. “Apart from law? How can this be?” they reason. “After all, God gave us the Law.” Since Jewish life revolved around the Law, the Jews resisted this idea—striving instead to merit God’s approval by Law observance. Before his conversion, Paul followed this same pattern.

“Apart from law” in this passage simply means that we cannot obtain this righteousness by observing the Law. It is a gift. If we could merit it, it would cease to be a gift. Does this mean that Paul rejects the Law? Absolutely not. The law is “holy, righteous and good” (7:12), and it supports Paul’s teaching, as we will see in a moment. It reveals sin (3:20), shows us our need of a Savior, and points us to Christ (Galatians 3:24). Does this mean that it was an instrument of salvation? No; God never intended it to be. As we saw earlier, a person “is not justified by observing the law, but by faith in Jesus Christ” (Galatians 2:16).

The Law and the Prophets testify (Romans 3:21) about this righteousness from God. They record the good news that God promised long ago (1:2) and substantiate Paul’s teaching. The gospel is neither an afterthought nor a new doctrine. For example, Moses recounts how the disobedience of Adam and Eve opened the door for sin to enter the human race. This story includes God’s judgment on Satan and the first promise of a Savior: “I will put enmity between you and the woman, and between your offspring and hers; he will crush your head, and you will strike his heel” (Genesis 3:15). Moses also records God’s promised blessings to Abraham that extend to all peoples (12:1–3) and come through his offspring (seed), who is Christ (22:18; Galatians 3:8, 16). Furthermore, the prophet Habakkuk affirmed, “The righteous will live by his faith” (Habakkuk 2:4). Thus the Law and the Prophets bear witness to this righteousness we receive through Christ.

Through faith (Romans 3:22). Paul places great emphasis on faith—a faith centered on Jesus Christ, who shed His blood for our redemption. “This righteousness from God comes through faith” (3:22) — suggesting a gift, not a reward for achievement. Good works do not unite us with Christ; rather, they grow out of that union. The order is first grace, then faith in Christ, then good works (Ephesians 2:8–10). The gospel does not reject good works; it identifies them as an outgrowth and evidence of our salvation—not the means of obtaining it.

A helpful technique in Bible study is to note the repetition of key words, since they provide a clue to the writer’s purpose and emphasis in a given passage. Faith is such a word. Underline faith each time you find it in Romans 3:21–31. How often does it occur? Observe how Paul stresses faith in this passage:

Verse 22:

“This righteousness from God comes through faith in Jesus Christ to all who believe.”

Verse 25:

“Through faith in his blood”

Verse 26:

God justifies those who have faith in Jesus.

Verse 27:

Faith excludes boasting.

Verse 28:

“A man is justified by faith apart from observing the law.”

Verse 30:

“God . . . will justify the circumcised [Jew] by faith and the uncircumcised [Gentile] through that same faith.”

Verse 31:

We do not “nullify the law by this faith . . . . Rather, we uphold the law.”

How many times did you underline faith ? In the NIV, it appears eight times. What is faith? Charles Spurgeon, the great English preacher, wrote:

Faith is not a blind thing; for faith begins with knowledge. It is not a speculative thing, for faith believes facts of which it is sure. It is not an unpractical, dreamy thing; for faith trusts, and stakes its destiny upon the truth of revelation. . . . Faith . . . is the eye which looks. . . . Faith is the hand which grasps. . . . Faith is the mouth which feeds upon Christ. (Boice 1991, 1:108)

“This righteousness from God” (Romans 3:22) is within the grasp of all who reach out in faith to Jesus Christ. It is available to Jew and Gentile alike since “there is no difference.” Here is hope for the thief and the tax collector, the prostitute and the priest, the greater sinner and the lesser sinner.

“There is no difference, for all have sinned and fall short of the glory of God” (Romans 3:22–23). To sin (Greek, hamartano ) literally means, “to miss the mark.” This brief summary reminds us of our need to be made right in God’s sight. Paul is not saying that ethnic, cultural, or religious differences no longer exist; they do. Yet sin makes us alike in God’s sight and admonishes us of our need for a Savior. “The glory of God” in 3:23 refers to what God intended humans to be when He created them. Sin broke their relationship with God, and they fell short of this glory. The gospel shows how this relationship can be restored through faith in Jesus Christ.

The Greek verb tenses in verses 22 and 23 suggest an interesting parallel. A specific act in the past has a continuing result in the present. That is, “all have sinned [definite past act] and fall short [are falling short—a continuing present result] of the glory of God” (Romans 3:23). Likewise, the single act of faith (3:22) brings righteousness followed by a continuing response of belief in God (see also 1:17). Repentance and faith in Christ interrupt the continuing consequence of sin.

Parallel in Romans 3:22–23

CRISIS ACT

CONTINUING RESULT

Righteousness by faith

Believing

Sin

Falling short of the glory of God

Salvation Defined (3:24–26)

Salvation is a broad term that includes not only deliverance from sin but also abundant life in Jesus Christ. A variety of words describe the many facets of our salvation in Christ. Conversion , for example, depicts our response to God’s offer of salvation and consists of turning away from sin in repentance and turning toward Jesus Christ in faith. While conversion is our response to God, regeneration is God’s response to us. When we repent and believe, God imparts life. We are “born again”—as Jesus explained to Nicodemus (John 3:1–21). Paul depicts this as a death-to-life experience: “God, who is rich in mercy, made us alive with Christ even when we were dead in transgressions” (Ephesians 2:4–5). After we are united with Christ, God continues to work in us, molding us into Christ’s image. This process of spiritual growth is called sanctification.

In Romans 3:24–26, three words stand out: redemption, propitiation , and justification . Each one portrays a different side of salvation, helping us to grasp what God has done for us through the death of His Son on the Cross. These three words form what James Boice calls “the salvation triangle” (1991, 1:380; see chart following). Although not alike, they are related, tied together, inseparable. Together, they help us to understand how a righteous God can justify the wicked and still remain just. We will consider the significance of each of these three words now.

The Salvation Triangle

(Adapted from Boice 1991, 382)

REDEMPTION

Jesus Christ redeemed us. He ransomed [purchased] us at the price of His own blood. It is what He did for us, not what we do.

PROPITIATION

This describes what Jesus Christ did for us in relation to the Father. He turns aside God’s wrath against sin through His sacrifice of atonement (3:25). By taking our punishment on himself, He satisfies God’s justice.

JUSTIFICATION

This describes the act of a judge in a court of law who acquits an accused person. On the basis of Christ’s redemptive and propitiatory work, God justifies those who put their trust in Him. Jesus paid the price for our salvation by dying in our place. In this way, God is just in justifying the ungodly.

FAITH

“This righteousness from God comes through faith in Jesus Christ to all who believe” (3:22). “He . . . justifies those who have faith in Jesus” (3:26).

Redemption, propitiation, and justification are inextricably bound together. Any one is impossible without the others. God’s condition for receiving salvation is faith in Jesus Christ.

Redemption

In Romans 3:24, Paul writes that all “are justified freely by his grace through the redemption that came by Christ Jesus.” Redemption (Greek, apolytrosis ) is the language of the marketplace—the language of buying and selling. In Paul’s day, people bought and sold every conceivable kind of item in the open market, including slaves.

The Old Testament speaks of redeeming people, property, animals, and even a nation. Redemption can mean “to rescue, deliver, ransom, or recover what was lost” (Numbers 3:49; Psalm 111:9; 130:7). The law of Moses provided for a near relative—a kinsman-redeemer—to aid a family member in distress. For instance, if a family member was forced to sell his property under dire circumstances, the kinsman-redeemer could buy it back. Or, he could redeem a relative captured or sold into slavery. For examples, see Leviticus 25:25–28, 47–49; Ruth 4:6; Jeremiah 32:6–16. These references help us to grasp what Jesus Christ did in redeeming us. He ransomed us from the slave market of sin by paying for our freedom with His own blood; that is, He gave His life as a ransom (Matthew 20:28). By His death He delivered us from the bondage of sin. Peter says, “It was not with perishable things such as silver or gold that you were redeemed . . . but with the precious blood of Christ, a lamb without blemish or defect” (1 Peter 1:18–19).

The deliverance of the Israelites from Egyptian bondage pictures redemption and includes release from slavery and entry into a new life of freedom. The Lord “brought you out” and He “will bring you to” the Promised Land (Exodus 6:6–8). The Passover lamb (Exodus 12) is part of that picture. The Israelites applied the blood of the lamb to the door frames of their houses, as Moses directed, and escaped judgment, since the Lord “passed over” those who were “under the blood.”

Isaiah describes God’s lamb as a man: “He was led like a lamb to the slaughter, and . . . he did not open his mouth” (Isaiah 53:7, italics added). John the Baptist identified this man as Jesus: “Look, the Lamb of God, who takes away the sin of the world!” (John 1:29, 36). Paul is also precise in comparing the Passover lamb to Christ: “For Christ, our Passover lamb, has been sacrificed” (1 Corinthians 5:7). Through Christ’s sacrifice, those who put their faith in Him escape judgment and receive eternal life.

Propitiation

“God presented him as a sacrifice of atonement, through faith in his blood” (Romans 3:25). Paul borrows from the language of the Jewish sacrificial system to explain the believer’s salvation. The essential idea here is that God’s holiness requires punishment for sin. At the same time, God in love intercedes on our behalf by presenting His Son as a propitiation or “atoning sacrifice for our sins” (1 John 2:2; 4:10). His sacrifice provides the grounds for our reconciliation with God. Propitiation is related to the Greek word hileos , meaning “merciful.”

“Sacrifice of atonement” (Greek, hilasterion ) in the NIV translation of Romans 3:25 is translated as propitiation in the KJV and NASB. Christ’s sacrifice is depicted as appeasing the wrath of God. The RSV and NEB translate it expiation , meaning “to provide a covering for sin.” The NIV text note gives this alternative translation of 3:25: God presented him, that is, Jesus Christ, “ as the one who would turn aside his wrath , taking away sin” (emphasis added; see also Hebrews 2:17).

Paul explains that God reveals His wrath against sin (Romans 1:18) and, at the same time, initiates a way to avert it. “God presented him [Jesus Christ] as a sacrifice of atonement, through faith in his blood” (3:25). In other words, Jesus Christ—not people—turns aside God’s wrath through His sacrificial death. Christ took our punishment on himself to satisfy God’s justice. Here is the idea of substitution—the innocent dying in the place of the guilty. Christ, who was without sin, died for us (2 Corinthians 5:21). “Thus God himself gave himself to save us from himself” (Stott 1994, 115).

So Paul places God’s love beside His wrath. The apostle John does the same thing in one of the best-known chapters in the Bible. Read what he says about God’s wrath and His love in John 3:16 and 36.

The “sacrifice of atonement” in Romans 3:25 is a reminder of the Old Testament sacrificial system and, more specifically, the atonement cover (mercy seat) of the Ark of the Covenant. The word translated as sacrifice of atonement here ( hilasterion ) is translated as atonement cover in Hebrews 9:5.

The ministry of the high priest on the Day of Atonement is instructive (Leviticus 16). Once each year, he entered the innermost room of the tabernacle, called the Most Holy Place. It housed one piece of furniture—the Ark of the Covenant. This gold-covered, portable wooden chest held the stone tablets, that is, the Ten Commandments. The atonement cover, or mercy seat, was a slab of pure gold. Formed together with it in one piece were two angelic-like figures with outstretched wings facing each other. God said that His presence would dwell between these cherubim. On the Day of Atonement, the most important day of the Jewish calendar, the high priest went behind the curtain with sacrificial blood and sprinkled it on the atonement cover. The fundamental idea symbolized here is this: From between the cherubim, God looks down but does not see the Law the people had broken. Instead, He sees the blood of the sacrifice and is satisfied that punishment has been measured out. Thus His wrath is turned aside, and fellowship is restored.

Because these annual sacrifices could never fully deal with sin, they were constantly repeated: “an annual reminder of sins, because it is impossible for the blood of bulls and goats to take away sins” (Hebrews 10:3–4). The sacrifices foreshadowed the coming “Lamb of God, who takes away the sin of the world” (John 1:29). As our great high priest, Jesus Christ offered His own blood as a sacrifice for sinners “once for all” (Hebrews 7:27; 9:11–14, 26; 10:10–12). His blood does more than “cover over” sin; it removes sin permanently.

We therefore see that Christ’s suffering on the Cross redeems sinners and turns aside God’s wrath. It also vindicates God’s justice: “He did this to demonstrate his justice” (Romans 3:25). God publicly displayed His justice through the Cross. But what about His forbearance in leaving “sins committed beforehand unpunished” (3:25)? Does this mean that He condoned evil? Actually, it is God’s extraordinary kindness, tolerance, and patience that lead to repentance (2:4; Acts 14:16–17; 17:30; 2 Peter 3:9). The Cross concerns not only the past but also the present. “He did it to demonstrate his justice at the present time” (Romans 3:26). God dealt adequately with sin at Calvary “so as to be just and the one who justifies [those who have] faith in Jesus” (3:26).

Justification

Justification is a legal term used in a court of law. Paul uses it to explain how God makes sinful people acceptable to Him. In the original language, it is closely related to righteousness . “The Greek word for ‘justification’ ( dikaiosyne ) is built on the word for ‘right’ or ‘righteousness’ ( dikaios )” (Boice 1991, 1:381).

“Justified freely by his grace” (Romans 3:24) describes what God does for all who believe in Jesus Christ as Savior (3:22). The word justify means “to pronounce, accept, and treat as just, i.e., as on the one hand, not penally liable, and on the other, entitled to all the privileges due to those who have kept the law” (Harrison 1987, 304).

The word justify , then, has both a positive and a negative meaning. In a negative sense, God declares the believer in Christ “not guilty.” In other words, he or she is no longer liable to punishment. The person’s sins are forgiven. Paul and Barnabas preached this to the Galatians in Pisidian Antioch: “I want you to know that through Jesus the forgiveness of sins is proclaimed to you. Through him everyone who believes is justified from everything you could not be justified from by the law of Moses” (Acts 13:38–39). In a positive sense, justify means that God declares the believer righteous : “His faith is credited as righteousness” (Romans 4:5–6).

Those who believe are “justified freely by his grace” (Romans 3:24). Everyone is guilty of sin and deserves judgment. Nevertheless, God graciously extends His favor and mercy to all through His Son. Paul says that God does this in several ways. He justifies us by grace : “For it is by grace you have been saved” (Ephesians 2:8). He justifies us freely . “And this not from yourselves, it is the gift of God—not by works, so that no one can boast” (2:8–9). He justifies us through faith (Romans 3:22)—a faith centered on Jesus Christ. He justifies us “through the redemption that came by Christ Jesus” (3:24). “He has rescued us from the dominion of darkness and brought us into the kingdom of the Son he loves, in whom we have redemption, the forgiveness of sins” (Colossians 1:13–14).

God pronounces a person righteous the moment he or she believes in Christ as Savior. While some teach that individuals can never know for certain if they are in a right relationship with God until after death, Paul makes it clear that believers can have the full assurance of being right before God now, in this present life.

Reflect again on the salvation triangle. God justifies sinners based on the twofold work of Christ. First of all, Christ rescues us from sin by redeeming us. (This is represented by the bottom line of the triangle.) Secondly, He turns aside God’s wrath through His sacrificial death (represented by the left side of the triangle). Therefore, on the basis of Christ’s redemptive and propitiatory work, God is just in justifying the wicked (represented by the right side of the triangle).

Three Questions Answered (3:27–31)

Paul now concludes his presentation up to this point. His conclusion consists of asking and answering three questions that might arise relative to what he has said about justification by faith.

1. Where, then, is boasting? (Romans 3:27)

2. Is God the God of Jews only? Is he not the God of Gentiles too? (3:29)

3. Do we, then, nullify the law by this faith? (3:31)

Where, Then, Is Boasting? (Romans 3:27)

To boast is to express pride in oneself or in one’s accomplishments. While this tendency obviously exists among all people, Jews in particular were guilty of it. As God’s chosen people, they boasted about their relationship to God and their possession of the Law (see 2:17–24). Furthermore, their zeal in Law observance led to pride.

Do you recall the parable about the Pharisee and the tax collector (Luke 18:9–14)? Jesus told this story for those who considered themselves better than others and who scorned everyone else. The self-confident Pharisee boasted in his own goodness—proudly pointing out that he did not cheat or commit adultery like other sinners. He paid his tithes; in fact, he even went beyond the requirements of the Law by fasting twice a week. Was he exaggerating? Probably not. Unquestionably, he was superior to the tax collector—much to his own satisfaction. But no one gains God’s favor on the basis of superiority to others.

The tax collector in this story is helpful too. His prayer—“God, have mercy on me, a sinner” (Luke 18:13)—demonstrates how we should approach God. He admitted his sin and put his trust in God’s goodness and mercy. Because of this, he—rather than the Pharisee—went home justified before God. “For everyone who exalts himself will be humbled, and he who humbles himself will be exalted” (18:14).

Righteousness “comes through faith in Jesus Christ” (Romans 3:22). Since it is by grace through faith, boasting is “excluded” (3:27). No one can earn righteousness or take credit for it; rather, “it is the gift of God—not by works, so that no one can boast” (Ephesians 2:8–9). Law observance gives rise to boasting because it is rooted in human effort. Therefore, Paul wrote, “We maintain that a man is justified by faith apart from observing the law” (Romans 3:28). Paul does not boast in himself or in his accomplishments. Like Jeremiah, he says, “Let him who boasts boast in the Lord” (1 Corinthians 1:31; Jeremiah 9:24).

Is God the God of Jews Only? Is He Not the God of Gentiles Too? (Romans 3:29)

God’s choice of Abraham never meant the exclusion of others. From the beginning, God intended to make him an instrument of blessing to all nations (Genesis 12:1–3). Paul concludes that “there is only one God” (Romans 3:30) who justifies Jews and Gentiles in the same way—through faith in Jesus Christ.

Do We, Then, Nullify the Law by This Faith? (Romans 3:31)

This, Paul suggests, is the question asked by the Jew who listens intently to him. Paul’s response is, “Not at all! Rather, we uphold the law” (3:31). “But how is this possible?” the Jew asks. “Law and faith seem to oppose each other.” Consider this: The person who confesses, “I have sinned,” essentially acknowledges that the Law is right. Through confession, he or she upholds the Law.

Here is another sense in which faith upholds the Law. A Christian who lives “according to the Spirit” fulfills “the righteous requirements of the law” (Romans 8:4; see also 13:8). Reflect on these words of Jesus in His Sermon on the Mount: “Do not think that I have come to abolish the Law or the Prophets; I have not come to abolish them but to fulfill them” (Matthew 5:17). Jeremiah, speaking God’s words, prophesied, “I [the Lord] will put my law in their minds and write it on their hearts” (Jeremiah 31:33). In addition, Paul writes, “It is God who works in you to will and to act according to his good purpose” (Philippians 2:13). The Spirit empowers the believer to fulfill the intent of the Law. Thus, justification by faith does not nullify the Law.

**Q:** What do the words “But now” in Romans 3:21 point to?

**A:** A logical turning point in Paul’s argument and a historical crossroads on God’s timetable.

**Q:** What do Paul’s words,“a righteousness from God, apart from law,” mean?

**A:** This righteousness is not obtainable by observing the Law. With the coming of Christ, God reveals what He had determined even before Creation. Faith in Jesus Christ is the basis for receiving “a righteousness from God, apart from law.”

**Q:** By now you may have memorized Romans 1:16–17 and 3:21–22. From memory, try to list the five key elements of the theme of Romans emphasized in these two passages.

**A:** The gospel reveals a righteousness from God. It is for all who believe. It is to the Jew first and then to the Gentile. It is through faith in Jesus Christ. The righteous live by faith.

**Q:** List as many words or phrases as you can that are related in meaning to the word redemption .

**A:** Student’s answers will vary but could include recover , pay a ransom , and rescue .

**Q:** What is meant by “sacrifice of atonement”?

**A:** Satisfying God’s judgment, propitiation, turning aside wrath, and atonement cover

**Q:** When does God pronounce a person righteous?

**A:** When the person accepts Christ as Savior.

**Q:** How would you define justification by faith?

**A:** Justification is based on Christ’s redemptive work, which is Christ’s sacrificial death to turn away God’s wrath. It becomes a reality in our life when we put our trust in Christ and means that God declares the believer in Christ “not guilty” and “righteous.”

**Q:** Explain why this statement is incorrect: Those who sincerely pursue righteous activities with right motives have earned the right to boast of their accomplishments.

**A:** Pursuing righteous activities with right motives simply means that, in gratitude for the salvation God freely gives to all who come to Him through Jesus Christ, we respond in loving, sacrificial service to Him. Our purpose is to glorify Him, not ourselves.

**Q:** Consider the three passages in the preceding paragraph—Matthew 5:17, Jeremiah 31:33, and Philippians 2:13—in relation to justification by faith. Briefly explain your understanding of the relationship of Christian faith to the Old Testament law.

**A:** Your answer should include these ideas: Those who respond to the gospel in faith, by confessing their sins and turning to Christ, sustain the Law. Believers who live by the Spirit (allowing the Spirit of Christ to lead and guide them), exhibit the intent of the Law and in this way uphold it.

### Justification Illustrated (Romans 4:1–25)

#### 3.2.1 — Derive from the lives of Abraham and David examples related to circumcision, Law, faith, and forgiveness of sin that illustrate the significance of justification by faith.

Paul has explained justification by faith and answered questions. Now he introduces Abraham and David to illustrate his message. His choice is not difficult to understand because every Jew looked up to these men. Abraham was their founding father, God’s friend, and a model of righteousness. David, of course, was Israel’s greatest king and a man after God’s own heart. Both Abraham and David honored God. It should not surprise us that Matthew chose these same men to introduce his gospel: “Jesus Christ the son of David, the son of Abraham” (Matthew 1:1).

To make his case, Paul adheres to an important principle: “A matter must be established by the testimony of two or three witnesses” (Deuteronomy 19:15). Therefore, he calls on Abraham and David to testify. Their testimony also provides precedent—something that lawyers look for. Prior court decisions in similar cases strengthen and help to prove current cases. In his illustration, Paul begins with Abraham and justification, then presents David’s testimony of forgiveness of sin, and concludes with the example of Abraham and circumcision. We shall follow the same order.

Abraham and Justification

Abraham illustrates Paul’s message in three ways:

1. He was justified by faith, not works (Romans 4:1–8).

2. He was justified before he was circumcised (4:9–12).

3. God fulfilled His promise to Abraham through faith, not the Law (4:13–25).

“What then shall we say that Abraham, our forefather, discovered in this matter?” (Romans 4:1). The Israelites esteemed Abraham, as did the Arab peoples. But Paul goes beyond natural descent to make his case. Spiritually speaking, Abraham is “the father of all who believe” (4:11; see also vv. 12, 16; Galatians 3:7, 9, 29), serving as an example for all believers.

Paul answers his own question: “If, in fact, Abraham was justified by works, he had something to boast about—but not before God” (Romans 4:2). Rabbis taught that Abraham was justified by works. By interpreting faith as faithfulness, they made it meritorious (Stott, 1994). They also used Genesis 26:4–5 to explain why God favored Abraham: “Abraham obeyed me and kept my requirements, my commands, my decrees and my laws.” However, this appraisal came long after God had credited him as righteous. Paul rejects justification by works on the basis that it allows for “something to boast about—but not before God” (Romans 4:2). We may boast before others, but not before God.

Notice how the Epistle to the Hebrews portrays Abraham. The writer highlights four major events in his life; then, for each event, he shows how obedience flowed out of Abraham’s trust in God. Abraham obeyed because he believed. “By faith Abraham, when called to go . . . obeyed and went” (Hebrews 11:8). “By faith he made his home in the promised land” (11:9). “By faith Abraham . . . was enabled to become a father” (11:11). “By faith Abraham . . . offered Isaac as a sacrifice” (11:17).

“What does the Scripture say? ‘Abraham believed God, and it was credited to him as righteousness’” (Romans 4:3). Paul appeals to Scripture because it is the highest authority. He quotes Moses (Genesis 15:6), reminding his readers that Abraham believed God. While faith is essential for salvation, it is not a meritorious act; that is, it is not something done for praise or reward. Scripture nowhere links God’s promise of land, ancestors, or blessings to Abraham as a reward for good works (Genesis 12:1–3). Nor does the Genesis 15:6 context suggest that Abraham earned God’s righteousness. Verse 6 simply says that God told Abraham his offspring would be as numerous as the stars in the heavens. At eighty-five years of age, with no children and with circumstances that shouted, “Impossible!” he took God at His word. “And it was credited to him as righteousness.”

Was this the extent of Abraham’s faith? Actually, he had deep spiritual insight. As we learn in Galatians, “God . . . announced the gospel in advance to Abraham: ‘All nations will be blessed through you’” (Galatians 3:8). This means Abraham believed the gospel and looked forward to Christ’s coming. Do I hear you protesting, “Isn’t Paul exaggerating to prove his point?” Consider what Jesus said: “Your father Abraham rejoiced at the thought of seeing my day; he saw it and was glad” (John 8:56). Admittedly, he did not know all the details recounted in the Gospels; but by faith he foresaw Christ’s day.

Today, we look back in faith to what Christ accomplished at Calvary. Abraham looked forward to the coming Savior, just as Moses, Isaiah, and other Old Testament saints did. Regardless of our place in history, we look toward the Cross.

“It was credited to him [Abraham] as righteousness” (Romans 4:3). The word credited (Greek, logizomai ) is a bookkeeping term that means “to credit to someone’s account.” It was credited is in the passive voice. Edwards calls it a divine passive, meaning, “God credited it” (1992, 115). God credited His righteousness to Abraham as a gift, not as something earned. For example, money can be credited in two ways. It can be put into an account as wages (money earned), or it can be put into an account as a gift (unearned). This is what Paul says in Romans 4:4–5. When a person works, his or her wages are an obligation, not a gift. “However, to the man who does not work but trusts God who justifies the wicked, his faith is credited as righteousness” (4:5).

Let us try to grasp the meaning of credited in another way. To illustrate this, Boice suggests, imagine that Abraham and Christ have ledger books: “God took the sin of Abraham from the ledger book of Abraham’s life and transferred it to the ledger book of Christ, who died for such sin. And he took the righteousness of Christ from Christ’s book and transferred it to Abraham” (1991, 1:438).

This illustration shows that Abraham had no righteousness of his own, but God credited him as righteous through his faith. It is the same for us. Jesus Christ had no sin, but He was counted as sin for us. “God made him who had no sin to be sin for us, so that in him we might become the righteousness of God” (2 Corinthians 5:21). Of course, this does not mean that Christ became a sinner. “To be sin for us” can also be translated as “be a sin offering” (NIV text note on 5:21). Only because He was sinless could He become our Savior. Philip Hughes qualifies the use of the word transfer to describe this by saying, “Not for one moment does He [Christ] cease to be righteous, else the radical exchange envisioned by the Apostle here, whereby our sin is transferred to Him and His righteousness is transferred to us, would be no more than a fiction or an hallucination” (1962, 214).

Abraham is a model for all who seek to be justified by faith—Jews and Gentiles alike—since he is the father of all who believe. “Those who believe are children of Abraham. So those who have faith are blessed along with Abraham, the man of faith” (Galatians 3:7, 9).

David and Forgiveness of Sin

Paul now introduces his second witness, David, who corroborates Abraham’s testimony: “David says the same thing” (Romans 4:6). He agrees about the “blessedness of the man to whom God credits righteousness apart from works” (4:6). His testimony (4:7–8) is taken from Psalm 32:1–2:

Blessed are they

whose transgressions are forgiven,

whose sins are covered.

Blessed is the man

whose sin the Lord will never count against him.

Transgressions Are Forgiven (Romans 4:7)

The one to whom God credits righteousness is forgiven of his or her sins and is reconciled to God through Christ’s sacrifice. The Greek word aphiemi can be translated “forgive,” “leave,” or “send away.” In Matthew 13:36, Jesus “ left the crowd” (NIV) or “ sent the multitude away ” (KJV) in order to instruct His disciples privately. “The idea is that of separation, and the bearing of the word upon the sin question is that it teaches that God is willing to separate the sinner’s transgressions and guilt from the sinner” (Boice 1991, 1:449).

This idea of separation also appears in the Law. On the Day of Atonement, Aaron laid his hands on the head of the scapegoat and confessed over it all the sins of the people (Leviticus 16:8–10, 20–22). Then the goat—also called the goat of removal —was sent out into the wilderness. This symbolized separation or removal of sin from the people. (See also Psalm 103:12.)

Whose Sins Are Covered (Romans 4:7)

This reminds us of the Day of Atonement when sacrificial blood was sprinkled on the atonement cover (Hebrews 9:5; Leviticus 16). As we saw earlier, this symbolized Christ’s redemptive work: “God presented him as a sacrifice of atonement” (Romans 3:25).

Whose Sin the Lord Will Never Count Against Him (Romans 4:8)

This is the same as counting a person righteous. This negative form reminds us that justification is a legal term. It carries the idea of acquittal , of being declared “not guilty.” God treats the sinner who believes in Christ as if he or she had never sinned. Christ’s righteousness is credited to the believer’s account. As Paul preached in Antioch, “In this man Jesus there is forgiveness for your sins. Everyone who believes in him is freed from all guilt and declared right with God [justified]—something the Jewish law could never do” (Acts 13:38–39, NLT).

Again, we see that justification has a positive and a negative side. Paul uses Abraham to illustrate the positive side and David to illustrate the negative side.

Abraham and Circumcision, Law, and Faith

After listening to David’s testimony, we return to Abraham and move from the subject of righteousness to circumcision, the Law, and faith. Although we have already dealt with these issues, they are recurring themes. Since opponents constantly contested Paul’s teaching, his gospel explanation addresses their persistent objections and repeats issues from various perspectives. Like a good lawyer or teacher, he uses repetition to drive home his point.

In Romans 4:9, Paul poses another question: “Is this blessedness only for the circumcised, or also for the uncircumcised?” In other words, is the gospel for Jews only, or also for Gentiles? What role does circumcision play? The Judaizers , you may recall, stirred up trouble in Galatia over this question. While the Council at Jerusalem officially resolved the matter (Acts 15), it nevertheless remained a point of controversy.

How did Abraham’s circumcision relate to his justification? That is, in what order did they occur? Did God declare him righteous before or after he was circumcised? Paul replies, “It was not after, but before!” (Romans 4:10). Abraham was eighty-five years old when God declared him righteous. Then, when he was ninety-nine years of age, God said, “You are to undergo circumcision” (Genesis 17:11). Thus, fourteen years elapsed between the time God declared him righteous and the time he was circumcised. (Compare Genesis 12:4; 15:6; 16:3, 16; and 17:1, 11.)

“So then, he is the father of all who believe” (Romans 4:11). Paul argues that Abraham is the spiritual father of all Gentiles who believe because he was justified before he was circumcised. At the same time, he argues that Abraham is the spiritual father of all Jews who believe because he was later circumcised. Paul extends his argument further to show that circumcision is irrelevant as far as justification is concerned, because “the promise comes by faith” (4:16).

“He received the sign of circumcision, a seal” (Romans 4:11). Opponents ask a valid question: “If Abraham was justified by faith, why was circumcision necessary?” Verse 11 gives the answer: circumcision served as a sign and a seal. A sign is “a visible object that points to something different from and greater than itself” (Boice 1991, 1:456). For example, a road sign may point you in the right direction so that you reach your destination. Although it is not, itself, your destination, it is valuable, because it helps you to get there. How does this relate to Abraham’s circumcision? It served as a sign that pointed to the covenant.

A similar question can be asked about water baptism and the Lord’s Supper. Are these sacraments necessary for salvation, or do they serve another purpose? We are saved by faith in Christ, not by water baptism. Nevertheless, baptism is ordained of God and serves a valuable purpose, as it pictures our identification with Christ in His death, burial, and resurrection. Likewise, the Lord’s Supper points back to Christ’s sacrifice: “This is my body, which is for you; do this in remembrance of me” (1 Corinthians 11:24). It also serves as a sign, pointing to the past as well as to what is still ahead, “until he comes” (11:26).

Circumcision, as a seal , confirmed God’s promise. A seal attests to the validity of a document but is worthless without it. Circumcision bore witness to Abraham’s right standing before God— confirming , but not conferring his position before God (Thomas, 1974).

Max Lucado offers this interesting perspective on circumcision: “[It] symbolized the nearness God desires with his people. God puts a knife to our self-sufficiency. He wants to be a part of our identity, our intimacy, and even our potency. Circumcision proclaimed that there is no part of our life too private or too personal for God” (1996, 47).

Thus we see that Abraham received the covenant promise neither by works (Romans 4:2) nor by circumcision (4:10). We have listened to Paul stress this theme repeatedly. Now he continues, “It was not through law that Abraham and his offspring received the promise . . . but through the righteousness that comes by faith” (4:13). Remember that the Law was given over four hundred years after God credited Abraham as righteous. It was not given to justify but to give a knowledge of sin. “Law brings wrath” (4:15) or “punishment” (4:15, NLT). Why these negative assertions? They identify human methods to gain acceptance with God.

Speaking of Abraham, Paul continues, “He is our father in the sight of God, in whom he believed—the God who gives life to the dead and calls things that are not as though they were” (Romans 4:17). Abraham was ninety-nine years old when God said, “I have made you a father of many nations” (4:17; Genesis 17:5). These nations were nonexistent at the time He spoke. Isaac had not yet been born. Still, God considered these to be accomplished facts. This is the meaning of verse 17: God “calls things that are not as though they were.” The birth of Isaac demonstrates God’s ability to bring into existence the nonexistent.

What was Abraham’s part? He inherited the promise through faith and patience. “Waiting patiently” and “faith” are linked together in Hebrews 6:12 and 15. The writer to the Hebrews gives this exhortation: “So do not throw away your confidence; it will be richly rewarded. You need to persevere so that when you have done the will of God, you will receive what he has promised” (Hebrews 10:35–36).

The phrase “the God who gives life to the dead” (Romans 4:17) refers primarily to the birth of Isaac, but it also alludes to Christ’s resurrection from the dead (4:24–25). Abraham and Sarah were advanced in age, “as good as dead” (4:19; Hebrews 11:11–12) and unable to produce children. Nevertheless, they believed God had the power to give them new life so that they could have the promised son. “Against all hope, Abraham in hope believed” (Romans 4:18). The result? He “became the father of many nations” (4:18). God fulfills His promises.

In verse 19, Paul indicates that Abraham did not deny reality; rather, “he faced the fact that his body was as good as dead.” The Phillips translation paraphrases it this way:

With undaunted faith he looked at the facts—his own impotence (he was practically a hundred years old at the time) and his wife Sarah’s apparent barrenness. Yet he refused to allow any distrust of a definite pronouncement of God to make him waver. (Romans 4:19–20)

Verse 20 tells us three things about Abraham’s faith: (1) he did not waver through unbelief regarding the promise of God, (2) he was strengthened in his faith, and (3) he gave glory to God.

How was Abraham’s faith strengthened in the face of this impossibility? He looked beyond his limitations to God’s limitless power. He focused on God—His promise, His trustworthiness, and His ability to fulfill His promises. Then he expressed his confidence toward God by giving him praise. The NLT says, “His faith grew stronger, and in this he brought glory to God. He was absolutely convinced that God was able to do anything he promised” (Romans 4:20–21). “This is why ‘it was credited to him as righteousness’” (4:22). God declared Abraham righteous because he believed. Paul explains that these words “were written not for him alone, but also for us, to whom God will credit righteousness” (4:23–24). We, too, believe in God “who brought Jesus our Lord back from the dead” (4:24, NLT). He gives spiritual life—eternal life—to all who believe.

“He was delivered over to death for our sins and was raised to life for our justification” (Romans 4:25). Christ’s sacrificial death and resurrection are closely related. In fact, they are inseparable. Philip Comfort explains, “His resurrection brought us justification before God, for the Resurrection proved the efficacy of Christ’s death and demonstrates that Jesus, the Living One, can make us right with God” (1992, 95).

**Q:** Give three reasons why Abraham is known as a man of faith.

**A:** Your answer may include, when God called Abraham to go, he obeyed and went. He trusted God and made his home in the Promised Land. He believed when God told him that he would become a father in his old age. He trusted God when he obeyed and offered Isaac as a sacrifice. In all of these things, Abraham believed God.

**Q:** According to Genesis 15, why was Abraham credited as righteous?

**A:** He believed that his children would be as numerous as the stars.

**Q:** Explain 2 Corinthians 5:21 as it applies to your salvation.

**A:** Answers will vary but should include: through my faith in Jesus Christ, God has credited me as righteous. Christ became my sin offering so that I might be counted as righteous before God—not because I deserve it, but because of His love, mercy, and grace.

**Q:** Explain the significance of David’s testimony recorded in Romans 4:7–8 as it relates to justification by faith.

**A:** Because of his faith, David’s sins were forgiven, covered, and never counted against him. This aspect of justification carries the idea of acquittal or being declared “not guilty.” The believer who trusts in Jesus Christ receives forgiveness of sins and is justified or declared right with God.

**Q:** Summarize what we have discussed concerning unbelief.

**A:** Answers should include: unbelief is sinful; unbelief causes wavering; unbelief causes a turning away from God; unbelief hears God’s promise and refuses to believe.

### Benefits of Justification (Romans 5:1–11)

#### 3.3.1 — Describe what Romans 5 teaches about benefits for those who are justified through faith in Jesus Christ.

Peace with God

“Therefore, since we have been justified through faith, we have peace with God through our Lord Jesus Christ” (Romans 5:1). Those who have been made right in God’s sight receive certain benefits. In Romans 5, Paul begins to enumerate some of these benefits. The first is peace with God .

Everyone wants peace. Nations pursue it; businesses and organizations strive for it. Families and friends seek to maintain it, and every individual longs for it. At whatever level we consider it, however, we must recognize that it ultimately comes “through our Lord Jesus Christ,” the Prince of Peace (Isaiah 9:6). People devise ways to obtain it—at least superficially. But true, lasting peace comes only out of a right relationship with God. Then we have “peace with God.”

Paul introduces the word reconciliation (Romans 5:11) in this passage. It is closely related to justification. Note the parallelism in verses 9–10 (NIV Study Bible 1985, 1712):

Verse 9

Verse 10

justified

reconciled

by his blood

through the death of his Son

shall we be saved

shall we be saved

Reconciliation (Romans 5:9–11) is also linked to “peace with God” (5:1). As sinners “we were God’s enemies,” alienated from Him because of our sin. But now, “through the death of his Son” (5:10; Colossians 1:20–22), we have been justified and reconciled to Him. The hostility has ceased, and “we have peace with God” (Romans 5:1). For this we rejoice.

“Let us have peace with God” is an alternative translation of Romans 5:1 (see NIV text note). This rendering arises from a slight variation in some Greek texts. The Greek word echomen is sometimes in the indicative (“we have”) and sometimes in the subjunctive (“let us have”). Some translators and commentators strongly support the subjunctive form, which makes this verse an exhortation to act on our justification. Nevertheless, authorities such as F. F. Bruce reject this view; Bruce insists that “the context supports ‘we have peace’” (1985, 116) and cites verse 11 (“we have now received reconciliation”). Stott (1994) also argues that the series of affirmations and lack of exhortations support the “we have peace” reading.
