“We have peace” (Romans 5:1) affirms the consequence of our justification. The winds of adversity may momentarily unsettle us, but they can never destroy our peace with God (see Philippians 4:6–7).

Other Benefits

Besides peace with God, Paul mentions other benefits in Romans 5:1–11, including these:

1. “We have gained access by faith into this grace” (5:2).

2. “We rejoice in the hope of the glory of God” (5:2).

3. “We also rejoice in our sufferings” (5:3).

4. “God has poured out his love into our hearts by the Holy Spirit” (5:5).

5. “We have now received reconciliation” (5:11).

We have gained access by faith into this grace (Romans 5:2). At the moment Christ died, “the curtain of the temple was torn in two from top to bottom” (Matthew 27:51). The curtain separated the Holy Place from the Most Holy Place where the High Priest alone was permitted to enter. Christ’s death opened the way for all believers to enter directly into God’s presence (Ephesians 2:18; Hebrews 4:14–16; 7:25).

We rejoice (Romans 5:2–3, 11). Three times in the first eleven verses, Paul says, “Rejoice.”

1. “We rejoice in the hope of the glory of God” (Romans 5:2). We fell short of the glory of God (3:23) because of sin. But Christ gives us hope that God’s original intent—the glory of God—will ultimately be restored (5:2).

2. “We rejoice in our sufferings” (Romans 5:3). Suffering (Greek, thlipsis ) primarily means “a pressing,” “pressure,” or anything that burdens the spirit (Vine 1996, 17). Opposition or pressure often arises from those who are hostile to Christ. For instance, Paul and Barnabas told the Galatians, “We must go through many hardships ( thlipsis ) to enter the kingdom of God” (Acts 14:22; Stott 1994). This same word is translated as “trouble” in John 16:33. Jesus called on His followers to rejoice when persecuted (Matthew 5:12)—and they did (Acts 5:41; also 2 Thessalonians 1:3–5). Paul does not say to rejoice because of suffering but in it. We rejoice because we know that God will bring us through it and cause good to come out of it. We refuse to be gloomy or sad; instead, we choose to rejoice and be glad.

3. “We rejoice in God” (Romans 5:11) because we are reconciled to Him through Jesus Christ.

**Q:** Our union with Christ provides many benefits. Identify benefits Paul mentions in each of the following references: Romans 5:1; Romans 5:2 (two benefits); Romans 5:3; Romans 5:5; Romans 5:11; Philippians 4:6–7.

**A:** Romans 5:2 We have peace with God. Romans 5:2 We have access into a new relationship of His grace. We joyously look forward to sharing in the glory of God. Romans 5:3 We rejoice in our sufferings. Romans 5:5 We have received the Spirit who fills our hearts with love. Romans 5:11 We are reconciled to God. Philippians 4:6–7 We have a peace that surpasses our human understanding.

### Basis for Justification (Romans 5:12–21)

#### 3.4.1 — Compare and contrast “one man” (Adam) with “the one to come” (Christ) based on Romans 5:12–21.

Now we come to the concluding verses of Romans 5 and Paul’s explanation of the basis for justification. Paul has been discussing the gospel as it relates to Israel. He talks, for example, about the Law and Abraham in chapters 2–4. Edwards explains, “Paul now extends his purview to show that Jesus not only fulfills the promise given to Abraham but also mends a deeper, fundamental rift stemming from Adam himself” (1992, 145).

Adam and Christ

Paul compares and contrasts Adam and Christ—introducing them without name and simply saying “one man” (Romans 5:12) and “the one to come” (5:14). This one man —Adam—was responsible for sin and death in the world. Three fundamental truths are evident in verse 12. First, “sin entered the world through one man.” Adam’s disobedience opened the door for sin to enter the human race. Second, death is a direct result of sin. Third, “in this way, death came to all men.” Adam’s disobedience affected the entire human race.

Adam is also “a pattern [Greek, typos ] of the one to come” (Romans 5:14)—the Messiah. Typos means “the impression made by a blow, hence a ‘stamp,’ ‘model,’ or ‘pattern’” (Edwards 1992, 150). Thus, Adam prefigures the one to come.

You may be wondering, “How can Adam possibly serve as a pattern of Christ?” After all, he brought sin and death into the world. Earle replies to this: “Obviously Adam was not in his transgression a type of Christ. It is rather that his disobedience affected the whole human race, and that Christ’s obedience did likewise (5:19)” (1986, 163). Their similarity, then, is in the universal consequence of their deeds—obedience or disobedience.

But there are dissimilarities as well. Paul, in fact, draws a sharp contrast between Adam and Christ. For instance, in verse 15, the expression not like contrasts Christ’s gift with Adam’s trespass. This same expression in verse 16 contrasts the gift of God and the result of sin, followed by the consequences: justification and condemnation. Then, the phrase how much more shows the superiority of the gift over the trespass (Romans 5:15) and the abundant provision of grace over the trespass (5:17).

Adam characterizes humanity without salvation. He is linked to “death” (Romans 5:12), “breaking a command” (5:14), “trespass” (5:15), “judgment” (5:16), “condemnation” (5:16), “disobedience” (5:19), and “sin” (5:20). In contrast, Paul connects Christ to “the gift” (5:15), “grace” (5:15, 17, 20–21), “justification” (5:16), “righteousness” (5:17), “obedience” (5:19), and “eternal life” (5:21).

Adam and Christ are not only contrasted but also compared. Paul uses the expression just as . . . so also three times (Romans 5:18–19, 21) to compare them. Stott says the “structure in each verse is intended to highlight the similarity between Adam and Christ: the one act of the one man determined the destiny of the many” (1994, 156). He also explains that these verses take up the results, nature, and resulting “reign” of these two men. Let us look at these three verses:

1. Verse 18 identifies the consequences of each man’s act: “ Just as the result of one trespass was condemnation for all men, so also the result of one act of righteousness was justification that brings life for all men.”

2. Verse 19 identifies the nature of their act: “For just as through the disobedience of the one man the many were made sinners, so also through the obedience of the one man the many will be made righteous.”

3. Verse 21 identifies the two kinds of “reign”: “ Just as sin reigned in death, so also grace might reign through righteousness to bring eternal life.”

What does Paul mean when he says, “because all sinned” in Romans 5:12? Does he mean that everyone followed Adam’s example? Or that everyone inherited Adam’s nature? Obviously, not everyone committed the same specific sin as Adam. But in a broader sense, “each person continues the rebellion and self-determination of Adam in his or her own life” (Brauch 1989, 32). Verses 13 and 14 show that sin was in the world before the giving of the Law because death reigned. Sin separates us from God and brings death. Ultimately, we are responsible for our own actions (see Ezekiel 18:19–20).

As Romans 5:12 indicates, sin entered the world through Adam. Stott explains, “Paul is not concerned with the origin of evil in general, but only with how it invaded the world of human beings . . . that is, through his [Adam’s] disobedience” (1994, 150). Edwards adds: “His purpose here is not the development of a doctrine of original sin but the establishment of a typological contrast between Adam and Christ. He is content to say typologically what he said in 3:23, ‘All have sinned and fall short of the glory of God’” (1992, 148–149).

Boice (1992) sees Adam as the representative of the race—much like an ambassador who acts on behalf of his or her country. The citizens are therefore bound by the actions the ambassador takes and the agreements he or she signs. Boice quotes Lloyd-Jones’ explanation:

Adam’s sin is imputed to us in exactly the same way that Christ’s righteousness is imputed to us. We inherit, of course, a sinful nature from Adam. . . . But that is not what condemns us. What condemns us, and makes us subject to death, is the fact that we have all sinned in Adam, . . . we are all held guilty of sin. . . . It is our union with Adam that accounts for all our trouble. It is our corresponding union with Christ that accounts for our salvation. (Boice 1992, 566)

Throughout this passage, we see variations of “the many died by the trespass of the one man” (Romans 5:15). How can one man’s sin result in condemnation and death for all? Admittedly, this is difficult to grasp—especially in a culture that stresses individualism. Those who see themselves as part of a group—one family, one tribe, one nation—what sociologists call solidarity —more readily accept Paul’s concept.

Regardless of our cultural background, the Bible itself helps us to grasp Paul’s meaning. Stott (1994) introduces several striking illustrations. For example, Achan sinned at Jericho by taking what God had forbidden (Joshua 7:1–26). But notice what the Lord says: “Israel has sinned” (7:11). One man sinned, yet God held all Israel responsible! You may struggle with this next example from Hebrews:

We might even say that Levi’s descendants, the ones who collect the tithe, paid a tithe to Melchizedek through their ancestor Abraham. For although Levi wasn’t born yet, the seed from which he came was in Abraham’s loins when Melchizedek collected the tithe from him. (Hebrews 7:9–10, NLT)

Are you protesting, “It is unfair to say that we sinned in and through Adam”? Consider the other side of the analogy—our union with Christ. Through Christ we are made right in God’s sight. Paul injects sharp contrasts. “The gift is not like the trespass” (Romans 5:15). Adam’s self-will brought death. Christ’s self-surrender supplies life. The gift provides immeasurably more good than the evil that came out of the trespass. What is Paul saying? In Adam we are declared sinners, even though we did nothing. In Christ we are declared righteous, even though we have done nothing to merit it (Boice 1992). “Because one person disobeyed God, many people became sinners. But because one other person obeyed God, many people will be made right in God’s sight” (Romans 5:19, NLT).

“How much more did God’s grace and the gift . . . overflow to the many!” (Romans 5:15). The grace of God and the gift of God transcend Adam’s sin and its consequences. “Where sin increased, grace increased all the more” (5:20).

When we study God’s Word, it speaks to us in powerful ways. Even though this is a course for which you may fulfill academic requirements, the most important benefit of your study will be its impact on your personal relationship with Jesus Christ and your sharing of these great truths from God’s Word with others. We encourage you, as you continue to study Romans, to meditate on what God is saying to you and how you can use what you have learned in your ministry to others.

**Q:** What evidence does Paul give in Romans 5:12–14 that sin was in the world from the time of Adam to the time of Moses?

**A:** Death reigned, and all died.

**Q:** Reread Romans 5:13–21. What is Paul referring to when he says, “much more” in verse 15?

**A:** The surpassing provision of God’s grace over sin. UNIT 2

## 4 - God’s Shaping Process: Romans 6–7

### Freedom from Sin (Romans 6:1–14)

#### 4.1.1 — Identify the three major theological divisions in Romans.

The Philippian jailer asked Paul and Silas how to be saved. “Believe in the Lord Jesus,” they replied, “and you will be saved.” The jailer believed, along with his family. They were baptized and filled with joy (Acts 16:30–34). Undoubtedly, they received more teaching than this summary statement indicates. Yet critics denounced Paul’s message of salvation by grace through faith as being inadequate. “Where is the restraint to sin?” they demanded. Paul deals with this issue and, as you will see, explains that the believer’s union with Christ—and His nature within the believer—restrains from sin.

The Gospel’s Restraint of Sin (6:1–2)

Does the gospel encourage sinful living? “Shall we go on sinning so that grace may increase?” (Romans 6:1). This question arose from a misinterpretation of Paul’s teaching as summarized earlier; that is, “where sin increased, grace increased all the more” (5:20). Antinomians applauded this idea as an opportunity for license : “This means we are free to live as we please. Therefore, let us sin more so that grace will increase more.” Legalists , on the other hand, took this as evidence that Paul’s message was inadequate.

Does the gospel lead to a life of sin? Is this what Paul taught? “By no means!” (Romans 6:2). He emphatically rejects this idea as a perversion of grace. God’s grace produces righteousness, not sin. To suggest that Christians should go on sinning is unthinkable and fails to grasp God’s purpose. What Paul is saying is that God’s abundant grace exceeds our greatest sin. He not only saves us from past guilt and future judgment, but He also delivers us from sin’s control now , in the present. To continue in sin contradicts the very purpose of grace.

The apostle Peter issued a stern warning to any who would pervert the gospel—from legalists to libertines —in his reference to ignorant and unstable people who distorted Paul’s letters “as they do the other Scriptures, to their own destruction” (2 Peter 3:16). Jude also warned of “godless men, who change the grace of our God into a license for immorality” (Jude 4).

The Believer’s Union with Christ (6:2–4)

“We died to sin; how can we live in it any longer?” (Romans 6:2). This is the crux of Paul’s response. To suggest that we could continue in sin is inconsistent with our union with Christ. From this brief summary, Paul elaborates on the idea of dying to sin. For example: “All of us who were baptized into Christ Jesus were baptized into his death” (6:3); “we have been united with him in his death” (6:5); “our old self was crucified with him” (6:6); “anyone who has died has been freed from sin” (6:7); and “we died with Christ” (6:8). Then Paul urges the Romans to apply his teaching: “In the same way, count yourselves dead to sin” (6:11). Note the tense of that verb. Died describes an act that took place and was completed in the past. “We died to sin” is tied directly to the believer’s union with Jesus Christ (6:2, 5). The believer does not go on sinning because he or she is united with Christ in His death and resurrection.

“We died to sin” (Romans 6:2) is interpreted in various ways. One widely held view says, in effect, “Christians should react to temptation like a kicked corpse.” In other words, they should not respond at all! Why? A lifeless corpse cannot react. Those who hold this view reason that, since “we died to sin,” we are dead to the influence of sin and therefore cannot sin. But is this Paul’s meaning?

Stott rejects this view, saying, “It is not the literal impossibility of sin in believers which Paul is declaring, but the moral incongruity [inconsistency] of it” (1994, 169). It is a contradiction to testify that we died to sin and then continue to live in sin.

Consider Paul’s counsel to the Romans in 6:12: “Do not let sin reign in your mortal body.” This is meaningless advice for a dead person, as is his next statement, “Do not offer the parts of your body to sin, as instruments of wickedness” (6:13). The examples continue: “Let us put aside the deeds of darkness” (13:12); “do not think about how to gratify the desires of the sinful nature” (13:14). If our fallen nature has died so that we no longer react to temptation, then these exhortations are meaningless.

Let us pause for a moment in our quest to understand Paul’s teaching and listen to the words of Jesus. In Matthew 18, He answers a question from the disciples, “Who is the greatest in the kingdom of heaven?” (18:1). In His response, Jesus tells His disciples they must “change and become like little children” (18:3). Does He mean that they should imitate every trait of little children, including their immaturity and selfishness? Of course not! He means that they must become humble, trusting, and unpretentious, like children.

Therefore, when Paul says, “We died to sin” (Romans 6:2), we must ask, “What is the pertinent point? In what sense does this analogy apply?” A few verses later, he uses the same expression in relation to Christ: “The death he died, he died to sin once for all” (6:10). We understand that Christ died in our place as our substitute, so that we do not need to die for our sins. We are united with Him in His death and in His life. “He died for all, that those who live should no longer live for themselves but for him who died for them” (2 Corinthians 5:15). To suggest that at some point before Christ died to sin He was responsive to temptation is totally unacceptable.

What else does the Bible say about death? We see that it links sin and death together. For instance, God warned Adam that if he ate the forbidden fruit he would die (Genesis 2:17). The penalty of sin is death. Paul says that those who persist in living contrary to the known will of God “deserve death” (Romans 1:32). He also says death entered the world through sin (5:12) and “the wages of sin is death” (6:23). Stott comments, “Death is represented in Scripture more in legal than in physical terms; not so much a state of lying motionless but as the grim though just penalty for sin” (1994, 171). He concludes that the statement “we died to sin” refers to our justification; that is, death to the guilt of sin. In other words, it is totally inconsistent for a person who is justified—set free from the guilt and power of sin—to continue to live in sin.

Paul’s Explanation of Sanctification

Paul’s explanation of sanctification grows out of his response to those who object to the idea of salvation by grace through faith. He offers three practical steps to victory over sin that can be identified by the words know, count, and offer.

Know

A clear understanding of God’s provision for us in Christ is essential for victory, since faith begins with the known will of God. We were formerly identified with Adam in condemnation and death. But now we are identified with Christ in justification and life (Romans 5:12–21). Paul reminds us of three things that we should know concerning our identification with Christ (6:3, 6, 9).

First of all, Paul asks, “Don’t you know that all of us who were baptized into Christ Jesus were baptized into his death?” (Romans 6:3). The word baptize (Greek, baptizo ) literally means “to immerse.” It is sometimes used in a figurative sense, as in 1 Corinthians 10:2: “They were all baptized into Moses in the cloud and in the sea.” Here baptism depicts the Israelites’ identification with Moses as well as their submission to his leadership. Another example is 1 Corinthians 12:13: “For we were all baptized by one Spirit into one body.” This describes the work of the Spirit at conversion in uniting the believer with Christ and other believers.

Paul uses the concept of water baptism in this passage to illustrate what takes place when a believer is born again. While several modes of baptism are practiced today, most historians agree that the early church practiced immersion ; therefore, the Romans clearly understood the symbolism. Baptism by immersion pictures the believer’s identification with Christ in His death, burial, and resurrection (Romans 6:4–5). Fundamentally, it is an act of obedience to Christ that follows repentance and faith.

Union with Christ not only changes our relationship to sin but also introduces us to a new life. “Just as Christ was raised from the dead . . . we too may live a new life” (Romans 6:4). “United with him” in verse 5 further clarifies the nature of this union. Botanists use this term union to describe the grafting together of two branches (Greek, symphytoi , meaning “grown together”). Our union with Christ is likened to a grafted branch in which the life-giving sap of the tree flows. Paul personalized this intimate relationship in this way: “I have been crucified with Christ and I no longer live, but Christ lives in me. The life I live in the body, I live by faith in the Son of God, who loved me and gave himself for me” (Galatians 2:20).

Christ imparts His nature to those who are joined together with Him. The apostle John wrote, “No one who is born of God will continue to sin, because God’s seed remains in him” (1 John 3:9; see vv. 4–10). God’s seed in this passage carries the idea of human reproduction. Just as a child reflects the likeness of his or her parents, so a believer should reflect the likeness of Christ.

Now Paul cites a second fact about our union with Christ: “For we know that our old self was crucified with him so that the body of sin might be done away with, that we should no longer be slaves to sin” (Romans 6:6). We need to define several terms here. Our old self (literally, old man ) is our unregenerate self before we trusted Christ as Savior. The body of sin is a reference not to our physical body but to the sinful nature we inherited from Adam. Done away with ( destroyed , NRSV) does not mean “annihilated” or “ceasing to exist” but “rendered powerless” (NIV Study Bible 1985, 1714).

Our old self, then, was put to death with Christ so that our sinful nature is rendered powerless. This means that “we should no longer be slaves to sin” (Romans 6:6). Sin’s control is broken. Union with Christ frees us from the penalty and power of sin! (See also 2 Corinthians 5:17; Ephesians 4:22–24; Colossians 3:9–10.)

The third fact about our union with Christ is this: “For we know that since Christ was raised from the dead, he cannot die again; death no longer has mastery over him” (Romans 6:9). Nor can death dominate the believer any longer.

Count

With an understanding of God’s provision for us in Christ, let us take the next step to victory. It comes in the form of an exhortation—the first one in the entire Epistle. Paul exhorts us, as believers, to do something, to apply what we know. He says, “In the same way, count yourselves dead to sin but alive to God in Christ Jesus” (Romans 6:11). The word count (Greek, logizomai ) is a bookkeeping term. It does not refer to something imagined or unreal but describes or acknowledges what is real or true. You may recall that Paul uses this word eleven times in chapter 4 to describe God’s act of crediting Abraham and all believers as righteous.

Paul’s exhortation to count is based on two facts he discussed earlier: (1) we are “dead to sin,” and (2) we are “alive to God in Christ Jesus” (6:11). Counting is a step we take by faith based on these facts. Since faith begins where the will of God is known, Paul reminds us of what we know—God’s provision in Christ. By faith we put our position in Christ into practice in our daily lives. (See the chart below.) Read Colossians 3:1–10 for additional insight into this truth.

While God counts or reckons our justification, we are to count or reckon our sanctification. That is, by faith we act on what God says is so. Just as Abraham believed God’s promise, we believe what God says we are because of our union with Christ (Romans 6:5).

Offer

When you “offer yourselves to God,” you have taken the third step to victory over sin. Note the negative as well as the positive side: “do not offer” as contrasted to “but rather offer” in Romans 6:13:

Do not offer the parts of your body to sin, as instruments of wickedness, but rather offer yourselves to God, as those who have been brought from death to life; and offer the parts of your body to him as instruments of righteousness.

We choose to reject sinful desires and temptation and instead yield ourselves to God. Both our will and our faith enter into our response. Like a bicycle’s handlebars or a car’s steering wheel, our will controls the direction we go. We must decide to turn away from sin and turn toward God. This is a deliberate decision on our part. We decide not to offer the parts of our body to sin. At the same time, we decide to offer ourselves to God. We do both. When we do this, our faith and resolve are strengthened, because we know that our old self was put to death with Christ so that our sinful nature is rendered powerless (Romans 6:6). We also know that we should no longer be dominated by sin. Thus, we “do not let sin reign” (6:12). Rather, we follow Paul’s advice to the Galatian believers: “So I say, live by the Spirit, and you will not gratify the desires of the sinful nature” (Galatians 5:16).

Instruments of wickedness, . . . instruments of righteousness (Romans 6:13). The parts of your body can be used for wickedness or for righteousness (upright conduct). They may include one’s mind, eyes, ears, lips, hands, feet, or other parts. Since sin no longer has dominion over us, we resist evil and yield all of our capacities to God.

Where do we begin? Paul tells us to begin with our mind, even though we like to think of it as separate from our body. Note the parallel ideas in Romans 6 and 12: “Therefore . . . offer yourselves [the parts of your bodies] to God” (6:12–13). “Therefore, I urge you . . . to offer your bodies as living sacrifices. . . . Do not conform any longer to the pattern of this world, but be transformed by the renewing of your mind” (12:1–2).

Our mind influences all the activities of our body, so we begin—and continue—the sanctifying process with the renewing of our mind. Allow God to renew your mind by cultivating the habits of reading and meditating on His Word. Seek to understand His will and ways so that you align your thoughts and ways with His. Give this your top priority. “Let the word of Christ dwell in you richly” (Colossians 3:16). Your faith hinges on the degree to which you hide His Word in your heart. There is also a close relationship between the Word and the Spirit.

The word instruments (Romans 6:13) can also be translated as tools , implements , or weapons . As we offer ourselves to God, we might say that our bodies become God’s weapons in waging spiritual warfare, God’s tools in building His kingdom, or God’s implements in harvesting His fields. Our bodies are also God’s temple where He dwells (1 Corinthians 6:19).

**Q:** Evaluate this statement and write a response to it: “‘We died to sin’ means that a true believer is dead to the influence of sin and therefore cannot sin.”

**A:** Your answer might be similar to this: If a true believer is dead to the influence of sin, Paul’s counsel to the Romans is meaningless, as in the following examples: “Do not let sin reign in your mortal body” (6:12); “do not offer the parts of your body to sin” (6:13); “do not think about how to gratify the desires of the sinful nature” (13:14).

**Q:** Briefly summarize in one sentence each of the three facts Paul declares that believers know .

**A:** · Those who are baptized into Christ Jesus are baptized into His death. · We are no longer slaves to sin. Union with Christ frees us from the penalty and power of sin. · Death no longer has mastery over those who are united with Christ.

**Q:** To illustrate Paul’s teaching in Romans 6:12, read 2 Samuel 11 and state what parts of his body David yielded to sin as revealed in the following verses: Samuel 11:2, 2 Samuel 11:4, 2 Samuel 11:14.

**A:** 2 Samuel 11:2 His eyes and mind. David looked on his neighbor’s wife; his later actions show that he then devised an evil plan. 2 Samuel 11:4 His lips and mouth. He sent messengers to get her. 2 Samuel 11:14 His hands and fingers. He wrote a letter that resulted in Uriah’s death.

**Q:** Explain three steps to sanctification based on Romans 6:1–14.

**A:** First, know what God has provided in Christ. We are identified with Him in justification and life. Second, count yourselves dead to sin but alive to God in Christ Jesus. We put our position in Christ in practice in our daily lives. Third, offer yourselves to God and your bodies to Him as instruments of righteousness.

**Q:** Evaluate the role of these steps in helping the believer attain victory over sin.

**A:** If we put these things into practice and allow the Holy Spirit to work in our lives, we will have victory over sin.

When we do this, our faith and resolve are strengthened, because we know that our old self was put to death with Christ so that our sinful nature is rendered powerless (Romans 6:6). We also know that we should no longer be dominated by sin. Thus, we “do not let sin reign” (6:12). Rather, we follow Paul’s advice to the Galatian believers: “So I say, live by the Spirit, and you will not gratify the desires of the sinful nature” (Galatians 5:16).

Instruments of wickedness, . . . instruments of righteousness (Romans 6:13). The parts of your body can be used for wickedness or for righteousness (upright conduct). They may include one’s mind, eyes, ears, lips, hands, feet, or other parts. Since sin no longer has dominion over us, we resist evil and yield all of our capacities to God.

Where do we begin? Paul tells us to begin with our mind, even though we like to think of it as separate from our body. Note the parallel ideas in Romans 6 and 12: “Therefore . . . offer yourselves [the parts of your bodies] to God” (6:12–13). “Therefore, I urge you . . . to offer your bodies as living sacrifices. . . . Do not conform any longer to the pattern of this world, but be transformed by the renewing of your mind” (12:1–2).

Our mind influences all the activities of our body, so we begin—and continue—the sanctifying process with the renewing of our mind. Allow God to renew your mind by cultivating the habits of reading and meditating on His Word. Seek to understand His will and ways so that you align your thoughts and ways with His. Give this your top priority. “Let the word of Christ dwell in you richly” (Colossians 3:16). Your faith hinges on the degree to which you hide His Word in your heart. There is also a close relationship between the Word and the Spirit.

The word instruments (Romans 6:13) can also be translated as tools , implements , or weapons . As we offer ourselves to God, we might say that our bodies become God’s weapons in waging spiritual warfare, God’s tools in building His kingdom, or God’s implements in harvesting His fields. Our bodies are also God’s temple where He dwells (1 Corinthians 6:19).

**Q:** Evaluate this statement and write a response to it: “‘We died to sin’ means that a true believer is dead to the influence of sin and therefore cannot sin.”

**A:** Your answer might be similar to this: If a true believer is dead to the influence of sin, Paul’s counsel to the Romans is meaningless, as in the following examples: “Do not let sin reign in your mortal body” (6:12); “do not offer the parts of your body to sin” (6:13); “do not think about how to gratify the desires of the sinful nature” (13:14).

**Q:** Briefly summarize in one sentence each of the three facts Paul declares that believers know .

**A:** · Those who are baptized into Christ Jesus are baptized into His death. · We are no longer slaves to sin. Union with Christ frees us from the penalty and power of sin. · Death no longer has mastery over those who are united with Christ.

**Q:** To illustrate Paul’s teaching in Romans 6:12, read 2 Samuel 11 and state what parts of his body David yielded to sin as revealed in the following verses: Samuel 11:2, 2 Samuel 11:4, 2 Samuel 11:14.

**A:** 2 Samuel 11:2 His eyes and mind. David looked on his neighbor’s wife; his later actions show that he then devised an evil plan. 2 Samuel 11:4 His lips and mouth. He sent messengers to get her. 2 Samuel 11:14 His hands and fingers. He wrote a letter that resulted in Uriah’s death.

**Q:** Explain three steps to sanctification based on Romans 6:1–14.

**A:** First, know what God has provided in Christ. We are identified with Him in justification and life. Second, count yourselves dead to sin but alive to God in Christ Jesus. We put our position in Christ in practice in our daily lives. Third, offer yourselves to God and your bodies to Him as instruments of righteousness.

**Q:** Evaluate the role of these steps in helping the believer attain victory over sin.

**A:** If we put these things into practice and allow the Holy Spirit to work in our lives, we will have victory over sin.

### Freedom from the Law (Romans 6:15–7:6)

#### 4.2.1 — Explain the meaning of “freedom from the law” as revealed in Romans

Moral Restraint, Grace, and Law (6:15)

Does moral restraint cease when grace replaces Law? Paul poses this question in Romans 6:15: “What then? Shall we sin because we are not under law but under grace? By no means!” This objection is similar to the first (6:1). The Jews, established in their traditions, erroneously concluded that replacing law with grace removed moral restraint. Without the restraint of the Law, they believed, people would indulge in sin.

By no means! (Romans 6:15). Those who raise such a question understand neither law nor grace. The Law is incapable of producing righteousness. All it can do is condemn. Grace, on the other hand, does not open the door to sin; it empowers the believer to do what the Law cannot do—live in obedience to Christ! “Grace was given,” as Augustine said, “that the law might be fulfilled” (Cranfield, as cited in Bruce 1985, 154).

The Slavery Analogy (6:16–23)

Paul now introduces an analogy from slavery (Romans 6:16–23) when he says, “You are slaves to the one whom you obey” (6:16). The basic idea is that no one is his or her “own boss” in the sense of absolute freedom. Every human being is limited in some way by someone or something. Everyone serves a master. The question, then, is, “Who is your master?” Paul narrows the answer to two possibilities: “You are slaves to sin . . . or to obedience” (6:16). This contrast suggests that the nature of sin is disobedience (see 5:19).

“I put this in human terms” (Romans 6:19). Paul acknowledges that his analogy is imperfect. We can readily grasp the concept of slaves to sin . But when we say “slaves to God” (6:22), we may become uneasy—possibly because of its negative connotation. Some argue that the word slave (Greek, doulos ) is better translated “willing servant,” since a servant chooses to serve his or her master while a slave is subject to an owner and is not free to leave. However, compare what Paul says in 1 Corinthians 6:19–20 and 7:22–23: “You are not your own; you were bought at a price.” Keep in mind that Paul introduced himself to the Romans as “a servant”—or slave—“of Jesus Christ” (Romans 1:1). He even describes Christ as “taking the very nature of a servant . . . [and becoming] obedient to death—even death on a cross” (Philippians 2:7–8).

The Gospel writers never portray Jesus as acting independently of His Father but always in submission to Him. Likewise, Paul stresses his submission to Christ, exemplifying the believer’s position before God. A Christian rejects slavery to sin and offers himself / herself instead as a slave to obedience or a slave to God (Romans 6:16, 22). This decision triggers a series of benefits the believer will reap—a chain reaction or sequence of events called a progression (Hinckley 1987).

James Boice identifies five reasons Paul gives for not sinning: (1) Sin enslaves; (2) sin leads to death; (3) Christ delivers from slavery to sin; (4) a change in ownership leads to a change in authority; and (5) obedience to God leads to righteousness (1992, 2:691–695). Let us look at each of these reasons.

1. Sin enslaves and leads to “impurity and to ever-increasing wickedness” (Romans 6:19). “When you were slaves of sin, you weren’t concerned with doing what was right” (6:20, NLT). What was the end result? Slavery to sin caused you to do “things you are now ashamed of” (6:21).

2. Sin leads to death (Romans 6:16, 21, 23). Sin deceives by making wrongdoing appear attractive, often masquerading as freedom. The prodigal son left home with his inheritance, perhaps thinking he was free from all restraints and could do whatever he pleased. He squandered his wealth with wild living in a distant country (Luke 15:11–32). Eventually, his lifestyle stripped him of everything and left him humiliated, in bondage, and feeding pigs. Sin deceives, destroys, and eventually results in death. Paul is not referring to physical death here, however, because everyone dies. Instead, he is speaking about separation from God and about eternal punishment—direct consequences of sin.

3. Christ delivers from slavery to sin. Paul writes, “You used to be slaves to sin . . . You have been set free from sin” (Romans 6:17–18). Note the close relationship between faith and obedience. Faith in Christ leads to obedience: “You wholeheartedly obeyed the form of teaching to which you were entrusted” (6:17).

4. A change in ownership brings a change in authority. “But now . . . you have been set free from sin and have become slaves to God” (Romans 6:22). Our former master, sin, ceased its authority over us when we received Christ as our Savior and Lord. When Christ becomes our Master and we hold to His teaching, we experience true freedom (see John 8:31–32).

a What does sin do?

b What is the role of Christ in this analogy?

c What happens when there is a change in ownership?

d What does obedience to God do?

5. Obedience to God leads to righteousness (Romans 6:16, 18–19) and, in turn, holiness and eternal life (6:19–22). The grace of God working in the believer leads to holiness and fulfills this demand of the Law. (Read Leviticus 11:44–45; 19:2; 20:7 with Romans 6:19–20; Hebrews 12:14; 1 Peter 1:15–16.) Contrary to the charge of Paul’s opponents (Romans 6:1, 15), the gospel does not lead to sin but to righteousness.

Various Meanings of the Law

Before we consider the marriage analogy introduced in Romans 7, we need to look at the term Law . Paul refers to law over seventy times in Romans, with various meanings.

Six Meanings of Law in Scripture

The Law God gave to Moses . The Torah, or Pentateuch (Romans 3:21), including the Ten Commandments (Romans 7:7).

The law in general (Romans 4:15; 5:13). Usage may suggest any code of law, but Paul probably refers to the Mosaic law.

The Old Testament as a whole , as in John 10:34; 15:25; and 1 Corinthians 14:21. Law (Romans 3:19) refers to quotes from the Psalms, Ecclesiastes, and Isaiah in Romans 3:10–19.

A principle . For example, the “law of gravity” (Romans 3:27; 7:21, 23, 25; 8:1–4). A controlling power (Romans 8:2).

Inner law of conscience (Romans 2:14–15).

Rules made and enforced by government (1 Corinthians 6:6).

In following Paul’s teaching, we understand that “a man is not justified by observing the law” (Galatians 2:15–16). “We are not under law but under grace” (Romans 6:15). “We have been released from the law” (7:6). Is Paul saying he opposes the Law? Were the Jews right in accusing him of teaching against it (Acts 21:28)? To this charge we answer, “No.” As we learned earlier, Paul is opposed not to the Law but to its improper use (see the following chart). In fact, he demonstrates a very high regard for the Law by developing his teaching around it and quoting extensively from it.

The Purpose of the Law

“The law is holy, and the commandment is holy, righteous and good.” (Romans 7:12)

Contrary to the accusations of the Jews (Acts 21:28), Paul did not teach against the Law. They misunderstood both Paul and the Law’s purpose because they elevated it to a means of salvation. Paul had made the same mistake—seeking a righteousness of his own that comes from the Law (Philippians 3:9)—until his encounter with the risen Christ.

While Paul says, “the law is good,” he also says, “clearly no one is justified before God by the law” (Galatians 3:11). Paul is not rejecting the Law, but rather its improper use.

The Law is holy because it reveals sin (Romans 7:7). A mirror reflects a dirty face but cannot clean it. A plumb line reveals a crooked wall but cannot straighten it. In a similar way, the Law shows us we are sinners but remains powerless to cleanse or save us.

Bruce (1985) cites four reasons for the giving of the Law, as summarized below:

1. The Law reveals God and His will. The distinction between right and wrong is not a matter of social standards; rather, it is rooted in God’s nature and reflected in the commandments. It is also reflected in the makeup of humans, who were created in God’s image. According to God’s commandments (Exodus 20), for example, it is right to honor God, honor the Sabbath, and honor our parents. It is wrong to murder, commit adultery, steal, testify falsely, and covet.

2. The Law provides for the health and preservation of the human race. Civil governments use these guidelines to protect their citizens, encourage good behavior, and punish bad behavior.

3. The Law brings sin to light and leads the sinner to repent and trust in God’s grace.

4. The Law supplies guidance for the believer’s life.

Bruce’s first point is important. The Law tells us what God is like, at least in part. God’s character stands behind the commandments (Exodus 20). For instance, God is a life-giving Person. Thus, underlying the precept, “You shall not murder,” is the Giver of Life. The integrity of God is behind not only the command, “You shall not give false testimony [tell a lie] against your neighbor,” since God speaks only truth, but also the command, “You shall not commit adultery,” because He is pure and untouched by evil.

The Marriage Analogy (7:1–6)

Romans 7 is well known but controversial. Scholars especially debate the interpretation of the latter part of the chapter. Is Paul describing a real or a theoretical conflict? Is his repeated use of personal pronouns an editorial device? Is the passage autobiographical—describing his personal struggle with sin? If so, is he speaking about his experience before or after his conversion? We will address these questions later in this chapter.

As we have seen in Romans 6, Paul talks about the believer’s relationship to sin, saying, “We died to sin” (6:2); that is, we are freed from it. He illustrates our freedom with an analogy from slavery. A change in ownership, he says, brings about a change in authority. In Romans 7, he talks about the believer’s relationship to the Law, saying, “You also died to the law” (7:4). To illustrate this, he introduces an analogy from marriage (7:1–3):

Do you not know, brothers—for I am speaking to men who know the law—that the law has authority over a man only as long as he lives? For example, by law a married woman is bound to her husband as long as he is alive, but if her husband dies, she is released from the law of marriage. So then, if she marries another man while her husband is still alive, she is called an adulteress. But if her husband dies, she is released from that law and is not an adulteress, even though she marries another man.

“The law has authority over a man only as long as he lives” (Romans 7:1). This principle is true of both Jewish and Roman law. Nevertheless, Paul undoubtedly has the Mosaic Law in mind since he discusses the believer’s relationship to it. He refers to the Law about thirty times throughout chapter 7. The main point of Paul’s marriage analogy is that death ends all legal obligations. Under a marriage contract, a husband and wife are legally bound to each other for life. When one partner dies, however, the other is released from his or her marriage vows and is free to remarry.

“You also died to the law” (Romans 7:4). Paul applies the preceding principle to the believer and his or her relationship to the Law. When we died with Christ, the Law ceased to have authority over us. We were released from the Law so that we might “belong to another, to him who was raised from the dead” (7:4), that is, Christ. Our union with Him enables us to “bear fruit to God” (7:4). Note the contrast: “bear fruit to God” (7:4) and “bore fruit for death” (7:5). When we were controlled by the sinful nature, we did things that we are now ashamed of—things that end in death (6:21). But now that we belong to Christ and are released from the Law, we bear fruit to God. Our new relationship leads to holiness (6:21) and good works (Ephesians 2:10).

“When we were controlled by the sinful nature” (Romans 7:5) points to our unregenerate past. While our “old self” (6:6) was dominated by sin and was under the Law, Christ breaks sin’s control over us, and we enter into a new relationship with Him.

“We have been released from the law” (Romans 7:6), that is, released from its power to condemn us. Since we are united with Christ, “we serve in the new way of the Spirit” (7:6). This new way contrasts with “the old way of the written code” (7:6), namely, living under the Law. Formerly, we were guided by a code written on stone tablets. Now the Spirit of the living God writes on the tablets of our hearts (see 2 Corinthians 3:1–3; Jeremiah 31:33). The Holy Spirit empowers us to “live a new life” (Romans 6:4), “so that we serve in the new way of the Spirit” (7:6). Paul expands on this idea in chapter 8.

The Law’s Relation to Sin (7:7–13)

“What shall we say, then? Is the law sin? Certainly not!” (Romans 7:7). This verse introduces a third objection to Paul’s teaching. Let us review these objections:

The first objection dealt with a misunderstanding of what Paul said as expressed in Romans 5:20–21. His response to that was, “Shall we go on sinning so that grace may increase? By no means!” (Romans 6:1–2). Does the gospel encourage sinful living?

The second concern was, “Shall we sin because we are not under law but under grace? By no means!” (Romans 6:15). Is the restraint to sin removed since we are now under grace?

This present passage deals with another misconception. If the Law makes us conscious of sin (Romans 3:20), increases sin (5:20), and arouses sinful passions (7:5), does this mean it is evil? Otherwise, why would Paul say that we are “released from the law” (7:6)? Again, Paul responds to the question, “Is the law sin?” with an emphatic “Certainly not!” (7:7).

Paul raises the question because his readers might misunderstand his teaching. Then he defends the Law, showing that it is “holy, righteous and good” (Romans 7:12). Stott explains: “Our real problem is not the law, but sin. It is indwelling sin which accounts for the weakness of the law. The law cannot save us because we cannot keep it, and we cannot keep it because of indwelling sin” (1994, 204–205). Hendriksen (1981) compares the Law to a magnifying glass. Looking at dirty spots through the lens does not increase the size of the spots; it simply makes them stand out more clearly. In the same way, the Law identifies sin, enabling us to see our need of the Savior.

Paul explains, “Indeed I would not have known what sin was except through the law. For I would not have known what it was to covet if the law had not said, ‘Do not covet’” (Romans 7:7). Paul uses the tenth commandment, “You shall not covet” (Exodus 20:17), to illustrate how the Law reveals sin. Covetousness is difficult to identify because it is an attitude of the heart. The gospel account of Jesus and the rich young man illustrates this (Matthew 19:16–30). As the story shows, this young ruler was not guilty of adultery, murder, theft, false witness, or dishonoring his parents. Like Paul he considered himself “faultless” concerning “legalistic righteousness” (Philippians 3:6). But Jesus identified the man’s problem: “Sell your possessions and give to the poor” (Matthew 19:21). Covetousness was preventing him from entering into eternal life.

While covetousness is less apparent than other transgressions, it is just as deadly. Honoring God goes beyond external observance. Jesus spoke against those who kept the Law outwardly but broke it inwardly. For example, see His teaching on murder and adultery in Matthew 5:21–30.

“But sin, seizing the opportunity afforded by the commandment, produced in me every kind of covetous desire” (Romans 7:8). The Law stimulates sin in the sense that humanity’s natural tendency is to want what is forbidden. The word opportunity suggests a military strategy. Sin operates like an army establishing an advanced position in order to launch an attack into enemy territory. Sin uses the covetous desire produced by the Law to lure people into an unlawful act. Law points to the wrong but provides no means of resistance.

**Q:** Trace the progression of key benefits to those who choose to be slaves to obedience [that is, to God] according to Romans 6:16–22.

**A:** 6:16: Obedience leads to righteousness. 6:19: This in turn leads to holiness. 6:22: And this leads to eternal life.

**Q:** According to Romans 6:16–23, what is the end result of those who choose to be slaves to sin?

**A:** Death

**Q:** Summarize Paul’s slavery analogy by briefly answering these questions. (If possible, do this from memory.)

**A:** a What does sin do? It enslaves and becomes increasingly worse, leading to death. b What is the role of Christ in this analogy? He delivers us from slavery to sin. Our faith in Him leads us to obedience. c What happens when there is a change in ownership? Sin loses its authority over us and Christ becomes our Master. d What does obedience to God do? It leads to righteousness, holiness, and eternal life.

**Q:** Based on Romans 3 and 7, list what the Law does and what the Law does not do.

**A:** The Law does makes one conscious of sin (3:20), reveals sin (7:7), arouses sin (7:8–9), and shows how terrible sin is (7:12–13). The Law does not provide justification (3:20, 27–28); it does not enable one to do good (7:15–21) and does not set one free (7:21–24).

**Q:** Briefly refute this statement: The gospel of grace encourages sinful living.

**A:** Grace does not open the door to sin; rather, it empowers the believer to live in obedience to Christ.

### Freedom Without Self-Effort (Romans 7:14–25)

#### 4.3.1 — Evaluate ideas concerning the identity of the “man” in Romans 7:14–25 and what we learn from this passage concerning self-effort.

While we are painfully aware of the Law’s ability to reveal sin, we need to be equally aware of its inability to change our condition. It is somewhat like a thermometer that measures a patient’s temperature but is powerless to remove the fever. According to Edwards (1992), the function of the Law is diagnostic, not therapeutic.

The Struggle Within

“The law is spiritual” (Romans 7:14) as well as “holy, righteous and good” (7:12) because it originates with God. As we have seen, it was designed to reveal and condemn sin. At the same time, it is powerless to remove sin.

“But I am unspiritual” (Romans 7:14), that is, “a creature of the flesh” (Amplified), subject to the “gravitational pull of Adam” (Edwards 1992, 190). The word unspiritual (Greek, sarkinos ), or carnal (KJV) means “not fully spiritual; not fully sanctified” (Hinckley 1987, 111). Paul contrasts himself with the Law: unspiritual versus spiritual ; that is, the Law is spiritual and perfect while he is unspiritual and imperfect. Paul is not contrasting his spiritual state prior to and following his conversion, nor is he saying, “I am controlled by the sinful nature” (7:5; 8:8). He simply acknowledges that he is still in the process of growing in Christlikeness. “I have not yet reached my goal, and I am not perfect. But Christ has taken hold of me. So I keep on running and struggling to take hold of the prize” (Philippians 3:12, CEV).

The believer lives in two worlds—the natural and the spiritual. They exert their influence on him and produce conflict.

We live in two worlds—the natural and the spiritual. Our roots go back to Adam and his fallen nature. At the same time, we are made partakers of the divine nature through our union with Christ. The sinful nature and the Spirit are in conflict. They clash and oppose each other (Galatians 5:16–18). These two natures, as well as these two worlds, exert their influence on us and produce an inner conflict.

You might be saying, “But I thought my old self was crucified with Christ.” Yes, Paul did say this (Romans 6:6), but he also adds, “so that the body of sin might be rendered powerless.” The sinful nature we inherited from Adam is done away with or “rendered powerless.” Obviously, Paul does not mean that this nature ceases to exist at conversion. Otherwise, he would not say, “Do not let sin reign in your mortal body . . . Do not offer the parts of your body to sin” (6:12–13).

It is essential to understand God’s provision and then apply it by faith. The remainder of Romans 6 and the next chapter, Romans 7, make it clear that we are unable to do this in our own strength. We look to Christ and the Holy Spirit to change His provision into reality in our lives. Sanctification describes this ongoing process of change.

The “Man” in Romans 7

“What a wretched man I am!” Paul writes (Romans 7:24). Bible scholars have wrestled for centuries with the identity of the “man” in Romans 7, and their conclusions differ sharply. Is Paul describing his own pre-conversion experience? Or is he describing a believer’s ongoing struggle against sin? Edwards observes: “At no point in Romans do the fevers of interpreters reach a higher pitch. Solid arguments have been advanced . . . for each view, and it is quite impossible to cinch the argument either way” (1992, 184).

Should we avoid this passage, then, since it is so controversial? Or should we seek to identify some of the issues interpreters wrestle with? Since it deals with Christian life, it is important. Boice gives a helpful summary of the major interpretations. We will limit our study to three views and the general arguments for each: This man is (1) unsaved, (2) an immature believer, or (3) a mature believer.

View 1: This Man Is Unsaved

Advocates of this view believe Paul was describing himself before his conversion. They do not believe that Paul as a believer could say these things. For instance, he says, “I am unspiritual, sold as a slave to sin” (Romans 7:14). They couple this verse with his prior statement in Romans 6:17–18: “But thanks be to God that, though you used to be slaves to sin, you wholeheartedly obeyed the form of teaching to which you were entrusted. You have been set free from sin and have become slaves to righteousness.” How, they ask, can Paul tell Roman believers they have been freed from sin (chapter 6) and then describe himself as “a slave to sin” (7:14)? “Doesn’t he practice what he preaches?” they ask.

These advocates also cite statements such as “I know that nothing good lives in me . . . I have the desire to do what is good, but I cannot carry it out” (Romans 7:18), and “What a wretched man I am!” (7:24). Looking at all of these statements, they ask a valid question: “What is the value of conversion if you end up frustrated and miserable?”

Edwards challenges this view based on the position of Romans 7 in the overall structure of the Epistle. In this structure Paul proclaims the gospel (Romans 1:16–17), demonstrates the guilt of all humankind (1:18–3:20), explains the gospel (3:21–31), defines faith (chapter 4), and then moves to the life of faith (chapters 5–8). Edwards concludes, “If chapter 7 is a preconversion reflection, then it is a digression from an otherwise consistent and purposeful development of the epistle, thus far” (1992, 185).

Opponents of this view also argue that before his conversion Paul saw himself as “faultless” with respect to “legalistic righteousness” (Philippians 3:6). Bruce explains, “When Paul himself lived under the law, before the Damascus road event, he does not seem to have been aware of any such inward tension as is here described [7:14–25]” (1985, 143). Paul would have said, in effect, “I desire to do what is good and I am doing it” (Boice 1992, 2:757). Furthermore, it can be shown that self-effort to live the Christian life leads to frustration and misery.

View 2: This Man Is an Immature Believer

According to this view, Paul is an immature believer who has not yet fully surrendered his life to Christ. He experiences frustration and defeat because he centers his attention on himself and his abilities. For instance, he uses I, me, my, or myself thirty-eight times in these eleven verses. In contrast, the Holy Spirit—the source of the believer’s victory—is not mentioned once. Supporters of this view also point to Paul’s unfavorable comments about himself in Romans 7:14–15, 18–19, 21, and 23–24. Is he saying that he is a mere infant in Christ? (See 1 Corinthians 3:1.)

Opponents of this position argue that the more individuals mature in their walk with the Lord, the more concerned they become with sin. Edwards writes:

People who cave in to sin know very little about it; they normally think themselves as good or better than other people. One discovers the force of the current not by floating with it but by rowing against it. So it is with sin. It is saints, after all, not gangsters, who teach us the meaning of sin. The “flesh” does not roll over dead at conversion; neither does it die easily thereafter. When threatened it fights for its life. (1992, 191)

Godly, upright men like Job, Daniel, and Isaiah also spoke unfavorably of themselves. Job said, “I despise myself and repent in dust and ashes” (Job 42:6; see also Ezekiel 14:14; Job 1:1, 8). Daniel confessed, “We have sinned and done wrong. We are covered with shame” (Daniel 9:4–8). Isaiah lamented , “Woe to me! I am ruined! For I am a man of unclean lips” (Isaiah 6:5). These examples give evidence that the “man” described in Romans 7:14–25 is not necessarily an immature believer.

Consider this illustration: When one is driving toward the sun, especially in the early morning or late afternoon, every speck of dirt on a car’s windshield is clearly visible. However, these same specks become unnoticeable when one is driving away from the sun. In a similar way, a person with his or her back to God sees him- or herself differently than the person who looks intently toward Him. Our position before God influences how we see ourselves.

View 3: This Man Is a Mature Believer

Those favoring this view note that Paul writes in the present tense and uses the first person singular. According to Hughes, “it seems most natural to understand this section as Paul talking about what he was then experiencing” (1991, 142). Paul hates sin (Romans 7:15); wishes to do what is good (7:18–19, 21); delights in God’s law in his inner being (7:22); feels pain and disappointment for his sins (7:15, 18–24); and thanks God for his deliverance (7:25). “Can this language describe an unsaved man?” ask advocates of this view.

Supporters of this position insist that this passage is in harmony with Paul’s overall development of Romans. “If chapter 7 is descriptive of the believer’s experience, it is entirely consonant with the development of the epistle” (Edwards 1992, 185). Bruce says, “This unequal struggle against ‘the law of sin which dwells in my members’ (as Paul calls it) has been the real experience of too many Christians for the confident assumption to be made that Paul cannot be speaking auto-biographically here, that his ‘I’ is purely dramatic” (1985, 143).

Objections arise quickly. “Didn’t Paul say, ‘I am unspiritual, sold as a slave to sin’” (Romans 7:14)? This is true. Compared to the Law, he is imperfect. Even the most mature believer admits this. However, this phrase is “so strong that many refuse to accept it as descriptive of a Christian. However, it may graphically point out the failure even of Christians to meet the radical, ethical, and moral demands of the gospel. It also points up the persistent nature of sin” (NIV Study Bible 1985, 1716).

This third view, Boice acknowledges, is the most difficult to accept; yet he insists it is the correct one. He argues that we will get nowhere in our Christian walk by assuming that this chapter is about someone other than ourselves: “I repeat again that this describes the conflict of a mature Christian man, in fact, the conflict of an apostle of Jesus Christ in his later years. So the struggle Paul speaks of is a struggle we all face and will continue to face—if we are Christians. And the defeat he speaks of is the experience of all—even when we are well along in the Christian life—apart from the Holy Spirit” (1992, 2:765).

Paul obviously faced struggles that arose from preaching the gospel and struggles because of revelations he received (2 Corinthians 11:22–33; 12:1–7). Did his commanding nature introduce other kinds of struggles? Was he naturally meek and gentle, or did he need to tame his tongue (2 Corinthians 10:1)? Did he prematurely judge situations? Did he resent those who encroached on his apostolic service? He likened the Christian life to running a race and severely disciplined himself like an athlete so that he would not be disqualified (1 Corinthians 9:24–27). Victory may not come in a moment, but it does come. Bruce observes, “The present passage leads up to a paean of triumph, although it begins with a sad confession of inability. The inability persists only so long as I fight the battle in my own strength” (1985, 144–145).

“What a wretched man I am!” (Romans 7:24). The word wretched describes “a miserable distressed condition” (Hughes 1991, 144). Wiersbe (1977) says it indicates an exhausted person after a battle. He has exhausted his resources in seeking to please God and feels frustrated in realizing that his best efforts are not good enough. “ Legalism makes a believer wretched because it grieves the new nature and aggravates the old nature! The legalist becomes a Pharisee whose outward actions are acceptable, but whose inward attitudes are despicable” (82–83).

“Who will rescue me?” Paul cries (Romans 7:24). Then he answers his own question: “Thanks be to God—through Jesus Christ our Lord!” (7:25).

It is important to understand what Paul does not say in this passage. He is not saying that a Christian cannot live in victory. Nor is he saying this frustrating process is a necessary step to victory. Rather, he says that a Christian by his or her own effort cannot live in victory. This is an important distinction. Jesus Christ and the indwelling Spirit cause us to triumph.

W. H. Griffith Thomas believes the “man” in Romans 7 is an unregenerate Jew. Nevertheless, he makes this application: “And yet there is a sense in which the wretchedness here described is applicable to a believer who is struggling to make himself holy by his own efforts . . . Many who have come to Christ for justification try to obtain sanctification by themselves as though Christianity meant Justification by Faith, and Sanctification by Struggle and Fighting” (1974, 199).

In earlier lessons, we learned that we are made right in God’s sight when we put our trust in Jesus Christ, apart from Law observance. Now, in this lesson we have discovered a parallel idea. Sanctification, like justification, is unattainable by Law observance. Rather, God accomplishes sanctification in us through our union with His Son and the work of the Holy Spirit; that is, as we “keep in step with the Spirit” (Galatians 5:25). We will learn more about this in Chapter 5—the final chapter in this unit of study—as we look at Romans 8 and the subject of the empowering Spirit.

**Q:** Evaluate each of these three views; then consider your own spiritual experience. Based on your own views, discuss your impression of the spiritual condition of the “wretched man” in Romans 7.

**A:** Your answer. It is good to reflect on these things. Probably most of us, at one time or another in our Christian experience, have had the same feelings Paul expressed in Romans 7. Even mature believers encounter temptations in life, and in their own power, they struggle against these tests. Yet knowing that God is with us and has provided us with the enablement to overcome them is of great comfort and encouragement. 5 CHAPTER

## 5 - The Empowering Spirit: Romans 8

### Life in the Spirit (Romans 8:1–17)

#### 5.1.1 — Compare Paul’s references to the Holy Spirit in Romans 8 with the remainder of Romans, and summarize the key benefits that characterize a believer’s life in the Spirit as described in Romans 8.

In Chapter 4, we considered God’s continuing work in the life of those who are justified by faith (see the chart below). Righteousness leads to holiness (Romans 6:19); that is, sanctification flows from justification. Both are essential—one cannot exist without the other. We cannot separate justification from sanctification nor faith from obedience and remain true to the gospel. “Only he who believes is obedient, and only he who is obedient believes” (Dietrich Bonhoeffer, as cited in Edwards 1992, 166).

A Summary

(Adapted from Wiersbe 1977, 61)

Romans 3:21–5:21

Substitution

He died for me.

He died for my sins.

He paid sin’s penalty.

Romans 6–8

Identification

I died with Him.

He died unto sin.

He broke sin’s power.

Justification

Righteousness imputed

(put to my account)

Saved by His death

Sanctification

Righteousness imputed

(made a part of my life)

Saved by His life

Does justification by grace through faith encourage sinful living, as opponents claim? Paul strenuously objects to such a conclusion because believers are united with Christ and receive a new nature. He uses the figures of grafting, baptism, slavery , and marriage to illustrate the believers’ relationship to Christ as well as their freedom from sin and the Law. Then he dramatizes his own struggle to show that legalistic efforts to live for Christ are wearisome and do not produce maturity.

Do you recall Paul’s question to the Galatians who were being influenced by the Judaizers? “After starting your Christian lives in the Spirit, why are you now trying to become perfect by your own human effort?” (Galatians 3:3, NLT). We cannot perfect ourselves any more than we can save ourselves. This is a work of the Holy Spirit, who gives freedom and empowers us to do what we cannot do in ourselves.

In Romans 7, Paul showed us the weakness of the Law. Now, in chapter 8, he shows us the power of the Spirit. As Philip Comfort comments, “We must be aware of our need for the Holy Spirit before we are ready and willing to appropriate his help” (1992, 141).

Romans 8 opens with no condemnation (8:1) and closes with no separation (8:35). Commentators like to add, “In between there is no defeat.”

In this chapter, we will consider three aspects of a Christian’s life in the Spirit. Then we will compare the future glory God has prepared for His people with their present suffering. Finally, we will see how Paul demonstrates God’s faithfulness to His people.

Context of Paul’s References to the Holy Spirit

Life in the Spirit is described by Stott as essentially “a life which is animated, sustained, directed, and enriched by the Holy Spirit. Without the Holy Spirit true Christian discipleship would be inconceivable, indeed impossible” (1994, 216). The term life in the Spirit means that (1) we are free from condemnation, (2) we are free from the control of the sinful nature, and (3) we are God’s children. God has adopted us into His family and made us His sons and daughters. However, before we consider these benefits of our union with Christ and the indwelling Spirit, let us note Paul’s references to the Spirit in Romans 8 and elsewhere in Romans.

We learn these things about the Holy Spirit in Romans 8:

Romans 8:2

8:4–5

8:9

8:11

8:14

8:16

8:26–27

The Spirit sets us free from the law of sin and death.

He enables us to live a life pleasing to God.

He lives in us.

He gives life to our mortal bodies.

He leads us.

He testifies with our spirit that we are God’s children.

He intercedes for us.

Paul’s frequent mention of the Holy Spirit in chapter 8 catches our attention. But we should not overlook other references to the Spirit in Romans. For instance:

Romans 1:4

5:5

7:6

9:1

14:17

15:13

15:16

15:19

The Spirit raised Jesus Christ from the dead.

The Spirit pours out God’s love into our hearts.

We serve in the new way of the Spirit.

Paul testifies that the Spirit enlightened his conscience so he was able to speak the truth.

The Spirit supplies righteousness, joy, and peace.

He gives overflowing hope.

He works inwardly by sanctifying believers.

He works outwardly through believers with signs and miracles.

Freedom From Condemnation (8:1–4)

“Therefore, there is now no condemnation for those who are in Christ Jesus” (Romans 8:1). The word therefore sums up Paul’s argument to this point. Through Adam’s disobedience, sin entered the world and brought “condemnation for all [people]” (5:18). Through Christ, our condemnation is canceled. “God’s abundant provision of grace and of the gift of righteousness” is available to all who put their confidence in Him (see Romans 5:17; Philippians 2:8). In Adam we were condemned, but in Christ we are freed from condemnation.

Devout Jews such as Simeon had eagerly anticipated the coming of the promised Messiah. The Holy Spirit had revealed to him that he would not die before he had seen the Lord’s Christ (Luke 2:25–32). In the Temple, as he held the child Jesus in his arms, he praised God, saying, “My eyes have seen your salvation” (2:30). Salvation is no longer something to look forward to but is available now . Anticipation has become realization, as Paul explained: “But now a righteousness from God, apart from law, has been made known” (Romans 3:21). “Now is the time of God’s favor, now is the day of salvation” (2 Corinthians 6:2).

It is important to understand that salvation is more than a doctrine. It is realized in the person of Jesus Christ. Before his conversion, Paul had studied the Scriptures and understood the concept of salvation. But it was not until his encounter with the risen Christ that salvation became a reality in his life.

“There is now no condemnation” (Romans 8:1) is the negative counterpart of Paul’s positive declaration in 5:1: “Therefore, since we have been justified through faith, we have peace with God” (Stott 1994, 217). Condemnation (Greek, katakrima ) “is a forensic term which here includes both the sentence and the execution of the sentence” (Leon Morris, as cited in Boice 1992, 2:790). Since God condemned our sin in Christ (Romans 8:3), we are not under condemnation. “He canceled the record that contained the charges against us. He took it and destroyed it by nailing it to Christ’s cross” (Colossians 2:14, NLT).

Bruce believes that Paul is referring to “penal servitude”—the punishment following the sentence. He says, “There is no reason for those who are ‘in Christ Jesus’ to go on doing penal servitude as though they had never been pardoned, never been released from the prisonhouse of sin” (1985, 151). Seeing that we are “in Christ Jesus,” we testify: “My guilt is forgiven and my punishment has been canceled. I am free from the bondage of sin and the fear of future judgment.”

Two Reasons for no Condemnation

Paul gives two reasons why the believer is not under condemnation. Both are introduced by the Greek word gar , translated as “because” in Romans 8:2 and “for” in verse 3.

Reason 1

First of all, there is no condemnation “because (Greek, gar ) through Christ Jesus the law of the Spirit of life set me free from the law of sin and death” (Romans 8:2). The words set me free stand out because this is the only time Paul uses the first person singular in the entire chapter. The Spirit liberated him . It is the Spirit that “makes Christ’s redemption efficacious” (Edwards 1992, 203).

What does Paul mean by law in “the law of the Spirit of life”? Is it simply “the other side” of the Law, as some interpreters insist? They reason that, in the old way, it produced death; in the new way, it produces life. Yet this is a difficult concept to understand or explain. Others interpret “the law of the Spirit of life” as the gospel. In discussing the various views, Stott says, “This makes the best sense. It is certainly the gospel which has freed us from the law and its curse” (1994, 218).

Still others suggest that law in this passage refers to “a principle” or “a controlling power” (NIV Study Bible 1985, 1716). According to this view, “the law of the Spirit of life” describes the controlling power of the Holy Spirit, while “the law of sin and death” applies to the controlling power of sin that leads to death.

Paul declares in Romans 7 that we have been released from the old way of the written code “so that we serve in the new way of the Spirit” (7:6). In 2 Corinthians 3:7–18, he compares the old and new covenants, depicting the Law as a veil that covers the heart and as a ministry that condemns people and brings death (3:7, 9, 15). In contrast, the ministry of the Spirit brings righteousness, removes the veil, and gives freedom (3:9, 16–17).

Reason 2

Romans 8:3–4 gives the second reason why we are not under condemnation:

For what the law was powerless to do in that it was weakened by the sinful nature, God did by sending his own Son in the likeness of sinful man to be a sin offering. And so he condemned sin in sinful man, in order that the righteous requirements of the law might be fully met in us, who do not live according to the sinful nature but according to the Spirit.

“For [Greek, gar ] what the law was powerless to do . . . God did” (Romans 8:3). The Law is capable of condemning us—revealing and stimulating sin and even showing us how wicked sin is. But it is powerless to set us free, change us, or enable us to do good because our flesh is weak. Therefore, God did what the Law was unable to do. The Almighty, who is unlimited in power, has justified us (5:1) and has taken away our guilt and condemnation (8:1).

How did God remove our guilt and justify us? He did this “by sending His own Son” to save us (Romans 8:3). What unfathomable love! “God so loved the world that he gave his one and only Son” (John 3:16). This kind of love is almost incomprehensible—as in the case of Abraham who, at Mount Moriah, did not withhold his only son whom he loved (Genesis 22). His extraordinary display of commitment to God foreshadowed Christ’s coming.

By sending His own Son (Romans 8:3) describes the Incarnation. Jesus Christ, God’s Son, became flesh and “made his dwelling among us” (John 1:14). Paul elaborates on this even more in Philippians 2:5–11, where he states that Jesus was “made in human likeness.” In addition, John says, “This is how you can recognize the Spirit of God: Every spirit that acknowledges that Jesus Christ has come in the flesh is from God, but every spirit that does not acknowledge Jesus is not from God” (1 John 4:2–3).

False teachers introduced in the early church a heresy called docetism , which taught that Jesus Christ only seemed to have a human body. The name of this false teaching comes from the Greek word dokeo , meaning “to seem or appear.” Cerinthus and his followers denied the humanity of Christ in a different way. These false teachers taught that the divine Christ united with the man Jesus at the Jordan when John baptized him. Then, they say, the divine Christ left Jesus before his crucifixion at Calvary.

Paul confirms in Romans 8:3 that Jesus Christ had a human body; that is, it was “in the likeness of sinful man” but was sinless. While He was fully human, the Bible makes it clear that Jesus committed no sin. He was “tempted in every way, just as we are—yet was without sin” (Hebrews 4:15). Consequently, He became an effective sin offering (Romans 8:3).

In verse 3, the term sin offering is a translation of the Greek peri hamartias that literally means “concerning sin.” It is sometimes translated “for sin” as in the KJV and RSV. In other words, God sent His Son for sin. This idea includes the sacrificial nature of Christ’s death. The Septuagint—the Greek version of the Jewish Scriptures—translates the Hebrew expression for “sin offering” (see Leviticus 4, for example) as peri hamartias . On the basis of Christ’s sin offering , God “condemned sin in sinful man” (Romans 8:3). This means, according to Stott, that “God judged our sins in the sinless humanity of his Son, who bore them in our place” (1994, 220). Or, as Paul says in 2 Corinthians 5:21: “God made him who had no sin to be sin [or a sin offering ] for us.” He did this “in order that the righteous requirements of the law might be fully met [literally, fulfilled ] in us” (Romans 8:4).

Is Paul inconsistent here, as some scholars claim? Can he talk about dying to the Law and yet fulfilling it (Romans 7:4; 8:4)? Can believers be released from the Law and at the same time be committed to it (7:6; 8:4)? How do we explain this apparent contradiction? First, consider Jeremiah’s prophecy about the new covenant: “I [the Lord] will put my law in their minds and write it on their hearts” (Jeremiah 31:33). Reflect, as well, on Ezekiel’s prophecy: “And I will put my Spirit in you and move you to follow my decrees and be careful to keep my laws” (Ezekiel 36:27). Here God clearly foretold what He would do. He would enable believers, by His indwelling Spirit, to fulfill the intent and requirements of the Law. Edwards explains, “Paul does not say that one must keep the law in order to be saved but that one must be saved in order to keep the law!” (1992, 203). Freedom from the Law does not mean freedom to disobey or disregard it, but freedom to fulfill it.

In addition to these Old Testament excerpts, let us consider two New Testament passages. The first is Philippians 2:13: “It is God who works in you to will and to act according to his good purpose.” God knows we cannot live the Christian life in our own strength, so He gives us His Spirit to empower us to live in a way that pleases Him. As we walk in submission to Him, we grow spiritually. That is, we leave behind old things and embrace new things. Old habits are abandoned for the new way of the Spirit.

The second passage is 2 Corinthians 3:18: “And as the Spirit of the Lord works within us, we become more and more like him and reflect his glory even more” (NLT). As the fruit of the Spirit becomes evident in our lives, Christ’s thoughts and attitudes increasingly influence the way we act. Gradually we become more like Christ.

The Godhead Provides for our Salvation

In addition to the work of Christ, the Holy Spirit plays a prominent role in our salvation. In fact, in Romans 8:1–4, Paul makes it clear that the entire Godhead—Father, Son, and Holy Spirit—provides for our salvation. “The Spirit of life” (Romans 8:2), that is, the Holy Spirit, was active in creation (Genesis 1:2; 2:7); continues His life-giving activity today in uniting believers to Christ and His body (John 3:3, 5; 1 Corinthians 12:13); and empowers them for service (Acts 1:8). The works of the Father, Son, and Spirit are interrelated, as the table below demonstrates.

The Trinity

Each member of the Godhead participates in providing for our salvation.

Romans 8:1–4

THE FATHER

gave His Son in order to save us, since the Law is powerless to overcome sin (8:3; 2 Cor. 5:21). Those who put their trust in Him receive His righteousness and sin’s control over them is broken. He also gives us His Spirit (8:4).

THE SON

came in human likeness—except He was without sin (8:3; John 1:14; Phil. 2:8; Heb. 4:15). He became a sin offering, turned aside God’s wrath, set us free from sin and death, and became for us wisdom from God—that is, our righteousness, holiness, and redemption (1 Cor. 1:30).

THE HOLY SPIRIT
