The words set me free stand out because this is the only time Paul uses the first person singular in the entire chapter. The Spirit liberated him . It is the Spirit that “makes Christ’s redemption efficacious” (Edwards 1992, 203).

What does Paul mean by law in “the law of the Spirit of life”? Is it simply “the other side” of the Law, as some interpreters insist? They reason that, in the old way, it produced death; in the new way, it produces life. Yet this is a difficult concept to understand or explain. Others interpret “the law of the Spirit of life” as the gospel. In discussing the various views, Stott says, “This makes the best sense. It is certainly the gospel which has freed us from the law and its curse” (1994, 218).

Still others suggest that law in this passage refers to “a principle” or “a controlling power” (NIV Study Bible 1985, 1716). According to this view, “the law of the Spirit of life” describes the controlling power of the Holy Spirit, while “the law of sin and death” applies to the controlling power of sin that leads to death.

Paul declares in Romans 7 that we have been released from the old way of the written code “so that we serve in the new way of the Spirit” (7:6). In 2 Corinthians 3:7–18, he compares the old and new covenants, depicting the Law as a veil that covers the heart and as a ministry that condemns people and brings death (3:7, 9, 15). In contrast, the ministry of the Spirit brings righteousness, removes the veil, and gives freedom (3:9, 16–17).

Reason 2

Romans 8:3–4 gives the second reason why we are not under condemnation:

For what the law was powerless to do in that it was weakened by the sinful nature, God did by sending his own Son in the likeness of sinful man to be a sin offering. And so he condemned sin in sinful man, in order that the righteous requirements of the law might be fully met in us, who do not live according to the sinful nature but according to the Spirit.

“For [Greek, gar ] what the law was powerless to do . . . God did” (Romans 8:3). The Law is capable of condemning us—revealing and stimulating sin and even showing us how wicked sin is. But it is powerless to set us free, change us, or enable us to do good because our flesh is weak. Therefore, God did what the Law was unable to do. The Almighty, who is unlimited in power, has justified us (5:1) and has taken away our guilt and condemnation (8:1).

How did God remove our guilt and justify us? He did this “by sending His own Son” to save us (Romans 8:3). What unfathomable love! “God so loved the world that he gave his one and only Son” (John 3:16). This kind of love is almost incomprehensible—as in the case of Abraham who, at Mount Moriah, did not withhold his only son whom he loved (Genesis 22). His extraordinary display of commitment to God foreshadowed Christ’s coming.

By sending His own Son (Romans 8:3) describes the Incarnation. Jesus Christ, God’s Son, became flesh and “made his dwelling among us” (John 1:14). Paul elaborates on this even more in Philippians 2:5–11, where he states that Jesus was “made in human likeness.” In addition, John says, “This is how you can recognize the Spirit of God: Every spirit that acknowledges that Jesus Christ has come in the flesh is from God, but every spirit that does not acknowledge Jesus is not from God” (1 John 4:2–3).

False teachers introduced in the early church a heresy called docetism , which taught that Jesus Christ only seemed to have a human body. The name of this false teaching comes from the Greek word dokeo , meaning “to seem or appear.” Cerinthus and his followers denied the humanity of Christ in a different way. These false teachers taught that the divine Christ united with the man Jesus at the Jordan when John baptized him. Then, they say, the divine Christ left Jesus before his crucifixion at Calvary.

Paul confirms in Romans 8:3 that Jesus Christ had a human body; that is, it was “in the likeness of sinful man” but was sinless. While He was fully human, the Bible makes it clear that Jesus committed no sin. He was “tempted in every way, just as we are—yet was without sin” (Hebrews 4:15). Consequently, He became an effective sin offering (Romans 8:3).

In verse 3, the term sin offering is a translation of the Greek peri hamartias that literally means “concerning sin.” It is sometimes translated “for sin” as in the KJV and RSV. In other words, God sent His Son for sin. This idea includes the sacrificial nature of Christ’s death. The Septuagint—the Greek version of the Jewish Scriptures—translates the Hebrew expression for “sin offering” (see Leviticus 4, for example) as peri hamartias . On the basis of Christ’s sin offering , God “condemned sin in sinful man” (Romans 8:3). This means, according to Stott, that “God judged our sins in the sinless humanity of his Son, who bore them in our place” (1994, 220). Or, as Paul says in 2 Corinthians 5:21: “God made him who had no sin to be sin [or a sin offering ] for us.” He did this “in order that the righteous requirements of the law might be fully met [literally, fulfilled ] in us” (Romans 8:4).

Is Paul inconsistent here, as some scholars claim? Can he talk about dying to the Law and yet fulfilling it (Romans 7:4; 8:4)? Can believers be released from the Law and at the same time be committed to it (7:6; 8:4)? How do we explain this apparent contradiction? First, consider Jeremiah’s prophecy about the new covenant: “I [the Lord] will put my law in their minds and write it on their hearts” (Jeremiah 31:33). Reflect, as well, on Ezekiel’s prophecy: “And I will put my Spirit in you and move you to follow my decrees and be careful to keep my laws” (Ezekiel 36:27). Here God clearly foretold what He would do. He would enable believers, by His indwelling Spirit, to fulfill the intent and requirements of the Law. Edwards explains, “Paul does not say that one must keep the law in order to be saved but that one must be saved in order to keep the law!” (1992, 203). Freedom from the Law does not mean freedom to disobey or disregard it, but freedom to fulfill it.

In addition to these Old Testament excerpts, let us consider two New Testament passages. The first is Philippians 2:13: “It is God who works in you to will and to act according to his good purpose.” God knows we cannot live the Christian life in our own strength, so He gives us His Spirit to empower us to live in a way that pleases Him. As we walk in submission to Him, we grow spiritually. That is, we leave behind old things and embrace new things. Old habits are abandoned for the new way of the Spirit.

The second passage is 2 Corinthians 3:18: “And as the Spirit of the Lord works within us, we become more and more like him and reflect his glory even more” (NLT). As the fruit of the Spirit becomes evident in our lives, Christ’s thoughts and attitudes increasingly influence the way we act. Gradually we become more like Christ.

The Godhead Provides for our Salvation

In addition to the work of Christ, the Holy Spirit plays a prominent role in our salvation. In fact, in Romans 8:1–4, Paul makes it clear that the entire Godhead—Father, Son, and Holy Spirit—provides for our salvation. “The Spirit of life” (Romans 8:2), that is, the Holy Spirit, was active in creation (Genesis 1:2; 2:7); continues His life-giving activity today in uniting believers to Christ and His body (John 3:3, 5; 1 Corinthians 12:13); and empowers them for service (Acts 1:8). The works of the Father, Son, and Spirit are interrelated, as the table below demonstrates.

The Trinity

Each member of the Godhead participates in providing for our salvation.

Romans 8:1–4

THE FATHER

gave His Son in order to save us, since the Law is powerless to overcome sin (8:3; 2 Cor. 5:21). Those who put their trust in Him receive His righteousness and sin’s control over them is broken. He also gives us His Spirit (8:4).

THE SON

came in human likeness—except He was without sin (8:3; John 1:14; Phil. 2:8; Heb. 4:15). He became a sin offering, turned aside God’s wrath, set us free from sin and death, and became for us wisdom from God—that is, our righteousness, holiness, and redemption (1 Cor. 1:30).

THE HOLY SPIRIT

unites us to Christ and His body (John 3:3,5,8; 1 Cor. 12:13). Through Christ’s sacrifice, the Spirit sets us free from the law of sin and death (8:2) and enables us to fulfill the righteous requirements of the Law (8:4). The prophet Ezekiel foretold this (36:27).

Freedom from the Sinful Nature’s Control (8:5–14)

Paul contrasts the saved (those who have accepted Christ as Lord and Savior) with the unsaved. The sinful nature dominates the unsaved, while the Spirit of God controls the saved. A regenerate (saved person) differs from an unregenerate in his or her thinking, way of life, and destination.

In Romans 8:5, Paul contrasts “those who live according to the sinful nature” with “those who live in accordance with the Spirit.” The word live (Greek, peripateo ), can also be translated as “walk.” Paul describes two ways of living—or, in a sense, two different roads along which we choose to walk. Jesus talked about a broad road that leads to destruction and a narrow road that leads to life (Matthew 7:13–14). Those who live “according to the sinful nature” (Greek, kata sarka , “according to the flesh”) are walking along a road that leads to death, while those who live “in accordance with the Spirit” (Greek, kata pneuma ) find life (Romans 8:6).

The sinful nature refers to the fallen, self-centered human nature. It dominates the thinking of the unregenerate who focus on themselves and on sinful things because their minds are “set on what that nature desires” (Romans 8:5). Those ruled by this nature are “hostile to God” (8:7), do not submit to God’s law (8:7), and cannot please God (8:8). A mindset like this leads to death (8:6, 13).

In contrast, those controlled by the Spirit “have their minds set on what the Spirit desires” (Romans 8:5). Thus, they seek to please God and submit to His way. If the Holy Spirit controls the mind, the believer has “life and peace” (8:6).

Following his comparison of life according to the sinful nature and life in the Spirit, Paul reassures the Roman Christians with these words: “You, however, are controlled not by the sinful nature but by the Spirit, if the Spirit of God lives in you” (Romans 8:9). Every Christian has the Spirit of Christ. Paul uses the Spirit , the Spirit of God , and the Spirit of Christ interchangeably. We become Christians through the work of the Holy Spirit (John 3:8). He convicts of sin, reveals Christ to us, unites us to Christ and His body, and leads us into all truth (John 16:5–16; 1 Corinthians 12:13).

In 1 Corinthians 6:9–11, Paul cites various lifestyles of those dominated by the sinful nature, characterizing such people as wicked, immoral, idolaters, adulterers, male prostitutes, homosexual offenders, thieves, greedy, drunkards, slanderers, and swindlers. “And that,” Paul says, “is what some of you were ” (6:11, italics added). By the grace of God, they no longer lived that way because they had been changed. Paul continues in verse 11, “But you were washed, you were sanctified, you were justified in the name of the Lord Jesus Christ and by the Spirit of our God.” When these Corinthians received Christ as Savior, they were no longer controlled “by the sinful nature but by the Spirit” (Romans 8:9). They graphically illustrate the transforming power of the gospel.

John the apostle records the story of a woman caught in adultery (John 8:1–11). Some Pharisees and teachers of the Law brought her to Jesus as part of a plot, using her in an attempt to deceive and trap Jesus. However, Jesus confounded them by saying, “If any one of you is without sin, let him be the first to throw a stone at her” (8:7). Then one by one her accusers left. Let us simply note John’s closing quote, since it illustrates the point Paul makes in our study: “Neither do I condemn you,” Jesus declared to the woman. “Go now and leave your life of sin” (8:11).

Of course, Jesus is not condoning adultery or this woman’s sin. Yet on the grounds of His approaching sin offering (Romans 8:3), He said, “Neither do I condemn you.” Clearly, this was not an end but a beginning because he added, “Go now and leave your life of sin.” An encounter with Christ leads to a change in lifestyle. Sanctification flows out of justification. Christ delivers from the condemnation of sin, the penalty of sin, and the power of sin, so that we leave our life of sin and walk in newness of life. “God destroyed sin’s control over us by giving his Son as a sacrifice for our sins” (Romans 8:3, NLT).

In Romans 8:12–13, Paul declares, “Therefore, brothers, we have an obligation—but it is not to the sinful nature, to live according to it. For if you live according to the sinful nature, you will die; but if by the Spirit you put to death the misdeeds of the body, you will live.”

“We have an obligation” to respond to God’s grace and goodness to us in Jesus Christ. We are obligated to reject the desires of the sinful nature and say “No” to godless living and sinful pleasures. At the same time, we are “to live self-controlled, upright and godly lives in this present age” (Titus 2:12).

Misdeeds (Greek, praxeis ) in Romans 8:13 is a reference to the practices of the sinful nature. Paul calls these misdeeds “the acts of the sinful nature” in Galatians 5:19 and issues a warning: “Those who live like this will not inherit the kingdom of God” (5:21; see vv. 19–21) and “will die” (Romans 8:13). These dire consequences prompt Paul to insist that the Romans must “put to death the misdeeds of the body” (8:13) in order to live. While believers cannot earn their salvation, they are nevertheless responsible for their conduct. Note that put to death is in the present tense. Our warfare with sin is a lifetime struggle, a battle we cannot win in our own strength. For this very reason, God gives us His Spirit—not as a good conduct award but to empower us to overcome sin, live in victory, and glorify His name.

Freedom as a Child of God (8:14–17)

Those who have put their trust in Jesus Christ as Savior are called “sons [and daughters] of God” (Romans 8:14) and “God’s children” (8:16). Paul characterizes the members of God’s family as “those who are led by the Spirit of God” (8:14). This is a distinguishing trait of God’s children.

“You received the Spirit of sonship” (Romans 8:15). Sonship (Greek, hyiothesia ) literally means “adoption.” Paul is the only New Testament writer who uses this term, employing it just five times. Although adoption was not a Jewish practice, it was an important part of Greek and Roman culture. Thus, Roman believers easily understood Paul’s meaning.

Bruce provides an explanation of adoption in Paul’s day: “An adopted son was a son deliberately chosen by his adoptive father to perpetuate his name and inherit his estate; he was no whit [not at all] inferior in status to a son born in the ordinary course of nature, and might well enjoy the father’s affection more fully and reproduce the father’s character more worthily” (1985, 166).

In essence, those who are adopted are taken from one family and placed in another. They lose their former family rights but inherit all the rights and privileges of their new family. Paul applies this concept to Christians, who in a sense have been removed from Adam’s family and adopted into God’s family through a work of the Spirit.

Who are members of God’s family? Paul mentions several identifying marks of a believer:

First of all, the Holy Spirit lives within a believer. Paul wrote, “If anyone does not have the Spirit of Christ, he does not belong to Christ” (Romans 8:9).

The believer’s conduct should give evidence of his or her relationship to Christ. Wiersbe comments, “It is not enough for us to have the Spirit; the Spirit must have us!” (1977, 90). Or, as Paul says in Romans 8:14: “Those who are led by the Spirit of God are sons of God.” Essentially, “led by the Spirit” means we willingly submit to Him so that He guides us and directs us in ways pleasing to God. Our conduct should give evidence of our relationship to Christ.

The Spirit works within believers, arousing them so that they instinctively cry, “ Abba , Father” (Romans 8:15).

Sons and daughters of God receive an inner witness of the Spirit. “The Spirit himself testifies with our spirit that we are God’s children” (Romans 8:16). The Spirit reassures us of our relationship with God.

Finally, God’s children become “heirs of God and co-heirs with Christ” (Romans 8:17). To these enormous benefits, Paul adds a qualifying condition: “if indeed we share in his sufferings” (8:17).

As adopted children, we cry “ Abba , Father” (Romans 8:15)—following the example of our Lord. The gospel writers repeatedly describe Jesus as addressing God as “Father.” In the Garden, He prayed, “ Abba , Father” (Mark 14:36). Abba , from the Aramaic, expresses intimacy. It is somewhat like a child calling his or her father “Papa” or “Daddy.” However, Boice observes, “To a Jewish mind, a prayer addressing God as daddy would not only have been improper, it would have been irreverent to the highest degree” (1992, 2:842–843). Jesus broke with tradition in addressing God as Father . It is true that at times Scripture does refer to God as “Father” and His people as “children” (for example, see Psalm 103:13; Isaiah 64:8; Jeremiah 3:19–20; Hosea 11:1–2). Nevertheless, it was not a common practice for Jews to address God in a personal way. As Boice indicated, Jews considered this kind of address improper and “even blasphemous” (1992, 2:843).

The Law imparted a spirit of fear. For instance, since Jews feared they might utter an inappropriate word that would profane God’s name and incur His wrath, they avoided using His name whenever possible. Paul writes, “You did not receive a spirit that makes you a slave again to fear” (Romans 8:15). Comfort says, “Paul implies that the absence of fear is an important indicator of the Spirit’s presence” (1992, 157). He cites 2 Timothy 1:7 to support his view: “God did not give us a spirit of timidity, but a spirit of power, of love and of self-discipline.”

Edwards draws this contrast between God and sin: “The imagery is that of a family. Sin abducts, God adopts. Sin makes slaves, God makes children. Sin provokes fear, the Spirit evokes trust. Sin foments rebellion and hostility, the Spirit leads believers to appeal to God in the most intimate and urgent cry, ‘Abba, Father.’ The paradox of grace is that those who deserve death are granted life” (1992, 207).

**Q:** How does Luke 2:25–32 present salvation ?

**A:** A provision of God made in the person of Jesus Christ

**Q:** What does it mean to say that since God condemned our sin in Christ we are not under condemnation?

**A:** Because Christ bore our sins on the Cross once for all, we are freed from the penalty of sin and are no longer under condemnation.

**Q:** List as many words and phrases as you can find in this passage that explain the Law and the Spirit.

**A:** The Law: Brings death; is like a veil that covers the heart; the old way; condemns The Spirit: The new covenant; gives freedom; brings righteousness; removes the veil; the new way

**Q:** Review the various interpretations of the meaning of law in Romans 8:2. Which one seems most likely to you? Explain your choice.

**A:** Your answer. Whichever view you support, it is clear in Romans 8:2 that it is “through Christ Jesus” we are set free from the law of sin and death.

**Q:** What has freed us from the Law and its curse?

**A:** Jesus’ substitutionary death in our behalf

**Q:** Based on Romans 8, describe how believers are delivered from condemnation.

**A:** Your answer should include: by sending His Son, God did what the Law was unable to do; through Christ Jesus, the Spirit liberates believers from the law of sin; God judged our sins in His Son, who became a sin offering for us; on the basis of Christ’s sin offering, God condemned sin in sinful people and provided freedom from the bondage of sin.

**Q:** Give an example of how the works of the Father, Son, and Holy Spirit on behalf of the believer are interrelated, based on Romans 8:1–4.

**A:** God sent His Son, Jesus Christ, to be our sin offering. Through Jesus Christ, the Spirit of life sets us free from the law of sin and death.

**Q:** Based on Romans 8:5–8, contrast those who live according to the sinful nature with those who live according to the Spirit. Write these comparative ideas side by side in your own words.

**A:** Your answer should include these statements Living According to the Sinful Nature Living According to the Spirit Thinks about sinful desires Thinks about what the Spirit desires Results in death Results in life Results in inner conflict Results in peace Is hostile to God Is at peace with God Refuses to submit to God’s law Submits to God’s law Cannot keep God’s law Fulfills the law of Christ Cannot please God Pleases God

**Q:** Compare the principle from 1 Corinthians 6:9–11 and John 8:1–11 with the account of Nicodemus in John 3:1–15. What conclusion do you draw from this comparison?

**A:** From the human viewpoint, the lifestyles Paul cites and that of the adulterous woman seem to differ greatly from the piety of Nicodemus. However, from God’s perspective, there is no distinction. All need the Savior.

**Q:** What is the key to putting to death the acts of the sinful nature?

**A:** Allow the Spirit of God to control your life and conduct.

**Q:** According to John 8:42, 47, what are the characteristics of a person who claims God as “Father”?

**A:** He or she loves God, loves the Son who came from the Father, and hears what God says.

**Q:** According to John 1:12–13, what are the conditions for membership in God’s family?

**A:** Believe in Christ and receive Christ.

**Q:** Why do believers have the right to address God as “ Abba , Father”?

**A:** Because they have been adopted into God’s family through a work of God’s Spirit

### Future Glory (Romans 8:18–27)

#### 5.2.1 — Contrast the future glory God has prepared for His people with their present suffering, in the context of which creation, believers, and the Spirit groan.

Present Suffering and Future Glory

Paul contrasts believers’ present suffering with their future glory. Creation, believers, and the Spirit groan in anticipation of future glory. We understand Paul when he says, “We ourselves . . . groan inwardly” (Romans 8:23), since this is part of living. But we are somewhat puzzled when he talks about creation groaning. Do plants and other material things suffer? Can they feel pain? And what does Paul mean when he says, “the Spirit groans”? We will look closely at what Paul says in these verses in Romans 8. But first, let us consider his contrast of present suffering with future glory.

How did Paul bear up under hardship? He put it into perspective by comparing it to future glory. By focusing on future blessings, he was able to maintain his present purpose and persevere. We know he suffered intense pain for the gospel (2 Corinthians 11:16–33). Compared to future glory, however, it was “light and momentary” (2 Corinthians 4:17).

Jesus overcame suffering in a similar way: “Let us fix our eyes on Jesus, the author and perfecter of our faith, who for the joy set before him [future glory] endured the cross [present suffering], scorning its shame, and sat down at the right hand of the throne of God” (Hebrews 12:2).

How can you handle stress more effectively? By putting your troubles into perspective, you will gain stability and stamina. Through Jesus Christ you have “an eternal glory that far outweighs them all” (2 Corinthians 4:17).

“I consider that our present sufferings are not worth comparing with the glory that will be revealed in us” (Romans 8:18). Paul does not dismiss suffering (Greek, pathemata ) as something imaginary but treats it as a present reality. Then he says that it is “not worth comparing” with future “glory” (Greek, doxa ). Suffering and glory contrast two eras: the present and the future. The troubles we face at the present time are relatively light and momentary compared to coming glory. Kabod , the Hebrew word for glory , means “heaviness” or “weight” (Stott 1994, 237). Future glory is eternal and “far outweighs” the troubles of this life (2 Corinthians 4:17).

Before considering Paul’s teaching in this passage, we should pause a moment and note that suffering and glory frequently occur together in Scripture. This idea actually flows out of Paul’s concluding remark in the previous passage: “But if we are to share his glory, we must also share his suffering” (Romans 8:17, NLT). Earlier, in describing the benefits of the justified, Paul linked suffering and glory (5:2–3). Peter also unites the two words when he says, “Rejoice that you participate in the sufferings of Christ, so that you may be overjoyed when his glory is revealed” (1 Peter 4:13). Even the risen Christ himself said, “Did not the Christ have to suffer these things and then enter his glory?” (Luke 24:26).

Suffering has never been a popular subject—even when taught by Jesus. Do you recall Peter’s reaction to Jesus’ prediction that He would suffer death at Calvary? Peter began to rebuke Jesus, saying, “Never, Lord!” Suffering did not fit into his concept of the Messiah. Jesus’ response to Peter was not unlike His response to Satan when he tempted Jesus at the beginning of His ministry. (Compare Mark 8:31–33 with Matthew 4:8–10.)

Three times in Romans 8:18–28, Paul speaks of groaning. First, creation groans as it waits in eager expectation for deliverance (8:18–22). Believers groan inwardly as they await the complete fulfillment of their inheritance in Christ (8:23–25). And the Spirit intercedes for believers with groans (8:26–27). Let us look at these passages now.

Creation Groans

“We know that the whole creation has been groaning as in the pains of childbirth right up to the present time” (Romans 8:22). Adam’s disobedience had an impact on the entire human race and brought all of creation under judgment (Genesis 3:17–18). The ground was cursed, causing thorns, thistles, decay, and death. Consequently, we sweat and struggle to produce food to live.

“For the creation was subjected to frustration” (Romans 8:20) because of the Fall (Genesis 3). It struggles with frustration because it is unable to fulfill its original, intended purpose. However, Paul says, there is hope (Romans 8:20–21, 24–25).

King Solomon, although renowned for his wisdom, learned about frustration the hard way. After attaining success and wealth, he drifted into sin and tried everything his heart desired. Yet instead of fulfillment, he found frustration and futility. Sin separates us from God and makes life meaningless. Thus, for Solomon, life was like chasing the wind, simply because he was living apart from God. In looking back over his life, he concluded that everything under the sun—or, apart from God—is empty and meaningless. Perhaps with a desire to help others avoid his mistake, he wrote the book of Ecclesiastes in which he appealed to his readers to do what he had failed to do: “Fear God and keep his commandments” (Ecclesiastes 12:13).

Creation groans like a woman in labor (Romans 8:22), waiting in eager expectation for deliverance. With the promise of new heavens and a new earth, God gives hope. One day the wolf and the lamb will eat together, the lion will graze on straw next to the ox, and poisonous serpents will neither harm nor destroy (Isaiah 65:17, 25; 66:22). Likewise, Peter foresaw a “new heaven and a new earth” (2 Peter 3:13; see also Revelation 21:1).

Believers Groan

Paul continues, “Not only so, but we ourselves, who have the firstfruits of the Spirit, groan inwardly as we wait eagerly for our adoption as sons, the redemption of our bodies” (Romans 8:23). We groan, as do most people in the world who suffer pain, loss, or tragedy. But here Paul speaks specifically about believers in Christ. Although they may grieve in the present because of surrounding sin, opposition from the ungodly, and struggles with their own weaknesses, they yet maintain a confident expectation.

Those who are in Christ “have the firstfruits [Greek, aparche ] of the Spirit” (Romans 8:23). Jews brought the firstborn of their flocks and a sheaf of the first harvested grain to the priest, expressing thankfulness to God for His goodness. They called this offering firstfruits (Leviticus 23:10; Numbers 28:26; 2 Chronicles 31:5).

In Romans 8:23, however, Paul is not portraying what believers give to God but what God gives to believers. This gift of the Spirit is likened to a seal or a deposit. “He anointed us, set his seal of ownership on us, and put his Spirit in our hearts as a deposit, guaranteeing what is to come” (2 Corinthians 1:21–22). The descriptions deposit , down payment , and identifying seal of ownership symbolize the idea of firstfruits . Thus, as the New Living Translation says, “We . . . have the Holy Spirit within us as a foretaste of future glory” (Romans 8:23).

As Christians, we receive the firstfruits of the Spirit when Christ redeems us. Nevertheless, as Paul says, we “groan inwardly” (Romans 8:23) because we have not yet reached our final destination, nor have we received all that God has prepared for us. We suffer now but foresee future glory. Therefore, “we wait eagerly for our adoption as sons, the redemption of our bodies” (8:23). An adopted son has all the rights and privileges of a natural son. Here in verse 23, Paul looks ahead to the consummation of our adoption, that is, “the redemption of our bodies.” Previously, Paul linked Christ’s resurrection to ours, saying that the Spirit who raised Christ from the dead would also give life to our mortal bodies (8:11). In Philippians 3:20–21, he makes a similar comment: “But our citizenship is in heaven. And we eagerly await a Savior from there, the Lord Jesus Christ, who, by the power that enables him to bring everything under his control, will transform our lowly bodies so that they will be like his glorious body.” Further, Paul emphasizes the redemption of the whole person—“spirit, soul and body,” in 1 Thessalonians 5:23.

Creation groans “as in the pains of childbirth” (Romans 8:22) and “we ourselves . . . groan” (8:23). What a striking analogy! Our present suffering is like the pains of childbirth. Our groaning does not express anguish but anticipation—like a woman awaiting the birth of a child. In the same way, we are filled with confident expectation. Full harvest follows firstfruits. Glory follows suffering.

a. “The whole creation has been groaning as in the pains of childbirth” (8:22).

b. “We ourselves, who have the firstfruits of the Spirit, groan inwardly” (8:23).

c. “We . . . groan inwardly as we wait eagerly for our adoption as sons” (8:23).

The Spirit Groans

“In the same way, the Spirit helps us in our weakness. We do not know what we ought to pray for, but the Spirit himself intercedes for us with groans that words cannot express” (Romans 8:26). As we have seen, the Spirit leads us (8:14) and gives us a sense of oneness with God so that we cry, “ Abba , Father” (8:15). He also assures us that we are God’s children (8:16), and “in the same way”—meaning in the same way that hope upholds us in suffering (8:24–25)—the Spirit helps us in prayer. We do not even know what we should pray for, but “the Spirit helps us in our weakness” (8:26). The apostle includes himself, saying our weakness. Our understanding of the activities in the natural and spiritual realm about us is limited, which restricts our effectiveness in prayer.

Yet the Spirit helps us (Romans 8:26). Boice explains that helps (Greek, sunantilambanetai ) actually comprises three words. Combined, these words “refer to a person coming alongside another to take part of a heavy load and help him bear it” (1992, 2:888). This expression is similar to one Jesus used when He spoke of “another Counselor” (John 14:16). The word Counselor (Greek, parakletos ) literally means “one called alongside to help” and can also be translated as “Comforter,” “Encourager,” or “Advocate” (Beers 1996, 934). In essence, Paul says that our weakness (or inability to know what to pray for) is like a heavy load; thus the Holy Spirit comes to our side and helps us bear it.

Paul mentions in his Epistles two divine intercessors: the Spirit and Jesus Christ. Christ intercedes for us in the Father’s presence in heaven (Romans 8:33–34; 1 Timothy 2:5; Hebrews 7:25; 8:1; 9:24; 1 John 2:1). The Holy Spirit intercedes on earth within or through believers (Romans 8:26–27).

An intercessor pleads for God’s favor in behalf of others. For example, Abraham pleaded with God in behalf of Sodom (Genesis 18). Moses entreated God for the Israelites (Numbers 14:1–20), and Daniel interceded for his nation (Daniel 9). Jesus prayed for His disciples (John 17), for Simon Peter that his faith would not fail (Luke 22:31–32), and for those who crucified Him (Luke 23:34). His uppermost intercessory act was at Calvary (Isaiah 53:12).

The Gospels vividly picture Christ’s intercession for transgressors at Calvary. Now, Paul says, “the Spirit himself intercedes for us with groans that words cannot express” (Romans 8:26). The NIV Study Bible note for 8:26 gives this explanation: “Whether Paul means words that are unspoken or words that cannot be expressed in human language is not clear—probably the former, though v. 27 seems to suggest the latter” (1985, 1718).

Concerning “groans that words cannot express,” Bruce says: “Speaking to God in the Spirit with ‘tongues’ (1 Cor. xiv. 2) may be included in this expression, but it covers those longings and aspirations which well up from the spiritual depths and cannot be imprisoned within the confines of everyday words” (1985, 175).

In 1 Corinthians 14:15, Paul talks about praying “with my spirit” and “with my mind.” The Amplified Bible expresses the thought this way: “I will pray with my spirit [by the Holy Spirit that is within me], but I will also pray [intelligently] with my mind and understanding.” Paul further exhorts us: “Pray in the Spirit on all occasions with all kinds of prayers and requests” (Ephesians 6:18). Clearly, the Holy Spirit enables us to pray effectively “in accordance with God’s will” (Romans 8:27).

In his book, The Beauty of Spiritual Language , Jack Hayford highlights three great truths in Romans 8:26–28 (1992, 68):

1. We all regularly face “things” we do not know how to pray about (8:26).

2. The Holy Spirit will dramatically assist in prayer at such times (8:27).

3. By this means, God’s purpose and power are introduced into the situation (8:28).

**Q:** Based on our discussion, how can a believer’s suffering best be explained?

**A:** By saying that it is the pathway to future glory

**Q:** Explain the significance of each of these statements in Romans 8 as they apply to the Christian life.

**A:** a “The whole creation has been groaning as in the pains of childbirth” (8:22). b “We ourselves, who have the firstfruits of the Spirit, groan inwardly” (8:23). c “We . . . groan inwardly as we wait eagerly for our adoption as sons” (8:23). a All creation is frustrated because it is unable to fulfill its intended purpose and longs for deliverance. b While awaiting redemption, believers grieve because of surrounding sin and opposition and their own weakness. c Believers long for the consummation of their adoption, the transformation of their mortal bodies to be like Christ’s glorified body.

**Q:** Briefly explain what is meant by the statement, “The Spirit groans.”

**A:** The Spirit intercedes when the saints do not know what they ought to pray for, and He intercedes in accordance with God’s will.

### More Than Conquerors (Romans 8:28–39)

#### 5.3.1 — Identify in Romans 8:28 five certainties about God that demonstrate His faithfulness to His people.

This final lesson reaffirms God’s faithfulness to His people. Stott notes that Paul discusses this from three perspectives. First, “five unshakable convictions” (Romans 8:28) show God working in behalf of His people. Second, “five undeniable affirmations (8:29–30) identify the successive stages of God’s saving purpose from eternity to eternity.” Third, “five unanswerable questions” (8:31–39) challenge “anybody to contradict the convictions and affirmations which he [Paul] has just expressed” (1992, 246).

As we conclude this lesson and our study of Romans 8, let us look at Paul’s five convictions, five affirmations, and five unanswerable questions.

Five Convictions (8:28)

As we noted above, God works in behalf of His people. Paul is so thoroughly convinced of God’s faithfulness that he begins his next statement by asserting, “ And we know that in all things God works for the good of those who love him, who have been called according to his purpose” (Romans 8:28, italics added). He does not present the unknown but the known. In like manner, John in his first Epistle stresses what believers know. He uses two different Greek words translated “know” forty-two times, underscoring the certainty a believer has in Christ. In Romans 8:28, Paul gives us five certainties about God and His faithfulness.

As we ponder the certainties in this well-known verse, keep in mind the context. Romans 8:28 is not an isolated principle; rather, it is tied directly to the preceding verses and the intercessory work of the Spirit in and through His people. Spirit-enabled intercession introduces God’s active intervention into adverse and even impossible situations for the good of His people. Here are the five convictions expressed in this verse:

1. God works in the lives of His people. Paul’s strongly held belief is rooted in Scripture. The pages of the Old Testament are filled with testimonies of God’s intervention in behalf of His people.

2. God works for the good of His people. Jesus came to reveal the Father and do what the Father does (Matthew 11:27; John 10:38; 14:9). With this in mind, consider Peter’s message to Cornelius and his household. Like Paul, he began his message with certainty. “You know . . . how God anointed Jesus of Nazareth with the Holy Spirit and power, and how he went around doing good” (Acts 10:37–38).

3. God works for the good of His people in all things . All things includes times of adversity, evil circumstances, and times of walking “through the valley of the shadow of death” (Psalm 23:4). Paul does not say that we understand everything which happens to us or that our circumstances are without pain. But as Joseph said to his brothers, “You intended to harm me, but God intended it for good . . . the saving of many lives” (Genesis 50:20), so also Paul affirms that God works for our good “in all things.”

4. God works for the good of those who love Him . This benefit—God working for the good in all things—is specifically promised to “those who love him.” What comfort this provides in those times of adversity that we do not understand.

5. God works for the good of those who have been called according to His purpose . Here God narrows His promise even further by specifically linking it to those He has chosen for His purpose. God did not send evil circumstances into Joseph’s life; but He foreknew that they would occur, worked for Joseph’s good in them, and ultimately fulfilled His purpose in this son of Jacob.

God’s ultimate purpose in working in all things for our good (those who love Him) is to conform us “to the likeness of His Son” (Romans 8:29). In a similar way, John looked to the future with confidence and certainty when he wrote, “We know that when he appears, we shall be like him” (1 John 3:2). To this he adds a note in line with Paul’s exhortation to put to death the misdeeds of the body (Romans 8:13): “Everyone who has this hope in him purifies himself, just as he is pure” (1 John 3:3). Believers are not perfect; they are being conformed into Christ’s likeness. Nevertheless, sin should not characterize their lives.

At times, God permits evil circumstances in the lives of His people, but He is not the source of evil. Job and Joseph are distinct Old Testament examples of God working for the good of His people when they encounter adversity.

Five Affirmations (8:29–30)

“For those God foreknew he also predestined to be conformed to the likeness of his Son, that he might be the firstborn among many brothers. And those he predestined, he also called; those he called, he also justified; those he justified, he also glorified” (Romans 8:29–30). Paul summarizes God’s purpose in five words: (1) foreknowledge, (2) predestination, (3) calling, (4) justification, and (5) glorification. Boice calls these words “a golden chain of five links” (1992, 2:786). These linking affirmations stretch from eternity to eternity and are unbreakable. They assure us of God’s unfailing love. This is illustrated in the graphic below, which is followed by a brief explanation of the five words.

Let us also consider Paul’s words, “that he might be the firstborn among many brothers” (Romans 8:29). Since we are God’s children, we are Christ’s brothers and sisters. As the “firstborn,” He is preeminent, holding the place of highest honor in God’s family.

FOREKNOWLEDGE

An aspect of God’s omniscience. He knows all things—past, present, future. He knew us before we were born. Before the creation of the world, He knew who would respond to His offer of salvation and who would reject it. He chose us according to His foreknowledge. (See Ephesians 1:4–5; 2 Peter 1:2.)

PREDESTINATION

God planned what He would do for those who would become His children. He determined beforehand that they would be conformed to the likeness of His Son (Romans 8:29). Paul uses this term in relation to believers, not unbelievers. The Bible does not teach that God predestines people to eternal condemnation. God does not want anyone to perish (2 Peter 3:9; John 3:18–21).

CALLING

God calls us through the gospel to “share in the glory of our Lord Jesus Christ” (2 Thessalonians 2:13–14). He invites all to come to Him.

JUSTIFICATION

God justifies those who believe the gospel. Freligh defines justification as “that act of God’s grace whereby He declares righteous the person who places faith in Jesus Christ as his Substitute and Savior” (1962, 69). In other words, Christ’s righteousness is imputed to the believer.

GLORIFICATION

A part of sanctification. They differ in degree, not kind. Sanctification is the transforming process God works in us to mold us into Christ’s image. Glorification is the end result. “Sanctification is glory begun. Glory is sanctification completed” (Bruce 1985, 168).

Five Questions (8:31–39)

“What, then, shall we say in response to this?” (Romans 8:31). With this question Paul inserts a transition, as he did in Romans 4:1; 6:1, 15; and 7:7. “In response to this” refers back to the five convictions (8:28) and the five affirmations (8:29–30) we have just examined. The five questions he now poses conclude his argument. They are designed to challenge anyone who might contest the certainties and claims he makes about God’s faithfulness. These five questions are as follows:

1. If God is for us, who can be against us? (Romans 8:31). What does Paul mean by this question? Clearly, he had enemies. The world opposed him and his message, just as it opposes believers today. And yes, the devil was against him as he is against us (1 Peter 5:8). We have enemies without and the flesh within (1 John 2:15–16). So what is Paul saying here? Note that Paul’s “if” in verse 31 is not an expression of doubt, for there is no uncertainty that God is for us. Boice explains that “if” as used here means “since” (1992, 2:953). In other words, “since God is for us, who can prevail against us?”

2. He who did not spare his own Son, but gave him up for us all—how will he not also, along with him, graciously give us all things? (Romans 8:32). Does this promise seem too good to be true? Will God really “give us all things”? We agree that the Creator and Ruler of the universe is omnipotent—unlimited in power and authority. He divides seas and shakes prisons to deliver His people. But we wonder, “Is He willing to help me?” Consider the leper in Matthew 8:2–3, who recognized Jesus’ power to heal but questioned His willingness to do so. Jesus removed all of his doubt, saying, “I am willing”—and immediately healed him.

How does Paul assure us that God will give us all things? He points to Calvary, saying of God, “He . . . did not spare his own Son” (Romans 8:32). This probably alludes to the story of Abraham; the word spare (Greek, pheidomai ) is the same word used in the Greek translation of the Old Testament to translate “withhold” (Genesis 22:12, 16). Abraham’s total commitment to God in not withholding his only son exemplifies what God did in giving His Son (John 3:16). Essentially, Paul reasons that if God gave us the ultimate gift of His Son, He will also give all things (of lesser value) to those who serve Him. This is the same logic he uses in Romans 5:9–10, where he goes from the greater to the lesser. If God saved us while we were His enemies, how much more will He keep us (those whom He has justified) in this life and save us from wrath.

3. Who will bring any charge against those whom God has chosen? (Romans 8:33). Enemies of the gospel constantly hurled accusations against Paul, as they did against Jesus. Likewise, charges are brought against Christians today—sometimes by false friends or even by their own conscience (1 John 3:20). Satan—the devil, “the accuser of our brothers”—accuses us before God day and night (Revelation 12:10). He accused Job (Job 1:9–11) and Joshua, the high priest (Zechariah 3:1). In both cases, however, God intervened on their behalf. In Romans 8:33, Paul indicates that no one, including Satan, can successfully bring charges “against those whom God has chosen.” Why? Because “it is God who justifies” (8:33); that is, “He is the one who has given us right standing with himself” (8:33, NLT). God, who presides over the highest court, has pronounced us “not guilty” on the basis of our faith in Jesus Christ.

4. Who is he that condemns? (Romans 8:34). There are, of course, many who seek to condemn God’s people, as our previous discussion shows. Paul’s question implies that no one can succeed in condemning God’s people. Verse 34 gives three reasons why this is true.

First: “Christ Jesus, who died.” John identified Him as “the Lamb of God, who takes away the sin of the world” (John 1:29). You will recall that earlier Paul described Him as a “sin offering” (Romans 8:3). We also read in Galatians that “Christ redeemed us from the curse of the law by becoming a curse for us” (Galatians 3:13). Consequently, we are no longer under condemnation.

Second: “Jesus Christ . . . was raised to life” (Romans 8:34). This confirmed God’s acceptance of the “sin offering” (8:3). Jesus Christ “was raised to life for our justification” (4:25).

Third: Jesus Christ “is at the right hand of God and is also interceding for us” (Romans 8:34). No one can successfully condemn God’s people because our great High Priest intercedes for us (Hebrews 4:14–16; 7:24).

Why can no one successfully condemn us? Jesus Christ died for us, was raised to life for us, and is now interceding for us in heaven. We might add a fourth reason: Here on earth, “the Spirit pleads for us believers in harmony with God’s own will” (Romans 8:27, NLT).

5. Who shall separate us from the love of Christ? (Romans 8:35). Can adversaries or adversities pry us apart from Christ? Can unfavorable circumstances undermine our confidence in Him? Paul cites seven possible things that might come between us and Christ. Hostile opponents, for instance, might stir up “trouble or hardship or persecution.” Paul experienced all of these in preaching the gospel. “Famine” and “nakedness” suggest adverse circumstances. Droughts, floods, or other natural disasters may at times cause hunger, poverty, and economic distress. Since Paul is addressing Christians here, however, it seems likely that he has more specific situations in mind that arise because of their faith.

Believers face “danger” and “sword” as well. Everyone faces danger in one form or another, but Christians are frequently singled out and beaten or abused. Paul’s missionary ministry was filled with danger, as he testifies: “I have been in danger from rivers, in danger from bandits, in danger from my own countrymen, in danger from Gentiles; in danger in the city, in danger in the country, in danger at sea; and in danger from false brothers” (2 Corinthians 11:26). Sword in Romans 8:35 suggests extreme violence ending in death, as in the cases of Stephen and James. Within ten years of the time Paul wrote this letter, many Roman believers would face martyrdom under Nero. They would die not because of war or wrongdoing but because of their faith in Jesus Christ. Psalm 44:22, which Paul quotes, makes this clear: “For your [God’s] sake we face death all day long” (Romans 8:36).

Romans 8:37–39 has been affirmed by Christians through the ages. In these verses, Paul declares victoriously:

No, in all these things we are more than conquerors through him who loved us. For I am convinced that neither death nor life, neither angels nor demons, neither the present nor the future, nor any powers, neither height nor depth, nor anything else in all creation, will be able to separate us from the love of God that is in Christ Jesus our Lord.

In all these things we are more than conquerors through him who loved us (Romans 8:37). We follow Paul’s logic leading up to his quote from Psalm 44:22, “We are considered as sheep to be slaughtered” (Romans 8:36). But then he jolts us by saying, “We are more than conquerors.” It seems absurd that helpless sheep can conquer. Has Paul suddenly become illogical? Not at all. His imagery is not preposterous, but precise. Conquest comes not through sheep power but through Shepherd power! We conquer through Him who loved us. And, as Paul concludes, nothing is “able to separate us from the love of God that is in Christ Jesus our Lord”!

What a powerful conclusion to a great chapter. Truly, God is faithful, and God’s people are more than conquerors! I suggest that you reread the introduction to this chapter. Now that you have studied Romans 8, have you been encouraged, strengthened, and built up in your Christian faith? In the face of troubles, hardship, persecution, famine, nakedness, danger, or sword, can you now affirm with the apostle Paul: “In all these things we are more than conquerors through him who loved us”? He is indeed our source of victory.

**Q:** Read Matthew 12:49–50. How does Jesus identify the members of His family in this passage?

**A:** His family consists of “whoever does the will of my Father in heaven.”

**Q:** Summarize each of Paul’s five questions, and answer each one as he would.

**A:** QUESTION ANSWER a Since God is for us, who can be against us? No one! b If God freely gave His Son, will He not freely give us all things? He is willing to do so. c Can anyone successfully bring charges against God’s chosen ones? No one! d Can anyone succeed in condemning God’s people? No one! e Can anything separate us from the love of God? Nothing! UNIT 3

#### 5.3.2 — Define given theological terms Paul uses in Romans 8:29–30 to explain God’s purpose for believers.

This final lesson reaffirms God’s faithfulness to His people. Stott notes that Paul discusses this from three perspectives. First, “five unshakable convictions” (Romans 8:28) show God working in behalf of His people. Second, “five undeniable affirmations (8:29–30) identify the successive stages of God’s saving purpose from eternity to eternity.” Third, “five unanswerable questions” (8:31–39) challenge “anybody to contradict the convictions and affirmations which he [Paul] has just expressed” (1992, 246).

As we conclude this lesson and our study of Romans 8, let us look at Paul’s five convictions, five affirmations, and five unanswerable questions.

Five Convictions (8:28)

As we noted above, God works in behalf of His people. Paul is so thoroughly convinced of God’s faithfulness that he begins his next statement by asserting, “ And we know that in all things God works for the good of those who love him, who have been called according to his purpose” (Romans 8:28, italics added). He does not present the unknown but the known. In like manner, John in his first Epistle stresses what believers know. He uses two different Greek words translated “know” forty-two times, underscoring the certainty a believer has in Christ. In Romans 8:28, Paul gives us five certainties about God and His faithfulness.

As we ponder the certainties in this well-known verse, keep in mind the context. Romans 8:28 is not an isolated principle; rather, it is tied directly to the preceding verses and the intercessory work of the Spirit in and through His people. Spirit-enabled intercession introduces God’s active intervention into adverse and even impossible situations for the good of His people. Here are the five convictions expressed in this verse:

1. God works in the lives of His people. Paul’s strongly held belief is rooted in Scripture. The pages of the Old Testament are filled with testimonies of God’s intervention in behalf of His people.

2. God works for the good of His people. Jesus came to reveal the Father and do what the Father does (Matthew 11:27; John 10:38; 14:9). With this in mind, consider Peter’s message to Cornelius and his household. Like Paul, he began his message with certainty. “You know . . . how God anointed Jesus of Nazareth with the Holy Spirit and power, and how he went around doing good” (Acts 10:37–38).

3. God works for the good of His people in all things . All things includes times of adversity, evil circumstances, and times of walking “through the valley of the shadow of death” (Psalm 23:4). Paul does not say that we understand everything which happens to us or that our circumstances are without pain. But as Joseph said to his brothers, “You intended to harm me, but God intended it for good . . . the saving of many lives” (Genesis 50:20), so also Paul affirms that God works for our good “in all things.”

4. God works for the good of those who love Him . This benefit—God working for the good in all things—is specifically promised to “those who love him.” What comfort this provides in those times of adversity that we do not understand.

5. God works for the good of those who have been called according to His purpose . Here God narrows His promise even further by specifically linking it to those He has chosen for His purpose. God did not send evil circumstances into Joseph’s life; but He foreknew that they would occur, worked for Joseph’s good in them, and ultimately fulfilled His purpose in this son of Jacob.

God’s ultimate purpose in working in all things for our good (those who love Him) is to conform us “to the likeness of His Son” (Romans 8:29). In a similar way, John looked to the future with confidence and certainty when he wrote, “We know that when he appears, we shall be like him” (1 John 3:2). To this he adds a note in line with Paul’s exhortation to put to death the misdeeds of the body (Romans 8:13): “Everyone who has this hope in him purifies himself, just as he is pure” (1 John 3:3). Believers are not perfect; they are being conformed into Christ’s likeness. Nevertheless, sin should not characterize their lives.

At times, God permits evil circumstances in the lives of His people, but He is not the source of evil. Job and Joseph are distinct Old Testament examples of God working for the good of His people when they encounter adversity.

Five Affirmations (8:29–30)

“For those God foreknew he also predestined to be conformed to the likeness of his Son, that he might be the firstborn among many brothers. And those he predestined, he also called; those he called, he also justified; those he justified, he also glorified” (Romans 8:29–30). Paul summarizes God’s purpose in five words: (1) foreknowledge, (2) predestination, (3) calling, (4) justification, and (5) glorification. Boice calls these words “a golden chain of five links” (1992, 2:786). These linking affirmations stretch from eternity to eternity and are unbreakable. They assure us of God’s unfailing love. This is illustrated in the graphic below, which is followed by a brief explanation of the five words.

Let us also consider Paul’s words, “that he might be the firstborn among many brothers” (Romans 8:29). Since we are God’s children, we are Christ’s brothers and sisters. As the “firstborn,” He is preeminent, holding the place of highest honor in God’s family.

FOREKNOWLEDGE

An aspect of God’s omniscience. He knows all things—past, present, future. He knew us before we were born. Before the creation of the world, He knew who would respond to His offer of salvation and who would reject it. He chose us according to His foreknowledge. (See Ephesians 1:4–5; 2 Peter 1:2.)

PREDESTINATION

God planned what He would do for those who would become His children. He determined beforehand that they would be conformed to the likeness of His Son (Romans 8:29). Paul uses this term in relation to believers, not unbelievers. The Bible does not teach that God predestines people to eternal condemnation. God does not want anyone to perish (2 Peter 3:9; John 3:18–21).

CALLING

God calls us through the gospel to “share in the glory of our Lord Jesus Christ” (2 Thessalonians 2:13–14). He invites all to come to Him.

JUSTIFICATION

God justifies those who believe the gospel. Freligh defines justification as “that act of God’s grace whereby He declares righteous the person who places faith in Jesus Christ as his Substitute and Savior” (1962, 69). In other words, Christ’s righteousness is imputed to the believer.

GLORIFICATION

A part of sanctification. They differ in degree, not kind. Sanctification is the transforming process God works in us to mold us into Christ’s image. Glorification is the end result. “Sanctification is glory begun. Glory is sanctification completed” (Bruce 1985, 168).

Five Questions (8:31–39)

“What, then, shall we say in response to this?” (Romans 8:31). With this question Paul inserts a transition, as he did in Romans 4:1; 6:1, 15; and 7:7. “In response to this” refers back to the five convictions (8:28) and the five affirmations (8:29–30) we have just examined. The five questions he now poses conclude his argument. They are designed to challenge anyone who might contest the certainties and claims he makes about God’s faithfulness. These five questions are as follows:

1. If God is for us, who can be against us? (Romans 8:31). What does Paul mean by this question? Clearly, he had enemies. The world opposed him and his message, just as it opposes believers today. And yes, the devil was against him as he is against us (1 Peter 5:8). We have enemies without and the flesh within (1 John 2:15–16). So what is Paul saying here? Note that Paul’s “if” in verse 31 is not an expression of doubt, for there is no uncertainty that God is for us. Boice explains that “if” as used here means “since” (1992, 2:953). In other words, “since God is for us, who can prevail against us?”

2. He who did not spare his own Son, but gave him up for us all—how will he not also, along with him, graciously give us all things? (Romans 8:32). Does this promise seem too good to be true? Will God really “give us all things”? We agree that the Creator and Ruler of the universe is omnipotent—unlimited in power and authority. He divides seas and shakes prisons to deliver His people. But we wonder, “Is He willing to help me?” Consider the leper in Matthew 8:2–3, who recognized Jesus’ power to heal but questioned His willingness to do so. Jesus removed all of his doubt, saying, “I am willing”—and immediately healed him.

How does Paul assure us that God will give us all things? He points to Calvary, saying of God, “He . . . did not spare his own Son” (Romans 8:32). This probably alludes to the story of Abraham; the word spare (Greek, pheidomai ) is the same word used in the Greek translation of the Old Testament to translate “withhold” (Genesis 22:12, 16). Abraham’s total commitment to God in not withholding his only son exemplifies what God did in giving His Son (John 3:16). Essentially, Paul reasons that if God gave us the ultimate gift of His Son, He will also give all things (of lesser value) to those who serve Him. This is the same logic he uses in Romans 5:9–10, where he goes from the greater to the lesser. If God saved us while we were His enemies, how much more will He keep us (those whom He has justified) in this life and save us from wrath.

3. Who will bring any charge against those whom God has chosen? (Romans 8:33). Enemies of the gospel constantly hurled accusations against Paul, as they did against Jesus. Likewise, charges are brought against Christians today—sometimes by false friends or even by their own conscience (1 John 3:20). Satan—the devil, “the accuser of our brothers”—accuses us before God day and night (Revelation 12:10). He accused Job (Job 1:9–11) and Joshua, the high priest (Zechariah 3:1). In both cases, however, God intervened on their behalf. In Romans 8:33, Paul indicates that no one, including Satan, can successfully bring charges “against those whom God has chosen.” Why? Because “it is God who justifies” (8:33); that is, “He is the one who has given us right standing with himself” (8:33, NLT). God, who presides over the highest court, has pronounced us “not guilty” on the basis of our faith in Jesus Christ.

4. Who is he that condemns? (Romans 8:34). There are, of course, many who seek to condemn God’s people, as our previous discussion shows. Paul’s question implies that no one can succeed in condemning God’s people. Verse 34 gives three reasons why this is true.

First: “Christ Jesus, who died.” John identified Him as “the Lamb of God, who takes away the sin of the world” (John 1:29). You will recall that earlier Paul described Him as a “sin offering” (Romans 8:3). We also read in Galatians that “Christ redeemed us from the curse of the law by becoming a curse for us” (Galatians 3:13). Consequently, we are no longer under condemnation.

Second: “Jesus Christ . . . was raised to life” (Romans 8:34). This confirmed God’s acceptance of the “sin offering” (8:3). Jesus Christ “was raised to life for our justification” (4:25).

Third: Jesus Christ “is at the right hand of God and is also interceding for us” (Romans 8:34). No one can successfully condemn God’s people because our great High Priest intercedes for us (Hebrews 4:14–16; 7:24).

Why can no one successfully condemn us? Jesus Christ died for us, was raised to life for us, and is now interceding for us in heaven. We might add a fourth reason: Here on earth, “the Spirit pleads for us believers in harmony with God’s own will” (Romans 8:27, NLT).

5. Who shall separate us from the love of Christ? (Romans 8:35). Can adversaries or adversities pry us apart from Christ? Can unfavorable circumstances undermine our confidence in Him? Paul cites seven possible things that might come between us and Christ. Hostile opponents, for instance, might stir up “trouble or hardship or persecution.” Paul experienced all of these in preaching the gospel. “Famine” and “nakedness” suggest adverse circumstances. Droughts, floods, or other natural disasters may at times cause hunger, poverty, and economic distress. Since Paul is addressing Christians here, however, it seems likely that he has more specific situations in mind that arise because of their faith.

Believers face “danger” and “sword” as well. Everyone faces danger in one form or another, but Christians are frequently singled out and beaten or abused. Paul’s missionary ministry was filled with danger, as he testifies: “I have been in danger from rivers, in danger from bandits, in danger from my own countrymen, in danger from Gentiles; in danger in the city, in danger in the country, in danger at sea; and in danger from false brothers” (2 Corinthians 11:26). Sword in Romans 8:35 suggests extreme violence ending in death, as in the cases of Stephen and James. Within ten years of the time Paul wrote this letter, many Roman believers would face martyrdom under Nero. They would die not because of war or wrongdoing but because of their faith in Jesus Christ. Psalm 44:22, which Paul quotes, makes this clear: “For your [God’s] sake we face death all day long” (Romans 8:36).

Romans 8:37–39 has been affirmed by Christians through the ages. In these verses, Paul declares victoriously:

No, in all these things we are more than conquerors through him who loved us. For I am convinced that neither death nor life, neither angels nor demons, neither the present nor the future, nor any powers, neither height nor depth, nor anything else in all creation, will be able to separate us from the love of God that is in Christ Jesus our Lord.

In all these things we are more than conquerors through him who loved us (Romans 8:37). We follow Paul’s logic leading up to his quote from Psalm 44:22, “We are considered as sheep to be slaughtered” (Romans 8:36). But then he jolts us by saying, “We are more than conquerors.” It seems absurd that helpless sheep can conquer. Has Paul suddenly become illogical? Not at all. His imagery is not preposterous, but precise. Conquest comes not through sheep power but through Shepherd power! We conquer through Him who loved us. And, as Paul concludes, nothing is “able to separate us from the love of God that is in Christ Jesus our Lord”!

What a powerful conclusion to a great chapter. Truly, God is faithful, and God’s people are more than conquerors! I suggest that you reread the introduction to this chapter. Now that you have studied Romans 8, have you been encouraged, strengthened, and built up in your Christian faith? In the face of troubles, hardship, persecution, famine, nakedness, danger, or sword, can you now affirm with the apostle Paul: “In all these things we are more than conquerors through him who loved us”? He is indeed our source of victory.

**Q:** Read Matthew 12:49–50. How does Jesus identify the members of His family in this passage?

**A:** His family consists of “whoever does the will of my Father in heaven.”

**Q:** Summarize each of Paul’s five questions, and answer each one as he would.

**A:** QUESTION ANSWER a Since God is for us, who can be against us? No one! b If God freely gave His Son, will He not freely give us all things? He is willing to do so. c Can anyone successfully bring charges against God’s chosen ones? No one! d Can anyone succeed in condemning God’s people? No one! e Can anything separate us from the love of God? Nothing! UNIT 3

#### 5.3.3 — Analyze five questions Paul poses in Romans 8:31–39 to determine their effectiveness in supporting his argument that God is faithful to His people.

This final lesson reaffirms God’s faithfulness to His people. Stott notes that Paul discusses this from three perspectives. First, “five unshakable convictions” (Romans 8:28) show God working in behalf of His people. Second, “five undeniable affirmations (8:29–30) identify the successive stages of God’s saving purpose from eternity to eternity.” Third, “five unanswerable questions” (8:31–39) challenge “anybody to contradict the convictions and affirmations which he [Paul] has just expressed” (1992, 246).

As we conclude this lesson and our study of Romans 8, let us look at Paul’s five convictions, five affirmations, and five unanswerable questions.

Five Convictions (8:28)

As we noted above, God works in behalf of His people. Paul is so thoroughly convinced of God’s faithfulness that he begins his next statement by asserting, “ And we know that in all things God works for the good of those who love him, who have been called according to his purpose” (Romans 8:28, italics added). He does not present the unknown but the known. In like manner, John in his first Epistle stresses what believers know. He uses two different Greek words translated “know” forty-two times, underscoring the certainty a believer has in Christ. In Romans 8:28, Paul gives us five certainties about God and His faithfulness.

As we ponder the certainties in this well-known verse, keep in mind the context. Romans 8:28 is not an isolated principle; rather, it is tied directly to the preceding verses and the intercessory work of the Spirit in and through His people. Spirit-enabled intercession introduces God’s active intervention into adverse and even impossible situations for the good of His people. Here are the five convictions expressed in this verse:

1. God works in the lives of His people. Paul’s strongly held belief is rooted in Scripture. The pages of the Old Testament are filled with testimonies of God’s intervention in behalf of His people.

2. God works for the good of His people. Jesus came to reveal the Father and do what the Father does (Matthew 11:27; John 10:38; 14:9). With this in mind, consider Peter’s message to Cornelius and his household. Like Paul, he began his message with certainty. “You know . . . how God anointed Jesus of Nazareth with the Holy Spirit and power, and how he went around doing good” (Acts 10:37–38).

3. God works for the good of His people in all things . All things includes times of adversity, evil circumstances, and times of walking “through the valley of the shadow of death” (Psalm 23:4). Paul does not say that we understand everything which happens to us or that our circumstances are without pain. But as Joseph said to his brothers, “You intended to harm me, but God intended it for good . . . the saving of many lives” (Genesis 50:20), so also Paul affirms that God works for our good “in all things.”

4. God works for the good of those who love Him . This benefit—God working for the good in all things—is specifically promised to “those who love him.” What comfort this provides in those times of adversity that we do not understand.

5. God works for the good of those who have been called according to His purpose . Here God narrows His promise even further by specifically linking it to those He has chosen for His purpose. God did not send evil circumstances into Joseph’s life; but He foreknew that they would occur, worked for Joseph’s good in them, and ultimately fulfilled His purpose in this son of Jacob.

God’s ultimate purpose in working in all things for our good (those who love Him) is to conform us “to the likeness of His Son” (Romans 8:29). In a similar way, John looked to the future with confidence and certainty when he wrote, “We know that when he appears, we shall be like him” (1 John 3:2). To this he adds a note in line with Paul’s exhortation to put to death the misdeeds of the body (Romans 8:13): “Everyone who has this hope in him purifies himself, just as he is pure” (1 John 3:3). Believers are not perfect; they are being conformed into Christ’s likeness. Nevertheless, sin should not characterize their lives.

At times, God permits evil circumstances in the lives of His people, but He is not the source of evil. Job and Joseph are distinct Old Testament examples of God working for the good of His people when they encounter adversity.

Five Affirmations (8:29–30)

“For those God foreknew he also predestined to be conformed to the likeness of his Son, that he might be the firstborn among many brothers. And those he predestined, he also called; those he called, he also justified; those he justified, he also glorified” (Romans 8:29–30). Paul summarizes God’s purpose in five words: (1) foreknowledge, (2) predestination, (3) calling, (4) justification, and (5) glorification. Boice calls these words “a golden chain of five links” (1992, 2:786). These linking affirmations stretch from eternity to eternity and are unbreakable. They assure us of God’s unfailing love. This is illustrated in the graphic below, which is followed by a brief explanation of the five words.

Let us also consider Paul’s words, “that he might be the firstborn among many brothers” (Romans 8:29). Since we are God’s children, we are Christ’s brothers and sisters. As the “firstborn,” He is preeminent, holding the place of highest honor in God’s family.

FOREKNOWLEDGE

An aspect of God’s omniscience. He knows all things—past, present, future. He knew us before we were born. Before the creation of the world, He knew who would respond to His offer of salvation and who would reject it. He chose us according to His foreknowledge. (See Ephesians 1:4–5; 2 Peter 1:2.)

PREDESTINATION

God planned what He would do for those who would become His children. He determined beforehand that they would be conformed to the likeness of His Son (Romans 8:29). Paul uses this term in relation to believers, not unbelievers. The Bible does not teach that God predestines people to eternal condemnation. God does not want anyone to perish (2 Peter 3:9; John 3:18–21).

CALLING

God calls us through the gospel to “share in the glory of our Lord Jesus Christ” (2 Thessalonians 2:13–14). He invites all to come to Him.

JUSTIFICATION

God justifies those who believe the gospel. Freligh defines justification as “that act of God’s grace whereby He declares righteous the person who places faith in Jesus Christ as his Substitute and Savior” (1962, 69). In other words, Christ’s righteousness is imputed to the believer.

GLORIFICATION

A part of sanctification. They differ in degree, not kind. Sanctification is the transforming process God works in us to mold us into Christ’s image. Glorification is the end result. “Sanctification is glory begun. Glory is sanctification completed” (Bruce 1985, 168).

Five Questions (8:31–39)

“What, then, shall we say in response to this?” (Romans 8:31). With this question Paul inserts a transition, as he did in Romans 4:1; 6:1, 15; and 7:7. “In response to this” refers back to the five convictions (8:28) and the five affirmations (8:29–30) we have just examined. The five questions he now poses conclude his argument. They are designed to challenge anyone who might contest the certainties and claims he makes about God’s faithfulness. These five questions are as follows:

1. If God is for us, who can be against us? (Romans 8:31). What does Paul mean by this question? Clearly, he had enemies. The world opposed him and his message, just as it opposes believers today. And yes, the devil was against him as he is against us (1 Peter 5:8). We have enemies without and the flesh within (1 John 2:15–16). So what is Paul saying here? Note that Paul’s “if” in verse 31 is not an expression of doubt, for there is no uncertainty that God is for us. Boice explains that “if” as used here means “since” (1992, 2:953). In other words, “since God is for us, who can prevail against us?”

2. He who did not spare his own Son, but gave him up for us all—how will he not also, along with him, graciously give us all things? (Romans 8:32). Does this promise seem too good to be true? Will God really “give us all things”? We agree that the Creator and Ruler of the universe is omnipotent—unlimited in power and authority. He divides seas and shakes prisons to deliver His people. But we wonder, “Is He willing to help me?” Consider the leper in Matthew 8:2–3, who recognized Jesus’ power to heal but questioned His willingness to do so. Jesus removed all of his doubt, saying, “I am willing”—and immediately healed him.

How does Paul assure us that God will give us all things? He points to Calvary, saying of God, “He . . . did not spare his own Son” (Romans 8:32). This probably alludes to the story of Abraham; the word spare (Greek, pheidomai ) is the same word used in the Greek translation of the Old Testament to translate “withhold” (Genesis 22:12, 16). Abraham’s total commitment to God in not withholding his only son exemplifies what God did in giving His Son (John 3:16). Essentially, Paul reasons that if God gave us the ultimate gift of His Son, He will also give all things (of lesser value) to those who serve Him. This is the same logic he uses in Romans 5:9–10, where he goes from the greater to the lesser. If God saved us while we were His enemies, how much more will He keep us (those whom He has justified) in this life and save us from wrath.

3. Who will bring any charge against those whom God has chosen? (Romans 8:33). Enemies of the gospel constantly hurled accusations against Paul, as they did against Jesus. Likewise, charges are brought against Christians today—sometimes by false friends or even by their own conscience (1 John 3:20). Satan—the devil, “the accuser of our brothers”—accuses us before God day and night (Revelation 12:10). He accused Job (Job 1:9–11) and Joshua, the high priest (Zechariah 3:1). In both cases, however, God intervened on their behalf. In Romans 8:33, Paul indicates that no one, including Satan, can successfully bring charges “against those whom God has chosen.” Why? Because “it is God who justifies” (8:33); that is, “He is the one who has given us right standing with himself” (8:33, NLT). God, who presides over the highest court, has pronounced us “not guilty” on the basis of our faith in Jesus Christ.

4. Who is he that condemns? (Romans 8:34). There are, of course, many who seek to condemn God’s people, as our previous discussion shows. Paul’s question implies that no one can succeed in condemning God’s people. Verse 34 gives three reasons why this is true.

First: “Christ Jesus, who died.” John identified Him as “the Lamb of God, who takes away the sin of the world” (John 1:29). You will recall that earlier Paul described Him as a “sin offering” (Romans 8:3). We also read in Galatians that “Christ redeemed us from the curse of the law by becoming a curse for us” (Galatians 3:13). Consequently, we are no longer under condemnation.

Second: “Jesus Christ . . . was raised to life” (Romans 8:34). This confirmed God’s acceptance of the “sin offering” (8:3). Jesus Christ “was raised to life for our justification” (4:25).

Third: Jesus Christ “is at the right hand of God and is also interceding for us” (Romans 8:34). No one can successfully condemn God’s people because our great High Priest intercedes for us (Hebrews 4:14–16; 7:24).

Why can no one successfully condemn us? Jesus Christ died for us, was raised to life for us, and is now interceding for us in heaven. We might add a fourth reason: Here on earth, “the Spirit pleads for us believers in harmony with God’s own will” (Romans 8:27, NLT).

5. Who shall separate us from the love of Christ? (Romans 8:35). Can adversaries or adversities pry us apart from Christ? Can unfavorable circumstances undermine our confidence in Him? Paul cites seven possible things that might come between us and Christ. Hostile opponents, for instance, might stir up “trouble or hardship or persecution.” Paul experienced all of these in preaching the gospel. “Famine” and “nakedness” suggest adverse circumstances. Droughts, floods, or other natural disasters may at times cause hunger, poverty, and economic distress. Since Paul is addressing Christians here, however, it seems likely that he has more specific situations in mind that arise because of their faith.

Believers face “danger” and “sword” as well. Everyone faces danger in one form or another, but Christians are frequently singled out and beaten or abused. Paul’s missionary ministry was filled with danger, as he testifies: “I have been in danger from rivers, in danger from bandits, in danger from my own countrymen, in danger from Gentiles; in danger in the city, in danger in the country, in danger at sea; and in danger from false brothers” (2 Corinthians 11:26).
