11:33–36

With Paul’s emphasis on Israel, we should not be surprised that he cites Scripture extensively. Old Testament quotations make up 30 percent of the material in these three chapters, and 40 percent of these quotes come from the prophet Isaiah (Edwards 1992). These chapters deal with Israel’s election, unbelief, and ultimate restoration. Together, they unfold Israel’s “divine purpose in history” (C. H. Dodd, as cited in Bruce 1985, 174).

What prompts Paul to write these chapters? Obviously, he is distressed about Israel’s rejection of her Messiah. But he is also concerned about God’s justice. Will people mistakenly conclude that God is unjust because of Israel? Therefore, Paul explains Israel’s election and defends God’s justice. The increasing Gentile dominance in the church also raises concerns about Jewish-Gentile relationships. Are Gentiles possibly looking down on their Jewish brothers and sisters? This prods Paul to correct any false notions of superiority and show how God values all people. Finally, he seeks to reconcile Israel’s unbelief with God’s declared purpose to bless the entire world through them.

With this preview, let us now address the subjects covered by Paul in chapters 9–11: Israel’s election, Israel’s unbelief, and Israel’s restoration.

The major theme of Romans 9 is Israel’s election. Verses 4 and 5 describe the ensuing benefits of this election. Yet, tragically, the nation for the most part rejected the promised Messiah. This troubled Paul (9:1–3) and perplexes us. Are not these Abraham’s descendants—the people God blessed and entrusted with His Word? How could they possibly refuse the gospel? Did God’s plan fail? Does this cast doubt on God’s integrity? Paul feels constrained to explain this tragedy and defend God’s actions; so he uses an analogy from the potter’s house to illustrate the concept of election. Then he brings Israel’s conduct into perspective with an explanation of the remnant.

Paul’s Concern for Israel (9:1–4)

In Romans 9:2–4, Paul expresses his concern for Israel: “I have great sorrow and unceasing anguish in my heart. For I could wish that I myself were cursed and cut off from Christ for the sake of my brothers, those of my own race, the people of Israel.” Paul loved his brothers and sisters—those of his own race—despite the fact that they considered him a traitor. They wondered, “How could a true Jew minister to Gentiles, and why did he contradict Moses?” (See Acts 21:28; 22:21–22.) These were false conclusions, of course, but this reveals the Jews’ failure to understand either Paul or his message. More importantly, it reveals their failure to understand Scripture.

Israel’s rejection of her Messiah deeply troubled Paul, causing him to wish that he, himself, were “cursed [devoted to eternal destruction] and cut off from Christ” for the sake of his brothers, if that could possibly save them (Romans 9:2–3). Obviously, Christ’s sacrifice alone can save, so Paul is not making a theological statement. He is simply expressing his deep love for his own people. Reflect for a moment on Moses’ intercession with God for the Israelites after they had made the golden calf: “But now, please forgive their sin—but if not, then blot me out of the book you have written” (Exodus 32:32). Compare this also to Samuel’s grief over King Saul (1 Samuel 15:35).

God’s Blessings on Israel (9:4–5)

Earlier, Paul had asked, “What advantage, then, is there in being a Jew?” (Romans 3:1). Then he answered, “They have been entrusted with the very words of God” (3:2). Now, in Romans 9:4–5, he describes more fully the benefits God bestowed on Israel.

With this list of benefits, Paul confirms that by adoption the people of Israel were chosen to be God’s special children. The divine glory is a reference to God’s presence among them, and the covenants are a reference to those made or confirmed with Abraham, Moses, and David. The Israelites were the ones privileged to receive the Law from God and to give service to God in temple worship. They were the recipients of the promises of God, and it was their ancestors—Abraham, Isaac, and Jacob—through whom the foundation for these privileges was put in place. Most important of all, the human ancestry of Jesus Christ is traced through them: “Christ, who is God over all, forever praised!” (Romans 9:5). Christ is the source of all of Israel’s blessings and privileges and of ours as well. This is one of the clearest assertions in the New Testament of the deity of Jesus Christ.

“Christ, who is God over all, forever praised! Amen.” (Romans 9:5)

God’s Selection of Israel (9:6–22)

Israel’s election immediately confronts us with problems. A major hurdle, as we have seen, is the Jews’ unbelief. God chose them and bestowed on them many benefits, such as entrusting them with His Word. So their unbelief puzzles us. We wonder, “Did this frustrate God’s plan? What does this say about God?” Consequently, Paul seems compelled to defend God’s sovereign acts. Concerning Paul’s defense, R. Kent Hughes observes, “Technically, this is called a ‘ theodicy ’—a vindication of the justness of God” (1991, 174).

Paul answers four questions, either direct or implied, as he explains Israel’s election and defends God’s justness. Interwoven with these questions and answers are the stories of Isaac, Jacob, and Pharaoh. The four questions are these (Stott 1994, 263):

1. Has God’s Word failed? (Romans 9:6)

2. Is God unjust in His sovereign choices? (9:14)

3. Why does God still blame us? (9:19)

4. What conclusion do we come to? (9:30)

Has God’s Word Failed?

“It is not as though God’s Word had failed” (Romans 9:6). Paul had assured the Romans that God is faithful (8:28–30). But they wonder, “What about the Jews that God had chosen? Did He keep His promises to them? Why do they seem to be set aside? If God was unfaithful to them, what hope do we have? Can we depend on Him?” Paul endeavors to correct these misconceptions and at the same time affirm God’s faithfulness.

“For not all who are descended from Israel are Israel. Nor because they are his descendants are they all Abraham’s children” (Romans 9:6–7). This is an important point in Paul’s argument. There are two Israels: the natural and the spiritual. The first Israel—those “who are descended from Israel”—refers to Abraham’s physical descendants through Isaac and Jacob (also called Israel). Abraham had many sons. Hagar bore Ishmael, while Sarah bore Isaac. Then, after Sarah’s death, Abraham married Keturah and had six more sons. But from “the natural children” (9:8), God chose Isaac, as He promised to Abraham. “At the appointed time I will return, and Sarah will have a son” (9:9); and again: “It is through Isaac that your offspring will be reckoned” (9:7; see Genesis 17:19–21; 18:10, 14; 21:12; 25:5).

Paul describes what is referred to as the second Israel—those who “are Israel” (Romans 9:6)—as “Abraham’s children” (9:7), “God’s children . . . the children of the promise” (9:8), and “the remnant” (9:27). The hearts of these Israelites were right before God. Paul wrote earlier, “For you are not a true Jew just because you were born of Jewish parents or because you have gone through the Jewish ceremony of circumcision. No, a true Jew is one whose heart is right with God” (2:28–29, NLT). By differentiating between these two Israels, Paul shows that unbelieving Jews do not nullify the purposes of God. In his letter to the Galatians, Paul makes this same distinction between Abraham’s physical and spiritual descendants. In John 8:31–47, we see Jesus doing the same thing in His clash with the Jews. People of all nations who belong to Christ are true children of Abraham (Galatians 3:29).

Not only that, but Rebekah’s children had one and the same father, our father Isaac. Yet, before the twins were born or had done anything good or bad—in order that God’s purpose in election might stand: not by works but by him who calls—she was told, “The older will serve the younger.” Just as it is written: “Jacob I loved, but Esau I hated.” (Romans 9:10–13)

The older will serve the younger (Romans 9:12). Ancient custom dictated that the younger son was subservient to the older. Thus verse 12 is contrary to the practice of that era. While God chose Jacob over Esau, this verse does not focus on them as individuals but rather on their descendants. The New Living Translation makes this clear: “The descendants of your older son will serve the descendants of your younger son” (9:12).

Before Rebekah’s twins were born, they struggled with each other in her womb (Genesis 25:21–23). In answer to her query, the Lord said, “Two nations are in your womb, and two peoples from within you . . . one people will be stronger than the other, and the older will serve the younger” (25:23). In reality, Esau never served Jacob, but his descendants did (Bruce 1985). For instance, three Old Testament passages—2 Samuel 8:14; 1 Kings 22:47; and 2 Kings 14:7—disclose that the Edomites (Esau’s descendants) were in servitude to Israel or Judah (Jacob’s descendants).

This prophetic word reveals God’s foreknowledge in predestination. God chose Jacob over Esau before either of them “had done anything good or bad” (Romans 9:11). His decision was not based on behavior or character; rather, He made this choice so that His “purpose in election might stand: not by works but by him who calls” (9:11–12).

Earlier, Paul talked about those “who have been called according to his purpose” (Romans 8:28). Now he speaks of God’s “purpose in election”—to restore a fallen creation. Thus, right choices are critical. As Edwards observes, “Paul is not discussing the eternal salvation of individuals, but [he is discussing] God’s purposeful choices in history from Abraham to Christ.” He goes on to explain, “The question is how God operates in history rather than what is the final fate of individuals or nations” (1992, 233).

“Jacob I loved, but Esau I hated” (Romans 9:13; Malachi 1:2–3). Bible scholars explain this quote in various ways. For instance, Mounce describes it as a Semitic idiom that intensifies the comparison by expressing it in absolute terms (1995, 199). The NIV Study Bible note for this verse says it is equivalent to “Jacob I chose, but Esau I rejected” (1985, 1719). Others point to Moses’ admonition—“Do not abhor an Edomite, for he is your brother” (Deuteronomy 23:7)—to demonstrate that hated is used in a relative sense and means “to love less.” This idea is supported by a comparison with the way Jesus uses the words love and hate. Jesus insisted that one who comes after Him must love Him more than even one’s own family (Matthew 10:37–39; Luke 14:25–27). As we have already observed, Bruce (1985) explains that verses 12 and 13 refer to nations, not to individuals. The Edomites, descendants of Esau, incurred God’s wrath because of their hostility toward Israel. (For example, see Psalm 137:7; Isaiah 34:5–17; Jeremiah 49:7–22; Ezekiel 25:12–14; 35:1–15; Obadiah 1:1–16.)

In his brief prophecy, Obadiah gives us further insight into “Jacob I loved, but Esau I hated” (Romans 9:13). He foretells God’s judgment on Edom. The centuries-long struggle between Jacob and Esau began in the womb (Genesis 25:22), intensified through a grudge (27:41), and reached a climax in 586 BC when Jerusalem fell to the Babylonians. Note especially verses 10–14 of Obadiah’s prophecy. While some scholars say Obadiah may have had in mind the invasion of the Philistines and Arabians (2 Chronicles 21:16–17), it seems most likely that he refers to the invasion of Jerusalem by the Babylonians (2 Kings 24–25). It is clear why God judged Edom. The Edomites rejoiced over Judah’s misfortune, gloated over the destruction of Jerusalem, looted the people’s homes to enrich themselves, killed fugitives, and captured survivors and handed them over to their enemies.

We can conclude, then, that Romans 9:13 simply states God’s selection of Israel and rejection of Edom. Edwards notes: “It says nothing about salvation or damnation of individuals in the respective groups. Although Edom as a nation was rejected, Edomites were beyond neither Israel’s compassion nor God’s” (1992, 234).

Is God Unjust in His Choices?

Is God partial in choosing one and rejecting another? “Is God unjust? Not at all!” (Romans 9:14). Paul upholds God’s justice by declaring His mercy: “I will have mercy on whom I have mercy, and I will have compassion on whom I have compassion” (9:15; Exodus 33:19). Here is assurance that “in his behavior toward humanity God will be true to his character of love and justice” (Edwards 1992, 237). God acts as He pleases, without being arbitrary, capricious, or unreasonable.

How does God’s mercy relate to His judgments? Moses wrote that God hardened Pharaoh’s heart (Exodus 4:21; 7:3; 9:12; and others), and Paul says, “He hardens whom he wants to harden” (Romans 9:18). Does this sound unfair? Leon Morris observes, “Neither here nor anywhere else is God said to harden anyone who had not first hardened himself” (as cited in Stott 1994, 269). The entire story makes it clear that Pharaoh repeatedly resisted God. “Who is the Lord,” he demanded, “that I should obey him and let Israel go?” (Exodus 5:2). By persisting in his own stubborn way, he hardened his own heart—the price of rejecting God. Compare this with Paul’s earlier teaching about judgment (“God gave them over . . .”; Romans 1:24, 26, 28).

God displayed power and spread His fame through Pharaoh (Romans 9:17). At the same time, He strengthened the Israelites by demonstrating His care and protection of them. But God did not provoke Pharaoh to do evil and then punish him for it; nor did He predestine Pharaoh to everlasting punishment. Pharaoh hardened his own heart by resisting God and pursuing his own way. News of Israel’s victory terrified neighboring nations (Joshua 2:8–11).

With a display of great power, God confirmed His Word as Moses declared it. Yet Pharaoh refused to listen to Moses, acknowledge God, or free the Israelites. The plagues reveal God’s extraordinary patience. He allows time for repentance because He does not want anyone to perish (2 Peter 3:9). Even in judgment God shows mercy, bearing “with great patience the objects of his wrath” (Romans 9:22).

Why Does God Still Blame Us?

In Romans 9:19–29, Paul addresses the next question. “Why does God still blame us? For who resists his will?” (9:19). Is God just in making decisions and then holding us accountable? Paul poses this question to those who argue with their Creator: “Who are you, O man, to talk back to God? ‘Shall what is formed say to him who formed it, “Why did you make me like this?”’ Does not the potter have the right to make out of the same lump of clay some pottery for noble purposes and some for common use?” (9:20–21).

Paul repudiates the notion that God is answerable to mere humanity, and his analogy of the potter reinforces it. The potter illustrates God’s sovereignty—His right to do as He pleases. But is Paul inconsistent? Does he contradict himself by stressing God’s sovereignty on the one hand, and humankind’s responsibility on the other? Whether or not we can fully understand or explain this antinomy —these seemingly contradictory positions that appear equally necessary and reasonable—they are nevertheless consistent with Scripture.

Perhaps an illustration will be helpful to clarify what we have just said. Clay vessels formed by the potter suggest service, and they remind us of how God chose Paul and set him apart for service (Galatians 1:15–16). Consider Paul’s comment to Timothy about the utensils in a wealthy home (2 Timothy 2:20–21). Expensive ones are reserved for special occasions, while cheaper ones are used every day. “If you keep yourself pure, you will be a utensil God can use for his purpose. Your life will be clean, and you will be ready for the Master to use you for every good work” (2:21, NLT). Thus, we recognize God’s sovereignty and at the same time accept our responsibility to keep ourselves pure. Submission to His will and purpose determines our usefulness in His kingdom.

Next, Paul refers to the objects of God’s mercy. As we have seen, God in His mercy chose the people of Israel; but He extends His mercy even further to include all people: “the objects of his mercy, whom he prepared in advance for glory—even us, whom he also called, not only from the Jews but also from the Gentiles” (Romans 9:23–24).

Now Paul reminds his readers that Hosea’s life and ministry illustrate God’s mercy (Romans 9:25–26). Gomer—the prophet’s adulterous wife—symbolized Israel’s idolatry and unfaithfulness to God (Hosea 1–3). This symbolism also extended to Hosea’s children. The Lord instructed Hosea to name his first child Jezreel, meaning “God sows.” The second was named Lo-Ruhamah, meaning “not loved.” The third child was named Lo-Ammi, meaning “not my people.” These children served as a living declaration of God’s message to His rebellious people. They would be scattered like sown seed, unloved and rejected as His people.

Eventually, God restored these rebels, saying, “I will call them ‘my people’ who are not my people; and I will call her ‘my loved one’ who is not my loved one” (Romans 9:25). Paul takes Hosea’s original message of Israel’s spiritual restoration and applies it to the Gentiles. As God restored Israel, He now extends His mercy to sinful Gentiles. Those who receive Christ as Savior are saved and forgiven, and they become His people.

“Though the number of the Israelites be like the sand by the sea, only the remnant will be saved” (Romans 9:27; see Isaiah 10:22). The remnant underscores Paul’s earlier comment, “Not all who are descended from Israel are Israel” (Romans 9:6). In our study of Romans 11, we will examine more fully the significance of the remnant.

What Shall We Say in Conclusion?

“What then shall we say?” (Romans 9:30). Paul concludes that Israel stumbled because she pursued righteousness by works, not by faith. In contrast, the Gentiles obtained righteousness because they believed. We will examine these contrasting ways of righteousness in greater detail in the next section.

**Q:** Romans 9:4–5 indicates that God granted many privileges to Israel. List here the benefits Paul cites in this passage.

**A:** All of these: The adoption as sons (God’s special children); divine glory (God’s presence among them); the covenants (with Abraham, Moses, and David); the Law; temple worship (service to God); the promises; the patriarchs (their ancestors Abraham, Isaac, and Jacob, through whom the foundation for these privileges was put in place), and through them the human ancestry of Christ

**Q:** In Romans 9:6, contrast those who “are descended from Israel” with those who “are Israel.” What point is Paul trying to make here?

**A:** Those who “are descended from Israel” are Abraham’s natural, physical descendants through Isaac and Jacob. Those who “are Israel” are the “true Jews” who have put their trust in God and His promises and who are being changed by His Spirit.

**Q:** What distinguished Abraham’s spiritual descendants from his natural descendants?

**A:** Belief in God and His promises

**Q:** What is the intent of Jesus’ words in Matthew 10:37–39 and Luke 14:25–27?

**A:** To emphasize that a true disciple of Jesus should love Jesus Christ more than anyone else, including his or her family.

**Q:** Reread Romans 8:28 and 9:11–13. With regard to God’s sovereign choice, briefly explain what these verses illustrate concerning God’s foreknowledge and predestination.

**A:** Your answer may include these ideas: God chose Jacob in order that His “purpose in election might stand [endure]” (Romans 9:11). He was called according to God’s purpose to be an instrument in the divine plan to restore fallen humanity. God knows those who will choose to follow Him, but He does not predestine one person to salvation and another to damnation.

**Q:** Describe a practical lesson you have derived from the Jacob-Esau relationship as discussed in this section.

**A:** Your answer may be similar to this: One practical lesson is the bitter harvest of bearing a grudge. How do we explain Edom’s gloating and hostility toward its close relatives during the invasion of Jerusalem by foreigners (as described by Obadiah)? This deep-seated animosity of the Edomites suggests that Esau passed on to his descendants his bitterness and resentment toward his brother Jacob. The end result was God’s judgment and their destruction.

**Q:** Read Romans 6:11–13 and 2 Timothy 2:20–21. Briefly summarize what is necessary for a believer to be a useful instrument in God’s kingdom.

**A:** First, refuse to let sin reign in your mortal body (Romans 6:12). Second, offer yourself to God as an instrument of righteousness (6:13). Third, keep yourself clean and pure (2 Timothy 2:20–21).

**Q:** Whom does Paul primarily refer to in Romans 9:26 as “sons of the living God”?

**A:** Gentiles who believe in Christ

**Q:** In Romans 9:29, Paul quotes Isaiah 1:9 (see also Genesis 18–19). What is Paul’s point in verse 29?

**A:** Sinful Israel, like Sodom and Gomorrah, deserved total destruction; but God in His mercy saved a remnant.

### Israel’s Unbelief (Romans 9:30–10:11)

#### 6.2.1 — Explain what Paul meant in Romans 9:30–10:10 by his references to a stumbling stone and two ways of righteousness.

Did Israel stumble because of an arbitrary divine decree? Paul moves from God’s sovereign choices to human responsibility to explain Israel’s present predicament. The Israelites insisted on pursuing righteousness in their own way rather than following God’s way. Their own God-given Scripture taught that salvation is by faith, not works. Paul discusses these two ways of righteousness and points out that by rejecting their Messiah in unbelief, they stumbled over the stumbling stone.

The Stumbling Stone (9:30–10:3)

What then shall we say? That the Gentiles, who did not pursue righteousness, have obtained it, a righteousness that is by faith; but Israel, who pursued a law of righteousness, has not attained it. Why not? Because they pursued it not by faith but as if it were by works. They stumbled over the “stumbling stone.” As it is written: “See, I lay in Zion a stone that causes men to stumble and a rock that makes them fall, and the one who trusts in him will never be put to shame.” (Romans 9:30–33)

They stumbled over the “stumbling stone” (Romans 9:32). The Messiah is described as “a stone and a rock” (9:33; Isaiah 8:14; 28:16). Our response to Him determines the kind of stone He becomes. If we reject Him, He becomes “a stone that causes men to stumble” (9:33). Peter and John told the Jewish rulers, elders, and teachers of the Law that Jesus Christ is “the stone you builders rejected” (Acts 4:11). By rejecting Christ, “they stumbled over the ‘stumbling stone’”; that is, they persisted in pursuing righteousness “not by faith but as if it were by works” (Romans 9:32). Unbelief was the root cause of their difficulty.

On the other hand, those who put their trust in Christ “will never be put to shame” (Romans 9:33). To believers, He becomes “a living stone,” a “precious cornerstone,” and the “capstone” (see Isaiah 8:13–15; 28:16; Acts 4:11; Ephesians 2:20; 1 Peter 2:4–8).

Paul draws a contrast between the Jews and the Gentiles. The Gentiles, “who did not pursue righteousness,” nevertheless obtained it because they responded to the gospel by faith (Romans 9:30). Conversely, the Jews failed, not because they pursued the wrong thing, but because they pursued it in the wrong way. Seeking God’s favor is commendable, but trying to attain it in their own way—by works (9:32)—caused them to stumble and fall.

Two Ways of Righteousness (10:1–11)

Is righteousness a reward or a gift? Paul sets forth two ways of righteousness in Romans 10: “the righteousness that is by the law” (10:5) and “the righteousness that is by faith” (10:6). The Jews pursued the first way, seeing it as a reward for human achievement. God makes the second way available as a gift to all—Jews and Gentiles alike—through faith in His Son. Christ’s redemptive work at Calvary makes this possible.

MacArthur helps our understanding of this truth and reinforces Paul’s point: “As far as the way of salvation is concerned, there are only two religions the world has ever known or ever will know . . . the religion of divine accomplishment, which is biblical Christianity, and the religion of human achievement which includes all other kinds of religion, by whatever names they may go by” (1994, 199).

Before Paul develops his teaching on these two ways of righteousness, he again expresses his deep concern for his own people. Reread Romans 9:1–3; 10:1; and 11:13–14. Although Paul could rejoice in the success of his Gentile ministry (11:13), his heart longed to see his Jewish brothers and sisters saved. “My heart’s desire and prayer to God for the Israelites is that they may be saved” (10:1). What does this say to us about the need to pray for unsaved loved ones?

Paul continues, “For I can testify about them [the Israelites] that they are zealous for God, but their zeal is not based on knowledge” (Romans 10:2). Zeal characterized Jews, and Paul exemplified it. In Galatians 1:14, he explains, “I was . . . extremely zealous for the traditions of my fathers,” and in Philippians 3:6, he emphasizes his misguided zeal: “as for zeal, persecuting the church.” He assures us that spiritual fervor is commendable (Romans 12:11) but also avows that his misdirected zeal led him astray.

Jewish zeal, Paul said, was “not based on knowledge” (Romans 10:2). Ironically, Jews had extensive knowledge—especially religious knowledge—because they had the Old Testament Scriptures, “the very words of God” (3:2). Coupled with this were the traditions of the elders—rules, interpretations, and applications of Scripture developed over the years by the rabbis. In New Testament times, these traditions were in oral form. Later, Jewish scholars put them in written form in a work comprising many volumes. Some versions of the Talmud are comparable in length to the Encyclopedia Brittanica (Edwards 1992), and rabbis typically knew large portions of it by heart! Thus their knowledge was extensive and surpassed that of other religious people, including Christians. Paradoxically, as Paul declared, they were ignorant of God’s righteousness!

“They did not know the righteousness that comes from God and sought to establish their own” (Romans 10:3). How could these well-educated Jews be ignorant of God’s righteousness? In Matthew 15:1–9, Jesus pointed to one reason for their spiritual blindness, saying, “You nullify the word of God for the sake of your tradition” (15:6). They were blinded because they valued tradition more highly than God’s Word.

Now Paul makes this further indictment of his own people: “They did not submit to God’s righteousness” (Romans 10:3). They zealously pursued their own plan instead of God’s. Paul Achtemeier makes this interesting comment about Israel: “They were so religious that they did not want to settle for something God could give them. They wanted to be religious enough so that they could become partners with God in the matter of their salvation” (as cited in Edwards 1992, 251). Edwards adds that this idea of partnership with God in salvation is still a part of Jewish theology today.

In the Sermon on the Mount, Jesus repeatedly said, “You have heard that it was said . . . But I tell you . . .” (see Matthew 5:21–22, 27–28, 31–32, 33–34, 38–39, 43–44). What did He mean? Was He contrasting His teaching with the Old Testament? Impossible, since He had just declared His intention to fulfill the Law and the Prophets, not abolish them (5:17). Jesus was pointing to popular, but erroneous, interpretations of the Law. Human logic can lead to error.

Paul understood Jewish attempts to establish their own righteousness (Romans 10:3) because he had followed the same path in seeking to merit God’s approval. He wrote, “I obeyed the Jewish law so carefully that I was never accused of any fault” (Philippians 3:6, NLT). This kind of righteousness is based on careful conformity to external standards. But all of this changed after Paul’s encounter with Christ. Legalistic righteousness—and everything else he had considered important—now became worthless, like rubbish (Philippians 3:7–9). Paul continues, “I no longer count on my own goodness or my ability to obey God’s law, but I trust Christ to save me” (3:9, NLT). Christ brought to Paul something better than he had ever had: “the righteousness that comes from God” (Romans 10:3; Philippians 3:9). This righteousness was based on Christ’s accomplishments, not his own.

I consider everything a loss compared to the surpassing greatness of knowing Christ Jesus my Lord . . . I consider them rubbish, that I may gain Christ . . . not having a righteousness of my own . . . but that which is through faith in Christ. (Philippians 3:8–9)

Paul continues in Romans 10:4: “Christ is the end of the law so that there may be righteousness for everyone who believes.” What does he mean when he says, “Christ is the end [Greek, telos ] of the law”? Telos , according to Stott, “could mean ‘end’ in the sense of ‘goal’ or ‘completion,’ indicating that the law pointed to Christ and that he has fulfilled it. Or it could mean ‘end’ in the sense of ‘termination’ or ‘conclusion,’ indicating that Christ has abrogated the law” (1994, 281).

Hendriksen (1981) prefers to translate telos as “goal” because Christ gives understanding to the meaning and substance of Old Testament law. Christ lived an exemplary life and satisfied all the conditions of the Law. This is in harmony with His own words, “Do not think that I have come to abolish the Law or the Prophets; I have not come to abolish them but to fulfill them” (Matthew 5:17).

Others favor “end” as a translation of telos, in the sense of termination. For instance, Bruce says, “Christ is the termination of the law in the sense that with Him the old order, of which the law formed part, has been done away, to be replaced by the new order of the Spirit” (1985, 203). Likewise, Hebrews 8:13 explains, “By calling this covenant ‘new,’ he has made the first one obsolete; and what is obsolete and aging will soon disappear.”

While scholars debate the merits of each view, it seems that Paul combines them. For example, consider Galatians 3:24–25: “So the law was put in charge to lead us to Christ that we might be justified by faith. Now that faith has come, we are no longer under the supervision of the law.” The law makes us “conscious of sin” (Romans 3:20) and thus aware of our need of a Savior. Therefore, when Paul writes that “the law was put in charge to lead us to Christ,” we see end in the sense of a goal or the completion of a process. And when he says, “We are no longer under the supervision of the law,” he implies termination. In this sense, Christ supersedes the Law and it is terminated (Edwards 1992).

The Contemporary English Version of Romans 10:4 gives additional clarification: “But Christ makes the Law no longer necessary for those who become acceptable to God by faith.” The Phillips version paraphrases it in this way: “For Christ means the end of the struggle for righteousness-by-the-Law for everyone who believes in Him.” This brings us back to Paul’s contrast: righteousness by the Law versus righteousness by faith.

As believers, we rejoice that we are not under law and its condemnation (Romans 6:15; 8:1). Does this mean, then, that the Law is now irrelevant? Or does the Law still have a place in the life of a believer? According to Paul, those who are in Christ fully meet (fulfill) “the righteous requirements of the law” (8:4) as the Holy Spirit enables them. Thus the Law plays a role in a Christian’s life—not as a way of salvation but as a moral and ethical guide (NIV Study Bible, 1985). Christ and the indwelling Spirit enable believers to satisfy the true intent of the Law. Two Old Testament passages foretell this:

1. Jeremiah 31:33: “I [the Lord] will put my law in their minds and write it on their hearts.”

2. Ezekiel 36:27: “And I will put my Spirit in you and move you to follow my decrees and be careful to keep my laws.”

Paul quotes Moses to explain “the righteousness that is by the law: ‘The man who does these things will live by them’” (Romans 10:5; Leviticus 18:5). Jews took this to mean that salvation is obtained by obeying the Law. While this is commendable, it is nevertheless impossible. Paul quotes these same words of Moses in Galatians to show that no one can please God by obeying the Law; in fact, anyone who tries to do so is under a curse because no one can keep the Law perfectly (see Galatians 3:10–12; James 2:10). Phillips has a good paraphrase of Romans 10:5, “Moses writes of righteousness-by-the-Law when he says that the man who perfectly obeys the law shall find life in it—which is theoretically right but impossible in practice.” Here Paul has in view the legalists who reject God’s grace and insist on pursuing righteousness by their own efforts. Righteousness by the Law is weak because humans are weak. Human effort, at best, is feeble and inadequate.

Now Paul contrasts “the righteousness that is by faith” (Romans 10:6). Biblical faith focuses on God: His promises, power, and provision. It does not demand heroic efforts or humanly impossible feats such as ascending into heaven to bring Christ down or descending into the grave to bring Christ up (10:6–7). Christ already came down to earth, became flesh, and made His dwelling among us (John 1:14). He has already risen from the place of the dead. Faith accepts what God has done for us in Jesus Christ. As Edwards says, “Our task, in other words, is not to be responsible for salvation, but to be respondable to it” (1992, 254).

“‘The word is near you; it is in your mouth and in your heart,’ that is, the word of faith we are proclaiming” (Romans 10:8). Paul takes Moses’ farewell address in Deuteronomy 30:11–14 and applies it to Christ. The similarity here between the Law and the gospel lies in their easy accessibility (Stott, 1994). Salvation is not remote or inaccessible but close at hand—as near as your mouth and heart. This “righteousness that is by faith” includes two inseparable elements: confession and belief. Saving faith, then, centers on confessing with our mouth what we believe in our heart.

“If you confess with your mouth, ‘Jesus is Lord,’ and believe in your heart that God raised him from the dead, you will be saved” (Romans 10:9). To confess (Greek, homologeo ) basically means “to say the same thing” or “to be in agreement with someone.” In this case, we say the same thing God the Father does about Jesus Christ: “He is Lord.” Thus confession with one’s mouth is important, whether it is about sin (1 John 1:9) or salvation (Romans 10:9–10); that is, we agree with God and declare the same thing He does about our sin and our salvation. Believing comes out of our innermost being. To believe means that we trust God for His provision of salvation in Jesus Christ. God gives to everyone a measure of faith (12:3, 6).

**Q:** According to Paul’s teaching, what does the “stumbling stone” describe?

**A:** What Christ becomes to those who reject Him.

**Q:** Explain what MacArthur means by “divine accomplishment” and how it relates to righteousness.

**A:** “Divine accomplishment” is a reference to biblical Christianity, which is based on one’s faith in Christ alone and not on one’s works.

**Q:** Based on what you learned in Chapter 5 about foreordination and predestination, what best explains Paul’s statement in Romans 10:1?

**A:** While God foreknows the destiny of each person, it is also true that some people respond to the gospel message as a result of the prayers of their loved ones.

**Q:** What are the two ways of righteousness Paul discusses in Romans 10:1–3 and Philippians 3:6–9?

**A:** That which is by the Law and that which is by faith

### Israel’s Restoration (Romans 10:12–11:36)

#### 6.3.1 — Summarize and evaluate Paul’s line of reasoning for Israel’s restoration in Romans 10:12–11:36.

Paul has described Israel’s election, the blessings bestowed, and the nation’s tragic rejection of the Messiah in unbelief. Does this mean that God has now rejected the Jews because they rejected His Son? We would expect this, but Paul says, “By no means!” (Romans 11:1) and points to “a remnant” as evidence: “There is a remnant chosen by grace” (11:5). Israel’s failure opened the door to the Gentiles. Then, when the full number of Gentiles has come in, Israel will turn to the Lord in faith and embrace the Messiah. Paul’s overall argument touches on four topics:

1. The need to preach the gospel (10:12–21)

2. A remnant (11:1–16)

3. God’s use of the Gentiles (11:11–15)

4. Two analogies (11:16–25)

Together, these subjects highlight Paul’s line of reasoning for Israel’s restoration.

The Necessity of Evangelism (10:12–21)

Evangelism is an essential part of God’s plan and involves the worldwide proclamation of the gospel. The gospel writers record Christ’s command to take the gospel to all nations (Matthew 28:18–20; Mark 16:15; Luke 24:46–48; John 20:21). Now Paul gives the logic for this by asking a series of questions, prefaced with a quotation from Joel: “Everyone who calls on the name of the Lord will be saved” (Romans 10:13; Joel 2:32). This is the same promise that Peter cited on the Day of Pentecost (Acts 2:21).

Paul begins his case for evangelism with the end result: those who are saved. But then he essentially asks, “What is the prerequisite of calling on the Lord?” Thus, with each part of this four-question series, he points to a prior condition necessary to fulfill that step. This is illustrated in the following chart.

Events Leading to Salvation

Romans 10:13–15

Christ sends messenger

Messenger

proclaims

gospel

Sinner

hears

gospel

Hearer

believes

gospel

Believer

calls on

the Lord

Those

who call

are saved

Natural order of events

Paul’s order of questions

Question 1. “How, then, can they call on the one they have not believed in?” (Romans 10:14). What is the basis for calling on the Lord? Believing. Yet in order to believe, the sinner needs to know about Jesus—that He died and was raised to life again for the sinner’s sins. Then, because he or she believes, the sinner calls on the Lord.

Question 2. “How can they believe in the one of whom they have not heard?” (Romans 10:14). Just as believing precedes calling, so hearing precedes believing. The sinner cannot believe in Christ until he or she has heard Him speak through His messenger. “We are therefore Christ’s ambassadors, as though God were making his appeal through us. We implore you on Christ’s behalf: Be reconciled to God” (2 Corinthians 5:20).

Question 3. “How can they hear without someone preaching to them?” (Romans 10:14). God uses men and women to proclaim the good news of Jesus Christ. While we are saved by faith in Christ and not by works, we are nevertheless “God’s workmanship, created in Christ Jesus to do good works, which God prepared in advance for us to do” (Ephesians 2:10). One of these “good works” is the proclamation of the gospel to sinners.

Question 4. “How can they preach unless they are sent?” (Romans 10:15). Who sends the messenger? Was Paul thinking of his own experience here? The word send (from the Greek word apostello ) suggests an apostle—“one who is sent.” Paul insisted that Jesus Christ, the chief apostle, had called and commissioned him. Furthermore, the church at Antioch recognized and confirmed this divine initiative. Under the direction of the Holy Spirit, the church sent Paul and Barnabas on their first missionary journey (Acts 13:1–4). Consequently, we see three agents—Jesus Christ, the Holy Spirit, and the church—involved in the sending process.

Paul testified before King Agrippa that Christ had appeared to him, appointed him as a witness, and sent him. His testimony enlarges our understanding of what the gospel accomplishes. The Lord told Paul, “I am sending you [to your own people and the Gentiles] to open their eyes and turn them from darkness to light, and from the power of Satan to God, so that they may receive forgiveness of sins and a place among those who are sanctified by faith in me” (Acts 26:17–18).

The word preach (Greek, kerusso ) in Romans 10:15 means “to announce, publish, or proclaim something.” In this passage, of course, Paul specifically speaks about the preaching of the gospel of Jesus Christ. It is not limited to a pulpit, church building, or formal setting; nor is it limited to apostles. It is the privilege of every believer, regardless of his or her surroundings, to share the good news with all people.

Essentially, then, this series of questions stresses the absolute necessity of preaching the gospel. Paul argues that there can be no salvation, no calling, no believing, no hearing, and no preaching without someone being sent.

Is there a practical aspect to sending? For example, who provides the funds for the messenger or preacher to travel to the field of ministry? Where do the messenger’s living expenses come from? What does this suggest to you about your responsibility and that of your local church?

“How beautiful are the feet of those who bring good news!” (Romans 10:15; Isaiah 52:7). The Old Testament messengers about whom Isaiah wrote announced to Jewish captives in Babylon that they would soon be released and permitted to return home. Paul applies Isaiah’s words to gospel preachers who proclaim deliverance from the bondage of sin.

In Romans 10:18, Paul poses another question: “But I ask, Did they not hear? Of course they did.” How does Paul apply this passage to Israel? How do we explain their unbelief? As he candidly states in verse 16, “Not all the Israelites accepted the good news.” Furthermore, he says, Isaiah had prophesied about their unbelief, putting it in the form of a question: “Lord, who has believed our message?” (10:16; Isaiah 53:1).

Paul continues, “Again I ask, did Israel not understand?” (Romans 10:19). Did Israel, perhaps, simply fail to comprehend the message? Paul rejects this explanation by quoting Moses and Isaiah (10:19–21; Deuteronomy 32:21; Isaiah 65:1–2). If the Gentiles—whom the Jews considered to be lacking in spiritual understanding—grasped the gospel message, then the Jews were without excuse.

Israel did not welcome the gospel. But for those who openly receive the good news of Jesus Christ, faith is awakened in their hearts. “Faith comes from hearing the message, and the message is heard through the word of Christ” (Romans 10:17).

A Remnant of Israel (11:1–16)

“I ask then, Did God reject his people? By no means! I am an Israelite myself, a descendant of Abraham, from the tribe of Benjamin. God did not reject his people, whom he foreknew” (Romans 11:1–2).

Paul asks, “Did God reject his people?” (Romans 11:1). With an emphatic “By no means!” he responds to his own question and quickly gives his reasons. He cites himself, first of all, as an example. Then he points to the faithful remnant in Elijah’s day for historical perspective. Finally, he cites a present-day remnant of true believers.

“I am an Israelite myself, a descendant of Abraham” (Romans 11:1). If God were to reject anyone, Paul would be among the most likely candidates because he persecuted Christ and His church. Thus he says, in effect, “Look at me! I am proof that God has not rejected His people.” Furthermore, he adds, “God did not reject his people, whom he foreknew” (11:2). He supports his claim by pointing to Elijah and the widespread apostasy in his day. Elijah complained, “Lord, they have killed your prophets and torn down your altars; I am the only one left, and they are trying to kill me” (11:3). God corrected Elijah by saying, “I have reserved for myself seven thousand who have not bowed the knee to Baal” (11:4).

With this, Paul reminds his readers that there was a remnant of believing Israelites even during the time of apostasy in Elijah’s day. Now he writes, “At the present time there is a remnant chosen by grace” (Romans 11:5). “At the present,” of course, speaks of the time when Paul wrote. Although Israel as a whole had rejected the gospel, nevertheless “there is a remnant”—just as in Elijah’s day. Many thousands of Jews, including large numbers of priests, had embraced the gospel (see Acts 6:7; 21:20). It remains true today; this remnant exists, not because of its good works but because of God’s grace (Romans 11:6).

Paul continues, “What Israel sought so earnestly it did not obtain, but the elect did” (Romans 11:7). This reinforces what we saw earlier in 9:30–33. Israel failed to obtain a right standing before God because the Israelites “pursued it not by faith but as if it were by works” (9:32). The elect (11:7) are the faithful remnant of Jews who received Christ. “The others were hardened” (11:7) because they repeatedly resisted God’s grace and truth (9:31–32). This hardening resulted in “a spirit of stupor ” (11:8)—that is, a spiritual numbness or insensitivity—so that they could not see or understand spiritual truth. The lesson is clear. Resisting God and going one’s own way leads to hardness and spiritual insensitivity. Moses described Israel’s resistance to God in the wilderness (Deuteronomy 29:4). Isaiah, too, spoke of the people’s spiritual deafness and blindness (Isaiah 6:9–10; 29:9–10). This spiritual stupor continued in Paul’s day.

“May their table become a snare and a trap” (Romans 11:9; Psalm 69:22–23). Paul refers to David, who was appealing to God to right the unjust wrongs inflicted on Israel by her enemies. Here, he applies David’s quotation to the Jews. Their table speaks of God’s goodness. Rather than responding with gratitude to His blessings, they resisted, and this became “a snare and a trap.” The early church used these Old Testament passages to explain Israel’s rejection of Jesus as her Messiah.

The Role of the Gentiles (11:11–15)

Again I ask: Did they [the Israelites] stumble so as to fall beyond recovery? Not at all! Rather, because of their transgression, salvation has come to the Gentiles to make Israel envious. But if their transgression means riches for the world, and their loss means riches for the Gentiles, how much greater riches will their fullness bring! (Romans 11:11–12).

“Again I ask: Did they stumble so as to fall beyond recovery?” (Romans 11:11). As in verse 1, Paul replies, “Not at all!” This is the tenth time he has used this expression in Romans. He acknowledges that Israel stumbled and fell, but insists that her fall is not “beyond recovery.” In fact, he presents an interesting course of thought showing how God uses Israel’s failure to enlarge His kingdom and eventually to restore Israel.

Here is an interrelated sequence of events: Israel’s transgression—the Gentiles’ salvation—Israel’s envy—Israel’s return—fullness and acceptance (Romans 11:11–15). First of all, Paul shows that Israel’s transgression and loss lead to the salvation and riches of the Gentiles. In turn, God’s acceptance of and blessings on the Gentiles provoke Israel to envy (11:11, 14). Finally, Israel’s envy and jealousy, in turn, lead the people to return to the Lord. Their return and acceptance will be like “life from the dead” (11:15).

Israel’s unbelief did not take God by surprise. Scripture foretells how their failure opens a door of opportunity to the Gentiles. Then, God uses the Gentiles to draw Israel back to himself. Paul records the words of God through Moses to the people of Israel: “I will make you envious by those who are not a nation; I will make you angry by a nation that has no understanding” (Romans 10:19; Deuteronomy 32:21; see also Isaiah 65:1). Thus, Paul looks ahead to the time when Israel will be saved.

Two Analogies (11:16–24)

Paul now turns from Israel’s future to her past. She has a unique heritage as God’s chosen people. In order to reinforce his argument that God has not totally rejected Israel, Paul introduces two analogies. The first is from the firstfruits offering at harvest time. The second is that of an olive tree.

“If the part of the dough offered as firstfruits is holy, then the whole batch is holy” (Romans 11:16). This comment is based on Numbers 15:17–21 (see also Leviticus 23:9–14). At harvest time, the Israelites made a cake from the first harvested grain and offered it to God. In doing this, they acknowledged God as the source of their harvest and expressed thanks for His blessings. By their consecration of the firstfruits to God, the entire harvest became holy. This essentially means that when God accepts the part, He sanctifies the whole. Paul applies the firstfruits to the patriarchs and the whole batch to the Jewish people. “And since Abraham and the other patriarchs were holy, their children will also be holy” (Romans 11:16, NLT).

“If the root is holy, so are the branches” (Romans 11:16). In verses 16–24, Paul uses the analogy of an olive tree that was sometimes used to symbolize Israel. For instance, consider Jeremiah 11:16–17 and Hosea 14:4–6. In Paul’s analogy, the root refers to the patriarchs, while the branches refer to the Jewish people. Unbelieving Jews are pictured as “branches . . . broken off” (Romans 11:17), while Gentile believers are portrayed as “a wild olive shoot” that has been “grafted in among the others and now share in the nourishing sap from the olive root” (11:17). Paul says this procedure is “contrary to nature” (11:24) because a shoot from a cultivated tree is normally grafted into a wild one to produce better fruit. While Paul acknowledges that this is an unnatural process, it nevertheless illustrates his point.

“If some of the branches have been broken off, and you, though a wild olive shoot, have been grafted in among the others and now share in the nourishing sap from the olive root, do not boast over those branches” (Romans 11:17–18). This comment is directed to Gentile believers who, perhaps, felt superior to Jews and tended to look down on them. Consequently, Paul warns them not to be arrogant (11:20) and explains, “You do not support the root, but the root supports you” (11:18).

As you may recall, Jesus told the Samaritan woman that “salvation is from the Jews” (John 4:22). The Messiah was a Jew whom Paul describes as the “seed” of Abraham (Galatians 3:16). For this reason, some scholars see the expression the root as referring to the history of salvation that extends from Abraham and the patriarchs to Christ (Edwards 1992). This root supports or gives life to Jew and Gentile alike.

“They were broken off because of unbelief, and you stand by faith” (Romans 11:20). The Jews stumbled because of unbelief; or, as in this analogy, they were branches broken off from the main tree. Unbelief has disastrous, far-reaching consequences. It determines one’s relationship to God and one’s eternal destiny. “You stand by faith” in verse 20 addresses the Gentiles. Paul admonishes them not to boast or to be arrogant because of their elevated position in relation to the broken-off branches. In reality, Jews and Gentiles alike “stand”—that is, they are established and sustained—by faith in God, not by human merit.

“Consider therefore the kindness and sternness of God: sternness to those who fell, but kindness to you, provided that you continue in his kindness” (Romans 11:22). God’s kindness coupled with His sternness presents a balanced view of God. To exclude either one distorts our understanding of Him. If we turn to Him in faith, we experience His kindness. If we turn away from Him in unbelief, we experience His sternness.

Israel Revived and Restored (11:25–31)

“I do not want you to be ignorant of this mystery” (Romans 11:25). Paul wants the Romans to understand “this mystery” so that they will “not be conceited” (11:25). Salvation is based on God’s grace, not on merit, so there is no room to boast (11:18) or to be arrogant (11:20). The word mystery (Greek, mysterion ), as used in the New Testament, describes new revelation God discloses that was formerly unknown. Paul characterizes the gospel as “the revelation of the mystery hidden for long ages past, but now revealed and made known through the prophetic writings by the command of the eternal God (16:25–26).

Here, Paul unfolds his explanation of this mystery relating to Israel in three phases: (1) Israel hardened, (2) the salvation of the Gentiles, and (3) the salvation of Israel. First, “Israel has experienced a hardening in part” (11:25). The phrase, in part, speaks of the majority of unbelieving Israelites as opposed to the believing remnant. Hardening, as Paul uses it here, differs from the Greek word he used earlier to describe the hardening of Pharaoh’s heart. The word in Romans 9:17–18 suggests “stubbornness,” while here it suggests “a dullness in understanding” (Comfort 1992, 223). The Israelites had earnestly sought a righteous standing before God, but it escaped their grasp because they refused the way of faith. Consequently, they “stumbled over the ‘stumbling stone’” (Romans 9:31–32) and became hardened (numb and insensitive) to spiritual truth (11:7–8).

Romans 11:25 also includes the second phase of this mystery: “until the full number of the Gentiles has come in.” Until indicates that this hardening is temporary. In God’s time and plan, it will end, when “the full number of the Gentiles has come in.” Jesus spoke about “the times of the Gentiles” (Luke 21:24). They now have the opportunity to receive the gospel. While Paul identifies the time, we should understand that God alone knows the precise moment in history when this will occur.

“And so all Israel will be saved” (Romans 11:26) sums up the third phase. And so suggests “in this way.” Scholars interpret all Israel in various ways. The NIV Study Bible note for this verse identifies three major views, as follows (1985, 1724):

1. It refers to elect Jews from all generations. This view considers Israel’s “fullness” (11:12) as analogous to “the full number of the Gentiles” (11:25).

2. It refers to elect Jews and Gentiles from all generations. This view includes the church.

3. It refers to the majority of Jews in the final generation.

Edwards (1992) argues that the first two views surrender the one issue Paul refuses to disclaim in these three chapters—that is, unbelieving Israel. All Israel consists of “the believing remnant plus those Jews who were hardened” (275). Earlier, in the analogy of the olive tree, Paul says, “God is able to graft them in again [the branches broken off because of unbelief]” (Romans 11:23). Those who hold the third view see all Israel as referring to the Jewish people collectively, as a unit. For instance, Bruce says all Israel does not mean “every Jew without exception, but Israel as a whole” (1985, 209).

“All Israel will be saved” (Romans 11:26) does not mean that historical Israel will be saved simply because the people are descendants of Abraham. Paul is not introducing another gospel. As Stott asserts, there is no “hint of a special way of salvation for the Jews which dispenses with faith in Christ” (1994, 304). “Only Jesus has the power to save! His name is the only one in all the world that can save anyone” (Acts 4:12, CEV).

In Romans 11:26–27, Paul cites several texts about a Redeemer to support his assertion that all Israel will be saved. “The deliverer will come from Zion [Jerusalem]; he will turn godlessness away from Jacob” (Isaiah 27:9; 59:20–21). The deliverer establishes His covenant, forgiving their sins (Romans 11:27; Jeremiah 31:33–34). This deliverer, of course, is Jesus Christ.

“They are enemies . . . they are loved” (Romans 11:28). Israel is God’s enemy (temporarily, “on your [the Gentiles] account”) as well as His loved one (“on account of the patriarchs”). The Jews became enemies when they rejected Jesus Christ and the gospel. At that time, as we observed earlier, God extended His mercy and blessings to the Gentiles (11:11). Nevertheless, God still loves the Jews because of His promises to Abraham, Isaac, and Jacob. “God’s gifts and his call are irrevocable ” (11:29); that is, they “can never be withdrawn” (11:29, NLT). In the end, God’s purpose for Israel will be fulfilled.

Now Paul returns to a major theme: God’s mercy extends to all people everywhere. “God has bound all men over to disobedience so that he may have mercy on them all” (Romans 11:32). Bruce indicates that the phrase on them all means “upon all without distinction rather than all without exception” (1985, 211). Paul is not teaching universalism . He does not say that someone who persistently rejects God will be saved in spite of his or her disobedience. God offers mercy to all through the gospel, but only those who respond in faith and receive Christ are saved.

Bound over (Greek, synkleio ) means “to lock up” or “to imprison.” (Compare with Galatians 3:22–23.) Paul says, in effect, that only those who are under condemnation can experience mercy. This is why he clearly establishes the fact that “all have sinned and fall short of the glory of God” (Romans 3:23). God desires greatly to show mercy to everyone. He “has imprisoned all people in their own disobedience so he could have mercy on everyone” (11:32, NLT).

Doxology (11:33–36)

Oh, the depth of the riches of the wisdom and knowledge of God!

How unsearchable his judgments,

and his paths beyond tracing out!

“Who has known the mind of the Lord?

Or who has been his counselor?”

“Who has ever given to God,

that God should repay him?”

For from him and through him and to him are all things.

To him be the glory forever! Amen.

Romans 11:33–36

Paul concludes this section of his letter with praise and worship to God. As he reflects on the great truths he has shared in these first eleven chapters, he is moved with gratitude and thanksgiving to God for His goodness and grace. Paul celebrates God’s wisdom and knowledge in providing a plan of salvation that is within the reach of both Jews and Gentiles. Paul’s worship of God (doxology) issues from his belief about God (theology). As Stott notes, “There can be no doxology without theology. It is not possible to worship an unknown god. All true worship is a response to the self-revelation of God in Christ and Scripture, and arises from our reflection on who he is and what he has done” (1994, 311).

**Q:** Briefly describe what you perceive to be your role in responding to the questions posed by Paul in Romans 10:14–15.

**A:** Your answer. Surely these questions should cause all believers to see that they can participate in one or more of these ways to take or send the message of salvation to all people.

**Q:** Who is the “remnant chosen by grace” in Romans 11:5?

**A:** Those Jews who embraced the gospel

**Q:** What had Israel sought so earnestly, and why did they fail to obtain it (11:7)?

**A:** Israel failed to obtain a right standing with God because the people resisted His grace and truth and relied on their own works.

**Q:** Who are “the elect” of Romans 11:7?

**A:** The faithful remnant of Jews who receive Christ

**Q:** List some truths we can glean from Paul’s inspiring doxology that reflect his theology.

**A:** Paul acknowledges the sovereignty, omnipotence, wisdom, and knowledge of God and ascribes glory to Him.

**Q:** Write a brief doxology of your own as an expression of your theology.

**A:** Your own response may include an expression of love and gratitude to our sovereign God for the “riches” of His grace. You may give thanks for the great gift of His Son that makes possible our redemption from sin’s punishment. You may praise Him because it is not by our own works of righteousness but by His blood shed for us that we are saved. He is worthy of all our praise and adoration forever. 7 CHAPTER

## 7 - Applying the Gospel: Romans 12:1–15:7

### Living the Christian Life (Romans 12:1–21)

#### 7.1.1 — Discuss practical steps a believer should take in presenting himself or herself to God.

In Chapter 6, we considered God’s plan for Israel and examined Israel’s election, unbelief, and ultimate restoration. The Israelites had experienced God’s blessings but tragically had turned away from their Messiah in unbelief. Yet Paul assures his readers that Israel has not been totally rejected—as the faithful remnant testifies. We also learned that Israel’s loss opened the door for the enrichment of the Gentiles. Paul gives the assurance that eventually, when the full number of Gentiles has come in, “all Israel will be saved” (Romans 11:26).

Now, in chapter 12, Paul urges the Romans to put their Christianity into practice. Paul’s exhortations show a striking similarity to Christ’s ethical teaching. Stott lists a number of parallels—not exact quotations by Paul but indirect references or allusions—to illustrate the resemblance (1994, 318–319). For example:

What Jesus said:

What Paul wrote in Romans :

“Bless those who curse you” (Luke 6:28).

“Bless those who persecute you; bless and do not curse” (Romans 12:14).

“Love your enemies, do good to those who hate you” (Luke 6:27).

“If your enemy is hungry, feed him” (12:20).

“In everything, do to others what you would have them do to you, for this sums up the Law and the Prophets” (Matthew 7:12).

“The commandments . . . are summed up in this one rule: ‘Love your neighbor as yourself’” (13:9).

Our salvation can be described in terms of family relationships. Through our union with Christ, we become part of God’s family and are called “children of Abraham” (Galatians 3:7), “sons of God” (3:26), and “brothers” (Romans 1:13; 7:1; 8:29; 15:14). Paul uses brothers more than one hundred times in his letters. Even Jesus, “the firstborn among many brothers” (8:29), uses family phraseology to portray the believer’s relationship to God: “Whoever does the will of my Father in heaven is my brother and sister and mother” (Matthew 12:50).

In this lesson we will consider Christian living in terms of relationships: how we relate to God, to fellow believers, and to those outside our fellowship. These relationships in turn touch on our character, our ministry, and our responsibility to governing authorities.

In Relation to God (12:1–2)

Therefore, I urge you, brothers, in view of God’s mercy, to offer your bodies as living sacrifices, holy and pleasing to God—which is your spiritual worship. Do not conform any longer to the pattern of this world, but be transformed by the renewing of your mind. Then you will be able to test and approve what God’s will is—his good, pleasing and perfect will. (Romans 12:1–2)

Paul’s counsel to believers is this: In order to put our faith into practice, each of us is to give our body, mind, and will to God. Offering ourselves to God—all that we are—allows Him to transform us into the image of His Son. Keep in mind that this is a lifetime process. Let us look at Paul’s development of this practical instruction.

In view of God’s mercy (Romans 12:1). This is where we begin—with God’s mercy . Our understanding of God and our relationship to Him arise from His mercy, regardless of who we are. Meditate for a moment on Paul’s remark near the end of Romans 11 (see Chapter 6): “God has imprisoned all people in their own disobedience” (11:32, NLT). Why is this so? It is “so that he may have mercy on them all” (11:32, NIV). Only the guilty fully appreciate God’s mercy.

Our sins have locked us up or imprisoned us. The only means of escape is through God’s mercy. Our own wisdom, goodness, and religious zeal are totally inadequate; His mercy alone provides deliverance. “He doesn’t punish us as our sins deserve” (Psalm 103:10, CEV) because He is merciful. He extends His grace to us, as well, through Jesus Christ.

Accordingly, Paul says, you (believers) “offer your bodies as living sacrifices, holy and pleasing to God—which is your spiritual worship” (Romans 12:1). This follows Paul’s earlier exhortation: “Offer yourselves to God, as those who have been brought from death to life; and offer the parts of your body to him as instruments of righteousness” (6:13). See the table below. The word offer is also translated “yield,” “give,” and “present.” Giving ourselves as instruments of righteousness for His service pleases God and opens the door to victory over sin.

A Comparison of Passages

Romans 6–8

Romans 12:1–2

Offer yourselves to God, as those who have been brought from death to life; and offer the parts of your body to him as instruments of righteousness. Romans 6:13

Offer your bodies as living sacrifices, holy and pleasing to God—this is your spiritual act of worship. Romans 12:1

Do not let sin reign in your mortal body so that you obey its evil desires. Do not offer the parts of your body to sin, as instruments of wickedness. Romans 6:12–13

Do not conform any longer to the pattern of this world. Romans 12:2

Through Christ Jesus the law of the Spirit of life set me free from the law of sin and death. You, however, are controlled not by the sinful nature but by the Spirit, if the Spirit of God lives in you. Romans 8:2, 9

Be transformed by the renewing of your mind. Romans 12:2

The words offer, sacrifice, holy, and pleasing are Old Testament terms that come from the Mosaic sacrificial system and prefigure the work of Christ. The word offer suggests the priest receiving and consecrating the offering brought to the altar by the guilty one. It became God’s possession the moment it was offered. This illustrates what happens when we offer ourselves to God. We are no longer our own, since we were bought at a price, and so we honor God in our bodies (see 1 Corinthians 6:19–20).

Living sacrifices (Romans 12:1) is a contradictory expression because sacrifice essentially means “to kill.” Obviously, the sacrificial animal died. So what does Paul mean by the term “living sacrifices”? Reflect on his earlier explanation about the believers’ union with Christ: “As surely as we died with Christ, we believe we will also live with him” (6:8, CEV). As we identify ourselves with Christ, we die to our old life. Now, as we devote ourselves fully to Him, we become “living sacrifices” that are “pleasing to God” (12:1).

How, then, can we summarize the meaning of living sacrifices ? We do so in the same way that Paul did for the Galatians when he wrote, “I have been crucified with Christ and I no longer live, but Christ lives in me. The life I live in the body, I live by faith in the Son of God, who loved me and gave himself for me” (Galatians 2:20).

Holy in Romans 12:1 describes God’s righteous nature. It also depicts a person or object that is “sanctified” or “set apart” for God’s use. In Christ we are separated from our former way of life and given over to God for His service. The word pleasing is related to an Old Testament offering. As God considered a burnt offering to be a pleasing aroma (Leviticus 1:9, 13, 17), so He considers our offering of ourselves to Him to be a pleasing fragrance and a spiritual act of worship. (Compare Ephesians 5:2 and Hebrews 13:15–16.)

“Do not conform any longer to the pattern of this world, but be transformed by the renewing of your mind” (Romans 12:2). Our relationship to God changes our relationship to the world. Translators also render world as “age” (Greek, aion ). Paul calls it the present evil age (Galatians 1:4) in contrast to the eternal age to come (Ephesians 1:21).

Satan, “the god of this age,” blinds unbelievers’ minds so they are unable to see the light of the gospel. For those who hear the gospel and believe, however, God dispels the darkness of sin and shines His light in them, just as He did at the time of creation in the beginning. (Compare Genesis 1:2–4; 2 Corinthians 4:4–6; 5:17). For those who are a “new creation” in Christ, God restores what sin destroyed, but He asks us to be involved in the process of our restoration.

Two other renditions of Romans 12:2 provide further clarification of this verse: “Don’t copy the behavior and customs of this world, but let God transform you into a new person by changing the way you think” (NLT). Or, as J. B. Phillips paraphrases the verse: “Don’t let the world around you squeeze you into its own mold, but let God remold your minds from within.” Thus we resist the way of the world and yield ourselves to God. We should neither copy nor submit to the world’s ways. This follows Paul’s earlier exhortation: “Do not offer the parts of your body to sin, as instruments of wickedness” (Romans 6:13). Since our goal is maturity in Christ, we should align ourselves with God and His will. Ask yourself, “Does my conduct conflict with—or conform to—God’s Word?”

Paul exhorts us to resist the pattern of this world and submit ourselves to God. He does not say, “Transform yourself.” Rather, he says, “(Let yourself) be transformed.” That is, it is the work of the Holy Spirit. “And as the Spirit of the Lord works within us, we become more and more like him and reflect his glory even more” (2 Corinthians 3:18, NLT).

How are we to be transformed? It is “by the renewing of your mind” (Romans 12:2). Reflect for a moment on Paul’s description of those who knew God yet refused to thank Him or glorify Him: “Their thinking became futile and their foolish hearts were darkened. Although they claimed to be wise, they became fools” (1:21–22). Compare this passage with Ephesians 4:17–19. Clearly, sin corrupted their minds.

The Holy Spirit uses the Word to renew our minds. Therefore, it is important that we saturate our minds with His thoughts and ways by reading, memorizing, and meditating on it. In this way, we gradually begin to see things from God’s perspective. (See the following graphic.)

Hiding God’s Word in Your Heart

“I have hidden your word in my heart that I might not sin against you.”

Psalm 119:11

Here are a few practical suggestions for hiding God’s Word in your heart. Word in Psalm 119:11 (Hebrew, ‘imrah) is also translated as “promise.” It provides understanding, wisdom, and nourishment to grow spiritually. And it guards against sin.

1

Read your Bible aloud. Imagine your eyes, ears, and mouth are like three highways. You carry truth from the pages of God’s Word along these roads to put in your heart.

Reading experts suggest that you follow along the margin with your finger as you move down the page. It helps you to keep your eyes on track, and if you are momentarily distracted, you will not lose your place.

2

Copy key verses the Lord impresses on your heart on something such as 3” x 5” cards that you can carry with you to review throughout the day.

The king of Israel was required to copy the Law on a scroll, keep it with him, and read it daily throughout his life (Deuteronomy 17:18–19).

3

If you have a recording device available, record Scripture and listen to it over and over. Faith comes by hearing the Word (Romans 10:17).

4

Memorize the Word. Meditate on it. “Chew” on it as a cow that ruminates (chews its cud) brings up food from its first stomach to be chewed again for greater digestion. Ask the Holy Spirit to help you digest God’s Word more fully.

As God changes the way we think, we begin to understand more clearly that His will is “good, pleasing and perfect” (Romans 12:2). This transforming process continues as we translate understanding into living.

“Continue to work out your salvation” (Philippians 2:12–13) essentially means that we do not remain passive and uninvolved in this process. Paul is not saying, “Earn your salvation,” but “Put into action God’s saving work in your lives, obeying God with deep reverence and fear” (2:12, NLT). God creates in us the desire to obey Him and provides the power for us to do so.

Let us use every avenue possible to take the truths from the pages of God’s Word and hide them in our hearts. One simple thing we can do is to read the Scriptures aloud. Our eyes, ears, and mouth then become highways that carry truth from the Word to our hearts. Through the Word we can find out what pleases the Lord (Ephesians 5:10), then do it. For example, in Ephesians 4:28 we read, “He who has been stealing must steal no longer, but must work, doing something useful with his own hands, that he may have something to share with those in need.” The thief who reads these words and takes them to heart changes his lifestyle. He stops stealing and starts doing good by supporting himself with honest work and by earning enough to help those in need.

Paul also speaks of the renewing of your mind in Romans 12:2. This involves a series of decisions. Your Christian life began with a decision to receive Christ into your life. Redemption is available to all, but only those who respond to Christ in faith are redeemed. In a similar way, the power of the Holy Spirit is available to enable each believer to live a godly life. But each one of us must seek what pleases Him, rejecting our own way and choosing His way. Step by step, then, as we walk with the Lord, we have opportunity to renew our mind and grow in grace.

Living by the Spirit brings victory. Let us be sensitive to Him and allow Him to transform our thinking and living. The attempt to conquer the sinful nature first, in the hope that it will lead to a victorious walk in the Spirit, only leads to frustration. Paul gives the right order: “Live by the Spirit, and you will not gratify the desires of the sinful nature” (Galatians 5:16). Turn on the light and the darkness will flee.

The change that takes place when the wormlike larva or caterpillar becomes a butterfly is a relatively short process. But the transforming work of the Spirit in a Christian is a lifetime process. Without question, we are born again the moment we receive Jesus Christ as our Savior, and we have eternal life. But God wants to do more—He wants to make us into the image of His Son. As we can see in the graphic below, Paul teaches this truth in several of his letters.

The Sanctifying Process
