2 Corinthians 3:18

In Relation to Other Believers (12:3–16)

The moment we receive Christ as our Savior, we enter into a new relationship with God. We rejoice that we are forgiven, justified, reconciled, and freed from condemnation. The new birth marks the beginning of a lifelong process of change as God molds us into the likeness of His Son. At the same time, we enter into a special relationship with fellow believers. Together with them, we form one body—the body of Christ.

Paul continues his discussion of Christian relationships in Romans 12:3 with these words: “Do not think of yourself more highly than you ought, but rather think of yourself with sober judgment.” How you see yourself influences your relationship with others. Do not allow pride to inflate—nor false humility to underrate—your self-appraisal. Your identity in Christ—not your position or title—gives you value. Ask God to help you use your renewed mind to evaluate yourself accurately.

Consequently, we evaluate ourselves honestly, “in accordance with the measure of faith God has given” to us (Romans 12:3). Faith , as used here, “has a rather different sense from that which it has in the earlier part of the Epistle,” says Bruce. “Here it denotes the spiritual power given to each Christian for the discharge of his special responsibility” (1985, 227–228). Comfort gives a similar explanation. The measure of faith , he says, “refers to the spiritual capacity and / or power given to each person to carry out his or her function in the church” (1994, 233). Let us consider our ministry, then, in the light of our God-given spiritual capacity. Since God is the source of our abilities—our spiritual capacity or power—(“God has given”), we have no reason to exaggerate our value, to boast, or to compare ourselves with others.

“In Christ we who are many form one body, and each member belongs to all the others” (Romans 12:5). Paul is the only New Testament writer who uses the human body to illustrate the believer’s relationship to Christ and to fellow believers (see also 1 Corinthians 12:12–31). It is “one body with many members” (Romans 12:4). As with the human body, each member has a unique task to perform; that is, they “do not all have the same function” (12:4). Diversity promotes unity rather than division because each part contributes to the healthy, harmonious functioning of the whole. Jealousy and boasting are unnecessary because each member makes its particular contribution. Christ, as the Head (Ephesians 1:22; 5:23; Colossians 1:18), directs the activities of each part of His body.

As members of Christ’s body, we function and develop best in relation to others, not in isolation. “But what about John?” you may ask. “He lived alone on the Isle of Patmos.” Yet John did not choose the life of a hermit. He was banished there because of his witness, and God obviously used those circumstances to give him a special revelation. Normally, however, we live and work in relation to others. The members of a healthy body are coordinated and move in harmony so that they can perform great tasks. And so it is with the body of Christ.

We, the body, rely on Christ to function effectively, as the vine and the branches illustrate (John 15). Branches are fruitful only when they are united with the life-giving vine. In a similar way, our fruitfulness depends on our union with Christ. “Apart from me you can do nothing” (15:5).

“We have different gifts, according to the grace given us” (Romans 12:6). Gifts (Greek, charismata ) are a form of grace (Greek, charis ). Paul links his ministry to God’s enabling grace: “For by the grace given me I say to every one of you” (12:3; see also 15:15–16 and Galatians 2:7–10).

Romans 12:6–8 lists seven gifts: (1) prophesying, (2) serving, (3) teaching, (4) encouraging, (5) contributing to the needs of others, (6) leadership, and (7) showing mercy. God gives each believer abilities to do certain things well. As each member speaks and serves, he or she contributes to the edification of the body of Christ. This list varies from those in 1 Corinthians 12 and Ephesians 4 because the emphasis in each passage is different.

Paul’s list of spiritual gifts is not exhaustive; it simply illustrates the diversity he is talking about. The gifts were never intended to establish our importance in the body of Christ, nor should we consider them to be treasured possessions—something to be hoarded. Instead, we should understand that God freely gives them to build His church and extend His kingdom.

Have you ever purchased or received a new piece of equipment? No matter how valuable it is, you must still become familiar with its functions; otherwise, you cannot operate it effectively. Gifts distributed by the Spirit do not nullify one’s need to “read the operating instructions” in God’s Word, nor do they negate one’s need for fasting, prayer, and faithfulness.

In Relation to Our Character (12:9–16)

Paul introduces Christian character alongside gifts in Romans 12, just as he does in 1 Corinthians 12–14. The effectiveness of our ministry— what we do —is enhanced or invalidated by our character— what we are . Paul does not place Christian character and spiritual gifts side by side for comparison, suggesting that we pick and choose. To debate the superiority of one over the other is to miss Paul’s point. His purpose is to show their interrelationship. Both are essential aspects of the Christian life.

The first characteristic mentioned by Paul is love : “Love must be sincere” (Romans 12:9). Love is a distinctive aspect of God’s character. Since we are sons and daughters of God, our lives should reflect this God-like trait. “By this all men will know that you are my disciples, if you love one another” (John 13:35). Love, Paul emphasizes, should be sincere , not synthetic. It should be genuine, that is, free of pretense.

In Romans 12:9–21, Paul gives practical instruction on how to practice Christian love. These Christlike characteristics are opposite to those of the unregenerate person who lives to please self and who is conformed to the world. What a contrast this is to the believer who loves God and manifests His love to others!

In Relation to Enemies (12:17–21)

In Romans 12:17–21, Paul counsels the Romans about dealing with enemies. Here the special challenge for us is not in understanding but in practicing his admonitions. We all face enemies at times, no matter how godly our lives. The apostles, and even Jesus, had enemies simply because they faithfully served God. Therefore, Paul’s advice is relevant to us all.

First, let us consider Romans 12:14: “Bless those who persecute you; bless and do not curse.” This instruction echoes our Lord’s counsel (Luke 6:27–29) and affirms His example. For those who were crucifying Him, He prayed, “Father, forgive them, for they do not know what they are doing” (Luke 23:34). As Paul dictated this advice to Tertius, he possibly reflected on a scene etched deeply in his mind—Stephen’s unforgettable prayer for his executioners: “Lord, don’t charge them with this sin!” (Acts 7:60, NLT).

“Do not repay anyone evil for evil” (Romans 12:17). Most people respond to good with good and to evil with evil. But Stephen and Jesus acted differently. Both returned good for evil. You may ask, “How does ‘eye for eye’ and ‘tooth for tooth’ fit in here?” This principle simply means that the penalty should be suitable but not go beyond the offense. Jesus cautioned His followers not to use it as an excuse for personal vengeance (see Exodus 21:24; Leviticus 24:20; Matthew 5:38–42). David provides another example. Saul and his troops tracked David and tried to kill him; yet on two occasions, David spared Saul’s life, even though it was within his power to retaliate and kill him. He refused the way of the world and the advice of friends (1 Samuel 24; 26). He exemplifies Paul’s advice: “Do not be overcome by evil, but overcome evil with good” (Romans 12:21).

Believers are to love, not hate. “Love your enemies and pray for those who persecute you” (Matthew 5:44) goes beyond human ability. God’s grace alone can empower us to exhibit this Christlike trait. Yes, even in painful circumstances, “the righteous will live by faith” (Romans 1:17).

Believers are to witness, not judge. We leave judgment in God’s hands, refusing to avenge ourselves or to “repay anyone evil for evil.” Moreover, we “leave room for God’s wrath,” because the Lord says, “It is mine to avenge; I will repay” (Romans 12:19).

Believers are to show kindness, not revenge. “If your enemy is hungry, feed him; if he is thirsty, give him something to drink. In doing this, you will heap burning coals on his head” (Romans 12:20). At first glance, this quote from Proverbs 25:21–22 might suggest, “Here is the Christian way to inflict pain on an enemy.” Of course, this idea would engage the believer in evil and contradict Paul’s counsel, “Never pay back evil for evil to anyone” (Romans 12:17, NLT). What, then, does Paul mean? An ancient Egyptian practice suggests an explanation. According to this custom, a wrongdoer carried burning coals on his head to publicly proclaim his remorse and repentance. Thus, the burning coals in Romans 12:20 symbolize the shame the wrongdoer feels as a result of the believer’s unexpected kindness. Hopefully, the kind deeds will move the wrongdoer to repent. However, we should not speculate about the response; we show kindness—regardless of the anticipated outcome—because it is the right thing to do.

“Do not be overcome by evil, but overcome evil with good” (Romans 12:21). If we respond to evil with evil, then evil has conquered us. Instead, we overthrow evil with good. God himself sets the example by demonstrating love. “While we were still sinners, Christ died for us” (5:8).

**Q:** Using the Scripture passages given in the adjacent table, summarize the difference between the life of a believer and that of an unbeliever.

**A:** Believer: Brought from death to life, an instrument of righteousness; a living sacrifice, separated from the world and sinful practices; controlled by the Spirit of God, with a renewed mind Unbeliever: Lets sin rule over him or her; obeys its evil desires; an instrument of wickedness; conforms to the pattern of the world and is controlled by the sinful nature

**Q:** Since sacrifices were a prominent part of the Mosaic Law, could anything else be more important? To answer this question, read 1 Samuel 15:22; Psalm 40:6–8; and Amos 5:21–25.

**A:** Obedience from the heart is more important than sacrifices.

**Q:** Is the principle you discovered in Study Question 2 also applicable to today’s Christian? What do we learn from 1 Corinthians 13:3 about this?

**A:** Yes, it is. Paul teaches in 1 Corinthians 13:3 that whatever great sacrifice the believer makes, he or she gains nothing if it is not done in love.

**Q:** How does Hebrews 13:15–16 describe spiritual worship?

**A:** Spiritual worship includes offering praise to God and confessing His name as well as doing good and sharing with others.

**Q:** What does Paul mean when he says, “Do not conform any longer to the pattern of this world”?

**A:** Stay away from the evil influences, customs, and behavior of worldly people.

**Q:** What does Paul mean when he says, “Be transformed”?

**A:** Submit to God and yield to the Spirit’s prompting.

**Q:** What do Romans 12:3–8 and 1 Corinthians 12:4–11 affirm about spiritual gifts?

**A:** Spiritual gifts are distributed to each believer as the Spirit determines.

**Q:** Explain your understanding of this statement: “The effectiveness of our ministry— what we do —is enhanced or invalidated by our character— what we are .”

**A:** If our actions indicate a strong Christian character, this will have a positive impact on our ministry. On the other hand, if our actions demonstrate a lack of Christian character, this will have a negative effect on our ministry. For example, suppose a minister of the gospel commits adultery and the sin is exposed. Even if he or she repents of the sin and receives forgiveness, people who trusted the minister and who benefited from his or her preaching ministry may then be inclined to distrust; as a result, his or her preaching will be ineffective.

#### 7.1.2 — Describe what Paul teaches in Romans 12:3–21 about living the Christian life in relation to one’s fellow believers, one’s personal character, and one’s enemies.

In Chapter 6, we considered God’s plan for Israel and examined Israel’s election, unbelief, and ultimate restoration. The Israelites had experienced God’s blessings but tragically had turned away from their Messiah in unbelief. Yet Paul assures his readers that Israel has not been totally rejected—as the faithful remnant testifies. We also learned that Israel’s loss opened the door for the enrichment of the Gentiles. Paul gives the assurance that eventually, when the full number of Gentiles has come in, “all Israel will be saved” (Romans 11:26).

Now, in chapter 12, Paul urges the Romans to put their Christianity into practice. Paul’s exhortations show a striking similarity to Christ’s ethical teaching. Stott lists a number of parallels—not exact quotations by Paul but indirect references or allusions—to illustrate the resemblance (1994, 318–319). For example:

What Jesus said:

What Paul wrote in Romans :

“Bless those who curse you” (Luke 6:28).

“Bless those who persecute you; bless and do not curse” (Romans 12:14).

“Love your enemies, do good to those who hate you” (Luke 6:27).

“If your enemy is hungry, feed him” (12:20).

“In everything, do to others what you would have them do to you, for this sums up the Law and the Prophets” (Matthew 7:12).

“The commandments . . . are summed up in this one rule: ‘Love your neighbor as yourself’” (13:9).

Our salvation can be described in terms of family relationships. Through our union with Christ, we become part of God’s family and are called “children of Abraham” (Galatians 3:7), “sons of God” (3:26), and “brothers” (Romans 1:13; 7:1; 8:29; 15:14). Paul uses brothers more than one hundred times in his letters. Even Jesus, “the firstborn among many brothers” (8:29), uses family phraseology to portray the believer’s relationship to God: “Whoever does the will of my Father in heaven is my brother and sister and mother” (Matthew 12:50).

In this lesson we will consider Christian living in terms of relationships: how we relate to God, to fellow believers, and to those outside our fellowship. These relationships in turn touch on our character, our ministry, and our responsibility to governing authorities.

In Relation to God (12:1–2)

Therefore, I urge you, brothers, in view of God’s mercy, to offer your bodies as living sacrifices, holy and pleasing to God—which is your spiritual worship. Do not conform any longer to the pattern of this world, but be transformed by the renewing of your mind. Then you will be able to test and approve what God’s will is—his good, pleasing and perfect will. (Romans 12:1–2)

Paul’s counsel to believers is this: In order to put our faith into practice, each of us is to give our body, mind, and will to God. Offering ourselves to God—all that we are—allows Him to transform us into the image of His Son. Keep in mind that this is a lifetime process. Let us look at Paul’s development of this practical instruction.

In view of God’s mercy (Romans 12:1). This is where we begin—with God’s mercy . Our understanding of God and our relationship to Him arise from His mercy, regardless of who we are. Meditate for a moment on Paul’s remark near the end of Romans 11 (see Chapter 6): “God has imprisoned all people in their own disobedience” (11:32, NLT). Why is this so? It is “so that he may have mercy on them all” (11:32, NIV). Only the guilty fully appreciate God’s mercy.

Our sins have locked us up or imprisoned us. The only means of escape is through God’s mercy. Our own wisdom, goodness, and religious zeal are totally inadequate; His mercy alone provides deliverance. “He doesn’t punish us as our sins deserve” (Psalm 103:10, CEV) because He is merciful. He extends His grace to us, as well, through Jesus Christ.

Accordingly, Paul says, you (believers) “offer your bodies as living sacrifices, holy and pleasing to God—which is your spiritual worship” (Romans 12:1). This follows Paul’s earlier exhortation: “Offer yourselves to God, as those who have been brought from death to life; and offer the parts of your body to him as instruments of righteousness” (6:13). See the table below. The word offer is also translated “yield,” “give,” and “present.” Giving ourselves as instruments of righteousness for His service pleases God and opens the door to victory over sin.

A Comparison of Passages

Romans 6–8

Romans 12:1–2

Offer yourselves to God, as those who have been brought from death to life; and offer the parts of your body to him as instruments of righteousness. Romans 6:13

Offer your bodies as living sacrifices, holy and pleasing to God—this is your spiritual act of worship. Romans 12:1

Do not let sin reign in your mortal body so that you obey its evil desires. Do not offer the parts of your body to sin, as instruments of wickedness. Romans 6:12–13

Do not conform any longer to the pattern of this world. Romans 12:2

Through Christ Jesus the law of the Spirit of life set me free from the law of sin and death. You, however, are controlled not by the sinful nature but by the Spirit, if the Spirit of God lives in you. Romans 8:2, 9

Be transformed by the renewing of your mind. Romans 12:2

The words offer, sacrifice, holy, and pleasing are Old Testament terms that come from the Mosaic sacrificial system and prefigure the work of Christ. The word offer suggests the priest receiving and consecrating the offering brought to the altar by the guilty one. It became God’s possession the moment it was offered. This illustrates what happens when we offer ourselves to God. We are no longer our own, since we were bought at a price, and so we honor God in our bodies (see 1 Corinthians 6:19–20).

Living sacrifices (Romans 12:1) is a contradictory expression because sacrifice essentially means “to kill.” Obviously, the sacrificial animal died. So what does Paul mean by the term “living sacrifices”? Reflect on his earlier explanation about the believers’ union with Christ: “As surely as we died with Christ, we believe we will also live with him” (6:8, CEV). As we identify ourselves with Christ, we die to our old life. Now, as we devote ourselves fully to Him, we become “living sacrifices” that are “pleasing to God” (12:1).

How, then, can we summarize the meaning of living sacrifices ? We do so in the same way that Paul did for the Galatians when he wrote, “I have been crucified with Christ and I no longer live, but Christ lives in me. The life I live in the body, I live by faith in the Son of God, who loved me and gave himself for me” (Galatians 2:20).

Holy in Romans 12:1 describes God’s righteous nature. It also depicts a person or object that is “sanctified” or “set apart” for God’s use. In Christ we are separated from our former way of life and given over to God for His service. The word pleasing is related to an Old Testament offering. As God considered a burnt offering to be a pleasing aroma (Leviticus 1:9, 13, 17), so He considers our offering of ourselves to Him to be a pleasing fragrance and a spiritual act of worship. (Compare Ephesians 5:2 and Hebrews 13:15–16.)

“Do not conform any longer to the pattern of this world, but be transformed by the renewing of your mind” (Romans 12:2). Our relationship to God changes our relationship to the world. Translators also render world as “age” (Greek, aion ). Paul calls it the present evil age (Galatians 1:4) in contrast to the eternal age to come (Ephesians 1:21).

Satan, “the god of this age,” blinds unbelievers’ minds so they are unable to see the light of the gospel. For those who hear the gospel and believe, however, God dispels the darkness of sin and shines His light in them, just as He did at the time of creation in the beginning. (Compare Genesis 1:2–4; 2 Corinthians 4:4–6; 5:17). For those who are a “new creation” in Christ, God restores what sin destroyed, but He asks us to be involved in the process of our restoration.

Two other renditions of Romans 12:2 provide further clarification of this verse: “Don’t copy the behavior and customs of this world, but let God transform you into a new person by changing the way you think” (NLT). Or, as J. B. Phillips paraphrases the verse: “Don’t let the world around you squeeze you into its own mold, but let God remold your minds from within.” Thus we resist the way of the world and yield ourselves to God. We should neither copy nor submit to the world’s ways. This follows Paul’s earlier exhortation: “Do not offer the parts of your body to sin, as instruments of wickedness” (Romans 6:13). Since our goal is maturity in Christ, we should align ourselves with God and His will. Ask yourself, “Does my conduct conflict with—or conform to—God’s Word?”

Paul exhorts us to resist the pattern of this world and submit ourselves to God. He does not say, “Transform yourself.” Rather, he says, “(Let yourself) be transformed.” That is, it is the work of the Holy Spirit. “And as the Spirit of the Lord works within us, we become more and more like him and reflect his glory even more” (2 Corinthians 3:18, NLT).

How are we to be transformed? It is “by the renewing of your mind” (Romans 12:2). Reflect for a moment on Paul’s description of those who knew God yet refused to thank Him or glorify Him: “Their thinking became futile and their foolish hearts were darkened. Although they claimed to be wise, they became fools” (1:21–22). Compare this passage with Ephesians 4:17–19. Clearly, sin corrupted their minds.

The Holy Spirit uses the Word to renew our minds. Therefore, it is important that we saturate our minds with His thoughts and ways by reading, memorizing, and meditating on it. In this way, we gradually begin to see things from God’s perspective. (See the following graphic.)

Hiding God’s Word in Your Heart

“I have hidden your word in my heart that I might not sin against you.”

Psalm 119:11

Here are a few practical suggestions for hiding God’s Word in your heart. Word in Psalm 119:11 (Hebrew, ‘imrah) is also translated as “promise.” It provides understanding, wisdom, and nourishment to grow spiritually. And it guards against sin.

1

Read your Bible aloud. Imagine your eyes, ears, and mouth are like three highways. You carry truth from the pages of God’s Word along these roads to put in your heart.

Reading experts suggest that you follow along the margin with your finger as you move down the page. It helps you to keep your eyes on track, and if you are momentarily distracted, you will not lose your place.

2

Copy key verses the Lord impresses on your heart on something such as 3” x 5” cards that you can carry with you to review throughout the day.

The king of Israel was required to copy the Law on a scroll, keep it with him, and read it daily throughout his life (Deuteronomy 17:18–19).

3

If you have a recording device available, record Scripture and listen to it over and over. Faith comes by hearing the Word (Romans 10:17).

4

Memorize the Word. Meditate on it. “Chew” on it as a cow that ruminates (chews its cud) brings up food from its first stomach to be chewed again for greater digestion. Ask the Holy Spirit to help you digest God’s Word more fully.

As God changes the way we think, we begin to understand more clearly that His will is “good, pleasing and perfect” (Romans 12:2). This transforming process continues as we translate understanding into living.

“Continue to work out your salvation” (Philippians 2:12–13) essentially means that we do not remain passive and uninvolved in this process. Paul is not saying, “Earn your salvation,” but “Put into action God’s saving work in your lives, obeying God with deep reverence and fear” (2:12, NLT). God creates in us the desire to obey Him and provides the power for us to do so.

Let us use every avenue possible to take the truths from the pages of God’s Word and hide them in our hearts. One simple thing we can do is to read the Scriptures aloud. Our eyes, ears, and mouth then become highways that carry truth from the Word to our hearts. Through the Word we can find out what pleases the Lord (Ephesians 5:10), then do it. For example, in Ephesians 4:28 we read, “He who has been stealing must steal no longer, but must work, doing something useful with his own hands, that he may have something to share with those in need.” The thief who reads these words and takes them to heart changes his lifestyle. He stops stealing and starts doing good by supporting himself with honest work and by earning enough to help those in need.

Paul also speaks of the renewing of your mind in Romans 12:2. This involves a series of decisions. Your Christian life began with a decision to receive Christ into your life. Redemption is available to all, but only those who respond to Christ in faith are redeemed. In a similar way, the power of the Holy Spirit is available to enable each believer to live a godly life. But each one of us must seek what pleases Him, rejecting our own way and choosing His way. Step by step, then, as we walk with the Lord, we have opportunity to renew our mind and grow in grace.

Living by the Spirit brings victory. Let us be sensitive to Him and allow Him to transform our thinking and living. The attempt to conquer the sinful nature first, in the hope that it will lead to a victorious walk in the Spirit, only leads to frustration. Paul gives the right order: “Live by the Spirit, and you will not gratify the desires of the sinful nature” (Galatians 5:16). Turn on the light and the darkness will flee.

The change that takes place when the wormlike larva or caterpillar becomes a butterfly is a relatively short process. But the transforming work of the Spirit in a Christian is a lifetime process. Without question, we are born again the moment we receive Jesus Christ as our Savior, and we have eternal life. But God wants to do more—He wants to make us into the image of His Son. As we can see in the graphic below, Paul teaches this truth in several of his letters.

2 Corinthians 3:18

In Relation to Other Believers (12:3–16)

The moment we receive Christ as our Savior, we enter into a new relationship with God. We rejoice that we are forgiven, justified, reconciled, and freed from condemnation. The new birth marks the beginning of a lifelong process of change as God molds us into the likeness of His Son. At the same time, we enter into a special relationship with fellow believers. Together with them, we form one body—the body of Christ.

Paul continues his discussion of Christian relationships in Romans 12:3 with these words: “Do not think of yourself more highly than you ought, but rather think of yourself with sober judgment.” How you see yourself influences your relationship with others. Do not allow pride to inflate—nor false humility to underrate—your self-appraisal. Your identity in Christ—not your position or title—gives you value. Ask God to help you use your renewed mind to evaluate yourself accurately.

Consequently, we evaluate ourselves honestly, “in accordance with the measure of faith God has given” to us (Romans 12:3). Faith , as used here, “has a rather different sense from that which it has in the earlier part of the Epistle,” says Bruce. “Here it denotes the spiritual power given to each Christian for the discharge of his special responsibility” (1985, 227–228). Comfort gives a similar explanation. The measure of faith , he says, “refers to the spiritual capacity and / or power given to each person to carry out his or her function in the church” (1994, 233). Let us consider our ministry, then, in the light of our God-given spiritual capacity. Since God is the source of our abilities—our spiritual capacity or power—(“God has given”), we have no reason to exaggerate our value, to boast, or to compare ourselves with others.

“In Christ we who are many form one body, and each member belongs to all the others” (Romans 12:5). Paul is the only New Testament writer who uses the human body to illustrate the believer’s relationship to Christ and to fellow believers (see also 1 Corinthians 12:12–31). It is “one body with many members” (Romans 12:4). As with the human body, each member has a unique task to perform; that is, they “do not all have the same function” (12:4). Diversity promotes unity rather than division because each part contributes to the healthy, harmonious functioning of the whole. Jealousy and boasting are unnecessary because each member makes its particular contribution. Christ, as the Head (Ephesians 1:22; 5:23; Colossians 1:18), directs the activities of each part of His body.

As members of Christ’s body, we function and develop best in relation to others, not in isolation. “But what about John?” you may ask. “He lived alone on the Isle of Patmos.” Yet John did not choose the life of a hermit. He was banished there because of his witness, and God obviously used those circumstances to give him a special revelation. Normally, however, we live and work in relation to others. The members of a healthy body are coordinated and move in harmony so that they can perform great tasks. And so it is with the body of Christ.

We, the body, rely on Christ to function effectively, as the vine and the branches illustrate (John 15). Branches are fruitful only when they are united with the life-giving vine. In a similar way, our fruitfulness depends on our union with Christ. “Apart from me you can do nothing” (15:5).

“We have different gifts, according to the grace given us” (Romans 12:6). Gifts (Greek, charismata ) are a form of grace (Greek, charis ). Paul links his ministry to God’s enabling grace: “For by the grace given me I say to every one of you” (12:3; see also 15:15–16 and Galatians 2:7–10).

Romans 12:6–8 lists seven gifts: (1) prophesying, (2) serving, (3) teaching, (4) encouraging, (5) contributing to the needs of others, (6) leadership, and (7) showing mercy. God gives each believer abilities to do certain things well. As each member speaks and serves, he or she contributes to the edification of the body of Christ. This list varies from those in 1 Corinthians 12 and Ephesians 4 because the emphasis in each passage is different.

Paul’s list of spiritual gifts is not exhaustive; it simply illustrates the diversity he is talking about. The gifts were never intended to establish our importance in the body of Christ, nor should we consider them to be treasured possessions—something to be hoarded. Instead, we should understand that God freely gives them to build His church and extend His kingdom.

Have you ever purchased or received a new piece of equipment? No matter how valuable it is, you must still become familiar with its functions; otherwise, you cannot operate it effectively. Gifts distributed by the Spirit do not nullify one’s need to “read the operating instructions” in God’s Word, nor do they negate one’s need for fasting, prayer, and faithfulness.

In Relation to Our Character (12:9–16)

Paul introduces Christian character alongside gifts in Romans 12, just as he does in 1 Corinthians 12–14. The effectiveness of our ministry— what we do —is enhanced or invalidated by our character— what we are . Paul does not place Christian character and spiritual gifts side by side for comparison, suggesting that we pick and choose. To debate the superiority of one over the other is to miss Paul’s point. His purpose is to show their interrelationship. Both are essential aspects of the Christian life.

The first characteristic mentioned by Paul is love : “Love must be sincere” (Romans 12:9). Love is a distinctive aspect of God’s character. Since we are sons and daughters of God, our lives should reflect this God-like trait. “By this all men will know that you are my disciples, if you love one another” (John 13:35). Love, Paul emphasizes, should be sincere , not synthetic. It should be genuine, that is, free of pretense.

In Romans 12:9–21, Paul gives practical instruction on how to practice Christian love. These Christlike characteristics are opposite to those of the unregenerate person who lives to please self and who is conformed to the world. What a contrast this is to the believer who loves God and manifests His love to others!

In Relation to Enemies (12:17–21)

In Romans 12:17–21, Paul counsels the Romans about dealing with enemies. Here the special challenge for us is not in understanding but in practicing his admonitions. We all face enemies at times, no matter how godly our lives. The apostles, and even Jesus, had enemies simply because they faithfully served God. Therefore, Paul’s advice is relevant to us all.

First, let us consider Romans 12:14: “Bless those who persecute you; bless and do not curse.” This instruction echoes our Lord’s counsel (Luke 6:27–29) and affirms His example. For those who were crucifying Him, He prayed, “Father, forgive them, for they do not know what they are doing” (Luke 23:34). As Paul dictated this advice to Tertius, he possibly reflected on a scene etched deeply in his mind—Stephen’s unforgettable prayer for his executioners: “Lord, don’t charge them with this sin!” (Acts 7:60, NLT).

“Do not repay anyone evil for evil” (Romans 12:17). Most people respond to good with good and to evil with evil. But Stephen and Jesus acted differently. Both returned good for evil. You may ask, “How does ‘eye for eye’ and ‘tooth for tooth’ fit in here?” This principle simply means that the penalty should be suitable but not go beyond the offense. Jesus cautioned His followers not to use it as an excuse for personal vengeance (see Exodus 21:24; Leviticus 24:20; Matthew 5:38–42). David provides another example. Saul and his troops tracked David and tried to kill him; yet on two occasions, David spared Saul’s life, even though it was within his power to retaliate and kill him. He refused the way of the world and the advice of friends (1 Samuel 24; 26). He exemplifies Paul’s advice: “Do not be overcome by evil, but overcome evil with good” (Romans 12:21).

Believers are to love, not hate. “Love your enemies and pray for those who persecute you” (Matthew 5:44) goes beyond human ability. God’s grace alone can empower us to exhibit this Christlike trait. Yes, even in painful circumstances, “the righteous will live by faith” (Romans 1:17).

Believers are to witness, not judge. We leave judgment in God’s hands, refusing to avenge ourselves or to “repay anyone evil for evil.” Moreover, we “leave room for God’s wrath,” because the Lord says, “It is mine to avenge; I will repay” (Romans 12:19).

Believers are to show kindness, not revenge. “If your enemy is hungry, feed him; if he is thirsty, give him something to drink. In doing this, you will heap burning coals on his head” (Romans 12:20). At first glance, this quote from Proverbs 25:21–22 might suggest, “Here is the Christian way to inflict pain on an enemy.” Of course, this idea would engage the believer in evil and contradict Paul’s counsel, “Never pay back evil for evil to anyone” (Romans 12:17, NLT). What, then, does Paul mean? An ancient Egyptian practice suggests an explanation. According to this custom, a wrongdoer carried burning coals on his head to publicly proclaim his remorse and repentance. Thus, the burning coals in Romans 12:20 symbolize the shame the wrongdoer feels as a result of the believer’s unexpected kindness. Hopefully, the kind deeds will move the wrongdoer to repent. However, we should not speculate about the response; we show kindness—regardless of the anticipated outcome—because it is the right thing to do.

“Do not be overcome by evil, but overcome evil with good” (Romans 12:21). If we respond to evil with evil, then evil has conquered us. Instead, we overthrow evil with good. God himself sets the example by demonstrating love. “While we were still sinners, Christ died for us” (5:8).

**Q:** Using the Scripture passages given in the adjacent table, summarize the difference between the life of a believer and that of an unbeliever.

**A:** Believer: Brought from death to life, an instrument of righteousness; a living sacrifice, separated from the world and sinful practices; controlled by the Spirit of God, with a renewed mind Unbeliever: Lets sin rule over him or her; obeys its evil desires; an instrument of wickedness; conforms to the pattern of the world and is controlled by the sinful nature

**Q:** Since sacrifices were a prominent part of the Mosaic Law, could anything else be more important? To answer this question, read 1 Samuel 15:22; Psalm 40:6–8; and Amos 5:21–25.

**A:** Obedience from the heart is more important than sacrifices.

**Q:** Is the principle you discovered in Study Question 2 also applicable to today’s Christian? What do we learn from 1 Corinthians 13:3 about this?

**A:** Yes, it is. Paul teaches in 1 Corinthians 13:3 that whatever great sacrifice the believer makes, he or she gains nothing if it is not done in love.

**Q:** How does Hebrews 13:15–16 describe spiritual worship?

**A:** Spiritual worship includes offering praise to God and confessing His name as well as doing good and sharing with others.

**Q:** What does Paul mean when he says, “Do not conform any longer to the pattern of this world”?

**A:** Stay away from the evil influences, customs, and behavior of worldly people.

**Q:** What does Paul mean when he says, “Be transformed”?

**A:** Submit to God and yield to the Spirit’s prompting.

**Q:** What do Romans 12:3–8 and 1 Corinthians 12:4–11 affirm about spiritual gifts?

**A:** Spiritual gifts are distributed to each believer as the Spirit determines.

**Q:** Explain your understanding of this statement: “The effectiveness of our ministry— what we do —is enhanced or invalidated by our character— what we are .”

**A:** If our actions indicate a strong Christian character, this will have a positive impact on our ministry. On the other hand, if our actions demonstrate a lack of Christian character, this will have a negative effect on our ministry. For example, suppose a minister of the gospel commits adultery and the sin is exposed. Even if he or she repents of the sin and receives forgiveness, people who trusted the minister and who benefited from his or her preaching ministry may then be inclined to distrust; as a result, his or her preaching will be ineffective.

### Relating to Authorities and to All People (Romans 13:1–14)

#### 7.2.1 — Identify biblical teaching on the extent of a believer’s responsibility to governing authorities and to all people.

Responsibility to Governing Authorities (13:1–7)

What is a Christian’s responsibility toward his or her government? Stott says, “[Paul’s] emphasis is on personal citizenship rather than on any particular theory of church-state relations . . . [since] at that time there were no Christian authorities (global, regional or local)” (1994, 339).

At the time of Paul’s letters, the political atmosphere was volatile. Emperors had expelled Jews from Rome twice. Tiberius ordered them to leave in AD 19, and Claudius repeated the order in AD 49–50. Records reveal that Claudius expelled them because “Chrestus” frequently created disturbances. This may refer to Christ since writers often misspelled Christ as Chrestus . “If ‘Chrestus’ refers to Christ, the riots obviously were ‘about’ him rather than led ‘by’ him” (NIV Study Bible note on Acts 18:2, 1985, 1681).

Wherever Paul preached, opponents always stirred up trouble; so civil authorities inevitably became aware of the apostle. In Thessalonica, for example, jealous Jews falsely accused him and Silas before city officials: “These men who have caused trouble all over the world have now come here, . . . defying Caesar’s decrees, saying that there is another king, one called Jesus” (Acts 17:6–7).

The silversmiths in Ephesus provide another example. Convinced that the multitudes turning to Christ in Asia would ruin their business, they incited riots. Soon the whole city was in an uproar. The city clerk—the most important local official—had to quiet the crowd (Acts 19:23–41), and Paul was forced to leave town.

Roman authorities were sensitive to all forms of unrest. The commander who rescued Paul from rioting Jews in Jerusalem mistook him for a terrorist: “Aren’t you the Egyptian who started a revolt and led four thousand terrorists out into the desert some time ago?” (Acts 21:38).

Jews and Christians alike were minorities in the vast empire. The Roman oligarchy (a government controlled by the few) allowed the Jews considerable liberty in governing themselves and in following their religious practices. Usually, during the first century, they considered Christians to be part of the Jewish community. Nevertheless, Christians were under suspicion because a Roman ruler had presided over the crucifixion of their Founder. The charge against Him read, “The King of the Jews” (Mark 15:26), inferring that He rebelled against Roman rule.

As these examples illustrate, false charges and misconceptions swirled around the gospel in Paul’s time. For this reason, he clarifies a Christian’s responsibility to the state, declaring first of all the authority of the state (Romans 13:1–3) and then explaining the function of the state (13:4–7).

“Everyone must submit himself to the governing authorities, for there is no authority except that which God has established” (Romans 13:1). Some Christians may protest and say, “Believers are strangers and aliens on earth and have their citizenship in heaven” (see Hebrews 11:13–16; Philippians 3:20). While this is true, believers also live in this world, representing Christ and seeking to make Him known to the masses as well as to the governing authorities. So what is the basis of Paul’s teaching? Is he too conciliatory to authorities? His teaching agrees with Daniel’s assertion that God exercises supreme authority over the nations. Concerning God’s dealing with Nebuchadnezzar, king of Babylon, Daniel records, “The Most High is sovereign over the kingdoms of men and gives them to anyone he wishes” (Daniel 4:17, 25, 32). Paul’s rationale, then, is this: “The authorities that exist have been established by God” (Romans 13:1). Since God is the underlying source of all authority, He is the ultimate authority. Therefore, Paul writes, “Consequently, he who rebels against the authority is rebelling against what God has instituted, and those who do so will bring judgment on themselves” (13:2).

You may recall that Pilate was aware of his own authority in addressing Jesus. He declared, “Don’t you realize I have power either to free you or to crucify you?” (John 19:10). How did Jesus respond? “You would have no power over me if it were not given to you from above” (19:11). Note, as well, these words of Jesus recorded by John: “No one takes [my life] from me, but I lay it down of my own accord. I have authority to lay it down and authority to take it up again” (10:18). Jesus recognized that earthly authority ultimately comes from God.

Thus Paul teaches submission to those in authority. Contrary to the charges of his accusers, he urges believers not to defy Caesar’s decrees. And contrary to what Antinomians might suggest, freedom in Christ does not exempt believers from the Law.

“For rulers hold no terror for those who do right, but for those who do wrong” (Romans 13:3). While this is normally true, exceptions obviously exist, as we will consider in a few moments; but Paul does not introduce them here. The CEV translation of Romans 13:3 is this: “Rulers are a threat to evil people, not to good people. There is no need to be afraid of the authorities. Just do right, and they will praise you for it.” Paul is certainly aware of imperfect rulers. Later, when facing injustice before Festus, he called on a higher court: “I appeal to Caesar!” (Acts 25:11).

What is the function of government? Paul has made it clear that the state derives its authority from God. Now he shows that it also receives its ministry from God. Speaking of the one in authority, he writes, “He is God’s servant” (Romans 13:4). We know God uses Spirit-anointed people like the apostles and prophets to accomplish His purpose. But here Paul introduces an idea that may at first seem hard to accept. Government authorities are also God’s agents—believers and unbelievers alike. For instance, consider the Babylonian and Persian rulers, Nebuchadnezzar and Cyrus. The Lord called Nebuchadnezzar His “servant” (Jeremiah 25:9) because he served to bring God’s judgment on the people of Judah (25:1–14; Daniel 1:1–2). He called Cyrus His “shepherd” and His “anointed” (Isaiah 44:28–45:7). God “moved the heart of Cyrus king of Persia to make a proclamation” (2 Chronicles 36:22) permitting the Jews to return to their land after seventy years of captivity. As God’s instrument, Cyrus decreed the rebuilding of Jerusalem and the temple. The Lord said, “I call you by name and bestow on you a title of honor, though you do not acknowledge me” (Isaiah 45:4). Although Cyrus was an unbeliever, God used him to accomplish what He pleased.

The ruler “is God’s servant to do you good. . . . He is God’s servant, an agent of wrath to bring punishment on the wrongdoer” (Romans 13:4). Civil authorities provide restraint from anarchy and lawlessness, maintaining order and providing security for society in a sinful world. Unfortunately, they are often quick to judge and slow to commend those who do good. Paul’s statement, “He [the one in authority] does not bear the sword for nothing” (13:4), provides a biblical basis for the use of force in maintaining order.

“Give everyone what you owe him: If you owe taxes, pay taxes; if revenue, then revenue; if respect, then respect; if honor, then honor” (Romans 13:7). This aspect of Christian living is all too frequently ignored, but Paul instructs believers to pay government-required taxes. In addition, we are to give honor and respect to governing authorities, whether we agree or disagree with their policies.

An incident in the life of Jesus illustrates Paul’s teaching about taxes. In Luke 20:20–26 it is recorded that Jewish leaders and teachers of the Law asked Jesus, “Is it right for us to pay taxes to Caesar or not?” Obviously, they were not seeking information but trying to trap Jesus. Luke uses terms such as “spies,” “pretended,” and “might hand him over” to describe their intentions. If Jesus had answered “No,” His questioners would have used the opportunity to accuse Him of treason. To answer “Yes” would give them occasion to denounce Him as disloyal to the Jewish people. Jesus confounded His critics by holding up a coin imprinted with Caesar’s portrait and inscription, and saying, “Give to Caesar what is Caesar’s, and to God what is God’s” (20:25).

Paul exhorts Christians to pray for rulers as well—whether they are godly or ungodly, just or unjust, moral or immoral. “I urge, then, first of all, that requests, prayers, intercession and thanksgiving be made for everyone—for kings and all those in authority, that we may live peaceful and quiet lives in all godliness and holiness” (1 Timothy 2:1–2).

Are there any exceptions to Paul’s teaching? Are Christians obligated to submit to authorities regardless of circumstances? Consider the following examples:

The king of Egypt ordered the Hebrew midwives to kill all male babies at birth (Exodus 1:15–22). “The midwives, however, feared God and did not do what the king of Egypt had told them to do; they let the boys live” (1:17).

Shadrach, Meshach, and Abednego refused to bow down and worship the image as Nebuchadnezzar commanded (Daniel 3). The Babylonian astrologers accused them before the king: “They neither serve your gods nor worship the image of gold you have set up” (3:12). Consequently, these godly men were thrown into a blazing furnace, but God was with them and rescued them (3:21–27).

The Sanhedrin (the supreme Jewish council) commanded Peter and John to cease speaking and teaching in the name of Jesus (Acts 4:18). When the apostles were again brought before the council, the high priest told them, “We gave you strict orders not to teach in this name [of Jesus] . . . Yet you have filled Jerusalem with your teaching” (5:28).

Observe that in each of these instances, the governing authorities demanded their subjects to violate God-given commands. Thus, there are exceptions to Paul’s teaching. However, believers should not disregard the laws of the land on the pretext that they are free in Christ to do as they wish. This is not a correct interpretation of biblical teaching.

Paul’s counsel in this regard ran counter to the conduct of the Jews who chafed under Roman rule. They considered Gentile rule a violation of their position as God’s chosen people and defended their position by citing Deuteronomy 17:15: “Do not place a foreigner over you, one who is not a brother Israelite.” A Seleucid king of Syria profoundly marked Jewish attitude toward foreign rulers. Antiochus IV Epiphanes affronted the Jews in 168 BC by sacrificing a sow on the temple altar in Jerusalem. His insult provoked a revolt led by the Maccabees . Success encouraged them to stage other rebellious acts in subsequent years.

In New Testament times, Jews held diverse political views. Herodians (Matthew 22:16; Mark 3:6) were influential but hated. They supported Roman rule and the dynasty of Herod. Pharisees opposed foreign dominion but considered it a divine punishment for sin and a call to repentance. Zealots “combined the orthodox theology of the Pharisees with the militant nationalism of the Maccabees” (Edwards 1992, 303). They rejected all rulers but Jehovah, staged protests, defied Roman rule, and refused to pay taxes. Pharisees, on the other hand, exercised more restraint because they were convinced that the Lord would intervene and liberate the nation when the people turned to God. However, repeated provocation by extremists invited Roman retaliation. In AD 70, General Titus totally destroyed Jerusalem, massacring more than one million people.

Edwards puts church and state relations in perspective with this explanation:

Paul thus approached the relation of church and state not as a Sadducee who lived from the advantages of the state, nor as a Zealot who lived to overthrow the state, nor as a Pharisee who divorced religion from the state, nor as a Roman citizen for whom the state was an end in itself. Paul wrote as a free man in Christ, and he appeals to the church to be equally free in obedience to the state, but not conformed to it. (1992, 308)

Responsibility to All People (13:8–14)

Paul now moves from a discussion of the believer’s responsibility to governing authorities to that of responsibility toward all people. He begins by saying, “Let no debt remain outstanding, except the continuing debt to love one another, for he who loves his fellow man has fulfilled the law” (Romans 13:8). Here he stresses love again (compare to 12:9)—love for fellow believers (“one another”) and love for all people (“fellow man” and “neighbor”; 13:8–10). We can never fully pay this “continuing debt”—a contrast to taxes that must be paid in full when due (13:7). Our personality and practice should reflect Christ’s love.

“He who loves his fellow man has fulfilled the law” (Romans 13:8). Love reaches beyond outward conformity to the Law to fulfill its intent. As Paul asked earlier, “Do we, then, nullify the law by this faith? Not at all! Rather, we uphold the law” (3:31). Edwards comments: “Keeping the law is not a prerequisite to saving faith, but saving faith is a prerequisite to keeping the law (3:31). Love is the visible side of faith in relation and responsibility to others” (1992, 312).

“Love your neighbor as yourself” (Romans 13:9; Leviticus 19:18). Some Jews wrongly concluded that this statement implied, “Love your neighbor and hate your enemy” (Matthew 5:43). The reference to neighbor was not limited to someone who lived nearby but included anyone with whom the believer made contact. In the Parable of the Good Samaritan, Jesus described a neighbor as one who showed mercy and ministered to a person who was a stranger in need (Luke 10:25–37).

**Q:** In general, what is the Christian’s responsibility toward his or her government?

**A:** The Christian must submit to civil authorities, respect and honor them, and pay taxes.

**Q:** What are the possible consequences of ignoring Paul’s exhortation in 1 Timothy 2:1–2?

**A:** Failure to pray for governing authorities may result in a lack of peace and tranquillity for the Christian.

**Q:** What are the practical consequences of praying for those in authority?

**A:** According to Paul, the practical consequences are peaceful, quiet, godly, and holy lives, as God answers the believers’ prayers.

**Q:** The practical outflow of our life in Christ becomes visible as we relate to others. Paul declared that this one rule sums up the commandments: “Love your neighbor as yourself” (Romans 13:9). What commandments does he specifically mention to illustrate this point?

**A:** Do not commit adultery. Do not murder. Do not steal. Do not covet.

**Q:** Consider Edwards’ words mentioned earlier: “Keeping the law is not a prerequisite to saving faith, but saving faith is a prerequisite to keeping the law” (1992, 312). Give your understanding of what he means in this statement.

**A:** Your answer should be similar to this: We are not saved—and cannot be—by keeping the Law. However, when we are saved by faith in Christ, God pours out His love into our hearts by the Holy Spirit (Romans 5:5), enabling us to love our fellow humanity and thus fulfill the Law (13:8).

### Relating to Weak and Strong Believers (Romans 14:1–15:7)

#### 7.3.1 — Examine Paul’s teaching in Romans 14:1–15:13 to determine how believers are to maintain unity despite disagreements, accept one another’s differences, and edify one another.

Maintaining Unity

Living at peace with our fellow believers pleases God, so we should give it top priority. Conflicts can quickly undermine harmony and dull our relationship to God. In Romans 14 and 15, Paul discusses ways to deal with disagreements and maintain unity in the church.

Instances of discord are common, as Paul’s letters reveal. For example, he warned the Galatians that a critical spirit is dangerous: “If you keep on biting and devouring each other, watch out or you will be destroyed by each other” (Galatians 5:15). He cautioned the Corinthians against quarreling about leaders or bringing lawsuits against one another (1 Corinthians 1:10–17; 6:1–8). He counseled the Ephesians—and all believers—to “make every effort to keep the unity of the Spirit through the bond of peace” (Ephesians 4:3).

The Roman church was not exempt from discord. Clashes erupted over days and diet—things we may consider irrelevant today. Yet this is the nature of many disagreements. The debate in Rome was a clash of cultures. Culture colored the believers’ theology and what they considered acceptable in God’s sight. What foods are permissible? Should believers eat meat, or are they restricted to vegetables? Are some days more holy than others? Rigid dietary laws and the observance of special days dominated Jewish thinking, while Gentiles never considered them. Thus, conflict was inevitable. How should the Romans go about resolving these differences? Paul discusses these issues in terms of those who are weak and those who are strong in faith.

Although we may say these issues do not apply to us, let us keep in mind that Paul’s guidelines are useful in resolving other kinds of conflicts. In this passage Paul warns the Romans to avoid criticism. Instead, the believers should accept one another (14:1–12), edify one another (14:13–23), and please and build up each other (15:1–7).

Mutual Acceptance

“Accept him whose faith is weak, without passing judgment on disputable matters” (Romans 14:1). The expression disputable matters is the key to keep in mind in understanding this passage. It is a reference to opinions of right and wrong—not clearly defined teaching. Fundamentals of salvation and activities the Bible commands or condemns are not disputable matters. What Paul refers to are nonessential issues. Personal opinions can lead to conflict and discord, so he counsels his readers to avoid criticizing fellow believers. Augustine put it this way: “In essentials, unity; in nonessentials, liberty; in all things, charity [love]” (as cited in Wiersbe, 1975, 152).

Paul begins by stressing acceptance— Accept him (Romans 14:1)—and repeats it later— Accept one another (15:7). Accept means “to receive” or “to welcome.” Believers should not reject one another because of differing personal views. Those who are strong should take the initiative: “Accept him whose faith is weak” (14:1). The weak, in turn, should accept the strong.

But who were the “weak” and “strong”? The weak were those with a sensitive conscience—probably Jewish believers who were uncertain about their obligations concerning dietary laws and times of worship. The strong were most likely Gentiles who enjoyed their freedom in Christ and felt no restrictions from the Law. We cannot conveniently divide the weak and strong wholly along ethnic lines, however. Paul was a Jew, yet he identified himself with the strong (Romans 15:1). Neither can we assume that all Gentiles were necessarily strong in faith.

The disputable matters in Rome had to do with days and diet. “One man’s faith allows him to eat everything, but another man, whose faith is weak, eats only vegetables” (Romans 14:2). The strong in faith are confident, knowing that diet does not determine their relationship to God. With confidence Paul declared, “As one who is in the Lord Jesus, I am fully convinced that no food is unclean in itself” (14:14). As a Christian he believed that the old Jewish dietary laws no longer applied. But not everyone shared Paul’s confidence. Those with a sensitive conscience remained doubtful. Therefore, he says, “But if anyone regards something as unclean, then for him it is unclean” (14:14). Paul is not suggesting that Christian living is determined by opinion—or by conscience. Neither does he say that sin and the Savior are open to personal opinion. The issue here is about “disputable matters . ”

Paul’s exhortation is for believers to welcome one another without passing judgment on disputable matters (Romans 14:1). He neither ridiculed the weak nor applauded the strong; his primary concern was unity. Thus, he cautioned against debate over secondary issues that would undermine unity. He warned the strong not to look down on the weak, and he told the weak not to condemn the strong. The strong felt that they were free from dietary restrictions and at liberty “to eat everything” (14:2). On the other hand, those with a sensitive conscience refrained from eating certain foods. Some ate only vegetables, but not for health reasons. Most likely, they abstained from meat altogether because of their concern about meat sold in the marketplace. Their questions were, “Has it been slaughtered according to Jewish food laws? Has it been offered to idols in a pagan temple?” Their uncertainty often led them to abstain completely, fearing that they might offend God. Paul discussed this issue in his first letter to the Corinthians (chapters 8–10).

“Who are you to judge someone else’s servant?” (Romans 14:4). Paul reminded the Romans that they were God’s servants regardless of their ethnic origin or personal persuasion. Each one was directly accountable to God, their Master. For this reason, they did not have the right to judge or criticize one another. Paul continued to emphasize this truth: “To his own master he stands or falls” (14:4). “We will all stand before God’s judgment seat” (14:10). “Each of us will give an account of himself to God” (14:12).

In essence, Paul’s teaching in chapter 14 is that unity does not require agreement on every detail of life. Consequently, we should not judge others but allow for differences about nonessentials.

Mutual Edification

“Let us therefore make every effort to do what leads to peace and to mutual edification” (Romans 14:19). Our overriding concern should be peace and edification . Living in harmony and building up fellow believers does not happen by chance. It requires effort as well as sensitivity to the needs and feelings of others. We must look for things that build others up in their faith and avoid things that offend.

Make every effort (Greek, diokein ) means “to pursue.” Christians are to pursue peace and edification. Edwards explains that peace and edification should not be thought of as passive but as active and positive. We should actively pursue peace and seek to build others up in order to bring “into reality something which does not yet exist” (1992, 330).

“Make up your mind not to put any stumbling block or obstacle in your brother’s way” (Romans 14:13). This is a deliberate decision on our part: Make up your mind not to . . . Strong believers should not flaunt their freedom in ways that offend their weaker fellow believers. Doing this can cause weak believers to stumble into sin, or it can injure their faith. For example, Paul says, “If your brother is distressed because of what you eat, you are no longer acting in love” (14:15).

The strong can argue, “No food is unclean in itself” (Romans 14:14). While this is true, Paul stresses that our behavior should take into consideration the weak conscience of another believer. Failure to do so indicates that we are “no longer acting in love.”

Weak Christians also have a responsibility. They should not obstruct the strong over trivial matters, nor should they seek to use their position as “a weaker brother” to control or limit the activities of “the strong.” Comfort makes a valid point in this regard:

While Paul urges us to be sensitive to those whose faith may be harmed by our actions, we should not sacrifice our liberty in Christ just to satisfy the selfish motives of those who are trying to force their opinions on us. Strong believers need not judge their own liberty by the troubled consciences of the weak. Each believer is to follow Christ. (1994, 268)

Earlier, Paul exhorted the Romans to “stop passing judgment on one another” (Romans 14:13), especially in matters of opinion about nonessentials. Instead, they should focus on efforts to build up fellow believers and live in harmony with them. Also, believers should not “put any stumbling block or obstacle in [their] brother’s way” (14:13). An obstacle would be anything that causes another believer to stumble or fall into sin.

“Each of us should please his neighbor for his good, to build him up” (Romans 15:2). Relationships are not limited to negative matters—that is, what to avoid. Paul includes a very positive exhortation about a believer’s association with another believer: build him up . Or, as we saw earlier, “make every effort to do what leads to . . . mutual edification” (14:19). The Greek noun oikodome (translated “edification”) is made up of two words: house and build ; it literally means “the building of a house.” Paul always uses it in a figurative sense, meaning “the promotion of spiritual growth” (Vine 1996, 194). This is Paul’s idea here: Christians are to seek to build others up in the faith. Edwards observes, “The faith which saves is a faith which can and must be lived, and only the faith which is lived is a faith which saves” (1992, 281).

In Romans 15:3, Paul reminds the Romans, “For even Christ did not please himself.” Paul here supports his argument for unity by pointing to Christ, who is our example. Paul continues, “Accept one another, then, just as Christ accepted you, in order to bring praise to God” (15:7). Jesus Christ welcomed each one of us when we came to Him in faith—regardless of our race, background, or status. Union with Christ overrides differences and forms a common bond among believers. Accepting one another will glorify God.

In this chapter we have studied Paul’s application of the gospel as recorded in Romans 12:1–15:7. These chapters contain sound instruction for living the Christian life, relating to authorities and to all other people, and, more particularly, relating to fellow believers—both the weak and the strong. As we conclude this study, contemplate your own relationships in each of these categories. Do you see areas that need improvement? Allow Paul’s teaching to sink deeply into your heart and guide you in your daily relationships with all people and with God.

In Chapter 8, we will conclude our study of Romans with a survey of Romans 15 and 16. In these two chapters, Paul reviews his ministry to the Gentiles, discusses his future plans, gives further exhortation to the church, greets his friends in the Roman church, and concludes with a dynamic doxology. May you be enriched and blessed as you consider Paul’s final remarks in this letter.

**Q:** Consider the cultural context in which you live. Are there differences of opinion among Christians in your church that could cause conflict?

**A:** Your answer. Hopefully, you will answer this question with careful consideration and reflect on ways to show acceptance of those whose personal views may differ from yours.

**Q:** What does the phrase “disputable matters” in Romans 14:1 refer to?

**A:** Nonessential issues; opinions of right and wrong

**Q:** Analyze Paul’s rationale for mutual acceptance and not judging others in Romans 14:1–12. Summarize four basic truths that can be derived from this.

**A:** God welcomes the weak and strong alike (Romans 14:2–3). God alone is the ultimate judge of everyone (14:4). Whatever the strong or weak do—or refrain from doing—they do so as unto the Lord (14:5–9). Each believer is directly accountable to the Lord (14:10–12).

**Q:** Summarize Paul’s instructions for believers in dealing with disagreements and maintaining unity in each of these passages: 14:1, 13; 14:19; 15:7.

**A:** 14:1 Avoid criticism; stop judging on disputable matters. 14:19 Pursue what leads to peace and mutual edification. 15:7 Accept one another (whether you are in full agreement or not). Follow Christ’s example; accept one another as Christ accepted you. 8 CHAPTER

## 8 - Final Comments: Romans 15:8–16:27

### Paul’s Ministry to the Gentiles (Romans 15:8–33)

#### 8.1.1 — Derive from Romans 15:8–33 guiding principles concerning the example Paul follows, the nature and scope of his ministry, and his plans for future ministry.

Our previous chapter considered ways to apply our new life in Christ to daily living. We learned that Paul presents Christian practice from the viewpoint of relationships—how we relate to God, to fellow believers, and to unbelievers. We carefully considered ways to enhance these relationships, and we identified our responsibility to governing authorities. Then, we faced an unpleasant but important task: how to respond to enemies in a Christlike way.

Paul’s closing remarks provide more than a postscript or brief summary statement—something that we might be tempted to skip over quickly as unimportant. As a matter of fact, we uncover a wealth of information about Paul and his ministry as he accounts for his deferred visit, so we will look at this with interest. Then, Paul’s greetings to friends and colleagues furnish insight about the church in Rome. Among the apostle’s final instructions is a sharp warning that requires our special attention.

The chart, Twos in Romans , highlights key issues that will help you in your review of the content of Paul’s letter to the Romans. Study it carefully and note the contrasts of the subject matter in each chapter.

Twos in Romans

1

the wrath of God; a righteousness from God

2

3

4

5

6

sin; Christ

7

8

9

10

11

12
