Why has Paul delayed his visit to Rome, and why does he write so boldly? In seeking to answer these questions—almost in an unintentional way—Paul furnishes insight about himself and his ministry. He is not just a theologian explaining doctrine but a real person who deeply cares about people; thus his remarks are intensely personal. Hendriksen observes, “We are arrested by his tact, modesty, prudence, humility, and concern for the feelings of others ” (Hendriksen 1981, 484). Here is a man who imitates Christ, shares with the recipients of his letter the nature and scope of his ministry, and discloses his future plans.

The Example Paul Follows (Romans 15:8–13)

In Lesson 7.3, we studied Paul’s counsel to the Romans about interpersonal relationships among believers. Specifically, he advised strong Christians—those settled in their faith—to be considerate of their weaker brothers and sisters. His advice was to live to please others and not yourself . He pointed to Christ as the believer’s example: “For even Christ did not please himself” (Romans 15:3). Doing the will of His Father was more important to Christ than pleasing himself. When Paul wrote, “We who are strong ought to bear with the failings of the weak” (15:1), he did not mean that we are to tolerate sin but that we are to be thoughtful of the rights and feelings of others—especially about issues not clearly defined in Scripture. Christ also models the acceptance of others (15:7).

Jesus Christ exemplifies what our relationship to fellow believers should be as well as our relationship to those outside the church. Paul indicates in Romans 15:8–13 that Christ, who ministered first to the Jews and then to the Gentiles, was the example he followed. The apostle wrote to the Corinthian church, “Follow my example, as I follow the example of Christ” (1 Corinthians 11:1). In Romans 15, Paul talks about the order of ministry.

“For I tell you that Christ has become a servant of the Jews on behalf of God’s truth, to confirm the promises made to the patriarchs so that the Gentiles may glorify God for his mercy” (15:8–9). Paul emphasizes here that Christ’s earthly ministry focused on the Jews. Jesus’ response to a Canaanite woman who was seeking healing for her demon-possessed daughter illustrates the Lord’s concern: “I was sent only to help the people of Israel—God’s lost sheep—not the Gentiles” (Matthew 15:24, NLT). When He sent out the Twelve, Jesus instructed them, “Do not go among the Gentiles or enter any town of the Samaritans. Go rather to the lost sheep of Israel” (Matthew 10:5–6).

Some theologians place these passages in Matthew 10 and 15 alongside the Great Commission (Matthew 28:18–20; Mark 16:15) and say, in effect, “Jesus changed His mind.” They reason that when the Jews rejected Jesus, He revised His original mission to include all nations. Is this point valid? Does Scripture support such a rationalization ? Actually, this view denies a clear biblical pattern. Return to Abraham for a moment. Why did God bless him? He blessed Abraham so that “all peoples on earth will be blessed through [him]” (Genesis 12:3).

Why did Christ “become a servant to the Jews”? Paul explains that Christ did this to confirm the promises made to the patriarchs (Romans 15:8). You can review some of these promises made to Abraham, Isaac, and Jacob in Genesis 12:1–3; 17:7; 18:19; 22:18; 26:3–4; 28:13–15; 46:2–4.

Christ’s coming, of course, demonstrates God’s faithfulness in fulfilling the promise of a Messiah. Yet Paul does not stop here. In Romans 15:9 he adds, “So that the Gentiles may glorify God for his mercy.” God’s promises ultimately extend to the Gentiles. Therefore, they glorify God for His mercy in making salvation available to them. God’s method is to go to the Jew first, but His mission extends to all people.

God did not choose the Jews because of their superiority or His partiality. The NIV Study Bible note on Romans 1:16 clarifies this: “It was necessary that the invasion of this world by the gospel begin at a particular point with a particular people, who in turn were responsible to carry that gospel to the other nations” (1985, 1706). Thus, Paul declares that salvation is for everyone who believes, “first for the Jew, then for the Gentile” (1:16). In doing this, he followed Christ’s example.

In Romans 15:9–12, Paul cites four Old Testament passages. They speak of God’s blessings on the Gentiles, giving evidence that God’s plan has always included Gentiles. Clearly Jesus did not change His mind or His mission, but He followed the established scriptural pattern. And, incidentally, He did heal the daughter of the Canaanite woman.

Quoting Isaiah, Paul confirms, “The root of Jesse will spring up, one who will arise to rule over the nations; the Gentiles will hope in him” (Romans 15:12). The root of Jesse will spring up . Jesse was King David’s father (1 Samuel 16:5–13; Matthew 1:6). Note that Matthew opens his gospel by saying that Jesus Christ is the Son of David (Matthew 1:1). Jews gave their promised Messiah the title “Son of David.” Two blind men used this popular expression when they cried out to Jesus for help: “Have mercy on us, Son of David!” (9:27). The crowds at the Triumphal Entry used it as well, shouting, “Hosanna to the Son of David!” (21:9).

The Gentiles will hope in him (Romans 15:12). Isaiah portrays the coming Messiah as a shoot growing out of the stump (the remnant) of David’s family. “A shoot will come up from the stump of Jesse; from his roots a Branch will bear fruit” (Isaiah 11:1). The Root of Jesse, then, is a messianic title. Christ serves the Jews and gives hope to the Gentiles. Eventually, He “will arise to rule over the nations” (Romans 15:12). “In that day the heir to David’s throne [Hebrew, the root of Jesse ] will be a banner of salvation to all the world. The nations will rally to him” (Isaiah 11:10, NLT).

“So that you may overflow with hope” (Romans 15:13). Hope is more than optimism; it is a God-given expectation. Paul prays that God will fill the Roman church with joy and peace so that the believers “may overflow with hope by the power of the Holy Spirit” (15:13). Believers rejoice in God’s mercy and have a calm assurance—a hope—that God will fulfill all of His promises.

The Nature of Paul’s Ministry (15:14–19)

Three words highlight the nature of Paul’s ministry: priestly (Romans 15:16–17), powerful (15:18–19), and pioneer (15:19–22) (Stott 1994, 379–381). Before we examine these characteristics of Paul’s ministry, however, let us look at his comments about the Romans and how he relates to them.

Paul begins his comments with these words: “I myself am convinced, my brothers, that you yourselves are full of goodness, complete in knowledge and competent to instruct one another” (Romans 15:14). I myself am convinced, my brothers. Paul understood those he served, which provides insight into his effectiveness. For instance, even though he had not yet visited Rome, he had learned that the church was spiritually mature. You yourselves are full of goodness, complete in knowledge (15:14) . Their level of understanding of the gospel made them competent to instruct one another , and they were strong in faith. Earlier, Paul wrote, “Your faith is being reported all over the world” (1:8). This is not flattery or an attempt to manipulate his fellow believers. As he says, “I myself am convinced” about these things.

“I have written you quite boldly on some points, as if to remind you of them again, because of the grace God gave me to be a minister of Christ Jesus” (Romans 15:15–16). Some call this a “reminding ministry.” For example, Stott says, “The apostles attached great importance to their reminding ministry” (1994, 378). They reminded churches of the original message—the unchanging truth God gave to His people once for all—and urged them to stand firm and not drift away. (See 1 Corinthians 15:1; Philippians 3:1; 2 Thessalonians 2:15; 1 Timothy 6:20; 2 Timothy 1:13–14; 3:14; Hebrews 2:1; 2 Peter 1:12–13; 3:1–2; 1 John 2:21–24; Jude 3.)

Paul wrote quite boldly on some points , giving advice, for instance, to the strong and the weak. Was he assuming too much authority? Was he arrogantly intruding into the affairs of a church he had never visited? He defends his boldness by saying it was “because of the grace God gave me” (Romans 15:15). His assurance is not in himself but in Christ. God’s grace working through him made him a bold and effective instrument.

Paul describes himself as “a minister of Christ Jesus to the Gentiles with the priestly duty of proclaiming the gospel of God” (Romans 15:16). God chose Paul to minister primarily to the Gentiles (1:5; 11:13; Acts 9:15; Galatians 1:16; 2:7, 9). The word minister (Greek, leitourgos ) means “a public servant.” In the biblical context, it refers to religious service.

Paul considered his ministry a priestly duty (Greek, hierourgounta ). This means “to work in sacred things” (Comfort 1992, 278). He did not serve as a priest in an ecclesiastical sense but in a figurative sense, proclaiming the gospel . Thus, figuratively speaking, his priestly duties included preaching and presenting offerings. In this case, he presents to God his Gentile converts. Because the Holy Spirit had sanctified them (Romans 15:16), they are “an offering acceptable to God.”

“Therefore I glory in Christ Jesus in my service to God” (Romans 15:17). Paul did not boast about himself, his converts, or the churches he had planted. Instead, he rejoiced in what God had accomplished through him. Hendriksen notes that Paul does not say “what I have accomplished through Christ” (1981, 487). Rather, he says, “I will not venture to speak of anything except what Christ has accomplished through me in leading the Gentiles to obey God” (15:18). He does not draw attention to himself but to Christ and His accomplishment. Glorifying Jesus Christ must be our aim, never self-glorification.

Leading the Gentiles to obey God summarizes Christ’s accomplishment through Paul. But why does he say obey and omit faith ? After all, he has constantly stressed salvation by faith. Obedience, as Stott explains, is “the indispensable consequence of saving faith” (1994, 380). Obedience to God demonstrates genuine faith.

Romans 15:19 furnishes further testimony of Christ’s accomplishments through the apostle Paul: “by the power of signs and miracles, through the power of the Spirit.” His ministry was powerful because the Spirit anointed him and Christ worked through him. The power of the Spirit made him effective and fruitful . Paul made a similar remark to the Corinthians: “The things that mark an apostle—signs, wonders and miracles—were done among you with great perseverance” (2 Corinthians 12:12).

Signs, wonders, and miracles give evidence of God’s presence. Signs (Greek, semeia ) are miracles that demonstrate God’s truth. A sign “points away from itself and sign ifies the qualities (power, wisdom, grace, etc.) of the one who performs it” (Hendriksen 1981, 487). Wonders (Greek, teras ) are miracles that create awe and amaze observers “so they want to know more” (Comfort 1992, 279). And miracles (Greek, dunamis ) indicate the supernatural—producing something beyond the ability of humanity or nature. Vine says, “A sign is intended to appeal to the understanding, a ‘wonder’ appeals to the imagination, a power ( dunamis ) indicates its source as supernatural” (1997, 682).

Luke preserves for us the circumstances surrounding the apostles’ miracles as well as their impact. At Iconium, Paul and Barnabas spoke boldly for the Lord, “who confirmed the message of his grace by enabling them to do miraculous signs and wonders” (Acts 14:3; note also Acts 13:6–12; 14:1–3; 14:8–10; 16:16–18, 25–30; 19:11–16). Mark records the relationship between preaching and signs. “The disciples went out and preached everywhere, and the Lord worked with them and confirmed his word by the signs that accompanied it” (Mark 16:20).

The supernatural element in Jesus’ ministry touched the heart of Nicodemus. He said to Jesus, “Rabbi, we know you are a teacher who has come from God. For no one could perform the miraculous signs you are doing if God were not with him” (John 3:2). The writer to the Hebrews further declared, “This salvation, which was first announced by the Lord, was confirmed to us by those who heard him. God also testified to it by signs, wonders and various miracles, and gifts of the Holy Spirit distributed according to his will” (Hebrews 2:3–4).

The Scope of Paul’s Ministry (15:19–22)

Paul was a trailblazer whose ambition was to preach the gospel where Christ was unknown (Romans 15:20). Let us explore his pioneer ministry that extended across the Eastern Mediterranean. He describes the scope of his ministry near the end of his third missionary journey with these words: “So from Jerusalem all the way around to Illyricum, I have fully proclaimed the gospel of Christ” (15:19).

This description raises some questions. Why, for instance, does he say from Jerusalem ? All three of his missionary journeys began from Syrian Antioch (Acts 13:1–3; 15:30–41; 18:22–23). Would “from Antioch” be more accurate? Or did the Lord speak to him again in Jerusalem following his Damascus Road experience? “Go; I will send you far away to the Gentiles” (Acts 22:17–21). Or did Paul say “from Jerusalem” because the leaders there had given him their full approval? (See Galatians 2:6–10; it may be helpful also for you to review the first two charts and the maps in Chapter 1 at this time.)

We know, of course, that Paul ministered briefly in Jerusalem near the beginning of his ministry (Acts 9:26–29). Moreover, the geographic location suggests a likely reason. Hendriksen notes that Jerusalem was at “the southeastern limit of the region covered by the apostle on his journeys” (1981, 488). When coupled with Illyricum in the northwest, this explanation gains validity. Together, these places define the geographical limits of Paul’s ministry.

Keep in mind that the church originated in Jerusalem. This is where Jesus was crucified and the Holy Spirit was poured out on the first believers. Jesus reminded His disciples that they were witnesses of His suffering, death, and resurrection. Furthermore, He commanded them to take the message of repentance to all nations “beginning at Jerusalem.” But He added, “Stay in the city until you have been clothed with power from on high” (see Luke 24:45–49). Also, He told His disciples, “You will receive power when the Holy Spirit comes on you; and you will be my witnesses in Jerusalem, and in all Judea and Samaria, and to the ends of the earth” (Acts 1:8). Thus, Paul’s comments are in harmony with Luke and with Jesus— from Jerusalem to the ends of the earth.

The Roman province of Illyricum joined the northern border of Macedonia. Today, this region is known as Albania, Croatia, and Serbia. Did Paul actually preach in Illyricum, or did he simply reach the border? We do not know. Acts is silent, and no other records are available. We do know that Titus—Paul’s coworker—went to Dalmatia (or Illyricum), but no details about the trip are available (see 2 Timothy 4:10).

All the way around . . . I have fully proclaimed the gospel (Romans 15:19). What does Paul mean? Obviously, he is not saying he preached to every individual in the region. But he does reveal his missionary strategy. He planted churches in major population centers, counting on converts to carry the gospel to surrounding areas. With these brief comments, Paul summarizes his intense labor during the preceding ten years.

Reflect for a moment on Paul’s ministry in Ephesus (Acts 19). He taught daily at the lecture hall of Tyrannus and worked extraordinary miracles. As a result, the sick were healed and evil spirits came out of people. As God worked mightily through him, the gospel spread “so that all the Jews and Greeks who lived in the province of Asia heard the word of the Lord” (19:10). A convert named Epaphras carried the gospel to Colosse and probably founded the church there. He also labored in Laodicea and Hieropolis (see Colossians 1:7–8; 2:1; 4:12–13).

Paul explains his missionary strategy in Romans 15:20: “It has always been my ambition to preach the gospel where Christ was not known, so that I would not be building on someone else’s foundation.” Once churches were established in strategic centers, he considered his work complete (15:23) and was ready to move on to new regions.

“This is why I have often been hindered from coming to you” (Romans 15:22). Paul’s ministry—ranging from Jerusalem to Illyricum—delayed his visit. His pioneer efforts took precedence over everything else. In this sense, it was his ministry that “hindered” his visit.

Paul’s Future Plans (15:23–33)

Having examined the nature and scope of Paul’s ministry, we now turn to his future plans. At the time of this letter, he is preparing to leave Corinth for Jerusalem, where he will deliver a contribution “for the poor among the saints” there (Romans 15:26). Once the gift has been delivered, he intends to sail to Spain, visiting Rome en route.

You will recall how Paul addressed this letter: “To all in Rome who are loved by God and called to be saints” (Romans 1:7). These are the people Paul has been “longing for many years to see” (15:23). He thanks God for them (1:8), prays for them (1:9–10), wants to encourage and strengthen them (1:11–12), and desires a harvest among them (1:13). If this is true, what has hindered the fulfillment of his desire? His Eastern Mediterranean ministry, he explains, caused the delay. “My visit to you has been delayed so long because I have been preaching in these places” (15:22, NLT).

“But now that there is no more place for me to work in these regions, . . . I hope to visit you” (Romans 15:23–24). Now, having finished his pioneer work, Paul is free to visit Rome. Established churches now exist in the major cities of Asia Minor and Greece. However, Christ is not known in Spain, so he sees a place for ministry there. Yet he hopes to see those in the Roman church on his way to Spain (15:24, 28). He does not foresee a prolonged visit that will intrude on the ministry of others, but he anticipates a refreshing time—“after I have enjoyed your company for a while” (15:24). This would give him an opportunity to strengthen their faith—“that is, that you and I may be mutually encouraged by each other’s faith” (1:12). Paul also hopes to establish a partnership or a base of operations for his ministry in Spain: “I hope . . . to have you assist me on my journey there” (15:24). He had developed such a partnership with the Philippian church that had helped him again and again with his needs (Philippians 1:5; 4:14–19).

Assist (Greek, propempo ) means “to send forward.” As used in Romans 15:24, it means to assist a person on a journey “in the sense of fitting him out with the requisites for it” (Vine 1996, 8). It is more than wishing a person well. Stott says, “In most cases it also involved supplying them with provisions and money, and sometimes providing them as well with an escort to accompany them at least part of the way” (1994, 385). For example, see Acts 20:38; 21:5; 1 Corinthians 16:6; Titus 3:13; 3 John 6.

“I am on my way to Jerusalem in the service of the saints there” (Romans 15:25). The churches in Macedonia and Achaia “were pleased to make a contribution for the poor among the saints in Jerusalem” (15:26), so Paul and his companions planned to deliver it. Eleven years earlier—prior to his first missionary journey—Paul and Barnabas took a famine relief gift from Antioch to Jerusalem (Acts 11:30; 12:25). At that time, he expressed an eagerness to help in the future (Galatians 2:10).

Was this trip a significant part of Paul’s ministry? Or did he see it as an opportunity for rest and relaxation before launching a vigorous campaign in the West? For Paul, it was so vital that he literally went out of his way to serve the saints. Rome and Spain are west of Corinth. Yet he traveled East—adding more than three thousand kilometers to his trip. Travel in those days was difficult, dangerous, and uncertain; Paul was willing to risk his life to complete this task (Acts 20:22–24; 21:4, 10–14; also see the graphic, The Scope of Paul’s Ministry ).

Contribution (Greek, koinonia ) means “a sharing in common.” It is also translated as “communion,” “fellowship,” and “participation.” Paul used it to describe “the right hand of fellowship” that church leaders extended to him and Barnabas (Galatians 2:9)—a gesture pledging friendship. So this contribution for needy believers included more than money. It expressed fellowship and acknowledged a mutual indebtedness. For that reason, Paul saw it as a means of uniting Jews and Gentiles. While this trip marked the close of Paul’s Aegean ministry, at the same time, it reflected his dedication and gratitude to God. The Corinthian letters also reveal the importance of this offering. Paul instructed believers to set aside money ahead of time so that a collection would be unnecessary when he arrived (1 Corinthians 16:1–4). In his second letter, he devoted two more chapters to the “grace of giving” (2 Corinthians 8–9). He lauded the Macedonians for their extraordinary generosity. Although experiencing severe trials and extreme poverty, their giving overflowed in “rich generosity” (8:2).

Paul says that the churches in Macedonia and Achaia “were very glad to do this because they feel they owe a real debt to them. Since the Gentiles received the wonderful spiritual blessings of the Good News from the Jewish Christians, they feel the least they can do in return is help them financially” (Romans 15:27, NLT). In essence, Paul likens this gift to the paying of a debt. This is, of course, a moral rather than a legal debt. Gentiles were indebted to Jews for spiritual blessings. Jews, in turn, were indebted to Gentiles for material blessings. These two segments of the church, Paul hoped, would be drawn together and united—not through strength, but through their mutual indebtedness. Does this serve as an example? Should Christians share with one another according to the blessings God has bestowed on them?

“After I have completed this task . . . I will go to Spain” (Romans 15:28). At the time Paul wrote, Spain had been a well-established part of the Roman Empire for 250 years. Roads and bridges linked the people of the peninsula together; some Roman-built bridges and aqueducts of that era still exist today. We know that Spain had an established Jewish community as well. Prominent writers such as Seneca, Martial, and Quintillian claimed Spain as their birthplace. Also, the emperors Trajan, Hadrian, and Theodosius I were born there (Edwards 1992). Thus, Spain exerted considerable influence on the Empire.

What motivated Paul to go to Spain? We know he aspired to reach the lost, and Spain had never been evangelized. Paul explained, “It has always been my ambition to preach the gospel where Christ was not known” (Romans 15:20). Did he actually reach Spain? The answer is somewhat obscured because the New Testament is silent about it. The book of Acts, for example, closes abruptly with Paul still in a Roman prison awaiting trial. Nevertheless, early Christian literature sheds some light on the question. It indicates that Paul was released from prison and went to Spain. For instance, Hendriksen quotes a comment made by Clement in a letter to the Corinthians circa AD 95–97:

Paul, having taught righteousness to the whole world, having gone to the limits of the West, and having given testimony before the rulers, thus was removed from the world and taken up into the Holy Place, having become the outstanding model of endurance. (1981, 492)

Since Clement penned these words in Rome, his reference to “the limits of the West” undoubtedly refers to the western part of Europe, and probably Spain. The Muratorian Canon, dated much later, lends support to this view. Scholars further surmise that Paul was arrested and imprisoned a second time in Rome and then, during the persecution under Nero, was beheaded.

Now we turn to Paul’s closing remarks to the Roman church. In his opening remarks, Paul assured the Romans that he constantly prayed for them. Now, he appeals to them to pray for him. “I urge you, brothers, by our Lord Jesus Christ and by the love of the Spirit, to join me in my struggle by praying to God for me” (Romans 15:30). Here, Paul connects struggle with prayer . The battle in the spiritual realm is a struggle against rulers, authorities, powers, and spiritual forces of evil in the heavenly realms (Ephesians 6:12).

The word struggle (Greek, sunagonisasthai ) means “to strive together with him.” This Greek term, as Comfort explains, “was often used in connection with athletic events where a team had to put forth a great, concerted action” (1992, 284). The English word agonize is derived from the Greek term. In this instance, the church needed to make a strong united effort in prayer to overcome the spiritual forces of evil.

Paul specifically asks the Romans to pray about three things: (1) that he will be protected from opponents, (2) that his gift will be accepted in Jerusalem, and (3) that he will be able to come to Rome with joy.

First , “Pray that I may be rescued from the unbelievers in Judea” (Romans 15:31). Jewish threats were real. (See, for example, Acts 9:23–25, 29; 19:23–41.) Even as Paul concluded this letter and prepared to leave Corinth for Syria, a Jewish plot against him was discovered. Consequently, he was forced to change his travel plans (Acts 20:3). The Holy Spirit had warned Paul, as well, that prison and hardship awaited him (Acts 20:22–24). He does not speak of imagined danger; the possibility of losing his life was real. Nevertheless, he felt “compelled by the Spirit” to go to Jerusalem (Acts 20:22; see also Acts 21:4, 10–14).

Second , “Pray . . . that my service in Jerusalem may be acceptable to the saints there (Romans 15:31). This journey “in the service of the saints” (15:25) could be frustrated. If the Jews rejected this gift, the gap between Jews and Gentiles could rupture beyond repair. Paul’s hope was that this gift would be a means of drawing them together. Thus united prayer was critical for victory.

Third , “Pray . . . that by God’s will I may come to you with joy and together with you be refreshed” (Romans 15:31–32). Paul anticipates the successful completion of his mission so that he can visit Rome. Fellowship with other believers should be a time of joy and refreshing.

How did God answer the Romans’ prayers? Did the Jews accept the contribution? Although there is no specific statement about this, note that Luke did report, “When we arrived at Jerusalem, the brothers received us warmly” (Acts 21:17). Also, in response to Paul’s report on his Gentile ministry, “they praised God” (21:20). This warm welcome infers that the offering was gratefully accepted. Later, Paul explained to Governor Felix that he brought aid for the poor in Jerusalem (24:17), but he says nothing about its acceptance. Stott observes, “The probability is that the gifts were accepted” (1994, 390).

**Q:** What does Jesus’ response to the Canaanite woman indicate concerning God’s plan?

**A:** a) Life in the Spirit b) Faith versus works c) The sinful condition of all people d) Israel’s divine purpose in history

**Q:** From the Bible passage reviewed here, the promised Messiah is described in what four ways?

**A:** a) flow out of his previous teaching and develop it. b) involve a side issue that was added as a parenthesis. c) give important instruction specifically for the Gentile church. d) serve as a practical application of what he has already written.

**Q:** What is the significance of this for both Jews and Gentiles in relation to Jesus Christ?

**A:** a) has gone through the Jewish ceremony of circumcision. b) was born of Jewish parents. c) has a heart that is right with God. d) obeys the Law faithfully.

**Q:** Based on the nature of Paul’s ministry as we have considered it here, develop a guiding principle that could help you in each of these areas:

**A:** a) Jacob’s natural right of inheritance. b) God’s rejection of the Jews as His chosen ones. c) an absolute expression of God’s foreordination. d) nations rather than individuals.

**Q:** Purpose or aim in ministry

**Q:** Evidence of genuine faith

**Q:** Paul’s “reminding” ministry

**Q:** What is the main purpose of signs, wonders, and miracles?

**A:** a) God’s actions are sometimes arbitrary. b) God does not harden anyone who does not first harden himself / herself. c) Pharaoh had no control over God’s hardening his heart. d) often God hardens those who do not resist Him.

**Q:** Briefly describe Paul’s missionary strategy, and give an example of how it worked.

**A:** a) insistence on circumcision. b) rejection of Jesus Christ. c) refusal to accept Gentiles into the church. d) failure to obey the Law at all times.

**Q:** In view of the fact that much of the world has now been evangelized, what guiding principle can be developed for ministers today from Paul’s example as a trailblazer?

**A:** a) Sinner hears the gospel. b) Those who call are saved. c) Messenger proclaims the gospel. d) Believer calls on the Lord.

**Q:** Why did this group of delegates accompany Paul? (See 2 Corinthians 8:16–21.)

**A:** a) people pray. b) God wills it. c) someone is sent. d) sinners respond.

**Q:** Based on his comments in Romans 15, what may we conclude is the reason Paul wanted to go to Spain?

**A:** a) branches. b) the root. c) nourishing sap from the root. d) a wild olive shoot.

**Q:** Suggest a guiding principle that can direct us in our prayers for fellow ministers, based on Paul’s prayer request to the saints in Rome.

### Paul’s Final Greetings to Friends (Romans 16:1–16)

#### 8.2.1 — Evaluate Paul’s descriptions of individuals in Romans 16 to determine what insights they provide concerning the Roman church.

Is the closing chapter of Romans an essential part of Paul’s message? Or should we discard it, as some theologians suggest? After all, it is basically a list of names—people that Paul and the Romans knew, but people unknown to us. What value does the list serve today? Should we skip over this chapter, as one prominent writer did? He eliminated it from his commentary, convinced that Paul would never conclude such a powerful Epistle with a list of names (Edwards 1992). However, since we believe that it is a part of God’s Word and that it has value, we will examine it.

The Roman Church

Paul’s list represents real people—children of God—saved by grace through faith, set free from sin, and valuable in God’s eyes. For this reason, we cannot divorce them from theological principles. To put it another way, they exemplify Paul’s teaching.

This list grants insight into the Roman church. It is not a total picture, of course, because the list is limited to people Paul knew. But it provides perspective. Twenty-seven of the twenty-nine people are mentioned by name. One-third of these individuals are women. In addition, Paul refers to households, house churches, and relatives.

Extensive name studies provide information about the nationality, ethnicity, gender, and social class of those in the list; yet we know frustratingly little about the people themselves. We can identify several Jewish names, but the majority are Greek or Latin. While the names Aristobulus and Narcissus are associated with the ruling class, most of them reflect the lower strata of the society of that day. Rufus , Asyncritus , Phlegon , and Hermes are names commonly linked to slaves. Thus the roster represents a diverse mixture of cultures and classes. Blended together, they form the Roman church and remind us of Galatians 3:28: “There is neither Jew nor Greek, slave nor free, male nor female, for you are all one in Christ Jesus.” We may therefore correctly conclude that this final chapter of Romans provides insight into the diverse mixture of cultures and classes in the Roman church. Let us examine a few of these people more closely.

Phoebe . Paul begins his personal greetings saying, “I commend to you our sister Phoebe” (Romans 16:1). Phoebe is most likely the one who carried Paul’s Epistle from Corinth to Rome, so the apostle provides a letter of introduction. He commends her to the church and asks them to “give her any help she may need” (16:2). Paul says that she is worthy of high honor because “she has been a great help to many people, including me” (16:2). A great help means “a protectress or patroness,” suggesting that she was a wealthy, influential woman (Edwards 1992, 354).

This sister in the Lord is “a servant of the church in Cenchrea” (Romans 16:1), a seaport ten kilometers from Corinth. Servant (Greek, diakonos ) can mean “one who serves” in a general sense, or “deacon” in a more technical sense. Did she hold the office of deaconess or simply serve in an extraordinary way? Theologians debate the issue. Hendriksen (1981) acknowledges the ecclesiastical office in a later century but insists that there is no New Testament evidence for the position at the time Paul wrote. Edwards argues that Paul’s word choice suggests a formal office, “whereas general help would more naturally have been expressed either by the verb diakonein (‘to serve’), or by the abstract noun diakonia (‘service’)” (1992, 353–354). Stott says the general meaning may be correct, but he recognizes that the office of deacon existed in an undeveloped form at that time. Furthermore, he notes that the margins in the RSV and NIV use “deaconess.” Then he adds, “Cranfield regards this not only as ‘very much more natural’ but as ‘virtually certain’” (1994, 393).

What kind of help did Phoebe give? Hendriksen (1981) believes that her ministry centered on hospitality, advice, and protection for strangers traveling through the port city. Regardless of our theological conclusion, let us note the superb service Phoebe rendered to the church of Christ.

Priscilla and Aquila. Paul describes Priscilla and Aquila as “my fellow workers in Christ Jesus” (Romans 16:3). Aquila was born in the province of Pontus in northern Asia Minor near the Black Sea, but he lived in Italy. He came to Corinth with Priscilla, his wife, after Claudius Caesar expelled all Jews from Rome. Paul became acquainted with them when he arrived from Athens (Acts 18:2). He lived with them and worked together with them in tentmaking and church planting.

Priscilla and Aquila accompanied Paul as far as Ephesus when he sailed for Syria at the close of his second missionary journey (Acts 18:18). During his absence, they met and instructed Apollos “more adequately” in the faith (Acts 18:24–26). When Paul rejoined them, they worked together to establish a church. The gospel had a great impact on Ephesus and spread throughout the entire province of Asia. Paul reported that this couple “risked their lives for me” (Romans 16:4)—possibly during the riot at Ephesus (Acts 19:23–41). Although we have no details, their heroic effort was widely known because Paul reports that “all the churches of the Gentiles are grateful to them” (Romans 16:4).

“Greet also the church that meets at their house” (Romans 16:5)—their house in Rome and earlier in Ephesus (1 Corinthians 16:19). Meeting in homes was the common practice of that day because churches did not have their own buildings.

Epenetus. He was “the first convert to Christ in the province of Asia” (Romans 16:5). Paul called him a dear friend. Hendrikson describes him as “Asia’s firstfruits for Christ” (1984, 503), suggesting that there were many others to follow, and they did. Paul and his coworkers reaped an abundant harvest that began in Ephesus and spread throughout the province.

Mary . Six New Testament women are known by this name, but Paul gives no other clues about her identity. The only thing he says is that she “worked very hard for you” (Romans 16:6). He made similar comments about Tryphena and Tryphosa (possibly sisters and perhaps twins) and Persis (16:12). Contrary to some portrayals of Paul, he honored women, as these greetings reveal. Their prominence is evident because one-third of the people on his list are women. Does his repeated use of “hard work” suggest a highly valued trait of the early church?

Andronicus and Junias, my relatives (Romans 16:7). These relatives are fellow-countrymen or fellow Jews as in 9:3 and 16:21. Paul says “[they] have been in prison with me” (16:7). Which prison, we do not know. Is Paul supplying another fact, or is he stressing their stature? In seeking to establish his own integrity on one occasion, he pointed to his suffering for Christ (2 Corinthians 11:16–33).

Paul adds, “They are outstanding among the apostles, and they were in Christ before I was” (Romans 16:7). This suggests that they may have been Christians for about twenty-five years. They were probably husband and wife or possibly brother and sister. The Greek name Iounian in the accusative form can be translated Junias (masculine) or Junia (feminine). While the masculine form is sometimes used, as in the NIV translation we have quoted, most commentators agree that the feminine form is more likely correct. In a recent study using 250 examples from Greek literature, not one masculine form was found (Edwards 1992).

They are outstanding among the apostles (Romans 16:7). Does Paul mean “those outstanding apostles Andronicus and Junias” as the Jerusalem Bible translates it? Or does he mean, “They are highly respected by the apostles” (CEV)? The NRSV favors the first view: “They are prominent among the apostles.” Bruce says, “They were ‘of note among the apostles,’ which probably means that they were not merely well known to the apostles but were apostles themselves (in a wider sense of the word), and eminent ones at that” (1985, 258). The Amplified Bible supports the second view: “They are men held in high esteem among the apostles.” And the NLT says, “They are respected among the apostles.”

Were Andronicus and Junias (or Junia) among the “visitors from Rome” (Acts 2:10) who were converted on the Day of Pentecost? Did they help to plant the Roman church? Of course, this is speculation, as Edwards (1992) stresses. All we know with certainty about these two is what Paul tells us here.

Rufus, chosen in the Lord (Romans 16:13). Is this Rufus the son of Simon of Cyrene, who carried Christ’s cross? We cannot be absolutely certain because his name was so common. Yet supporters of this view cite some evidence that we should consider. First of all, look at Mark’s comment: “A certain man from Cyrene, Simon, the father of Alexander and Rufus, was passing by on his way in from the country, and they forced him to carry the cross” (Mark 15:21). Note that those Mark addressed apparently knew Alexander and Rufus, because he refers to them to identify their father. Keep in mind that Paul is writing this Epistle to the Romans almost thirty years after Christ’s crucifixion. According to tradition, John Mark wrote his gospel account in Rome to the Romans. His explanation of Jewish customs and Aramaic words indicates that he was writing to Gentiles.

Rufus . . . and his mother, who has been a mother to me, too (Romans 16:13). When did this relationship develop? Again, we cannot be absolutely certain. A likely time seems to be during Paul’s ministry with Barnabas in Syrian Antioch (see Acts 11:25–30; 13:1). Simeon, one of the teachers in the church there, has been identified with Simon of Cyrene. Bruce observes, “If Paul lodged with him we can well envisage the wife of Simeon (or Simon) playing the part of a mother to their disinherited guest” (1985, 261).

Paul’s list includes others: namely, Urbanus; Stachys; Apelles; the household of Aristobulus; Herodion (another relative); the believers in the household of Narcissus; Asyncritus, Phlegon, Hermes, Patrobas, Hermas, and the brothers with them; Philologus, Julia, Nereus and his sister; and Olympas “and all the saints with them” (Romans 16:8–15).

“All the churches of Christ send greetings” (Romans 16:16). Representatives from Macedonia, Asia, and Galatia are with Paul at Corinth (Acts 20:4); and his associates represent Achaia (Romans 16:21–23). Thus it is not an exaggeration for the apostle to say that all the churches greet the Romans.

The Ephesus Theory

Critics have attacked Romans 16 from two directions. Some want to discard it, saying it is just an insignificant list of names. Others assault it with speculation. They theorize that it was a cover letter attached to Romans and sent to Ephesus. According to their theory (called the Ephesus theory ), the book of Romans ended at 15:33. Furthermore, they suggest that the Epistle circulated in two versions: a shorter version (chapters 1–15) sent to Rome, and a longer version (including chapter 16) sent to Ephesus. In time, they say, these versions became mixed up, and this caused discrepancies in the closing chapters.

ROMANS 16: THE EPHESUS THEORY

Four Arguments

Argument 1

Paul could not have had as many friends in Rome as chapter 16 suggests.

Argument 2

The list of friends was associated with Ephesus and Asia, not Rome.

Argument 3

Paul was an outsider in Rome, so his admonitions in verses 17–20 are out of place for Rome.

Argument 4

A papyrus discovered in 1935 places Paul’s doxology (16:25–27) at the end of chapter 15.

What is the basis of the Ephesus theory? Edwards summarizes their four major arguments (1992, 352–353, 362–363). First, he indicates that they, in effect, ask, “Could Paul possibly have as many friends in Rome as this chapter suggests?” The answer is, “Yes.” A constant flow of people visited the hub of the empire. Considering Paul’s extensive travels, it is not surprising that he would have numerous friends in the capital. “All roads lead to Rome” is more than a cliché. It reflected the ceaseless flow of travelers to the capital city.

Next, the critics argue that the list was associated with Ephesus and Asia. Can we say that Paul’s roster applied to Ephesus? Actually, only three names can be applied with certainty to Ephesus: Priscilla, Aquila, and Epenetus. It is not unreasonable to believe that Priscilla and Aquila eventually returned to Rome after Claudius died and his edict expelling the Jews was revoked. Nor is it unreasonable to believe that Epenetus moved to Rome. His being the first convert in Asia is insufficient grounds to deny that he went to Rome.

A third argument says that because Paul was an outsider, his admonitions in Romans 16:17–20 are out of place. Does chapter 16 fit Ephesus better than Rome? (Compare with Acts 20:25–31.) Paul’s long list of colleagues and friends refutes this view. Furthermore, his bold writing arose from the grace God gave to him (Romans 15:15).

A fourth argument cites a papyrus discovered in 1935 that places Paul’s doxology (Romans 16:25–27) at the end of chapter 15, as the Ephesus theory suggests. But this text “is both fragmentary and idiosyncratic ” according to Edwards (1992, 362). Two things further weaken this argument: (1) it is the only papyrus that places the doxology in this position, and (2) it includes 16:1–23 after the doxology.

Here is a further observation about the Ephesus theory. The pattern of Paul’s letters was to greet people by name solely in churches he had not founded (Romans and Colossians, for example). Why? He sought to develop a rapport with the people—something already accomplished in churches he had founded. Suppose, for a moment, that this letter was read aloud in a church meeting in Ephesus. Can you envisage the response of those whose names were omitted? “Why did he leave me out?” (Bruce 1985, 255).

We come to the conclusion that the Ephesus theory is ill founded. Bruce observes, “That a separate letter to the church in Ephesus has somehow been tacked on to a letter addressed to Rome is highly improbable” (1985, 253). Stott comments, “There is no manuscript evidence that these greetings were ever detached from their place in Romans; the names fit Rome better than Ephesus; and if Paul had sent this list of greetings to Ephesus, it would have been too short rather than too long” (1994, 394).

Plainly, evidence argues that chapter 16 was part of the letter to Rome and not a cover letter to Ephesus. However, rejection of the Ephesus theory does not deny the possibility that copies of Paul’s letter to the Roman church were sent to Ephesus and to other Christian communities.

**Q:** Describe what we know about the following people from Paul’s greetings in Romans 16: Priscilla and Aquila, Andronicus and Junias, Epenetus, Phoebe, and Apelles.

**A:** a) no longer be conformed to the patterns of this world. b) allow the Holy Spirit to guide in the renewing of one’s mind. c) be conformed to the likeness of God’s Son, Jesus Christ. d) put to death whatever belongs to one’s earthly nature.

**Q:** Think of people in your church community who have made a significant contribution to the work of God or whose ministry has touched your life in a positive way. Write the names of at least three of them, and state what they have done that has been a blessing to you personally.

**A:** a) One’s humanity and spirituality b) Spiritual gifts and Christian character c) Duty to self and duty to others d) Preaching and teaching

#### 8.2.2 — Identify arguments that support or refute the Ephesus theory concerning Romans 16.

ROMANS 16: THE EPHESUS THEORY

Four Arguments

Argument 1

Argument 2

### Paul’s Final Instructions (Romans 16:17–27)

#### 8.3.1 — Identify the value for believers today of reading Paul’s final instructions, greetings, and doxology.

In these concluding comments, Paul warns the church about troublemakers. Then he adds some more greetings to the Romans—this time from his associates at Corinth. Finally, he closes with a majestic doxology.

A Warning (16:17–19)

I urge you, brothers, to watch out for those who cause divisions and put obstacles in your way that are contrary to the teaching you have learned. Keep away from them. For such people are not serving our Lord Christ, but their own appetites. By smooth talk and flattery they deceive the minds of naive people. (Romans 16:17–18)

Paul’s warning here raises questions. For example, why does he make a sudden change from warm greetings to a sharp warning? His earlier admonitions, such as those dealing with Jewish-Gentile relationships (Romans 14:3–4, 15; 15:7), seemed conciliatory. Now he is abrupt. Why? Does this warning differ in substance and style from his other admonitions? (See 2:1–5; 6:1–2; 9:19–20; 11:20; 14:15–16.) Is this sufficient ground to cast doubt on the authenticity of Romans 16:17–20, as some have suggested?

We may ask, What is the source of this danger? Is it internal or external? Does it agree with what we know about the Roman church? For example, Paul writes, “Everyone has heard about your obedience, so I am full of joy over you; but I want you to be wise about what is good, and innocent about what is evil” (Romans 16:19). The threat apparently arose from outside the church. Hendrikson observes, “Nowhere does the apostle say or imply that these troublemakers were members of the Roman church. They were probably outsiders, traveling propagandists of error” (1984, 510). Let us look more closely at Paul’s instructions concerning this danger.

I urge you, brothers (Romans 16:17). Paul appeals to the Romans to (1) watch out for those who cause divisions, (2) avoid those who serve their own interests and not the Lord’s, and (3) discern between good and evil.

Watch out for those who cause divisions and put obstacles in your way (Romans 16:17). Who are these troublemakers? Paul does not say. But his remark reminds us of the Judaizers (see Galatians 1:6–9; 3:1–5; 5:3–12). Had Paul detected a new effort to reemphasize legalism? Or does he anticipate a permissive teaching by the libertines, since he refers to those who serve “their own appetites”?

In his farewell address to the Ephesian elders, Paul warned about false teachers: “I know that after I leave, savage wolves will come in among you and will not spare the flock. Even from your own number men will arise and distort the truth in order to draw away disciples after them. So be on your guard! (Acts 20:29–31). The church—regardless of its location—should be alert at all times for troublemakers.

Keep away from them (Romans 16:17). Or, as the NKJV says, “avoid them.” Paul urges the Romans to watch out for those who are “not serving our Lord Christ” and to “stay away from” those who are “serving their own personal interests” (16:18, NLT). These people use “smooth talk” and “deceive the minds of naive people” through flattery.

“I want you to be wise about what is good, and innocent about what is evil” (Romans 16:19). The word innocent (Greek, akeraious ) in verse 19 means “simple” or “pure” and was used to describe undiluted wine (Comfort 1992). The New Living Translation says, “I want you to see clearly what is right and to stay innocent of any wrong.” J. B. Phillips paraphrases verse 19 this way: “I want to see you experts in good, and not even beginners in evil.”

How do we discern good from evil? Stott suggests three tests we can apply to doctrine and ethics: “Does it agree with Scripture? Does it glorify the Lord Christ? Does it promote goodness?” (1994, 400). The Bereans set a good example, as seen in Acts 17:11: “The people of Berea were more open-minded than those in Thessalonica, and they listened eagerly to Paul’s message. They searched the Scriptures day after day to check up on Paul and Silas, to see if they were really teaching the truth” (NLT).

“The God of peace will soon crush Satan under your feet” (Romans 16:20). Here is assurance that God’s people will triumph over troublemakers. Paul identifies Satan as the underlying source of divisions, obstacles, and false teachers; and he probably alludes to God’s promise in Genesis 3:15: the woman’s offspring (Christ) will crush the head of the serpent (Satan). This, of course, was accomplished at Calvary. Those who are in Christ should anticipate the same sort of victory—crushing Satan under their feet.

Greetings from Fellow Workers

Earlier, Paul sent personal greetings to friends and colleagues in Rome. Now he includes greetings from eight associates who are with him in Corinth as he writes.

“Timothy, my fellow worker, sends his greetings to you” (Romans 16:21). The mention of Timothy in the introduction of six of Paul’s letters reveals the apostle’s high regard for Timothy. Paul also addressed two letters to him (1 and 2 Timothy) while the younger man ministered in Ephesus. Paul wrote to the Philippians: “I have no one else like Timothy, who genuinely cares about your welfare . . . . [He] has proved himself. Like a son with his father, he has helped me in preaching the Good News” (Philippians 2:20–22, NLT).

Timothy, who was born in Lystra, was probably converted through Paul’s ministry. Paul calls him “my true son in the faith” (1 Timothy 1:2). His father was Greek and his mother Jewish. He joined Paul on the latter’s second missionary journey and became a constant companion, working closely with Paul in planting churches in Macedonia, Achaia, and Asia. Luke mentions Timothy with those who accompanied Paul to take the contribution for the poor to Jerusalem (Acts 20:4). He was with Paul during his Roman imprisonment and traveled some with him after Paul’s release (after the events of Acts 28). Then, Paul sent him to Ephesus to handle problems that had arisen there. We know that Timothy was imprisoned on at least one occasion (Hebrews 13:23). As the time of his departure drew near, Paul asked Timothy to join him in Rome (2 Timothy 4:6–9, 21).

“Lucius, Jason and Sosipater, my relatives” (Romans 16:21). Paul calls these fellow countrymen “my relatives.” Since these names were common and Paul gives little information about them, we cannot identify them with certainty. It stirs our curiosity about them. Is this the Lucius “of Cyrene” who worked with Paul in Antioch (Acts 13:1)? Is this the Jason with whom Paul stayed in Thessalonica? We know that a mob searched his house looking for Paul and Silas, and we know that he was forced to post a bond (Acts 17:5–9). Is this Sosipater the Berean church delegate who was also known as Sopater (Acts 20:4)? We may never know the details of their lives.

“Tertius” (Romans 16:22) served as Paul’s secretary and “wrote down this letter” as Paul dictated it to him. A Christian, he says, “I greet you in the Lord.” It was a common practice in rabbinic literature of that day for an amanuensis (secretary) to add his personal greeting. We may also ask whether Timothy occasionally served as Paul’s amanuensis. F. F. Bruce (1985) believes that the frequency of his name in Paul’s letters suggests this.

“Gaius” (Romans 16:23) is probably the Gaius whom Paul baptized at Corinth (1 Corinthians 1:14). Some scholars further identify him as Gaius Titius Justus (Acts 18:7) who lived next door to the synagogue (Stott 1994). He evidently opened his home to many believers, because Paul writes of him, “whose hospitality I and the whole church here enjoy” (Romans 16:23).

“Erastus, who is the city’s director of public works” (Romans 16:23). In 1929, archaeologists discovered a first-century marble paving stone with a Latin inscription saying, “Erastus, commissioner of public works, bore the expense of this pavement” (NIV Study Bible note on Romans 16:23, 1985, 1731). Since no other public official in Corinth named Erastus is known, scholars assume that he is the same person Paul names here (Edwards 1992). The inscription uses the word aedile , meaning “a commissioner of public works.” Some object to this title, however, noting that Paul uses the Greek word oikonomos , which means “treasurer.” Hence, the RSV translates 16:23 as “Erastus, city treasurer.” Bruce seeks to reconcile these differences by suggesting that Erastus was possibly promoted from the office of aedile to “city treasurer” by the time Paul wrote this letter (1985, 280).

“Our brother Quartus” (Romans 16:23). Undoubtedly, this fellow Christian had friends in Rome, but we know nothing else about him.

Doxology

Now to him who is able to establish you by my gospel and the proclamation of Jesus Christ, according to the revelation of the mystery hidden for long ages past, but now revealed and made known through the prophetic writings by the command of the eternal God, so that all nations might believe and obey him—to the only wise God be glory forever through Jesus Christ! Amen.

— Romans 16:25–27

“Now to him who is able to establish you by my gospel” (Romans 16:25). My gospel (Paul’s gospel; see 2:16) is the same one Peter and the other apostles preached. Paul personalizes it because he “received it by revelation from Jesus Christ” (Galatians 1:12).

The gospel is the power of God to save (Romans 1:16) and to establish (16:25). The word establish (Greek, sterizo ) means “to nourish and strengthen” new converts and new churches (Stott 1994, 403). Paul and his coworkers revisited churches they had planted in order to strengthen and encourage them (Acts 14:21–22; 15:41; 18:23). He also encouraged and strengthened the Thessalonians by prayer (2 Thessalonians 2:16–17) and by sending Timothy (1 Thessalonians 3:2).

Inherent in the gospel is a divine power to strengthen or establish believers. Essentially, the gospel is “the proclamation of Jesus Christ.” It is also “the revelation of the mystery hidden for long ages past” (Romans 16:25). We can understand God’s plan and purpose only as He makes it known to us. The gospel was concealed “for long ages past” but it is “now revealed and made known through the prophetic writings by the command of the eternal God, so that all nations might believe and obey him” (16:26). This mystery that was once hidden is now revealed and made known. God chose Christ before the world began, but He revealed Him in these final days (1 Peter 1:20). His purpose is clear: “so that all nations might believe and obey him” (Romans 16:26).

Paul condenses the major themes of his letter into this final doxology, as Stott points out: “The power of God to save and to establish; the gospel and the mystery, once hidden and now revealed, which are Christ crucified and risen; the Christ-centered witness of Old Testament Scripture; the commission of God to make the good news universally known; the summons to all the nations to respond with the obedience of faith and the saving wisdom of God, to whom all glory is due for ever” (1994, 406).

“To the only wise God be glory forever through Jesus Christ! Amen” (Romans 16:27). Christ reveals the only wise God, “in whom are hidden all the treasures of wisdom and knowledge” (Colossians 2:3). Calvary uncovers God’s wisdom. In the eyes of the world, it is foolishness, but it surpasses the wisest human plan. As Paul wrote, “we preach Christ crucified . . . the power of God and the wisdom of God” (1 Corinthians 1:23–24).

“You are worthy,” sang the twenty-four elders and four living creatures, “because you were slain, and with your blood you purchased men for God from every tribe and language and people and nation” (Revelation 5:9). Then John heard the voice of angels and a multitude that encircled the throne singing, “Worthy is the Lamb, who was slain, to receive power and wealth and wisdom and strength and honor and glory and praise!” (5:12).

“And now, because of Jesus Christ, we can praise the only wise God forever!” (Romans 16:27, CEV)

**Q:** This instruction has value for us today. What is the best way to defend against false teaching? What kind of outcome should we anticipate when we do this?

**A:** a) show kindness, such as feeding them if they are hungry. b) leave them alone and let God judge their actions. c) stand up for his or her own rights and show the adversaries where they are wrong. d) defend himself / herself no matter what it requires.

**Q:** What insights have we gained about the following fellow Christians who greeted the church in Rome: Timothy, Erastus, Tertius, Gaius?

**A:** a) know what is best for their citizens. b) can be trusted to make the right decisions. c) treat all citizens fairly, without prejudice. d) derive their authority from God.
